INTRODUCTION 
I started this book before the pandemic. I did a few interviewsÑMort Sahl, Whitney Cummings, Nathan Fielder, Gary GulmanÑbut I wasnÕt exactly putting a ton of time into it. Then the pandemic hit and I realized that most of the people I wanted to speak with were stuck at home with nothing to do, too. ItÕs hard to say no to an interview when it is clear you are available. We all were availableÑfor everything. 
So, I began making calls and lining up conversations. And as we talked, a weird thing happened: Many of these conversations became way more personal and honest than they otherwise might have been, because we were in this vulnerable, raw space together. ItÕs hard to hold back in an interview when you have been pondering your life (and death) for the past few months Ñwhen not overeating, drinking, or watching streaming programming you donÕt even like. 
Finishing this project was challenging. There were so many people I wanted to speak to, and I knew that as soon as the world calmed down it would become much harder to get access to them. As things opened up again, I was forced to give up my quest for Pete Townshend and Meghan Markle. Maybe for the next book. (Or the next pandemic?) 
ItÕs hard to write the intro to this book because I still feel so in between. I am not who I was before the pandemic began and yet I am not sure who I am now. I am, frankly, existentially confused. What meaning does my life have? What is the point of all of the work I have done? Why am I so disinterested and interested at the same time? How come I have become so close to my cats? Why do I keep getting more cats? Maybe the conversations in this book will shed some light on these questions. 
Other than my love for my family, the one consistent observation I have had, during all of this madness, is that I needed to laugh. I needed the insights of comic minds. I also was told by a lot of people that my work had given them brief, happy breaks from all we are experiencing, which was nice. I spent months getting those same breaks with Ted Lasso, SchittÕs Creek, Jackass, and anything by Maria Bamford. 
I have always seen comedy as a lifelineÑwhich is why IÕve been interviewing comedians about why they do what they do since I was fifteen years old. Without comedy, I donÕt know how I would survive. When the pandemic was at full force, I grabbed my family and made a really silly movie. I didnÕt know what else to do. Is that healthy? Is it denial? Is it medicine? Is it sick? I am not sure. But now I know that when the world seems to be collapsing my reaction is to make a movie about a group of people having a meltdown during a pandemic as they attempt to make a movie about flying dinosaurs. The process of making that film with my family got me through. It gave me purpose: to be ridiculous. IsnÕt it all ridiculous? It also got me out of the house and into a community of people with the same goalÑto make people smile. When the shit hits the fan that is all I have to offer. I may not know how to turn the gas off when the building is on fire, but I might be able to make you piss your pants. ThatÕs got to be worth something? 
I am still struggling. I donÕt feel right. But maybe if things get back to normal, and maybe after I do another book and make a few more ridiculous movies, I will feel right again. And the world will have some more weird stuff to read and some more stupid shit to watch with the shades closed as the world teeters outside. 

AMBER RUFFIN 
December 2020 
Judd: I am amazed at how strong so much of the political work that people like you and the other writers at Late Night with Seth Meyers are doing. IÕm astounded that people can take things that are so horrifying and find a way to turn it into something that has joy and entertainment in it. The country has always been a disaster, but itÕs hit another level these last few years. How has it been for you to develop this skill of political comedy at a time like this? 
Amber: IÕm always pressing right up against it. I think to myself, Can I talk about all of this stuff? And youÕre right, the ugly stuff has always been there. But I feel like IÕm slowly revealing it, little by little. Plus, theyÕll let me talk about all kinds of shit now. There are no rules. 
Judd: ItÕs really changed, the idea that you were allowed to say that things are fucked, right? 
Amber: You canÕt complain too much, no matter how true it is, or else youÕre a bad guy. So you have to choose what you swallow and what you call out. Luckily, IÕm in comedy so no one cares if you say something provocative. But for some normal lady in an office to be like, ÒIÕm not Black Linda. IÕm LindaÓ? And people go, ÒYeah, it was shitty of me to say thatÓ? 
Judd: I often think about the journey comedians go on, before they fully mature and come into their own. First, you have years of just figuring out how to get up onstage and not be terrified. Then, slowly, you get older and you realize what you want to talk about, now that you have the skills to talk about it. It takes a long time to get comfortable enough and to be strong enough to then move into what matters to you, whether itÕs politics and social issues or not. Did you have an arc like that, or did you talk about these things from day one? 
Amber: When I did the Second City Mainstage, we did all kinds of political stuff. We were performing for a young, liberal audience, and they loved it. But you canÕt do that with your regular improv show because you donÕt necessarily know the audience or the people youÕre improvising with. So, I certainly didnÕt start out making any provocative political points right off the bat. I also did Boom Chicago for years. Are you familiar with it? 
Judd: Jason Sudeikis started there. 
Amber: Jason Sudeikis, Seth Meyers, Jordan Peele, Kay CannonÉAnd actually, Boom Chicago is located in Amsterdam. ThereÕs no Boom Chicago in Chicago. But they audition you in Chicago, L.A., New York, and so on. Once youÕre picked, you go to Amsterdam, and you have four corporate shows a week. I hosted the International Paper Awards, for instance. And there are a number of game shows that are based in Holland, so when they have an idea and they want to shoot a pilot, they go to Boom Chicago, and they pick us. 
Judd: How long did you go for? I was recently talking to someone who did that for a year or two. 
Amber: Almost everyone was there for two years. 
Judd: ThatÕs so long. Do you get better at what you do in that environment? 
Amber: You become untouchable when it comes to writing, but itÕs a lot of short-form comedy. ThatÕs not everyoneÕs favorite form, but thatÕs all you need, fundamentally. All I need to do is be able to come up with a punch line under pressure; thatÕs all any comedy is. So, once you are doing nine, ten, eleven shows a week over there, you are sharpened by the audience. One time, I was performing for three hundred Italian sailors, and English was their third language. I realized if I could get them to laugh, then I can do anything. An experience like that knocks the fear right out. 
Judd: On some level, itÕs all terrifying, even when youÕre performing with other people. I remember I had to give out an award with Maya Rudolph at the Critics Choice Awards. And there were about seven thousand people in the crowd in L.A., at the Nokia Theatre. As we were walking up the stairs to the stage to do our bit, I had never seen anyone look more confident than Maya Rudolph. IÕm shitting a brick, thinking, Damn I hope this thing works. 
Amber: I think you have to have failed really loud, really big quite a few times. And it has to sting really bad. You have to lose some sleep over how bad it went. And that kind of failure can only happen to you a couple of times before you say to yourself, No more. 
Judd: Or till you start laughing. I remember Norm Macdonald used to enjoy the bomb. YouÕd see it in his eyes when it was happening. He just lit up. Did you study improv in college? 
Amber: I never went to college. I donÕt know how I could have ever gotten into college with my grades and lack of money. I also figured out that a lot of college was children talking instead of the teacher. There is no way I could have lasted there for one minute. Also, our drama teacher in high school would say, ÒPeople from now on are going to try to teach you how to do theater, but you canÕt learn unless you do it.Ó 
Judd: ThatÕs kind of arrogant of the teacher. He sort of told you guys, ÒI just taught you everything that you could ever learn about this.Ó 
Amber: He was cocky, and right. YouÕre created by the audience. 
Judd: What kind of improv group were you a part of in Omaha? 
Amber: It was the best little group of friends rehearsing and doing shows downtown together. We just did long-form improv. Everyone who came to see one of those shows is a saint. It was so bad. So bad. 
Judd: Did you think you would ever get anywhere else? 
Amber: Never. Firstly, everyone was white. But also, I just thought, When I get to perform at a tiny theater downtown, itÕll be the best. And I was right. Every time, it was a delight. I loved those people so much. We put on beautiful shows. But what did stick with me was when we would do a show that someone in that group wrote, I would always ask, ÒHow did you do that?Ó and they would always say, ÒI sat down and I wrote it.Ó That was interesting to me, but I also didnÕt think that it was actually possible. 
Judd: What was your day job when you were doing that at night? 
Amber: I was having people sign things downtown. I had a clipboard, and people would sign forms and stuff. I was selling them things, but out on the street. 
Judd: ThatÕs a form of improv. ThatÕs confrontational. You learn self-esteem right there, talking to strangers. When did you first make enough money from comedy that [it] allowed you to quit the day job? 
Amber: Never. IÕm still doing it. Would you buy a magazine? It was after I auditioned for Boom Chicago. 
Judd: And then you went to Amsterdam for two years. Did you join Second City when you came back? 
Amber: I had just finished up my second year in Amsterdam and had grown tired of it. So, I called Second City on the telephone and I asked, ÒWill you give me a job?Ó They said yes, and they moved me to Denver to do a Second City show there for a year. Then I went back to Chicago and did [Second City] Mainstage for two years, and then I went back to Boom Chicago for another three years. 
Judd: So, you did a total of five years in Amsterdam. At the end of the last year, was there a moment where you thought, IÕve been here long enough? 
Amber: I did think, Maybe I can have a career here. I was happy with the thought of living in Amsterdam forever. But I was very aware that, eventually, I was going to be an old Black ladyÑand IÕve never seen an old Black lady do improv. So, I got married to a Dutch guy and then moved to Los Angeles to make it as an actress. 
Judd: And how did that go? 
Amber: Horribly. It was truly terrible. I hate auditioning. IÕm bad at it. 
Judd: Kevin Hart would always say, ÒIÕm just trying to make this casting person laugh so they remember me for something at some point. This audition is just for me to show you IÕm talented, I donÕt even care if I donÕt get the part.Ó But I know so few people who could emotionally pull that off. It is a soul-crushing experience, because you also think to yourself, I could do hundreds of these and never work the rest of my life. 
Amber: Absolutely. 
Judd: Did you have to bail on L.A., or did you get something that kept you there for a little bit? 
Amber: While I was in L.A., I was a part of a theater called Sacred Fools Theater. We all just wrote stuff and put it up all the time. That gave me life. It was a really nice thing to hold on to. Then I got the call to audition for SNL. But I didnÕt get it, and I felt like I could have died. I donÕt really know what this says about me, but I thought for sure I was going to get the job. I was so certain, which is so unlike me. 
Judd: Did you feel like you had a good audition? 
Amber: Yeah, it was great. But I went up just as Leslie Jones was finishing. And as IÕm coming in, people are still laughing. SheÕs yards and yards away, sheÕs done with her audition, and people are still laughing. 
Judd: ThatÕll hurt your confidence, just stepping into the energy of that room. But you still had a good audition. 
Amber: I had a good audition. Four of us got to stay and watch the show and talk to everyone so they can see if youÕre crazy or not. And the other three womenÑSasheer Zamata, Leslie Jones, and LaKendra TookesÑall got hired. 
Judd: How soon after that audition did Seth call you about his show? 
Amber: Three days after I heard about SNL, Seth called me and asked, ÒWill you do my show?Ó We knew each other previously, too. He would come to Boom Chicago every year and would see me up there. So, he knew who I was, and I knew who he was. 
Judd: How do you look at the SNL audition in retrospect? Do you go, Oh, thatÕs a missed opportunity? 
Amber: Not so much, because at Late Night, everyone is very kind and supportive. IÕm not going to say that they coddle you, but they do a little bit. 
Judd: SethÕs a special type of leader. IÕve heard people talk about him as the best head writer ever at SNL. His people felt supported and encouraged. He specifically creates an environment where people do their best work. 
Amber: And thatÕs all he wants. Some managers think itÕs important for you to show up on time and format your scripts a specific way, or else. ItÕs important that you never make a mistake and always have your this and your that. But for Seth, you get the sense that all he wants is our best work, and that however heÕs going to get itÑif you have to be ten minutes late to work every dayÑhe doesnÕt care. And if a script isnÕt perfect, he doesnÕt careÑthe script department will help fix it. Seth is just like, ÒGet me my laughs.Ó And I like that. 
Judd: ItÕs a different environment also because youÕre featuring yourself. Your ideas are not watered down; youÕre able to come up with things for yourself, such as characters and sketches. It doesnÕt sound like youÕre at war with the other writers, competing to get stuff on the air. That kind of competition can crush people. 
Amber: I donÕt know that I would have been up for the challenge. I think they might have been able to shout me down. And I would have easily been like, ÒYeah, yeah, let me write for you. You want something?Ó I think I could have become that person. ItÕs scary to think. 
Judd: Now youÕre doing your own show. How do you split your time between writing your own show and writing for Seth? 
Amber: IÕm always writing as much as possible. Sometimes what I write goes into the Seth pile, and sometimes it goes into the Ruffin pile. I would love to be the kind of person who says, I specifically think of this for this show, and bada boom! But my process looks more like, Which of these ideas make it to the end of the sketch? Okay, those are the ideas that are good to go. Which one of these should I hand in to Seth, and which one should I keep? I do it like that. 
Judd: You started your show without a live audience. YouÕre completely exposed because thereÕs no support of an audience or the feedback of their laughter. But instead, it looks like youÕre creating this intimate connection with the audience at home, I have no tricks. ItÕs me and you, right here. How did that feel to launch? What was your personal take on how to even approach a challenge like this? 
Amber: The Amber Ruffin Show started after months and months of quarantine. We had already been doing shows from home, so I had already done bits from my couch. Once we started, and we got to shoot from the studio, I thought, I love this. IÕm not on my couch anymore. I never missed the audience, even though I was looking at two hundred empty seats. It doesnÕt feel weird. Performing in front of an audience feels like a million years ago. And comics always say, ÒOh, you miss the laughs.Ó But you know what? I donÕt miss the silence. No one talks about that, about what it feels like to do a joke and it just doesnÕt land. I know IÕm going to get wrecked when the audience comes back in there. IÕll be standing out on the edge of a punch line to nothing. ItÕs going to be horrible. 
Judd: You must write differently when you know you donÕt have to worry if a joke bombs, because as long as you believe in the joke, it doesnÕt matter, right? I wonder what itÕll feel like for everybody when showbiz returns. Will people be better? Will it be richer in some way? 
Amber: Everything is getting so weird. We have built this whole show in quarantine thus far. If we ever get the chance to do it in front of an audience, will they be like, We donÕt like this. You already sang a song, what are you doing? Who knows how people are going to react? IÕm also scared that when we get an audience, IÕm just going to be hamming it up and go way off script because IÕm just feeling the vibe. ThatÕs entirely possible, too. Who knows? I just know itÕs going to be a mess. 
Judd: Did you feel like you learned something from the lack of a crowd? When you were doing the bits about racism and police brutality, it seemed like it would have felt different with a crowd. Maybe you would have felt the need to be funny, but the lack of crowd may have allowed it to be exactly what it was. 
Amber: It was certainly an exercise in learning that we donÕt always have to go up there and tell a bunch of jokes. Sometimes, you can just be honest. You donÕt have to say something you wrote, rewrote, and then rewrote again. You can just say your true feelings. I kind of already knew that that was true, but I just didnÕt know it was true to that extent. 
Judd: People had such a strong reaction to your stories about everyday racism and your interactions with the police. 
Amber: IÕve worked here for six years, going on seven. And over that time, IÕve maybe received four pieces of fan mail. When I got back to the office after doing that show, there was a huge stack of cards from white people who said, ÒI cannot believe this, I cannot believe that this happened to you. This really makes me take a hard look at what the world is like for other people. It makes me look back at my life and remember all the times where I believed the police instead of the people.Ó People just really understood it. They took what I said, and they wrung all the truth out of it. I couldnÕt believe it. 
Judd: What is your process when youÕre working on a bit like that, where itÕs funny, but itÕs also about this really urgent, weighty issue? 
Amber: When George FloydÕs murder happened, I immediately wrote a sketch and turned it in. Less than twenty-four hours later, the whole country shifted, and I realized that I couldnÕt do that sketch anymore. I thought, Let me write a rant, instead. A real mean rant where I stick it to everybody. But by the time IÕd written that rant, everything else changed again. The whole thing felt crazy. And while there was a national outcry, there were some people who were saying that police brutality just wasnÕt that big, or widespread, of a problem. There were some people saying that George FloydÕs death was one isolated incident, and that just made me feel crazy. So, then I pitched to the showrunners that I tell a story, just a real story from my own life. And they said okay. I recorded it, they played it at the top of the showÑwhich I didnÕt expectÑand it was ultimately well received. Afterward, I went to my colleagues and said, ÒHey, how many of these do you want to do? I have a million of these stories.Ó 
Judd: That experience is a great example of how comedy can be hugely impactful. But there has also always been that element of comedy thatÕs just plain stupidÑa break from the ugliness of the real world. When Trump lost the election, for example, I wondered if some people in comedy thought, Thank God. I just want to do some fart jokes for a year or two. Your show is very much focused on the news, and I think weÕre still going to be deeply polarized going forward. Has this experience changed your thoughts about the purpose of comedy? 
Amber: My goal with my show is always to de-gaslight a nation. ThatÕs always the first goal. Wait, sorry, thatÕs always the second goal. My first goal is to get them sweet, sweet laughs. Second goal is de-gaslight a nation. But comedy is just telling the truth. Eventually weÕll get to a point in our society where weÕre going to be able to say, ÒThis horrible thing happened, but this is how I feel about it. And this is a part of our history. LetÕs fix it.Ó But to get to your question, I also think comedy is whatever people want it to be. IÕm sure thereÕs someone out there right now farting into a microphone, and their friends love it. ThatÕs fine, too. I donÕt think comedy has to just be commentary about whatÕs happening in the world. Not everybody has to do that. Not everybody has time for it. Not everybody feels like doing it. But we canÕt all just be one thing. I need to be able to watch a dinosaur slam-dunk a basketball. And I need to be able to watch someone satirize what is happening. IÕve got to have all of it. And even though Trump lost the election, I donÕt think weÕre going to go back to the way things were before he came into the office: You canÕt unring this bell. We know too much now, as a society. Everyone feels like itÕs not enough to just not be racist. WeÕve got to be antiracist. And my hope is that, going forward, when people see racist shit, theyÕll call it out for what it is, and that will just be a regular part of our reality. 
Judd: People are wrong to think of entertainment and show business as unilaterally progressive. YouÕre the first Black woman with a political comedy talk show, and itÕs 2020. Historically, talk shows almost never had female writersÑeven some of our favorite people took forever to hire a woman on the writing staff. 
Amber: There was Whoopi Goldberg and Wanda Sykes and Robin Thede. Other shows had Black female writers, like ChappelleÕs Show. But in terms of network TV, then yes, itÕs true that I was the first Black woman writer with a show. 
Judd: WeÕre now seeing a sea change when it comes to representation both onscreen and throughout the business, where everyoneÕs like, ÒWe canÕt do this anymore, we canÕt just hire white men.Ó It feels very real right now, and it feels as though it will stick. 
Amber: Yes, and also, the word is out about how good Black women are at the job, no matter what that job is. The only true stereotype about Black women is how Black women will say, ÒWell, no oneÕs doing it, so I guess IÕll do it.Ó 
Once you put that lady in your office, no matter what your office is, if you treat her right, she is going to do your job and her job. And I think people are slowly realizing that. So, I agree with you that this shift in representation, this shift in hiring practices, might just stick around, because once you create a safe space for Black people at your job, which a lot of television shows are capable of doing now, then you see Black people excel. It really is electrifying. 
Judd: So many comedians are sensitive to being criticized when theyÕre awful. TheyÕll defend themselves by saying, ÒOh, IÕm just working that premise out.Ó Obviously we all believe in freedom of speech, but it seems like comedians in particular donÕt want to be criticized when they say things that are wrong or hurtful. They can be the most thin-skinned people in the world in that regard. In this moment, you do feel the division in the comedy world. There are the free-speechers who are like, ÒLeave me alone. IÕm allowed to make mistakes.Ó And then there are other people who want to be sure that comedians are tuning in to these cultural shifts, where weÕre trying to figure out what hurts people and what makes people feel seen. 
Amber: I havenÕt had to make a public apology yet, but I think I can imagine what that would look like. I think if I said something that hurt a bunch of peopleÕs feelings, I would hate it, because thatÕs not my goal. That is severely off-brand for me. IÕm trying to give everybody a hug, and I donÕt want to hurt peopleÕs feelingsÑunless theyÕre racist, of course. I do believe that you do not have to be nice to people who do not value you as a human being. When someone treats you like shit, raise your hand and say something. We have to shake off that conditioning that taught us that we canÕt do anything about mistreatment. I canÕt act like youÕre a nice person if you donÕt care if my workplace is safe, if you donÕt care if I canÕt breathe because a police officer is using excessive force. I want people to think about what it must do to minorities to be told that they are second-class citizens by their president and then to be expected to look at his supporters in the face and smile. That is not good for you, and you donÕt have to be part of it. 

BOWEN YANG 
June 2021 
Judd: I want to talk about Las Culturistas, the podcast youÕre doing with Matt Rogers. How did you two meet? 
Bowen: Matt and I met in college. We were both in the closet at NYU, which was just such a funny place to be in the closet, especially circa 2008. I was in the improv group, and he was in the sketch group, and we were both just obsessed with comedy. Recent alums of note from NYU at that point were, like, Donald Glover and D.C. Pierson, and we were just like, ÒThis is the future.Ó And we put in the work early on and just started to write. My awful snobbery with people who are working in comedy is This canÕt be your plan B. 
Judd: Sometimes I go to a comedy club and see all these hilarious people onstage, and I wonder if theyÕve ever sat down and written a sketch or a screenplay. Or taken the effort to ask themselves, What else can I do with my talent and ideas? 
Bowen: I think I heard the legend that you told Bill Hader, maybe during his second season of SNL, that he had to start putting the wheels in motion then, so that things will bear fruit further down the line. 
Judd: Absolutely. And I did it with Bill, where he worked on a screenplay for us and with some writers from SNL, and we just never cracked it. That screenplay was never made. But we did write a lot together. He said that he was writing like seven screenplays over the course of his time at SNL, of which none were made. But that was all his rehearsal for Barry. He was learning what to do. You have to write, because nobody will be able to write the thing for you that you probably should be doing. ItÕs daunting to figure out, How does this character or persona work in a full story, in a full movie? That has been the challenge for some SNL people: ItÕs about figuring out who you are separate from your characters. Once you get past that initial challenge, thereÕs still the precariousness of the movie business, where you could commit the time to get a movie going, and you still donÕt know if someone will want to make it happen. 
Bowen: All of that runs counter to the SNL process, where thereÕs such an efficiency with how things are made. Nothing gets made like that anymore except for summer stock theater. IÕm getting a little too used to this. But even now IÕm realizing, Oh the credits donÕt transfer from SNL, because none of it is made like this. And also, the performance style is youÕre pitching to the rafters and youÕre screaming and youÕre reading off a cue card. ThatÕs not how it works anywhere else. 
Judd: It must be an interesting moment for you. When I first started, I felt unrepresented in Hollywood. It was like, Why canÕt the goofy, unattractive person ever be the lead? So, in my work over the years, I tried to prove that that was possible, because the non-cool guy as the lead wasnÕt available in the culture when I was young. Now our culture has evolved to where people are trying to correct for the fact that performers of color and LGBTQ+ performers have been criminally underrepresented. What does this moment feel like for you? 
Bowen: I think, for a long time, Asian people would be used as an existential punch line. Now whatÕs happening is that, on a collective scale, everyoneÕs kind of getting on the same pageÑweÕre catching up to some baseline understanding about what the material realities are for Asians in the business. And I donÕt know where thatÕs going yet; I have no foresight into what it will be like. This is all kind of a new thing, and IÕm trying not to erase and destroy what people like Margaret Cho have accomplished, or what Alec Mapa has done as a queer Asian comedian. All the groundwork has been laid out by that generation; IÕm just benefiting from coming in at the right time. ThatÕs basically how I feel about being at SNL, too. ItÕs like, Oh I lucked out so, so, so deeply. 
Judd: Do you feel like you have a responsibility to use your position? 
Bowen: I approach every week at SNL without a care for how something will be received on a social level. The week after the Atlanta shooting in March, I called the one other Asian writer on the show. We got on the phone and were just in a terrible mood, because everything around that story was so bleak. But we asked each other, ÒHow do we want to approach this? Do we address it at all? Do we take a hard left and just not even think about that, and do something that has nothing to do with what happened?Ó By Saturday, the end result of those conversations was this kind of strident rallying cry, a ÒWeekend UpdateÓ piece that wasnÕt particularly funny, but it did bring levity to the situation in whatever way it could. And I had such a weird hangover the next week about it being received well. People were very kind about it, but I was like, I donÕt know, I donÕt want this to be my role at the show. 
Judd: You didnÕt want to be like Jon Stewart after 9/11 every week. 
Bowen: Even for just one week. Jon Stewart shouldnÕt even be in that position, you know? 
Judd: The people who are doing political comedy these days have to be the commentators on tragedy and the worst of humanity. ItÕs such a strange thing thatÕs happened in comedy, and people are brilliant at it. But I know what you mean. I canÕt even imagine being in their shoes, thinking, This shooting just happened, let me try out this piece. 
Bowen: Well, the thought was I donÕt have all the answers, that I shouldnÕt even be doing this. Afterward, I really couldnÕt make sense of how I felt about it. Then, two weeks later, I did another ÒWeekend UpdateÓ piece where I was the iceberg who sank the Titanic, and that was just purely me doing my job. It was me co-writing this piece with one of the head writers, Anna Drezen, and creating a fun moment of pure absurdity. Yet the social and political comedy is something that you have to do, especially at a show like SNL, and especially now where thereÕs a dearth of Asian people who have this big of a platform. So, I donÕt bemoan it. ItÕs just an interesting thing to think about. 
Judd: You came to America as a kid, and you didnÕt speak English that well because you were still learning the language, but you obviously had a sense of humor. Did you realize at some point, ThatÕs my thing, IÕll be the funny person? Because thatÕs how I felt as a kid. I was terrible at sports, I didnÕt think I was as attractive as the people around meÑI guess making people laugh is my thing, because no one else is trying to do it. Was it similar for you? 
Bowen: It was exactly like youÕre describing it, where I realized, Humor can be my thing. I would come into school on Mondays and ask everyone, ÒDid you guys watch SNL or MADtv from Saturday?Ó And some people would be like, ÒYeah.Ó Most kids would be like, ÒNo, what are you talking about? My parents donÕt let me watch that yet.Ó I had these immigrant parents who had no idea what the programming was. So, beyond it being my identity where I was funny and goofy, there was also this extra layer on top where itÕll be my thing to nerd out on comedy, too, which at that age just meant watching SNL every week. That became the keystone for my identity early on, and then I built concentric circles from there. 
Judd: As you were watching SNL as a kid, did you ever look at the cast and think, IÕm going to be on there someday? 
Bowen: I have to be very honest with myself and say I never specifically said the words, ÒI will be on SNL someday.Ó But I did have this feeling that it would work out somehow. 
Judd: If you have that confidence and drive, then you also know that you need to be ahead of the curve. You know you need to work harder than everybody 
to realize that dream. 
Bowen: Totally. My little secret pet peeve now is the people who are getting into comedy as a fallback. People who were musical theater majors who didnÕt want to audition and didnÕt want to do Broadway because that is a whole crazy world of its own. There are those sorts of people who go, Well, IÕll just do comedy instead. And IÕm like, ÒUh, it doesnÕt work like that.Ó I put forward this crazy purity test for those people, where I was like, ÒNo, you didnÕt write down who the writers were on this one episode of The Office as it was airing.Ó I just got so snobbish about that, and I need to unlearn that now, because I canÕt put those gates up for other people who want to get into it through their own means, through their own journey. 
Judd: When you went to college, was it your familyÕs dream that you would go into comedy? 
Bowen: No. 
Judd: Did your parents see this career as an option for you at all, even if they didnÕt love it for you at first? You did premed for a bit. Did you eventually open up to them and say, ÒIÕd rather do stand-upÓ? 
Bowen: My parents could tell that I took comedy seriously even when I was in high school. It was a very big thing for me as a fifteen-year-old to be a part of this short-form improv group in high school. I thought it was the best thing in the world. I would go downtown every week on Mondays, perform with a bunch of thirty-year-olds, and think, This is the thing that I enjoy the most. And my parents could tell I was enjoying it too much. And they had tried to be likeÑ 
Judd: ÒDonÕt have joy.Ó 
Bowen: Not even that. I had expressed many times before that I wanted to be an actor someday. And my parents were like, ÒWell, Bowen, thatÕs really hard, because there arenÕt a lot of Asian ones, and that world is full of rejection.Ó They werenÕt even being domineering; they were just being protective. It was their version of loving me. But when it came time for me to decide on a college, I would visit these campuses and ask them, ÒCan you tell me about your sketch team here?Ó ThatÕs essentially what I based my whole college decision on, and I ended up at NYU. I knew all of the peopleÕs names in the improv group by the April before I matriculated. And then I was just obsessed. Everyone around me knew this is what I wanted to do. But I had to take cover behind the premed thingÑand itÕs so ironic that I was such a jerk about being a purist in terms of pursuing comedy, because my backup plan was medicine. 
Judd: You just want to yell at all the other people who are being distracted. 
Bowen: My distraction was academics. The thing that got me out of bed every morning was the excitement of seeing everyone in my troupe later that day, and that we would run sets, and do this and that. That improv group was my life. 
Judd: Did it make your grades better or worse? 
Bowen: Worse, oh worse. 
Judd: You would have been a terrible, distracted doctor. When did you stop doing premed? 
Bowen: I graduated with my degree, and I had my applications in for medical school. And I was taking my MCAT for the second time, and there was the short-answer portion. When I got to that, I remembered the Steve Carell interview where he said that he was thinking of applying to law school after things werenÕt working out for him acting-wise or comedy-wise, but then he got to the essay portion of the LSAT, and he realized then that he couldnÕt do it. He couldnÕt follow through on this law school thing. And then, at that moment, I had this out-of-body experience, thinking about Steve Carell, and zoomed back down to me, and it was just like, Oh, this is whatÕs happening to me right now. I canÕt do this. I voided my score, left the testing center, and called my parents. I was like, ÒI donÕt think I can go to med school. IÕll figure out how to stay in the city and live in the city and work in the city and make money, but med school isnÕt going to happen.Ó And they were very confused. To their credit, they gave me a shot to figure it out within two years. 
Judd: ThatÕs a big biopic moment, walking out of the testing center in protest. IsnÕt it funny that you have to take it that far, till your entire mind and body break down in the middle of the test. Why do you think your parents gave you a chance to figure things out after that? 
Bowen: The Big Sick was the first time that we watched a movie that helped my parents understand what I was doing, because all the scenes where heÕs doing stand-up, IÕm like, ÒThatÕs what IÕm trying to do, Mom and Dad.Ó And they were like, ÒThatÕs interesting,Ó and that was a little proof of concept for them. So, I do my best to not retroactively make my parents out to be people who never rooted for me or who counted on me to fail. 
Judd: Did they find you funny, though? 
Bowen: No, thereÕs just a big cultural difference between being funny at school with a bunch of kids who speak English and then going home to your parents, who grew up in Communist China. Humor can be culturally specific, and I wasnÕt nimble enough to pivot between those two worlds. So, I would come home [and] just be this shy, boring kid. 
Judd: When was the moment when you would say, ÒCome to the improv showÓ? 
Bowen: It was in high school, and I donÕt know if it helped or hurt my case. Because they went, and they didnÕt get it. They were like, ÒWhat is this? We donÕt understand.Ó Then, at one point during college, they came to see one of my sketch shows at [University of California, Berkeley], and they were like, ÒWe didnÕt like that. ThatÕs not for us.Ó 
Judd: They were just honest. 
Bowen: They just said, ÒWhat are you doing?Ó And to be fair, I would have rated it as, like, a ÒsixÓ of a show. It wasnÕt until Matt and I did this quick little bit on Fallon. My mom went into work the next day, and her coworkers were like, ÒOh my God, arenÕt you so proud of Bowen for being on TV last night?Ó It took other people to mirror it to her that her son was doing well. Then she was like, ÒOkay, maybe this will work out.Ó 
Judd: For me, my dream to get into comedy wasnÕt questioned. My family was like, ÒYeah, go to that as a career.Ó Even though I had no confidence in anything else in life, I had a delusional confidence in that. 
Bowen: I love that for comedy. Either your dream to pursue it is challenged or itÕs reinforced and supported. Either way, we both end up in the same place where we love comedy. I always love telling people my whole story with my parents and how they have come around only recently. But I also donÕt think the environment you were raised in, and how comedy figured into it, really makes a difference. If you love it, you love it, and you pursue it. And it has to be a sustained, lifelong thing. You canÕt have a cursory engagement with it later on in life. 
Judd: Your story is so interesting because now you come across as a very bold performer and a very confident performer, but you fought against a lot of judgment. ThatÕs the hardest part: to be doubted or to feel a lack of engagement from your parental figures when youÕre still in your formative years. 
Bowen: Sure, and IÕm still working through this in therapy, where I donÕt know how much of it had to do with what it meant to just be a gay kid in the early aughts. All the queer people I know are, in some way, a little damaged from just the process of understanding themselves to be queer. ThereÕs this bell hooks quote about how itÕs not queerness in terms of who I have sex with or what gender I am. ItÕs queerness in terms of living in a world where things are hostile toward you. And I think thatÕs it. I think it transcends my weird little relationship with my parents. 
Judd: Is it now possible to feel close and happy and celebrate this with them? 
Bowen: For sure. Definitely. My mom came to the show on MotherÕs Day, and we just all paraded out our moms. And then we got lunch with my dad the next day, and their whole thing to impart on me was like, ÒYouÕre able to do this, but you should realize and remember that this is very lucky, very fortuitous.Ó I was like ÒYeah, of course.Ó And then, ironically enough, Lorne [Michaels] said the same thing to me at that dinner: to just enjoy and appreciate the moment. 
Judd: When youÕre doing something thatÕs working on SNL, do you feel how good it is in the moment? Or are you so focused that you donÕt get the enjoyment till afterward? 
Bowen: The only time thatÕs happened has been with the iceberg bit. I knew at the time, and I was like, IÕm having so much fun. And: Thank God the audience is on board. Because every other time IÕve done something on the show, itÕs only been thinking ahead to: How are people going to respond? How do I look? Are they going to use this on my reel when I die? So, thatÕs been the only time. And I donÕt mean to reduce it down to a mindfulness thing, but itÕs something where you just feel your feet, you feel the weight of yourself, where you are, and that is enough to bring you to the moment. Right now, IÕm tired, but IÕm still recouping from just how exhausting the last season was. But I am really grateful to have done it. I keep wanting to frame things as luck. 
Judd: What season is this as a performer on the show? 
Bowen: IÕve just finished my second season as a performer. And I had written one season prior to that. 
Judd: The show has also never been as multicultural as it is now, and it looks like itÕs going to keep moving in that direction. That makes it very exciting to watch. 
Bowen: I keep thinking about SNL as this monolithic thing, but then I remember that it aired in a completely different cultural environment when [Adam] Sandler and [David] Spade and Chris Rock and [Chris] Farley were on. I canÕt believe that is the same container as what it is now. Or SNL in the mid-aughts, when it was [Andy] Samberg and [Bill] Hader and [Kristen] Wiig. Even that feels like a completely different show, too. So often, youÕre only thinking of the showÕs highlight reel, where itÕs just the best stuff, which gives the illusion that itÕs always at a certain quality level. But really, SNL has always been this weird little petri dish of ideas, and itÕs just stood on the counter in the lab for all these decades. I love the fact that I donÕt have that much in common with Michael Che in terms of our backgrounds and our approaches to writing and what weÕve seen in the comedy world thus far. We came from completely different poles. But now he and I have some common ground as far as working at the show goes. We had this really nice moment at the finale, where he brought everyone to his apartment at, like, four A.M., and he and I just had a nice little heart-to-heart. And I couldnÕt believe it. I would never have expected to get this from Michael Che, of all people. Yet, there he was, with this, like, sageness that I never would have thought that I could have access to. 
Judd: This was in appreciation of the show and your work for the year? 
Bowen: Just for each other, in the way that we both look to each other, like, 
Hey, IÕm so glad that we experienced this pressure cooker of a show together. 

CAMERON CROWE 
September 2020 
Judd: What is that line in Almost Famous, like, ÒYou are home?Ó 
Cameron: That was improv. A Judd-Apatow-at-the-side-of-the-camera move. 
Judd: When were you shooting that movie? 
Cameron: Ninety-nine. 
Judd: IÕm shooting Freaks and Geeks at the same time, and I was very aware that you were making a movie that I was so desperate to see. Because your movies before then, Fast Times at Ridgemont High and Say AnythingÉ, taught me how to express my experience of being young in a movie. 
Cameron: Do you remember Over the Edge, with Matt Dillon? ItÕs like the forgotten cousin. ItÕs preÐJohn Hughes, and itÕs about being young from a young personÕs point of view, and it goes to a crazy place. ThatÕs what taught me how to capture being young in a movie. I was like, Oh, wow, you can do this. And that gave us Fast Times. But the studio always said, ÒYouÕre never going to have a movie that will work and be seen by an audience if itÕs just for kids.Ó It needs to be American Graffiti; then youÕll get the audience. We were told that you need the Ray Walston crowd for it to be a hit. But Over the Edge was like somebody bled to make that movie, and Matt Dillon is amazing in it. 
Judd: I paid close attention, especially to Say Anything. For some reason, it was that and the kid who works at the movie theater in Fast Times. What was that characterÕs name? 
Cameron: The Rat? 
Judd: I really recognized the sweetness of that kid at the movie theater dreaming of being with the pretty girl or the cool girl and going, Am I allowed in this universe to believe I can be loved by her? 
Cameron: When the Rat leaves Jennifer Jason Leigh and does not follow up and kiss her and stay there, as heÕs walking down the walkway, having fucked up, half of the audience feels bad for him and half of them wants to throw shit at the screen because he was a wimp. 
Judd: On Freaks and Geeks, we always talked about cocky nerds. YouÕre a nerd, and everyone can beat the shit out of you, and youÕre not an athlete, but on some level you think, IÕm better than them. IÕm always amused by the parallels of our high school experience in terms of loving somethingÑmusic for you, comedy for meÑand going, I need to connect to it. Not just, IÕm gonna listen to the records, but IÕm gonna go fucking talk to Gregg Allman. I did that, but it might have been Steve Allen or Jerry Seinfeld. What do you think it is that made us take that leap? 
Cameron: My mom would say that we had magic boots on, that our fervor for this thing that moved us so much pushed us out the door to find the people behind it and complete the fan experience and interview them. We needed to learn about, as a young writer not looking to be a character or an adversary, but a frontline emotional reporter, this feeling that we love so much. ThatÕs how it felt for me. I just wanted to get closer to the flame. And I was always worried that somebody was going to be a dick and say, ÒWhat are you doing here?Ó That only happened once. Mostly people were just really grateful for the worshipful interest. 
Judd: I remember walking in to interview Jerry Seinfeld in 1983 or Õ84. His apartment had nothing on the walls, no books in the bookshelvesÑhe was just there to write his jokes. And he looked at me when I walked in like, I canÕt believe I have to do this with this child. But, ultimately, he felt my love for the craft and what he did, and he started enjoying it and letting me know how it worked. ItÕs funny that even in rock and roll, which you think would be more intimidating than nerdy comedians, they sense your knowledge and your affection for it. 
Cameron: The Allman Brothers [Band] experiences, the first time I went on the roadÉI couldnÕt believe that all these people were talking to me. There had been a Rolling Stone reporter named Grover Lewis who went on the road with the Allman Brothers Band and did a hit piece on Duane and GreggÑ basically called them crackers. That band was really wounded by that and did not want to talk to Rolling Stone, but for some reason, Dickey Betts let me in, and then Gregg started talking to me. But the night before I left, Gregg freaked out and wanted all the tapes back. This kid could be from the FBI and be here to bust us. So, that really is the scene in Almost Famous, where IÕm in the airport and I run into my sister, who I havenÕt seen in a while, and sheÕs like, ÒWhat are you doing here? YouÕre a wreck.Ó That moment for me was like, I have no magic boots. I am a fool. Everything IÕve ever thought of myself and everything that anybody ever said to me at school that was brutal and vicious is true. And itÕs over. I went home a failure, and it was photographer Neal Preston who went on the tour and got my tapes back from Gregg. I never called Rolling Stone, because they would have fired me for even having had that happen, much less not having written about it yet. So, I owe everything to them getting those tapes back from Gregg, because I would have never had another shot at Rolling Stone. It would have been over. I probably would have been a poor, untalented lawyer, because my heart would have been with music. 
Judd: Who was your first mentor? Was it Lester Bangs? Who was the first person that actually walked you through it? 
Cameron: There were a number of them. Ben Fong-Torres was one. Bill Maguire, the editor of the San Diego Door. He was pretty hilarious. Robert Hilburn was another person that gave me an assignment but said, ÒYouÕre on a road here where you could be the victim of publicists. Be careful.Ó But Lester was so charismatic and such a big figure that you got a physical buzz around him. You knew that whether it was part performance or charisma or passion or whatever, this was a full-blooded dude that you wanted to be like, so he was probably the most all-purpose mentor. Until Jim Brooks. 
Judd: Do you remember any of the things he told you about the actual writing? 
Cameron: He said, ÒHey, man, sometimes I stay up all night. I just drink a little cough syrup, and I just write.Ó This little underground paper in San Diego, they had this black cabinet, which was files and articles and promotional things that had been sent to them. I was looking through it, because they let me hang out in their communal office, and I found this big fucking wad of stuff that was in a file. I started looking at it, and it was all reviews by Lester Bangs. Unsolicited, just shit that he had written in those hallowed nights where he was just writing to write. HeÕd write them on the back of promotional bios, eight-by-tens of Bruce Lee and shitÑon anything thatÕs nearby. None of them were published. He would delete going backward and just writing xxxxx over the sentence he had just written, so it was all in a rush, and I read those things and it was like, This is the shit. This is like a guy opening up his veins. Not doing it for any agenda except to get it out of his system and put it in the mail and send it to this little newspaper. You got to love writing to do this. You got to love it. And nobody can say, ÒNo, this is not the right job for you.Ó Nobody can say that, because you turned on the spigot out of pure love, and itÕs coming out of you now. That was the biggest lesson of Lester. 
Judd: I always say, ÒItÕs so weird that you get paid to make movies and make comedy,Ó because thereÕs no scenario [in which] I wouldnÕt do it. It just happens to be something that thereÕs a marketplace for. You could love something, like playing the spoons, and you never make any money, but you got to play the spoons. I always felt that way in my gut. This is the thing that IÕm supposed to do. I thought that on some level from the time I was nine or ten years old. I was fascinated by these people, but also fascinated by rock and roll and jazz. My grandfather Bobby Shad produced the first Janis Joplin record. 
Cameron: I love that story. 
Judd: They just talked about it all the time, ÒJanis was the best,Ó but also, ÒJanis fucked it all up,Ó ÒShe died from drugs, donÕt do drugs.Ó So, it was both the thing that inspired me, like, My grandfather was a hustler who found Janis Joplin, and at the same time, this thing that was scary, like, Oh, you can die from this. They also loved comedy; they would go see Lenny Bruce. There were a lot of records around, but for them, it was mainly jazz and blues, which I didnÕt understand as a little kid. I understood the Beatles. I was born in Õ67, so I didnÕt have any sense of the world existing without the Beatles. When I think about how popular they must have been when I was three, it must have just been everything. It just programs your mind. I was readingÑyou were talking about Joni Mitchell as being someone that represented that to you in a lot of ways: This is the kind of person that I am just completely hypnotized by. 
Cameron: I remember one of the first times we got to bring rock into the house wasÑmy mom liked Dick Cavett, you know, because he was an intellectual. So, we would watch Dick Cavett, and he had an amazing show that he did on the night of Woodstock. It was just supposed to be Joni Mitchell, but some of the Grateful Dead, and Jefferson Airplane, and Crosby, and all these people showed up. But I was mesmerized by Joni, who wasnÕt high and was keyed into Cavett and the jokes that Cavett had that were three levels above your head. Joni was right there and gorgeous; and then she plays ÒBoth Sides NowÓ or ÒCactus Tree,Ó and youÕre like, Oh my God, she plays guitar like that, too. So, that was my Beatles, in a lot of ways. 
Judd: When you first started writing about music, you were thirteen or fourteen. Where were you in the hierarchy of school? I know you were a smart kid who skipped grades, but were you considered someone that people got a kick out ofÑor were you a forgotten kid? 
Cameron: They say you look back and you always make yourself out to be more of a loner than you really were, but I was alone. Or, I had one friend. I was younger than everybody else, a mascot at best, and ridiculed often. 
Music was the thing where I just felt like, This is a club that I belong to, in my head. Maybe thatÕs the quest to go and start doing interviews. Could I really be in this club somehow because I was drawn to it? There was one day where I brought Randy Newman Live, David BowieÕs Hunky Dory, and maybe Joni MitchellÕs Blue to my English class, and I played pieces of those records for everybody. And the teacher was like, ÒYes, thatÕs good music.Ó I remember walking around the campus with the Hunky Dory cover under my arm, and I felt cool, like I finally felt like I had something that was my own. IÕm never gonna be a jock. I totally will never be a surfer in San DiegoÑthat was the hierarchy. So, music gave me a card to punch in my head that there was a place to belong, if only when the lights are out and youÕre listening to 
Hunky Dory. 
Judd: ThatÕs how I felt about comedy, which no one else was interested in. I thought, Oh, I have a thing. I donÕt know whatÕs happening with my popularity level or the fact that I donÕt get great grades, but I think this is my ticket out. It meant a lot to me, like a secret mission that was going to pay off. Then I moved to L.A., and I met all these other peopleÑit was like the Judd of every high school in America had moved to L.A. ÒOh, so you all know every sketch from Monty Python?Ó Suddenly, weÕre here and weÕre gonna try to take it to the next place and see if we can really do this. 
Cameron: Would you do the routines? 
Judd: Senior year of high school, in May, I started going to open mics, but I was very scared to admit I wanted to do it. I knew it was gonna be painful. It was to get up and go, Okay, now I have to prepare to be really bad and be humiliated for a couple of years. But I was ready, and I knew it was going to happen, and it did happen. The first interview I ever did was with Mookie Wilson from the New York Mets, in seventh or eighth grade. My grandfather told me he met someone who worked for the Mets, like the vice president, so my friend and I knocked on the door of the Mets offices at Shea Stadium and said, ÒMy grandfather knows this guy.Ó He let me in and gave me an autographed ball and bat, and later I just called him or sent him a letter and said, ÒCan I interview Mookie Wilson?Ó They set it up, and thatÕs the first time I realized, Oh, you can meet people. 
Cameron: Now, what if they [had] said, ÒFuck off, you little kidÓ? Would it have wounded you? Or would you have just kept going? 
Judd: I think I would have kept going. I think I was crazy. I knew that my grandfather had just decided to be in music. He would pay money out of his own pocket to jazz musicians, record them, have the records manufactured himself, and then go to record stores and say, ÒHey, you want a hundred twenty of these Charlie Parkers?Ó and on some level, I think I realized, Oh, thatÕs how you make it in the world. IÕm not gonna climb the ladder; IÕm gonna do something weird and flip it on its side. ItÕs wild, because I would put myself in situations with people that I really admired, but also not sure how I could keep up. How was it for you being very young and being in rooms with people like Bob Dylan and having to talk in a way where you could get his respect? 
Cameron: Oh wow, thatÕs really a good question. They were so kind of open-door Mookie Wilson-ish in the beginning. So, I would take little steps out into being relaxed. I remember the Allman Brothers [Band was] in the Continental Hyatt House, and theyÕre all sitting around in this room, and theyÕre playing ÒCome On in My Kitchen,Ó and the roadies are pulling up a guitar and playing a little something and, man, itÕs like Macon, Georgia, and you can almost feel Duane in the room. ThereÕs a guitar next to me, and I couldnÕt play, but I held it and kind of acted like I could play. Gregg Allman took one look at me and then looked away, and one of the roadies saw the one lookÑhe just gently eased the guitar out of my hands, like, YouÕre in heaven, and itÕs all beautiful, and you just canÕt play this anymore. And that was me going like, Okay. I have some guardrails. But they made me feel so good about it. It was like, ItÕs not really your thing. And I just went, ÒOkay.Ó I remember that feeling so clearly right now, and my feeling was: If I love the music, theyÕre gonna know it, and I will belong. It stays with me to this day. Scorsese has that thing that if you meet a hero that you had when you were seventeen, youÕll always be nervous. So, IÕm still kind of nervous around Neil Young or somebody like that because, Oh, thatÕs a poster in my room. Living molecules are almost too much. 
Judd: TheyÕre not easy characters, Neil Young and Dylan. TheyÕre not the easiest people in the world to talk to. 
Cameron: No, but they sense authenticity. Pete Townshend was a true hero of mine, and I loved his rock writing. I still think heÕs the best rock journalist. He covered rock for Rolling Stone. So, when I met him, I felt like, This guy knows my world because heÕs a rock journalist, too. And he made me feel that way. That was the first amazing interview that I ever got to do. What was your first epic, they-spent-four-hours-with-me-and-I-asked-everything interview? 
Judd: ThatÕs the funny part, I donÕt think theyÕre good interviews. TheyÕre veryÑI didnÕt know much about comedy the way I thought that I did. There were people who were nice to me, who answered very simple questions deeply. They were very helpful to me because I would ask basic questions: ÒHow long does it take to get good?Ó ÒHow do you get stage time?Ó 
Cameron: But these are great questions. I tried to interview some comics in the day. I went backstage and tried to interview Franklin Ajaye. He was fucking dark. He went on a thing to me like, ÒYou expect me to be funny, donÕt you? Everybody expects me to be funny. The cop pulls me over and expects me to be funny. You know what? IÕm not funny. Okay?Ó IÕm like, Argh! Take me back to the musicians. He was great, but he was honest, and I canÕt say that the comedians that IÕve read and interviewed after didnÕt have a piece of that. I got scared by the comics a little bit. George Carlin was nice to me. 
Judd: YouÕre writing profiles about these musicians, and thereÕs an enormous amount of heartbreak and death and struggleÑall the things of life. YouÕre a very young person, and these people are dealing with grief and their addictions. How are you even able to process it? When youÕre interviewing a band like the Allman Brothers, and their brother just died, what was your level of comprehension of what you were dealing with? 
Cameron: That question makes me emotional. I have to think about it. I think youÕre right, because seeing what happened on the road with the Allman Brothers [Band] was heartbreaking. There was all of that in the air, all of itÑ death was hanging there and the loss of their leader; it was all fresh. Life had given them some strange lightning-strikes-twice moments, like Pearl Jam many years later. But Dickey Betts moves up, Gregg becomes more of a leaderÑsuddenly the band is bigger than ever. That was an intense life experience. But really, as you asked me that, I go back to the living room, because my mom was a counselor as well as a teacher. I would come home from, like, interviewing the Eagles, who [had] just put out ÒTake It EasyÓ or something, and I was so high from having met these guys, and Glenn Frey was the coolest guy in the world. And I found a way back home, because they didnÕt drive, and I walked in, and there were all these students from my momÕs college crowded around her, and my dad was trying to comfort her. Something bad had happened. There had been a political movement and somebody had tried to force her out of her job at the college, and she was crying. And the young people were comforting her. That night really was the yin and yang: You can have this incredible high two miles away at the Concourse, but at the same time, they have ganged up on your mom, and sheÕs weeping in your living room. And this is all part of the human experience. ItÕs the roller coaster, youÕve got to love that roller coaster. ThatÕs everything. The biggest gift is to be able to put that in a movie where people go to that place together, and sadness becomes laughter, and laughter becomes sadness. All that stuff is the best. 
Judd: If youÕre interviewing Neil Young and heÕs talking about all these losses in his life, and theyÕre talking about how theyÕre dealing with it and their spirituality, were you picking up on that? 
Cameron: Absolutely. There had been a death in my family, but I was kind of too young to process it. I really keep going back to this Allman Brothers tour. 
ItÕs not too long of a story, but I loved that song ÒBrandy,Ó by Looking Glass. They were playing this club, the 21 Club in San Diego, so I interviewed them in their bus. Then Elliot Lurie, the lead singer of Looking Glass, was like, ÒCome, now letÕs go in the club.Ó The bouncer at the door stopped me and said, ÒYouÕre too young.Ó And Elliot Lurie said, ÒHeÕs a reporter,Ó and now the owner of the club comes in and says, ÒOkay, mention my club a lot, whatever you write, and I will put you in this dressing room. You canÕt leave the dressing room, but youÕll be in the club, and Looking Glass can come back into the room after. But you donÕt leave that dressing room.Ó And I was like, ÒOkay,Ó so IÕm in this dressing room, and IÕm hearing the muffled show thatÕs starting, and the door opens and in walks Jim Croce, and he sees me with a tape recorder and a bunch of questions, and he goes, ÒMy first interview; this is fantastic.Ó I wasnÕt even that big of a Jim Croce fan, but I just interviewed him on the spot. He was fantastic. And then I interviewed Gram Parsons right after that. The point of all this is IÕm on the road with the Allman Brothers, with Neal Preston. WeÕre waiting for Gregg to call, because itÕs two in the afternoon and weÕre waiting to interview him, and over the radio comes this announcement that Jim Croce has died in this plane crash with the dude that he was with, who was also really great (his accompanist, Maury Muehleisen). Then the guy said on the radio, ÒThis is a terrible thing, because weÕve already lost Gram Parsons.Ó And I remember sitting in the Hyatt House lobby with Neal, just going like, Two people that I knew and kind of bonded with are dead. And these are the first dead people IÕve ever known. It was a huge thing, because it all felt a little bit fantasy-like, and I was able to be empathetic toward everything, but thereÕs a body count in this job, and people die, and it just really rocked me, so when Neil Young talks about Danny Whitten dying, and Ronnie Van Zant dies later, this is all stuff that really was emotional. I always wanted to write about loss, because loss is the other side of the giddy highs of the music, and loss is probably part of the reason why the giddy highs can feel so powerful, because it all fits together. Suddenly, youÕre such a far way away from high school where people laughed at you for not having enough pubic hair yet. And if I can capture how this feels, I could be a writer. 
Judd: Your transition to filmmakingÑwas it because you wrote the book of Fast Times at Ridgemont High and you stumbled in that direction? Or was there an intention to make it into a movie? I know as a kid I didnÕt watch movies thinking I would direct or write movies. 
Cameron: Me, neither. 
Judd: It feels like journalism and the type of profile writing you were doing was good preparation for understanding the human condition in a way that naturally sets you up if youÕre now inventing a scene. 
Cameron: Transcribing those interviews, really, to this day, is probably the best thing that could have ever happened for my screenwriting. Just to feel how people talked. You become aware that people are inelegant speakers a lot of the time, and thatÕs the beauty of the way they speak. They do say Òlike,Ó they do say Òum,Ó they do fall apart in the middle of a sentence and find another way to say thingsÑand thatÕs the way real life sounds. That helped me want to try out screenwriting. When Art Linson was like, ÒYouÕre the cheapest person available to write your own screenplay. Why donÕt you do it?Ó that was really helpful. Then that happened later, when everybody turned down directing Say AnythingÉJim Brooks was so cool. He was like, ÒBuddy, you have one more on your list, and if they say no, youÕre up.Ó I was like, ÒI donÕt know anything about blocking or any of that stuff,Ó and he was like, ÒYou just need to learn to change your socks several times a day. YouÕll feel a lot better on the set.Ó Later, of course, I realized he was being very generous and inviting, and it was a little more than that. Mostly we loved Mike Nichols movies in our house, so there was a part of me that thought, Well, you know, maybe I can do that as the director, but it was never a dream or anything. I just loved movies and music and the idea of putting music in a movie, like Mike Nichols had done. And thatÕs never changed. 
Judd: When you were writing the Fast Times book, you went to high school? How many months did you do it for? 
Cameron: Two semesters. Pretty loose second semester. 
Judd: But you had this idea to go back to school and write about high school in the early eighties. What year was it that you did that? 
Cameron: Nineteen seventy-nine was the research year andÑfuck, man. ItÕs really weird to think that that was the seventies, but life is the best writer. You just canÕt top it. Like, Jeff Spicoli was a real guy. All those characters in Fast Times were real people. ThereÕs a little bit of compositing going on, but theyÕre real people. IÕd been really doing a lot of clandestine reporting, and the realization was TheyÕre all trying so hard to be adults at younger and younger ages, because they have this lifestyle that needs to be supported. So, theyÕre like forty-year-old parents. ItÕs like, How do I supply? How do I buy the shit that I need? I need to work. TheyÕre all stressing about their jobs, which was a change from when I was in high school a few years earlier. There was one day where a kid came into a journalism class in this big puffy jacket, and he said, ÒIÕve decided that Ronald Reagan is the future.Ó And I was like, ÒWhat?Ó This is the death of liberalism. This is the death of so much. Ronald Reagan has infiltrated the English journalism class. And nobody argued the point, and I realized, This is the future. This quest to support their lifestyle is changing their politics; itÕs going to change the future. That was what I wanted the book to be: the changing of the guard, where theyÕre trading their adolescence now for a position in the big capitalistic train. They want to get on that train already at fifteen and sixteen. That was really just journalism. I couldnÕt make that up, the stuff that went on that year. But that became the movie of Fast Times. 
Judd: How old were you? 
Cameron: Twenty-two. I went for four months. The end of the school year, I think I had two classes a week. I had never had a senior year. My sister was very popular in school, so I knew these things existed, like prom and grad night at Disneyland. So, it was a way to go do all that. 
Judd: But on some level, did you feel like a narc? Were you having real relationships with friends where you felt there was some sort of Donnie BrascoÐtype betrayal? 
Cameron: I told a few of them by the end of the year, one in particular. But I didnÕt feel like I was narcÕing as much as kind of catching something that hadnÕt been written about. Because there were times in doing these [music] stories for Rolling Stone where I would go in the [concert arena] parking lot and interview fans. The stories from the fans were better than Rod StewartÕs storiesÑjust how they got their tickets and how itÕs so great. So, to me, it was like I got to tell their story now. 
Judd: Which is more like your story. ItÕs almost like the path to talking about yourself. Which you then actually did, in Almost Famous. In my moviesÑ and I learned this from Garry Shandling, where he was taking a version of himself and fictionalizing huge hunks of itÑIÕve worked with people who take their history and try to capture the spirit of it, and itÕs so hard to do. Freaks and Geeks isnÕt real, but to Paul Feig, it captures his family. And he doesnÕt even have a sister in real life. ItÕs a soup of true and made-up stuff trying to capture the essence of something. 
Cameron: I love this whole conversation because itÕs reminding me how thereÕs a spiritual center to what you do, and thatÕs why it resonates. Shooting Almost Famous in San Diego did give us one little leg up on having a spiritual center, because we shot in all the places where that stuff happened: the sports arena, the radio station where I met Lester Bangs, the street where we talkedÉThere was a mojo that was still operating, if we could catch it. But when I saw the four-hour version of it, I thought it was a complete disaster. I showed it to my mom, and I said, ÒI donÕt know what to do,Ó and she said, ÒIf you just calm down, thereÕs a great movie in here.Ó 
Judd: IÕm fascinated by your mom, in that she resisted music and then, at some point, she got it. What was she thinking when she didnÕt get it? 
Cameron: She thought it was a hand being slipped into the pockets of American youth, using sex and drugs to get your money and lower the intellectual standards of a generation. And she was kind of right. Her thing about adolescence as a marketing tool was very real. Though she loved The Graduate, she thought [the song] ÒMrs. RobinsonÓ was snidely antireligious and Jesus-referencing. So, she was weaponized about that, and she didnÕt want it in the house. I could never buy tickets to a show, but I thought if I won the tickets on the radio, she would have to deal with the fact that theyÕre free and [that] I did something enterprising to get them. So she went to see Elvis Presley with me. They said to call in at a certain time and get tickets to an amazing show. I was hoping it was Creedence Clearwater or something. Then I won a ticket to go see Derek and the Dominos a week later, so I made a deal with her: ÒLetÕs go see Elvis together, and letÕs go see Eric Clapton, and weÕll decide together whatÕs authentic and whatÕs not about this music.Ó So, we go to see Elvis, and he was high as shit. 
Judd: What year? 
Cameron: It was 1970, and it was a forty-two-minute show that was mostly selling scarves during the set. It was so ridiculous. The blue-haired ladies were going nuts, and [my mother] was very somber about it. SheÕs like, ÒThatÕs not my generation. I like Mike Nichols. I like Dick Cavett. This is BS.Ó Then we went to see Eric Clapton, and there was a riot going on in this big glass window behind him as he played, dope everywhere. A guy offers my mom cocaine in the audience, and we left, and she said, ÒYouÕre right, your music is better.Ó So, it was a Derek and the Dominos show at the UC [San Diego] gym. 
Judd: WhatÕs it like having a mom that was such a force like that and such a character? My mom was a real character, too, but I canÕt say it was all for good. 
Cameron: My dad watched her like Cary Grant watches Katharine Hepburn. SheÕs a big character. ThatÕs another thing about movies. There are big characters in life, and celebrating the big characters is the greatest. I think sheÕd had sadness in her life and wanted to process it. The knocks can become a gift, and she turned her life into finding the light in the dark. I havenÕt really talked about this, but her thing was I had to do the play of Almost Famous. I would say to her, ÒI donÕt know how to do this. I have really great people that IÕm working with, but I think we got lucky on the movie, and I think this is going to the well one too many times. Plus, weÕre talking about putting this on at the Old Globe Theatre, across from where we live. What is worse than failing in your hometown? I donÕt want to do this.Ó She said, ÒYou got to do it. You are creating the negativity. Stop it. YouÕve got to do this, and youÕll love what you do.Ó And she died unexpectedly two days before the first paying audience came to see the play. 
Judd: Did she get to see a dress rehearsal? 
Cameron: She saw a tape and dug it. I have one picture of her watching it. She and I can be really tough on my stuff. The play was good. She was right. IÕm really glad we did it. If Broadway never opens up, and we never have any more life to the play, I still walk away with probably the best experience I ever had right across from where I grew up, in the theater where we used to go see Shakespeare. Joni Mitchell came on the first premiere night, down from L.A. with her girlfriends, to watch it. I was terrified. BlueÕs ÒRiverÓ is in the play, so IÕm like, Oh my God. This is more stress. She comes out of it, and she goes, ÒBetter than the movie.Ó And in many ways, sheÕs right. 
Judd: When you revisit that material as an older person, do your instincts change in terms of how you adapt it and discuss the older characters in the play? 
Cameron: Maybe thatÕs why the mother character really stands out in the play Ñbecause when you revisit it, youÕre the older character now. 
Judd: Who was the person with Fast Times that was the mentor and helped you get that script right? 
Cameron: Art Linson. He was also the guy that saw the short that Amy [Heckerling] did, ÒGetting It Over With,Ó and said, ÒSheÕs raw and real and authentic and sheÕs a woman, and she knows how this feels from the other point of view on some of these relationships.Ó Amy came in and knocked it out of the park and was all with my mom. I love that weÕve talked so much about my mom. My mom always used to say, ÒYou should be so happy that Amy Heckerling directed Fast Times at Ridgemont High, because if you [had] directed it, you would have turned it into an art film. ItÕs funny when they smell the Xerox papers. ThatÕs funny.Ó 
Judd: Then the other mentor you had, who was also a mentor for meÑJames 
L. Brooks. I got to work on the cartoon The Critic. You had to pitch him the story ideas. Mike Reiss and Al Jean ran the show, but he was a presence and at the tables and giving notes, and I got to watch how he ran a show. I also remember, when I was around the Gracie offices, that they had a little library, and there were some papers stuck in there that I pulled out, and it was their notes on Say Anything. 
Cameron: Whoa! 
Judd: What was your experience like with Jim? 
Cameron: I think about Jim every day I write. Jim is just so pure. Nobody was going to care as much as Jim, and nobody was gonna care as much when a cut didnÕt work. YouÕd be in a dark room, and heÕd be watching a cut, and you would just hear these yellow legal tablets. The anger or the frustration that he would never show you, because heÕs too classy, comes out in the sound of the yellow legal tablets whipping over from page to page. YouÕd be looking at each other like, Okay, weÕre gonna have some notes after this. But generally, he had the most amazing ideas. He was saying stuff like, ÒWhat if Jerry Maguire had a religious father and Jerry tithed his money?Ó It turned into a monologue about ÒMy dad spent his whole life with one comfortable chair.Ó And you leave the office after two hours of talk like this, and youÕre fucking high. You want to write all night, and you want to capture what that comfortable chair could be. I donÕt know that IÕve ever gotten that or seen that in anybody else. I think youÕre like that. I see it in the way Billy Wilder would talk about how he dealt with [screenwriter] I. A. L. Diamond. I donÕt see it a lot. 
Judd: YouÕve said that Jerry Maguire started just from a photo of an agent with a young athlete. 
Cameron: It was a picture that Jim showed me at lunch. We were just trying to figure out if thereÕs another movie we could do, and he was like, ÒWhat is that relationship?Ó Then I went off and wrote a whole thing and didnÕt do as much research as I should have and gave him a big, thick draft of stuff. Jim took a weekend, and I met with him, and he said, ÒWell, IÕve never seen so much story with no plot.Ó I think about that all the time. ÒLetÕs not go down too many story roads; there needs to be a plot.Ó 
Judd: What was the moment where you realize what the essence of that movie was? 
Cameron: ItÕs interesting. We made the movie Singles, and the whole grunge scene exploded in the time after we shot the movie and before we were out. The studio hung on to it for a long time, not knowing how to sell it. Then the grunge thing happened, and they figured out how to sell it. ÒLetÕs call it Come as You Are, and weÕre goodÓÑwhich didnÕt work out, mercifully. The people in Seattle, some of my friendsÑnot Eddie and not the Pearl Jam guys, butÑsome of the people really turned their noses up at that movie. I felt some ridicule in some places and the feeling that IÕd capitalized on the scene or something. But the oddest people supported that movie, and it surprised me. I was so taken with the idea that when you really hit a wall, the people that come to help you are very rarely the people you expect to be there. And thatÕs Jerry Maguire. I knew what that movie was about when our conversations became about expressing that kind of abandonment that IÕd felt in Seattle and turning that into a story of the redemption of the sports agent that makes it with the woman in the office that he never even knew. And the player that was the last on the list of the people that he was pleased to support. Those two people come together to put him on the path, and I just thought, ThatÕs what the movieÕs about. 
Judd: When my mom died, I was in the middle of writing Funny People, and I was trying to write about how, when my mom thought she was going to die, she was much saner than when she thought she was going to live. And every time she thought the medicine was working, she became neurotic and materialistic and kind of crazy.
