When they said, ÒYou have no shot,Ó she became so cool and real and grounded and right there. So, thatÕs what it was. I never talked about it at the time, but it was trying to express this thing I was watching my mom go through. Can you learn the lessons from your near-death experience, or not? I was quietly in so much pain, I didnÕt know how to process it. You go into a trance and you just turn it into the next piece of work. Like Jerry Maguire. You would never think Jerry Maguire is related to Singles. Now, letÕs talk about when you visited the set of Knocked Up with Tom Cruise. 
Cameron: That was an amazing day. I was stuck on some ideas, I think it was the whole Marvin Gaye movie that I wanted to do, and it had been taking forever. He just said, ÒLook, man, you got to get out of your house. I want to take you to Judd ApatowÕs movie set. ItÕs just two minutes away, and youÕre gonna see something thatÕs happening. ItÕs gonna fire you up.Ó And he took me over to the set, and it fired me up. It was like the guys from Say AnythingÉ, like [John] Cusack and his gang, if theyÕd moved on to another movie. 
Judd: I remember Leslie [Mann] was about to shoot something, and I had to go to Leslie like, ÒTom Cruise is here. HeÕs watching. And Cameron CroweÕs watching, too.Ó Because Leslie read for you for Jerry Maguire and got really down to the end, and she didnÕt get the part. And the letter you wrote telling her that she didnÕt get the part gave her so much confidence. It was such a kind, thoughtful letter. That movie is really about just trying to be a better version of yourself. ThatÕs something that I find that I always want to have these movies be about. 
Cameron: ItÕs always the best type of theme, right? We want to tell stories that make people want to take the risk to love better or connect more, to take a risk to improve somehow. Do you have whole drafts where you were just getting something out emotionally, and itÕs like, IÕm never gonna make that movie, but I wrote it, and I have it, and it represents this time in my life? 
Judd: I donÕt write a lot of extra stuff. I just obsess until itÕs somewhat makeable and maybe not in a good way, either. I think itÕs related to working in television. If we have to shoot an episode next week, and weÕre going to write it this week and give it to Garry [Shandling] on Sunday and then do the table read Monday, at a certain point, my head thinks, The train is unstoppable. Do you notice a common theme when you look back at all of your work? For me, people say, ÒYou do a lot of coming-of-age stories.Ó Recently, I keep thinking every story is a coming-of-age story. 
Cameron: I like coming-of-age stories. And I definitely like the embattled optimist. I keep getting drawn to aspects of that. If your heartÕs in the right place, you have the shot at a sequence that can make you feel like all the oxygen in the world is just inside you right now. I go to the Billy Wilder thing, of ÒIf you do your job right, they might talk about your movie for fifteen minutes after they see it.Ó ThatÕs the most you can ever hope for. 

DAVID LETTERMAN 
November 2020 
Judd: How are you doing? I think the last time I saw you was at that USO show with Joe Biden. We were all excited to meet Barack Obama, and we didnÕt know we should be as excited about meeting Joe Biden. 
David: I remember meeting Joe Biden at The Regis Philbin Show. I was with there with my friend and colleague Mary Barclay, whoÕs from Pennsylvania. And in the dressing room was Vice President Biden, just seated by himself, and he got up, and I said, ÒHello,Ó and I introduced myself, and then he and Mary had a lovely conversation about being from Pennsylvania. And thatÕs really all you want. 
Judd: I remember when I was doing the documentary about Garry Shandling, he guest-hosted for Regis and interviewed Trump. Then he wrote about it in his diaries at the time, that it scared him how empty [Trump] wasÑand this was before Trump was in politics. Shandling was like, ÒIÕd never met a person like this. It was like looking into a black hole.Ó 
David: I, like everyone else, have grown fatigued trying to understand what it is. Because this is not what I knew of him when he was on the show maybe two dozen times. 
Judd: You had a lot of politicians on your show over the years. Who were the politicians, from the earliest part of the talk show, that you remember being on? 
David: We probably had Ed Koch on. But itÕs so different, at least for me and people doing what I did back thenÑhow much we paid attention to politics then compared to what people are doing now. It was different. Politics was like knowing a lot about European soccer. We sort of knew about it, but then, suddenly, you realize later, Oh my God, this is something to be taken seriously. 
Judd: Now, every other night, you see hosts dealing with some massive tragedy or a school shooting. What you had to deal with on 9/11, it didnÕt seem like it happened a lot on the show in that era. 
David: ThatÕs right. And thank goodness, because I donÕt think, at that point, I would have had the energy to do it justice. You can talk about it for a month, talk about it for six weeks, but to have to address it night after night after night for over four yearsÑthat takes great resources, great durability, and real presence of mind. And the guys who are doing it, the women who are doing it Ñgood for them. TheyÕre doing a good job, but I was tapped out by then. 
Judd: Now youÕre doing your show on Netflix, My Next Guest Needs No Introduction. I was watching your Kim Kardashian interview last night, and I thought that her telling the story of getting robbed is one of the most riveting things IÕve ever seen on television. How does it feel to be in a different format? When you were doing interviews on your old shows, did you wish you could just have a real talk with your guests and not feel pressure to be amusing for five minutes? 
David: It used to be weÕd do four minutes in the first segment and then three minutes in the second segment, and then itÕs goodbye. But this way, you get to follow your curiosity and the terrain of the conversation. I just thought Kim was fantastic. I got to know a little bit about her when we talked to Kanye for the show, and I realized that I was under the wrong impression about who she was. So, I felt good about correcting that for myself. I think, in the beginning, people were saying, ÒGood Lord, why are you talking to Kim Kardashian?Ó I felt like I had to be defensive about it. Then I talked to her, and I realized, No, no, no, this is a woman of substance. Someone who knows 
what sheÕs up to, who is up to quite a lot, and who is making her mark other than just being on her TV show. 
Judd: When you were at the end of the CBS show, you would talk about not wanting to attempt to do what everyone else was doing in terms of viral videos and Instagram jokes. Does it feel like youÕve figured out how to do it in your own way? 
David: Well, I just kind of fell into it. I didnÕt want to be part of conventional television. I had done that and did it too long. This seemed different enough, but also withinÑexcuse the expressionÑmy skill set. So, this seemed about right. 
Judd: When youÕre picking the guests for your show, what do you find youÕre drawn to in people as subjects of a long-form interview? 
David: I always look for the front door; I look for a way for me to get in. The most recent version of this is Lizzo. She had been on our show once, and thatÕs the extent of what I knew of her. And she was nice enough to let us come to her home. This was right after the pandemic had really started to get everyoneÕs attention. She and I just started talking, and she was telling me about a birthday party sheÕd had, and what she was eating and drinking at her birthday party, and she referenced something in the pharmaceutical world that I have no knowledge of. So, I thought, Great, I want to know everything about this. And that was our in. She was satisfying my curiosity of something that IÕm too inexperienced and ignorant to understand. I felt like, Well, this is what you would do with a friend. So, thatÕs what IÕm always looking for, and then everything else that you know about that person, or donÕt know about that person, is much easier to access. 
Judd: I donÕt know if youÕve ever seen Gary GulmanÑwe did a stand-up special with him about depression, where he talked about it for an hour. 
David: I know the psychiatrist featured in that offering. 
Judd: Do you? Yeah, heÕs very good. 
David: And I said to him, ÒWait a minute, I thought that we had a deal here. YouÕre only supposed to treat one depressed comedian at a time.Ó 
Judd: What is the common trait there? I produced two stand-up specials about depression. It seems to be a very common thing that we all go through at some point. I donÕt know if comedians are just more open about it. YouÕve talked about how you didnÕt even know thatÕs what was happening. 
David: I knew something had gone haywire. And I wonder if people are talking about it more, not just because comedians are talking about it, but because it doesnÕt seem to have the stigma that it used to. But I didnÕt know what was happening to me until a psychiatrist said, ÒOh hereÕs what this is. This is depression.Ó And I always thought depression was, Oh jeez, the Reds got beat last night. My day is ruined. But you donÕt know itÕs a real sinkhole. And when you understand it, you have a greater awareness for the trap that it can be, because itÕs not just Go out and run around the block, youÕll feel better. 
Judd: It was interesting when Bruce Springsteen started talking about it. WeÕve all been watching him do these huge shows for decades, and then he finally said, ÒI didnÕt want to get off the stage.Ó The moment he leaves, heÕs bummed out. HeÕs said the only place he wasnÕt depressed was while doing a show. 
David: ThatÕs him self-medicating. And you know, good for him and good for everybody else who really benefited from that. Millions of people around the world get the benefit of that, but who knew the guy was suffering? I experienced a much smaller version of the self-medicating dynamic. It started for me in high school, when the only time I felt any self-worth at all was when I started taking a public speaking course. I couldnÕt do algebra. I couldnÕt do history. I couldnÕt do chemistry. I couldnÕt do anything. But public speaking, jeez, it was the first time I didnÕt feel like the dumb kid in my class. I thought, All you have to do is find out where this will take you in life, and it will help support you. It will support your impression of yourselfÑ which was, and still is, dented. 
Judd: You started out doing the weather and having a local broadcasting career. Was it in your mind that, at some point, it would turn into a career in stand-up? 
David: No, I originally just thought, I gotta find out how you can get on radio. That was my original thought. And then I got very lucky, and a friend of mineÕs brother hired me to work at a TV station when I was in college. ThatÕs when everything changed for me. I was afraid of being in front of people, so the camera or the microphone is ideal. There were stand-up comedians I would see that just made me laugh silly. But I did not think, Oh, thatÕs what IÕm gonna do. I always thought if I can just get to a fifty-thousand watt, clear-channel, middle of the road radio station, I can live a happy life. 
Judd: And then you did that? 
David: I got to do radio, I got to the television, I got to do weather, I got to do news, I got to do public service shows, I got to do announcing, I got to do commercials. And after a while, you realize, IÕm tired of this. Then I became aware of You know what? Maybe I could just get out of here and try something else. 
Judd: Was there someone you saw as a model? Someone who had pulled it off where you thought, IÕm gonna move to L.A. and try to do this path? 
David: ItÕs like a kid who gets a look at Joe DiMaggio and says, ÒYou know what? I think I can do that.Ó But I used to watch Johnny Carson, Steve Allen, and I thought, Wait a minute. These guys are broadcasting, but theyÕre also really, really funny. Let me just see if that can be done. But the whole thing from that moment, my sophomore year in high school to the first time I hosted The Tonight ShowÑitÕs just luck; it just worked. I canÕt understand the good fortune, and the only reasonable explanation is the people around me, who helped me and encouraged me and opened doors for me that I did not feel deserving of, but certainly took great advantage of. 
Judd: What do you think formed your point of view or your sense of humor, because you showed up with your take, even from the first. It wasnÕt very long from the moment you got to the Comedy Store until you were hosting The Tonight Show, like a ridiculously brief amount of time. Did you have a sense of what you were doing? What you felt was funny about yourself? 
David: Well, thereÕs two things. When I got to California, I was twenty-eight, and I had hosted a show called Clover Power. It was with kids, and theyÕd have their pet goat on, or theyÕd show me how they made a pie. So, the skill is no different: A kid brings on a goat. Is there something funny to say about the goat? Great. You do The Tonight Show, and on comes an actor or actress Ñis there something funny to say? Especially if the actor or actress has a goat. Whoa, thatÕs pay dirt. I think it was more the broadcasting experience that made me suitable as a possibility for hosting The Tonight Show. 
Judd: ThereÕs also a delusion of youth. I always think about when I lived with Adam Sandler, when he went and did your show for the first time, when he was a kid. I think all of us had this lunatic belief that it was all going to happen. ThereÕs something about being young: YouÕre just so stupid. You must have had some of that, to think, I can host The Tonight Show. 
David: For me, the awareness that you just described took place almost the minute I crossed from Arizona into California. I thought, At least geographically, I know IÕm in the right place. I think I got a shot at this. ItÕs interesting that these other people shared that. 
Judd: So, when you get to L.A., your crew of guys was, like, Witherspoon and Dreesen and George Wallace? 
David: Pretty good group: George Wallace, John Witherspoon, Tom Dreesen, George Miller, Jeff Altman, Johnny Dark, Jay Leno, Elayne Boosler. And then, a year later, Robin Williams arrived. Paul Mooney was also in that group. I know IÕm overlooking people that will come to me. But we thought we were pretty good, and we thought, Oh, these are the golden years. 
Judd: Were you writing for Jimmie Walker just to pay your bills? 
David: I left Indiana with five thousand dollars. But Jimmie Walker was so kind to me, and he hired me and Elayne and Jay Leno and Byron Allen. And every Sunday, we would go over to JimmieÕs house and pitch him jokes. I got a hundred bucks a week for that. Then, when I would emcee at the Comedy Store, I would get twenty bucks. So, unlike the typical story for people of that era, I had a little bit of cash flow. 
Judd: What was your impression of the scene at the time? 
David: We always made fun of guys who did impressions, and we always made fun of guys who closed their set with a song. We liked guys like Tim Thomerson, who could come in and do his thing, and the walls would collapse in on themselves from people laughing so hard. And then you had the guy that came out and was, ÒHereÕs Jimmy Stewart doing a medley of Beatles songs.Ó ThatÕs impressions and music. What? Get off! We felt like you had to stand by your materialÑand no gimmicks. 
Judd: What was it like in that era, when someone like Richard Pryor or Robin Williams comes in, and itÕs just another level? Is it inspiring? Or does it make everyone want to quit comedy? 
David: In the beginning, lineup for Tuesday night or Wednesday night or whatever would be set. ThereÕd be two shows. YouÕd call, then youÕd find out, Did I get a time? Yes. YouÕre eight-fifteen, first show Wednesday. So, you fell prey to the idea that there was an order to this. But all of that went out the window when a guy like Steve Landesberg would show up. A guy like Gabe Kaplan would show up, or Freddie Prinze would show up. And then the eight-fifteen meant nothing. What that really meant was eleven-fifteen, maybeÑbecause these guys would get up and do an hour, and why not? It was great for business. But there was nothing like Richard Pryor. He was like the Manhattan Project. It was an explosion. It really didnÕt matter what Richard was doing. He suddenly decided that he was working on a new album, and I donÕt know the chronology of which album was which, but this was an album he was going to work out at the Comedy Store. And he came in two or three nights a week. People got wind of this, and so the place was packed, and Richard would come onstage andÑI mean, just when he was in the room, you could feel it. Even as he walked onstage, and you saw him silhouetted in the darkness, it was hard to describe. And then he would get onstage andÑoh my God. Because he was the God of comedy of his day, and to be looking at him, and then to hear himÑit was delightful. Delightful and the kind of electricity you only feel every now and then. 
Judd: And youÕre seeing how he writes, because youÕre seeing the set come together. 
David: And whatever heÕs doing, you donÕt care. Because he could be up there tying his shoes. And then [after his set,] people would leave, because they thought, IÕve been to the top of the mountain. IÕve seen everything you could want in comedy. LetÕs go home. 
Judd: I remember when Robin Williams used to come into the Improv when I was hosting. You really felt like you had to shut the show down afterward. You couldnÕt even do a show. It kind of sucked the energy out on their exit. I remember Sandler had a good set following Robin, as an unknown comic, and I thought, Oh, SandlerÕs gonna make it. He took that energy and somehow found a way to keep it going. 
David: I remember George Miller and I used to stand in the back of the Comedy Store when Robin was onstage. You know, comics are the best of friends and also hate each other. Like, What do you mean heÕs going to be on The Dinah Shore Show? Why am I not on Dinah? But weÕre all in the same battle. But Robin Williams comes in, and George and I actually discussed whether the arrival of Robin Williams meant that we were going to have to leave show business. 
Judd: I stopped doing stand-up when Jim Carrey hit. I used to open for Jim Carrey on the road. I was doing some writing for him, and I thought, IÕm not even in the same business as him. 
David: There are many people that caused me to feel that way about myself. But as it turns out, there is room for a wide variety of things, and if youÕre funnyÑreally, thatÕs all that counts. 
Judd: When you were trying to get on The Tonight Show, and youÕd been rejected a couple of times, were you feeling it was the right path? Was that becoming a more specific goal? 
David: ThatÕs all I wanted. ThatÕs all anybody wanted. You either wanted to get a situation comedy like a Gabe Kaplan, like Freddie Prinze, or you wanted to host a talk show. I felt like the single purpose was for me to be there. I had gone on the road. I got so comfortable working at the Comedy Store, that I could do it easily and loved it. But what you learn quickly is you have to be comfortable everywhere you go. And I was not comfortable everywhere I went. So, when I would go on the road, I would bomb, and thatÕs when I knew I have to just concentrate on getting on The Tonight Show, because IÕm never going to be these guys. Leno is probably on the road right now, in the middle of three or four shows somewhere. A lot of guys, a lot of women, thrived on it. I couldnÕt do that. So, I knew that I had to sharpen my focus to get on The Tonight Show. 
Judd: Did you feel likeÑ[from the perspective of] this era, where you donÕt have the pressure of the showÑthat how youÕre perceiving the world has shifted? IÕve only hosted a talk show once. For two days before Corden took over, I guest-hosted when they would let anybody do it just to fill some days. And the thing I noticed about it that was panic-inducing was when you interview someone, the second they sat down, there was a clock over their shoulder counting down the amount of time you had with them. It made your heart race, the need to have this not be awful. What does it feel like to be in that creative flow, but also panic, for so long? And then have it go away? 
David: Having it go awayÑit took me about a year to realize that the moving sidewalk had stopped. So, that was a huge adjustment, as with anybody who does something all their life and then does not do it. A huge adjustment. At least about a year. But what youÕre discussing thereÑI always felt that if the guest was not entertaining, not interesting, not funny, then itÕs my responsibility to fix it. Then the other thing, the pressure of the clock: I got to a point where I just ignored that. I just let it go. For the last ten years of the show, theyÕd be editing almost up to the time that the show had to be sent out to network. And weÕd be eighteen to twenty minutes over. ItÕs a hugely inconsiderate thing for me to have done. But at the time, I just didnÕt care. I had things I wanted to talk about. 
Judd: YouÕve said that you felt like you hosted your show for ten years too long. How do you categorize all the decades youÕve worked? 
David: I donÕt know the exact time line; I just picked ten years because had I left ten years earlier than I did, I still would have been on the air for twenty years or so. And in those ten years, I think it would have been nice to experience a different kind of stimulation in my life. Because, in the day, it was Leno and myself going head to headÑpreoccupied with They had so.and-so. WeÕve got to get so-and-so. HeÕs killing me in the ratings. What are we going to do? I kept thinking, IÕm going to get better, IÕm going to get better. So, that was the motivation for staying on so long. But sadly, the motivation for staying on so long was not commensurate with the desire to go to work and make the thing better. I just got lazier and lazier. 
Judd: It certainly is one of the few jobs that people really see as a lifetime gig when they take it. 
David: I think that model was set by Johnny Carson. Prior to that, Jack Paar only did it for four or five years. Steve Allen only did it for two or three years. Rick Dees only did it for a week and a half. But thatÕs the idea. I think if you got somebody like Kimmel, or Colbert, or Jimmy Fallon, or Seth Meyers, and when the showÕs successful, itÕs just a license to print money. TheyÕre pretty inexpensive to produce, because everything is right thereÑthe host, the theater. And itÕs fun, to be sure. I donÕt know, maybe itÕs just something to say, maybe itÕs just hollow rhetoric, but I do wish I had more time to examine other things. 
Judd: When I look at periods of my career, or a movie IÕve made, sometimes I feel like I lost my mind and created something almost in a fever dream. Where I donÕt even know how it happened. Is there a part of the show that feels that way for you? 
David: It comes right back around to the discussion of depression and self-medicating. If itÕs Monday and the show was great, IÕd feel great about myself. Then Tuesday, if the show sucked, IÕd start to slip. And then if Wednesday, it sucked again, I would slip a little deeper. And then Thursday, if the show was better, then IÕd feel human again. So, for me, I think the real reason I stayed so long was selfishness, because I was using that as a prescription to make myself feel worthwhile. I was using the show to try and prove myself to myself. 
Judd: When you were first starting on the morning show, did you have a sense that you were about to dismantle talk shows? Was there a philosophy to what you were doing? 
David: I wouldnÕt say a philosophy. From my standpoint, I thought, I am the guy television has been waiting for. Wait till I get there. IÕll straighten it out. Six weeks later, when we were packing up the office, I realized, Well, thereÕs something else I was wrong about. 
Judd: Morning television wasnÕt that interesting, at that time, especially at ten-thirty in the morning. 
David: We did a thing with one of the writers. That buildingÑthereÕs a roof garden about halfway up. You can gaze out on the city and still see up at the main tower of 30 Rock. ThereÕs a ledge that is about four feet above the next level on the roof. We had Rich Hall as the writer, and the premise was he was up there taking a nap, sleeping on the ledge. Now, from the cameraÕs point of view, youÕre looking at the horizon. You donÕt know that just below that ledge is another level and four feet of grass. So, IÕm at the desk and I say, ÒRich, Rich, you got to come back to theÑÓ And he rolls over, and it looks like heÕs now rolled down ten floors to Sixth Avenue. And the person from NBC in charge of the show comes up to me during the commercial, and she says, ÒPeople are gonna think heÕs dead.Ó I said, ÒYeah, I know. ThatÕs the joke.Ó And she says, ÒWe canÕt have people thinking weÕve killed somebody on television. Put him in the audience. Let people know heÕs still alive.Ó ThatÕs when I knew we didnÕt have much more time there, and it was canceled like a month or six weeks later. 
Judd: I did a sketch show with Ben Stiller for Fox. We did thirteen episodes, and they only aired twelve. It was a similarly ridiculous time slot, and I think we had the exact same experience as you, where six months later, we won the Emmy for best comedy writing. And then, suddenly, it was considered a cool thing. Did it help when you guys won the Emmy for the morning show? Or were you already going to do The Tonight Show? 
David: No, it was a year of darkness, because I had no plan. Nobody had any plans for me, because the first show had tanked. I think Brandon Tartikoff saved us because he realized that maybe it was the time slot that was part of the problem, and so he gave me a shot at night. 
Judd: YouÕre a father now. How old is your son? 
David: HeÕs seventeen, a junior in high school. How old are your kids? 
Judd: I have a daughter who is a senior in high school, and my other daughter is twenty-two. And I feel like I am an inverted version of you. You waited till you were wise. I did it by accident really young, when I had no clue what to do. We were in a wide-eyed panic. ThatÕs why we made movies about it: because we were not emotionally or mentally prepared to do it. 
David: ThatÕs interesting because, irrespective of age, IÕm not sure anyone is ready to be a parent. 
Judd: WhatÕs your son into? 
David: HeÕs not into his father. DoesnÕt like being seen in public with me, because he doesnÕt like it when I engage with people. And IÕm so desperate now for an audience that itÕs usually me engaging with strangers. He doesnÕt like that. 
Judd: Does he have any sense of what youÕve done? I mean, my kids care so little. 
David: I told this story before. When I said to Harry, ÒIÕm quitting, IÕm retiring. I wonÕt be at work every day. My life is changing; our lives will change.Ó And he kind of nodded and said, ÒWill I still be able to watch the Cartoon Network?Ó I said, ÒI think so. Let me check.Ó 
Judd: And since leaving, what has been the best part of not working that busy schedule? 
David: It used to be really fatiguing, the fact of doing it every day. Now when I do something, I think, Wow, this would be great if I could do something like this once a month. You still get the little jolt, and the hope of self-esteem, but youÕre not in the makeup chair every dayÑwhich, by the way, was my favorite part. 
Judd: Do you have other things youÕre looking to do? Are you developing other projects in addition to the Netflix show, or is that enough for you right now? 
David: I have a lot of things that I would like to do that IÕm afraid to tell people. Like when I left Indiana, I didnÕt tell people what I wanted to do. I said, ÒI want to be a writer.Ó ThatÕs part of what I wanted to do. And now I have a half-dozen ideas that I would like to try, but IÕm afraid that people will look at me and laugh, so I havenÕt verbalized them, and I havenÕt made a move. So, IÕm doing things that people come to me with, which is great. IÕve been very lucky. People have been very nice to me. But I have things I want to do that IÕm afraid to mention. I mean, theyÕre all legal. 
Judd: IsnÕt it weird that we still can be that insecure? After doing so much. 
David: Yeah, well, IÑsee, I find it hard to believe that you might be that insecure, but IÕm guessing youÕre finding it hard to believe that I might be that insecure. 
Judd: The thing I like about comedy is any success does not do anything to support the next project. Every single joke is an experiment that may fail terribly, which I think is kind of great. I donÕt mean to embarrass you, but I just want to say that for all of us in comedy, so many people, their dream was to get on [your] show or to get your approval. ItÕs probably hard for you to understand how many people are doing great, and great work, because they loved you and what you did, and you kind of gave them an opportunity. And the impact is massive. There are so many people who wanted to be funny and go into this business because of you. 
David: Thank you. Let me just add what I think youÕre maybe too timid to get to, which is the fact that, without me, there would be no comedy. 
Judd: Exactly. 

ED TEMPLETON 
June 2018 
Judd: I was working on a movie, and I met with Warren Zevon to talk about scoring it and I told him, ÒIÕm waiting to get notes from the studio.Ó He said, ÒI donÕt understand,Ó and I said, ÒI have to wait to see if they want me to make any changes.Ó And he said, ÒWhy would you change anything?Ó I felt so shamed by him and his artistic integrity. How important is that independence for you? 
Ed: I feel like filmmaking is super collaborative overall, and everything I do is coming from a dictatorial standpoint. With Toy Machine, I am the only person who makes decisions for the skate company, for the paintings I make Ñwhatever I do. ItÕs total control, and if someone came and said, ÒYou should change this,Ó I would be like, ÒWhat? What are you talking about?Ó 
Judd: Do you ever look for feedback on your stuff? 
Ed: I do, because I think it helps to have someone you trust say something constructive. How much do you have to listen to those notes from the studio? 
Judd: The funny thing is you could be resistant to notes and be wrong. IÕve had movies where people say, ÒMaybe you should do this, maybe you should shorten [that],Ó and IÕll have a very strong reaction and think, No, this is what it is. And then, ten years later, you go, I could have cut eight minutes out of that. But it took me ten years to figure out what the eight minutes would be. Sometimes thatÕs the hard part: When do you listen to people? 
Ed: For sure. When I edit an art book or photo book, for instance, I get to a certain point and then I usually put it away for a few days. And I come back, and I do in my head what is called a Òharsh editÓ: I look at it as if IÕm someone who hates me, someone out there who thinks, This guy is just an asshole, I hate everything he does. I look at it from that angle, and it really helps, because IÕll look at a photo with a more critical eye. You usually have to edit down pages anyway, so that helps you make it airtight and forces you to have really strong images in the final cut. I think it takes a certain kind of person that can work in two minds like that. IÕve talked to friends who canÕt get on that other side and [who] look at themselves critically in that way. I think itÕs helpful, and you ultimately have to end up going with your gut. 
Judd: When people ask you what you do, what do you tell them? 
Ed: ItÕs a little easier now, because I feel like I just closed a chapter on pro skateboarding. It used to be the case where I would be on a plane, and there would be a guy next to me, and heÕd ask, ÒWhat do you do?Ó And I have to assess the guy first to decide what I want to tell him. I could either say IÕm a businessman or pro skateboarder or artist or something like that. And I decide which answer would be easier to get through this conversation I donÕt want to have with that guy on a plane. But I still donÕt know what to say. I donÕt like the word artist that much. Especially coming from my world, where weÕre always giving each other crap about everything, leading with ÒIÕm an artistÓ just feels weird. 
Judd: I think that I struggle with that word sometimes. If you talk about film or writing and you talk about it as your art, as it comes out of your mouth you want to punch yourself in the face. But then, if you take a beat, it is exactly what youÕre doing, and why should it be shameful that you need to express yourself? 
Ed: It sounds weird, but thatÕs the only language thatÕs there to describe what youÕre doing. So thatÕs what you have to do. But the minute you start talking about your workÉ 
Judd: YouÕre a dick. 
Ed: You sound like a pretentious ass. 
Judd: Did you grow up here in Huntington Beach? 
Ed: Yes, IÕve been in Orange County my whole life. Born in Garden Grove, right over there, by Disneyland. We moved around a bit before settling in Huntington Beach. I take pride in having white trash credentials, because I technically lived in a trailer park at one point. 
Judd: How long did you live in a trailer park for? 
Ed: It wasnÕt that long. ItÕs kind of a crazy story. When I was around eight years old, my dad ran off with our babysitter. He was in his forties, and he left our family for this girl, and I think she just used him to get to a ride to Colorado, where she hooked up with her real boyfriend. 
Judd: Oh no. 
Ed: He just bailed out on us. I did badly in school after that, after the divorce and a broken home. But thatÕs how I found skateboarding. 
Judd: How so? 
Ed: Well, all the kids I was hanging out with when I started skateboarding were from broken homes. I started realizing thisÑlike, every single one of us has some weird, bad family story. You just end up not being the kind of kid who fits in playing basketball or on teams, and so you go out and be a nerd in the parking lot by yourself. And thatÕs what attracted me to skateboarding. 
Judd: My parents got divorced and it was my mom who ultimately ended up moving out, which never happens. The dad always leaves. I think that trying to process that kind of personal history, that kind of early abandonment, pushed me to become creative. 
Ed: For me, it never became a thing where IÕm driven because of this bad thing that happened. I could never trace anything that I did to some inner pain. That story of him leavingÑit was a good thing. ThatÕs how I saw it. Five years into skateboarding I think I looked back and went, I wouldnÕt be doing any of this if he stayed. 
Judd: You donÕt think you would have ever skateboarded? 
Ed: My mom couldnÕt control me like my dad could, so when he left, I just ran rampant. I just did what I wanted, and she couldnÕt really do that much about it. I knew I wasnÕt doing bad things; I was just skateboarding. IÕve never gotten into drugs or anything like that. So, as much as sheÕd be freaking out if I broke curfews, I was always like, IÕll just deal with whatever sheÕs gonna yell at me about because I know IÕm just out skateboarding. 
Judd: I got into comedy because I wanted answers. I had that same thing, where my parents werenÕt that strict, and I was always rambling around. 
Ed: My grandparents were essentially my father and mother figures, since my mom also had like a little bit of brain damage when she was younger. SheÕs totally fine and raised two kids and knows right from wrong, but her mentality is simple. I was basically raising myself at some point. Discipline and stuff like that came from my grandparents. If I did something bad, IÕd have to go stay at their house, which was great, because they cooked great food and had all this knowledge. I think being semi-raised by that older generation helped me a lot. 
Judd: What did they do? 
Ed: My grandfather was in graphic design, but my creativity definitely came from my grandmother. She was always doing arts and crafts with us, took us to museums, so I had that exposure of looking at art. I would watch my grandmother sit there and look at a Modigliani painting at LACMA [the Los Angeles County Museum of Art] or something for five minutes and then discuss it. Like, Okay, thatÕs what we do. A guy paints this thing, and we look at it and we talk about it. ThatÕs cool. 
Judd: What did your grandfather think of your skateboarding? 
Ed: He couldnÕt fathom skateboarding. My grandfather was always super straitlaced, like, I donÕt cuss, IÕm religious, IÕm RepublicanÑall that stuff. After Pearl Harbor, he signed up and went straight into the navy. To his credit, he loved us unconditionally, and he basically did support my skateboarding, even if he didnÕt understand it, didnÕt think it was worth anything, and thought I was wasting my time like someone from that generation would. But he still let me do it and kind of supported it. 
Judd: How old were you when you first started making money from skateboarding? 
Ed: I was eighteen when I brought the first check home, like, IÕm a pro skater now, hereÕs three thousand dollars I made this month as a pro. 
Judd: ThatÕs a lot of money. 
Ed: I was making around three to eight grand a month right off the bat. But I always felt guilty about that success and that I had all this culture and skateboarding and all this stuff that made me happy, whereas my brother was just doing nothing. He was, like, a construction guy, and I had weird guilt about him that would bring me to tears thinking about how he got the short end of the stick. 
Judd: Is he happy? 
Ed: I think I eventually realized that I canÕt be sad, because heÕs not sad. For him, coming home from a hard day of work and opening a beer and watching the Packers game is all he needs, thatÕs it. For me, that would be torture; I need so much more. I have so much more I want to do than that. But thatÕs him, and heÕs fine with it. 
Judd: That sounds like survivorÕs guilt. 
Ed: ItÕs exactly that. I feel like I got the good end. We both had the same grandparents helping us and everything, and somehow I came out where I am. But, then, maybe itÕs conceited to think that IÕm so great. I just felt like I came out on top for what was offered to me. 
Judd: I think itÕd be better for everyone if they all expressed themselves through art, culture, through making things, but a lot of people just donÕt want to, because they couldnÕt care less and theyÕre happy as can be. There are people who, all day long, are thinking, How can I help the people in Syria? Or, How can I design this shoe to help you run faster? They are certainly not writing jokes. 
Ed: HereÕs one way to look at it for me. As a photographer, youÕre walking around, youÕre making a reading of somebody based on their looks. I sometimes realize that I have so much crap in my head that maybe these other people donÕt have, and IÕm reading into stuff way more than they are. For instance, IÕm trying to read whether this guy is going to kill me if I try to shoot his photo. I once saw this girl with a mohawk hanging out with this other kid who has all this skinhead gear on. They were smoking and hanging on this corner, and they started kissing, and I was just like, Dude, this is such an incredible photo. IÕm just gonna go up and start shooting this. But back in my childhood, you would not go up to that guy. He would kill you. In Õ85, if you went up and tried to talk to a skinhead, you were going to get beaten up. ThatÕs how it was down here in Huntington at least. But that doesnÕt mean anything anymore. 
Judd: Now theyÕll say, ÒCan you take a selfie on my phone while youÕre doing it?Ó 
Ed: Exactly, I had to gather up all this bravery to go up to them, when their reaction was just like, ÒOh cool.Ó And I go, what am I thinking? This is a new generation. They all want to be famous. They love the attention. ThatÕs why they have a mohawk: because they want to be seen. So, it helps you realize, I have all this baggage that hinders me sometimes. The things that IÕm thinking about and my assumptions about peopleÑI feel like IÕm relearning that every day out shooting. 
Judd: When you started skateboarding, was the skateboarding scene pretty new? 
Ed: I started in Õ85, so it had been going on for a while. I always thought I came in kind of late, because it was the late seventies when skateboarding started becoming its own thing, where it wasnÕt just a thing that surfers did to pass the time when the waves were flat. By Õ85, when I started, there was a big spike. Tony Hawk and Steve CaballeroÑthese were the hot guys. And I think I was just one of the thousands of kids that got caught up in skateboarding in that time period. 
Judd: So, Tony Hawk is in the generation before you, by just a couple of years? 
Ed: I mean, at the time, when youÕre young and youÕre watching him, youÕre like, HeÕs super old. HeÕs an adult compared to me. But now IÕm looking at it like, Oh, Tony HawkÕs only four or five years older than me, at the most. 
Judd: What about that movie Dogtown and Z-Boys? Was that a couple of years before you? 
Ed: ThatÕs documenting the sixties. Craig Stecyk is the guy who was a big inspiration to me, and he still is. He does art and everything now, but heÕs the first person to contextualize skateboarding and see it as an art form, which I donÕt think the people doing it even thought. This guy was smart enough to see, Whoa, whatÕs happening here is incredible. This is a super weird, separate subculture that no one knows about. And IÕm gonna shoot photos of it and share that with people. 
Judd: And what years was he doing that? 
Ed: I think he wrote a famous article about it in the seventies. ItÕs the piece that everyone points to as the real beginning of skate culture. 
Judd: What were your intentions when you started skating? Was there a moment where itÕs like, ThereÕs the potential for it to be more than just fucking around. What was that transition for you? 
Ed: Do you know the actor Jason Lee? HeÕs been a pro skateboarder his whole life, and we both grew up together in Huntington. We skated together, and our healthy competition is what pushed each of us, I think. We loved each other, but we also kind of hated each other. We skated every day, but if he learned something new, then I had to learn it, and vice versa. It was a healthy competition that made us both get better, and we both started separating from the rest of the pack. And itÕs also all chance, too, becauseÑlo and behold, we happened to live in Southern California, where skateboarding was huge. So, if you were any good, the chances that you were getting seen were just insanely high. If I had been this good in Texas, no one would have ever known. 
Judd: What do you mean, Ògetting seenÓ? 
Ed: ThereÕd be, like, a trade show, and all the skateboarding companies would get together, and we would go out there. We wanted to be seen by these bigwigs, so we were like, ÒLetÕs go where we know all these industry guys are walking in and out, and weÕre gonna skate out in front and destroy it.Ó And thatÕs what happened. Steve Rocco, this guy who ended up starting one of the biggest companies in the late eighties, early nineties, saw Jason Lee skating out in front of a trade show and basically sponsored him on the spot. 
Judd: ThatÕs crazy. YouÕre just a kid, and thereÕs this thing youÕre doing for fun, and then youÕre like, ÒLetÕs show off in front of the building here,Ó and someone says, ÒHey, IÕll pay for all your stuff.Ó 
Ed: Yeah, basically. It starts with free boards, and then a sponsorship deal. 
Judd: Did you ever have a vision of skateboarding as a way to make a living? 
Ed: No, never. Cash was never a part of it. It was just about living that life. Had I never been sponsored, I think that everything would have been the same, almost. Skateboarding lets you not grow up, which has been amazing. 
IÕve been doing basically the same thing IÕve been doing since I was fourteen, and IÕve never stopped, so thatÕs super fortunate. 
Judd: At the same time, youÕre beginning to do art. 
Ed: I started skating in 1985, and by 1990, I was pro, and I started painting in 1990. 
Judd: When did you start photography? 
Ed: I think the moment where I decided I really need to shoot here was in Õ94. ThatÕs when it hit me, that I had been living this super-charmed life, where you get paid to cruise around the world skating in front of people. That is insane. And the culture that I was a part of was insane, because I happened to be the only one who wasnÕt doing drugs and stuff, so I was the sober guy amongst a bunch of hard-partying kids. 
Judd: Did art replace partying? 
Ed: I donÕt know. I just never tried it. 
Judd: That takes balls when everyone around you, in your subculture, is high all the time. 
Ed: That was part of it. I had a little nugget of self-confidence, even in those early days, when all my friends were passing the joint around, like, ÒHey donÕt you want to smoke?Ó and I just said, ÒIf IÕm not gonna be your friend because of this, then fuck you guys.Ó And they didnÕt care. 
Judd: My mom gave me five bucks to never smoke when I was in fifth grade, and for some reason I wanted to honor that for a long time. 
Ed: I remember seeing my dad drunk all the time and smoking weed all the time. And there were a couple of moments when both my mom and my dad would pass out, and IÕd be sitting there unable to wake them up. It was just me and my brother sitting in the house, like, ÒWhat do we do?Ó So, by the 
time I got into skateboarding, I was like, I donÕt want to be part of that whole party scene. 
Judd: Another example of rebelling in a healthy way. 
Ed: Exactly. I just wanted to get stuff done. Four years into that scene is when I realized I have something here to document. I sometimes try to explain it like, what if Robert Plant was a documentary photographer and could document those crazy stories you hear about Led Zeppelin on tour? I kind of have that situation here, where I can document this scene. 
Judd: Did you have any influences for that style of documentary photography? 
Ed: There was this photographer named Nan Goldin. And Larry Clark put out Teenage Lust, a book that documented his junkie loser scene in Tulsa, Oklahoma. I saw that book, and I realized, I have my own world here. IÕm going to shoot this world like these guys did theirs. I started taking it super seriously and started carrying a camera everywhere and just documenting everything. 
Judd: When did you first have somebody show interest in showing your work? Did it become art for you, or something that could also pay your bills? 
Ed: As much as I want to say that I dug my way into this position with hard work, being a famous skateboarder helped a lot. None of the galleries I worked with knew I was a skateboarderÑthey all worked with me because of the work, which IÕm happy about. But for ten years, I used my skateboard money to fund my art career. I was making money doing one thing, so I was able to do this other thing. 
Judd: There are a lot of skateboarders who are into art. What do you think that connection is? I was talking to Spike Jonze about it, and he said filmmaking is like skateboarding because youÕre always trying to invent tricks. The whole point of skateboarding is to be inventive. ThatÕs why, when he started filmmaking, he thought, I need to reinvent the wheel every time and come up with a way to shoot that no oneÕs done before. 
Ed: ThatÕs a good way to see it. When I started, I was instantly put in this world of creative people. Then, as a sponsored skater, IÕm suddenly around all these photographers who have to shoot me for magazines. Every day, I was going out and skateboarding with a dude who had a camera, and I would ask him, ÒHow does that work? Tell me about exposure and stuff.Ó So, I feel like the whole endeavor was creative and do-it-yourself, because the whole attitude at the time was No oneÕs going give you a shot at the Whiskey a Go Go, so you might as well just rent a warehouse and make your own show. As kids doing art stuff, no oneÕs going to put you in Artforum. Instead, somebodyÕs going to make a zine, make a bunch of copies at KinkoÕs and give them out for free. 
Judd: That seems different from comedy, or from people who want to be onstage and perform, where thereÕs creativity and entrepreneurship, but also egomania to it. ThereÕs a sense of, I want to be Eddie Murphy, or I want to be Bill Murray. Millions of people are going to pay attention to me and like me. 
What youÕre describing feels very different, because it is art for artÕs sake. ItÕs not like you thought this was going to take off. 
Ed: Yes, but thereÕs an ego part of it, for sure. As much as I try to suppress it and be humble, you just know that itÕs there, itÕs part of you. I want to have books out. I want to put stuff out there. And there is an ego to that. I look at an artist like David Hockney and go, I want that giant studio. I want that acclaim even when IÕm that old. I want to look back and say I inspired all these people. You have to have a goal. I donÕt try and hide that fact. 
Judd: When you look at all of your work, is there a consistent idea that runs through everything? 
Ed: I always wanted to have a take, to have a point. And it changes, of course ÑitÕs never a fixed thing. My book Deformer is about how you get deformed into the person you are from all the stuff that happens to you, whether itÕs your crappy family life or whatever. ThatÕs the deformer that makes you who you are. And as a photographer, what IÕm showing you is what I look at. As much as I say IÕm just documenting life as it is, IÕm hoping that, as the viewer, you get into my body and go, Okay, this is how he sees the world. 
Judd: You got hurt this year. Is this the first year in a while that you havenÕt been able to skateboard? 
Ed: I can still skate, but three years ago, when I broke my leg, I slowed down a lot. I had to decide whether or not I was going to try and come back to the pro level, and if I did want to do that, I would have to have one of these plates taken out, and the doctor was like, ÒIÕd rather have you leave it in.Ó So, he was saying retirement. I was just like, As long as I can skate, IÕm fine. I just didnÕt want to stop skating. 
Judd: ThatÕs a long run though. ItÕs a Nolan RyanÐtype of skating career. 
Ed: I was lucky. The kids nowadays are higher, faster, and stronger than we were, but their careers are shorter Õcause theyÕre jumping down crazy high heights, and they get hurt a lot. IÕm lucky to have had this really long skate career and have stayed relatively healthy. I mean, I broke my neck and I broke my leg real bad. But relatively, IÕve been lucky. 
Judd: The people that you started skating with, where are they now? 
Ed: Well, Jason Lee went on to acting. He had that personality type that was going to succeed, whatever he did. Spike is also another person who went on to bigger fame once he left the skateboarding scene. 
Judd: Mike Mills. 
Ed: Mike Mills. He wasnÕt in my skate scene, but he was a skater. 
Judd: I thought that movie was great, the Beautiful Losers documentary. 
Ed: ItÕs funny. I feel like a lot of us have tried to distance ourselves from that. I think that trying to make us sound like an artistic movement is weird. The Cubists were like, We have a manifesto. WeÕre all going to paint this way. But with the Beautiful LosersÑeveryoneÕs all over the place. I shot photos, I do paintings. And my art or style has nothing to do with Clare Rojas or Barry McGee. The only connection is that we all come from some kind of alternative subculture, whether itÕs the world of surfing, skating, or graffiti. ThatÕs the connection. 
Judd: ItÕs the complete wrong word to describe all of you, because everyoneÕs doing really well, and thereÕs no loser aspect to that, but itÕs kind of catchy for your show. And then people call you that for the rest of your life. 
Ed: Yeah, weÕre the Beautiful Losers, like Freaks and Geeks. 

GARY GULMAN 
January 2020 
Judd: YouÕre on the tour following the special, so now you have the crowd that watched and appreciated the special. What does that feel like? Are they coming for pain that youÕre not providing, or are they so psyched that youÕre happy? 
Gary: I wonder. There are people who meet me in line after the show, and theyÕre in tears. You and I have been aware of depression for many years, and people have been open and abundant in our world, so, when I first started working on this, I thought maybe IÕd missed the wave, that This is something that everybody is familiar with, and I wonÕt be saying anything new. To the contrary: Everyone is relieved that I want to tell them. I wasnÕt the first person to bring this up. I could sense this from Richard Lewis in the eighties. 
Judd: But Richard Lewis never really told us. 
Gary: He wasnÕt explicit about his diagnosis, and he wasnÕt clear on his treatment, so maybe that was my adjustment or addition to it. Who knows how many people it reached, but all the people who are coming to my show have either seen it or knew somebody who it affected. The people who are in tears after the showÑtheyÕve been so thankful for it. I donÕt know what to say except that it was a pleasure. I mean, IÕve been rewarded for it to such an extraordinary degree. By the time I started writing it, the hard part was really over. The livingÑthatÕs the brave part. When people say, ÒYou are so braveÓÑthe bravery was getting out of bed and walking my dogs. That was really hard. Talking about it to audiences who were complimentary and appreciativeÑthatÕs been easy; thatÕs been so rewarding. 
Judd: When youÕre in the throes of depression, does it almost feel like youÕre another person? 
Gary: It lies so beautifully. IÕve never been addicted to a drug, but people always say the addiction lies to you and tells you anything to get you into the position where youÕll feed the drug habit or the alcohol habit. Depression is the same thing. ItÕs telling you youÕre talentless, and then you donÕt want to get onstage, because What have I got to say thatÕs worthwhile? 
Judd: What else is it telling you other than that youÕre talentless? What is your depression saying? 
Gary: That this is who I am, this is not a disease. This is who you are, and you have made so many mistakes over the years that youÕll never come out from under. YouÕre a bad person, youÕre a really bad person, and if anybody knew who you really were, they would be horrified, and you wouldnÕt have a friend in the world, because you are petty and you are jealous and youÕre undisciplined and youÕre weak and lazy andÑ 
Judd: So, itÕs a critical voice? 
Gary: ItÕs so critical. 
Judd: Because, when I was depressed, I just cried a lot. I didnÕt think I was a bad person. I felt like I had switched gears somehow and felt weak and shaky. I remember I went to a psych pharmacologist. My therapist said, ÒYou have to go see this person. He will decide what drugs you should be on, or if you should be on drugs.Ó And he just says, ÒHow you doing?Ó and I just started crying so hard. 
Gary: ThatÕs triggering, I felt the same way when people asked me. I remember going to see this pharmacistÑhe was behind the counterÑand he said, ÒHow you doing, big guy?Ó And I put my head on his shoulder and I wept. He said, ÒAll right, come into the office,Ó and we talked. Obviously, it wasnÕt a CVS. It can be as simple as How are you doing? I was always on the verge of tears, and that was another reason why I couldnÕt go out and do shows. There was one night where I rushed to the end of my set because I was about to break down in tears. While reciting the set, I was also thinking, 
After this is over, IÕm going to have to go home and be with myself. 
Judd: I remember when I was at the therapist, I was crying before I even got into how I was feeling. I just felt like crying instantly. Even when youÕre depressed, there is a solid part of your mind thatÕs going, Wow, youÕre really depressed right now, and is observing it. So, as IÕm crying to this guy uncontrollably, harder than IÕve cried to almost any person in my life, thereÕs another part of my mind that goes, I think youÕre going to get the medication. 
Gary: That is hilarious. IÕve been trying for years to make crying funny. Because itÕs one of those things where, if the six-day crying jag could be a bit, then IÕve gotten something out of it. 
Judd: One of the magical things about being a comedian is when bad things happen you think, I can use this in an act, and itÕs not purely bad. 
Gary: ThereÕs revenge and redemption over these tragedies if you can get a joke out of it. 
Judd: What other reactions did you get from the comedy community about the special? 
Gary: The thing that I couldnÕt get over was how people who I didnÕt think were even aware of me were telling me how happy they were for me. That was so nice. We learn over the years how to discount compliments, because some of the hyperbole is so ridiculous. We have to figure out from the people we work with what their compliment sounds like. Sometimes people damn you with faint praise. I had a friend who, no matter how well I did on a show, would say, ÒYou looked great.Ó 
Judd: Whose appreciation meant the most to you? 
Gary: David Letterman said really great things. He really liked it. And Amy Schumer wrote something nice, and her sister wrote something in a similarly thoughtful manner that wasnÕt the typical, ÒWe loved it!Ó Everybody loves everything out here, but they said things. ThenÑso many messages from people in the mental health community. That was really moving. Doctors and professors, psychologists, social workersÉ 
Judd: What did they say is the benefit of having done it? When they thank you for doing it, what are they thanking you for? 
Gary: One thing a lot of them say is, ÒIÕm going to show it to my clients,Ó or, ÒIÕm going to show it to my students.Ó The other thing is that they said it reduces the stigma. ItÕs been so hard to get people to go into the hospital or use ECT or medicine. ThereÕs so many people who are reluctant to even try medication for short term. Even people in my world, whoÕve seen what itÕs done with me, and theyÕre still reluctant. The other thing with comedians is they say theyÕre afraid to lose their edge, their comedy, their sharpness. If that were the case, then you can just stop using the medication, but I found that I wrote maybe five, ten minutes during the two and a half years I was sick. Since then, I wrote The Great Depresh and IÕm touring with itÑninety minutes to an hour and forty-five, depending on the audience. 
Judd: That is the lie of all drugs and alcohol and medication. When you feel good, you generally write better. IÕve never written anything fantastic in a terrible mood. 
Gary: IÕm critical when IÕm feeling well, but the criticism when IÕm not feeling well is paralyzing. 
Judd: I went on Prozac for six months, and my therapist said, ÒItÕs to even you out so you can go to therapy, start absorbing those lessons, and then, when you go off of it, maybe youÕll be in a new place.Ó And it turned out to be that way. That was the one time I had clear depression, which was uncontrollable. I had very bad panic attacks. I started getting them because I would take on these jobs punching up scripts, and in my head I thought, If this movie is bad, itÕs only my fault. I put this ridiculous amount of pressure on myself. So, IÕd be rewriting Happy Gilmore, and IÕm with all my friends, and weÕre all having fun, but IÕm having this irrational thought: I can take them all down if I donÕt make this scene historically great. When I started having the panic attacks, I didnÕt know what they were. Just my temperature is rising, my heart is pounding, IÕm claustrophobic, IÕm melting down, but IÕve never heard of a panic attack. IÕm a kid. So, I just think IÕve gone insane. I had them for a while before I finally spoke to a therapist, who said, ÒOh, thatÕs just a panic attack. HereÕs what you doÉÓ 
Gary: ÒJust.Ó 
Judd: The secret was you donÕt try not to have a panic attack, because that makes it worse. You donÕt run away from it. You allow yourself to feel it, and you remind yourself that everything will be fine, that nothingÕs going to happen. When you try to stop it, itÕs like taking a mirror and smashing it on the ground and stamping on the bits and creating a thousand mirrors. 
Gary: ThatÕs a great analogy. 
Judd: I think when youÕre young, you donÕt know what this is, you donÕt know how to take care of yourself, and you donÕt know who to speak to. 
Gary: You donÕt understand that not everybody feels this way. 
Judd: ThatÕs what the special does. It sends a message to everyone that you didnÕt get as a [young] person: that tons of people have this. ItÕs not as big a deal as you think it is. 
Gary: Jerry West the basketball star talked about having depression, and Bruce Springsteen said he had depression, and it made me feel better because I thought, If I had their lives, I would be happy and wake up every day in a great mood. While I donÕt think IÕm anybodyÕs Springsteen, it made other comedians think, Oh. Being at his level is not the answer. That was a great thing in Funny People, which I rewatched after the special, because you called me the next day, telling me, ÒItÕs normal for you to feel hungover and guilty about not feeling on top of the world after this special airs. Your adrenaline has been squirting constantly for three weeks and now itÕs over.Ó 
Judd: ItÕs normal to get depressed after success, because you realize that it doesnÕt really make you happy in your core. 
Gary: That was the Funny People message as well: that all this stardom wasnÕt the answer. It was the people, the misfitsÑthatÕs the answer. 
Judd: I needed a seventy-million-dollar budget to make the movie, but now people can say it in a podcast for free. My version of The Great Depresh was Funny People. I want to express it to other people, but itÕs a message to myself. 
Gary: I feel the same way with The Great Depresh. So many times, I think to myself that I got more out of it than the audience. Because I would forget the lesson every single time. The lesson was always to do the thirty things every dayÑor however many it is to feel goodÑand not chase some achievement and not get wrapped up in an outside external project that was supposed to make me happy and didnÕt. 
Judd: Now that you have some distance from the special, what do you think is the main idea that you wanted to tell people or yourself? 
Gary: I think maybe two or three things. One: YouÕre not alone. A lot of us are feeling this way; they just havenÕt told you. My therapist had told me fifteen years before, ÒIf you tell everybody everything, theyÕll have nothing on you. You donÕt have to be so private. You donÕt have to keep all your flaws to yourself. ItÕll strengthen your relationships; people wonÕt run away.Ó That taught me youÕre not alone. And as soon as you say, ÒYouÕre not alone,Ó other people are saying, ÒOh, I felt alone.Ó Then, two: That there was hope, that it ends, that thereÕs treatment, a lot of options for coming out of it, and that itÕs worth it. ItÕs worth it. The line I really thought came off as too schmaltzy was ÒIÕm so glad I stuck around for this,Ó but it was true. I wanted to kill myself. I thought about it. I had plans. I had garages, I had things to jump off of. And thank God, I stuck around. Did you ever read ÒAn Occurrence at Owl Creek BridgeÓ? Sometimes it feels like thatÑthat IÕm living all this [while] falling from the gallows. IÕm pinching myself all the time, because IÕm thinking theyÕre showing me what I missed out on by killing myself at the worst of it. 
Judd: And how close do you think you got? 
Gary: Well. 
Judd: Or, is there a comfort in just thinking it through? 
Gary: There was the time in L.A. that I stood in my windowsill, but then, luckily, I pictured myself halfway down, thinking, I regret this, because I had read a New Yorker article about peopleÑor just the entire San FranciscoÕs Golden Gate Bridge. 
Judd: ThereÕs a documentary about that. 
Gary: Oh, really? 
Judd: About all the people who tried to kill themselves. 
Gary: Yes. Every person who survived said that they regretted it almost immediately after they started falling. Have I told you the comedy condo story? In Denver, at the Comedy Works, thereÕs a great comedy condo. WeÕve all stayed at the ones that are shitholes, but this one is actually really nice. ItÕs in a luxury building, and it had a kitchen with that butcher block of knives. I pulled out the biggest one, held it to my wrist, and luckily thought, 
ThereÕs a person whoÕs going to have to clean this up on Monday and hates cleaning up after comedians who are alive, and this is going to ruin their life. 
That was how I rationalized my way out of that. And then I had all kinds of pill things. And then there was the night I put a plastic bag over my head. It doesnÕt work. I think you need another person with the plastic bag technique. 
Judd: Is that because of the depth of the place youÕre going to or how long you had been depressed? 
Gary: For how long I had been sick and that there didnÕt seem to be any coming out. My depression also told me, Even if you were to turn your life around at this point, whatÕs the point? I tell everyone who will listen that thereÕs no Òtoo late to have made it.Ó ThereÕs no point where youÕre like, ÒOh, I was too old; I canÕt enjoy it.Ó 
Judd: Throughout this, you were married, or you were [still] dating your wife, Sade? 
Gary: I was living with Sade since 2015. 
Judd: ItÕs also clearly a chemical meltdown, because youÕre having it at a moment when youÕre also in love and supported by someone. So, the cloud isnÕt about the circumstances of life. 
Gary: I think itÕs a chemical disorder, because there have been moments in my life where I was in such a worse position. I had been engaged in 2008 to a woman who was into conspiracy theories. The stock market was crashing, and she convinced me to take my retirement fund and buy a house upstate in New York, a farmhouse on a farm, because the economy was going to fall apart, and we needed to live off the land. Six months in, she got bored and less interested in me, moved out, and I was stuck with a farmhouse in the middle of the woods. My closest neighbor was a mile away. Most people just had it as a summer place. There was really nobody around, and I became best friends with my dog groomer. To this day, weÕre close friends. She would say, ÒDo you want to go to yoga today?Ó and then we would go to an AA meeting. IÕm not sober, I donÕt drink, but I would go to those meetings, and those were my people. I had no money, I had this mortgage, and I was having to drive two hours to go in and do spots. 
Judd: For thirty dollars? 
Gary: Yes. I was losing money hand over fist, and I was much happier than I was coming off a special, making a living, having a Midtown apartment for four thousand dollars a month and a woman who loved me and was doing everything in her power to save me. So, yeah. 
Judd: When I was a kid, my parents got divorced, and it was really ugly. I wish my parents [had] sent me to a therapist. I wish IÕd learned some mental health skills and understood what was going on. It took until my mid-twenties to begin to start to understand it, and my most recent therapist is saying things I have never heard before that are life-changing, and IÕm fifty-two. 
Gary: Like what types of things? 
Judd: He talks a lot about evolution and fight-or-flight responses. Cavemen were trying not to get eaten by predators, so your memory is programmed to remember bad things. If thereÕs a bear in a cave that you walk into, youÕll remember never to walk in that cave, because thatÕs where you saw the bear, but if something good happens to you, youÕre not programmed to remember it unless something highly emotional happens around it. As a result, your mind is filled with more bad things than good things, because the bad things served your safety. But now weÕre in modern life, and things arenÕt bad, but your brain is still seeking to protect you by scaring the shit out of you. He also says that, to your brain, the safest place you could be is in bed in your apartment. ItÕs not just you. ThatÕs how weÕve evolved. WhatÕs the safest thing I can do for you? Make sure youÕre not around where the bears are. And it does make perfect sense, this inclination to be negative. YouÕre really going against your mind if youÕre the crazy positive person. If you want to be, it has to be a practice. You have to rewire your brain. 
Gary: But can you rewire it? 
Judd: You canÕt if you donÕt know that. It took until I was fifty-two years old to realize that when IÕm hypervigilant and when IÕm looking for trouble for no reason, it has nothing to do with me. Maybe a hundred of those happen a day. Like, IÕm going to make you fight with your wife about leaving early to the airport because we got to get there even though thereÕs no reason to get there early. The lack of control is terrifying. I always connected that to my parentsÕ divorce, but I think itÕs well beyond that. The idea that if IÕm very organized and I keep my shit together and IÕm successful, life wonÕt collapse around me, but that makes you argue with someone if theyÕre going to make you late for the airport because youÕve raised the stakes. 
Gary: A lot of philosophers and psychologists will say you have no control ever, and to them itÕs very freeing. To meÑit makes me want more control. 
Judd: But is there peace in letting go? The only mystical experience IÕve ever hadÑI was on my way to promote Funny People in Australia. I thought the movie was going to be a big hit. It wasnÕt. It was very expensive, and it got reasonably good reviews, but at the box office, it didnÕt do as well as it needed for how expensive it was. And I really had put so much of my heart into it. My mom died the year before, and I hadnÕt dealt with it in a healthy way. I dealt with it by making a movie about it. And I must have been very stressed out. On the plane, I heard a voiceÑthe only time that had ever happened to me in my lifeÑand it just said, Surrender. It wasnÕt even a concept that I knew a lot about at the time. It wasnÕt like I was reading a lot of self-help books using that word. But it felt like God. And I [had] never had an experience like that before. It was a couple of minutes on a plane where I really understood what the word surrender meant. 
Gary: It was a voice or a thought? 
Judd: Well, it was beyond a voice and beyond thought. It was just in me, a message. 
Gary: And you were awake? 
Judd: IÕm awake, and itÕs not coming off thinking about the subject. It just hit me like a truck. Powerfully, physicallyÑjust Surrender. Stop trying. Just let it all go. Let everything go. And of course I ignored it. 
Gary: How did that change your trip to Australia? 
Judd: I did have a good trip, and I think it was very helpful to me for a long time. I certainly havenÕt thought enough about it. But it was the only religious experience IÕve ever had, whatever that means. 
Gary: I think that all along the way on this special and everything since, IÕve had to divorce myself from the results and reset the expectations. The healthiest thing was that once I had something that people were reacting to in the live show, I said, As long as youÕre making these live shows, people feel less alone, feel a little bit better, feel understood, whether HBO passes or whether HBO puts it on and people donÕt like it, you still helped a lot of people. I just pray to God, had it not been well received, that I would have gotten through the way I got through the last quote-unquote failure. 
Judd: Because, in your head, you didnÕt like the last special, and it started a spinout. Would you have had the spinout anyway if it wasnÕt a failure? 
Gary: Exactly. It made me reckon with how I viewed myself since five or six years old: If IÕm great at something, then IÕll feel good about myself. And also: IÕm not a real man. IÕm not aggressive. I read an article by Peggy Orenstein in The AtlanticÑshe had covered girls for twenty-five years as a reporter, and now she wanted to write a book about boysÑand I really expected to find out that Millennial boys were in touch with their emotions and could cry and were able to express traditionally feminine ideas and thoughts. But this woman, in her reporting, found that guys still equate manhood with being aggressive and dominant and that silence was the key to being a man and that crying was humiliating. I thought, Wow, I had no idea that itÕs still going on. 
Judd: But that also can be partially evolutionary. It could have something to do with thousands of years of men thinking, This is the way IÕm supposed to behave, this is my role, I fight the bear. You canÕt cry while fighting the bear. I like blaming everything on evolution. 
Gary: When I was reading Feeling Good, which is sort of the bible of cognitive behavioral therapy, the fight-or-flight panic reaction is in the first chapter. And the cortisol is poison. 
Judd: I was reading this morning that when your cortisol and adrenaline kick in, your brain goes into a fog, and it has trouble making choices. It delays the amount of time it takes for you to figure things out. So, the moment when youÕre in a panic is a moment when youÕre less clear than you normally are, and youÕre more reactive, so youÕre more likely to make the wrong decision if youÕre in a panic. That explains every horror movie. 
Gary: That makes perfect sense. But the viewer has a different perspective, because theyÕre thinking clearly, because theyÕre not in danger; theyÕre in a movie theater. ItÕs taken me all these repetitions to figure out, This is where your brain goes. On Wednesday, I listened to a set that I did because I didnÕt want to repeat the jokes, and I was like, Oh, IÕm terrible. I am a terrible comedian. And then I went over my notes, put together a setlist for the Wednesday night show, and before the show started, I was anxious, and I thought, This is where they find me out. But I was able to step outside of that, like another narrator saying, This is just what you do. YouÕre anxious because you havenÕt been onstage in three or four days, and you just listened to a set, and everyone listens to a set and hates it. 
Judd: I donÕt listen to the sets. My fear of losing material for the ages isnÕt enough to get me to listen to it. ThatÕs why Adam Driver doesnÕt watch his movies. He feels like it will make him self-conscious, that he has to be so committed and put himself out there that if he increases the critical voice in his head by watching all of his movies and being hard on himself, he wonÕt do as good a job on the next one. Bill Hader was like that. But then he created his own show and directed it and had no choice but to pay attention to what he does, and I think it really benefited him. But there are a lot of people that never watch their own movies. 
Gary: The adjustment I made with this project was I trusted Mike Birbiglia and you to tell me if it was terrible. I just have to accept that itÕs good enough for them, so it has to be good enough for me. Because when I watch myself, I can see every flaw, everything that I can change, so I just have to trust some people whose taste I believe in. 
Judd: Sometimes when I watch myself, IÕll think, Look at that guy acting all cocky. Shut the fuck up. 
Gary: ThatÕs exactly what I say to myself. Who do you think you are? What a fucking phony. 
Judd: Even when itÕs going well, you hate that guy: Oh, look at you, having a good time. Shut up. Go home. For me, the negative voice just always said, DonÕt be a comedian. Even when I have a good set, sometimes I feel it creep in. I did a set the other night, and the show went well, and I had a good set and enjoyed talking to everybody, and then I drive homeÑitÕs about a thirty-minute driveÑand after just ten minutes, the voice just goes, What did you say to everybody? What were you talking about in the greenroom? Was that set okay? It wasnÕt that good. I think Tom Papa killed way harder than you. 
ItÕs very hard to let that voice go or distract myself to not engage it. Because, for me, it really wants me to stop. It really says, You should not be doing this. ItÕs embarrassing. ItÕs embarrassing that you want to do this. I have to constantly remind myself why I do it, how it makes me feel, that maybe some people get a kick out of it, but that voice wants me to not get up again. ItÕs still a pretty loud voice. When you go to a club, and thereÕs comedians around, do you start trying to tune in to Do people here think I know how to do this? 
Gary: I mean, thatÕs the thing. ItÕs good for us, though, I think, because we donÕt want to be the comedian who we roll our eyes at. The guy who doesnÕt know that heÕs dated. When somebody in their thirties or younger tells me that they enjoyed themselves, IÕm like, Okay, IÕve got another four or five years to rock this. If I can just keep watching the younger people in the show. I take Emmy Blotnick on the road with me, and then Dina Hashem and this other woman who IÕve seen at the Comedy Cellar a few times, named Brittany Carney. Then there are guys older than me, like Eddie Pepitone, and I think, All right, if I can be embraced by Eddie or Todd GlassÉ 
Judd: I think one of the hard things about being a comedian is you meet so many other comedians and you want all of them to like you. 
Gary: No matter what the style is. 
Judd: No matter who they are, no matter their personalities, you want every single one of them to like you, respect you, and have a warm relationship with you, and itÕs statistically impossible to not have five or ten percent of those people hate you just based on your personality. I noticed, when I was doing stand-up these last few years, that IÕm meeting a lot of people and IÕm getting a little neurotic. Suddenly, IÕm in the clubs a couple nights a week. IÕm meeting hundreds of people, and thatÕs a lot of people to wonder if they respect you, why they donÕt return your text quickly. If youÕre a neurotic person, itÕs a lot to tap your neurosis into. 
Gary: ItÕs unenviable going in after you havenÕt been onstage in twenty years, in a position where if youÕre not immediately up to speed with the other comedians, people are like, Why is this guy getting back into it? That is not easy. I used to think if you didnÕt do it for twenty years, even five years, you would never be able to pick it up again. I think it was helpful seeing you, because I was planning on giving it up for six months, and part of me was like, You may never get it back. YouÕll lose your spot in line. But you made the right choice because you could have killed at mainstream clubs, the small improvs, but to go to Largo and the Comedy Cellar is going off the high-dive board. 
Judd: I didnÕt know what I was walking into. The CellarÑeveryone is such a killer there. Everyone is a beast, but you just get used to walking in there, and they say, ÒYouÕre following Ray Romano tonight,Ó or, ÒYouÕre following Jim Norton tonight.Ó ÒYouÕre following Amy Schumer.Ó I can see how it would be hard to do, year after year after year: to generate more material, feel engaged, to not feel repetitive to yourself.
