Gary: That was part of my breakdown. I didnÕt have new stuff to go out with, and so I stopped going out, and what I didnÕt realize was that part of what was keeping me sane was getting out of the house once a day to be with a bunch of people who I could be myself with. 
Judd: It does make you happy to just go there. I found that itÕs put me in a much better mood. Even making a movie this year, which I really enjoy, I wind up in an editing room for six months, but I feel like my mood has been much worse the last six months. With a set, the stakes are lower, but the joy is higher. 
Gary: The feedback is instant. 
Judd: There is something of value when you make a movie in that it can touch people for a long time, but in terms of your own endorphins, just trying out a bunch of stupid jokesÑthe amount of chemical joy I got out of it was so high. I never want to feel like my self-esteem comes from getting approval, but I also feel like it reminds me what I do, what my contribution is, so itÕs nice to be around people. ItÕs not just that I want to be applauded; itÕs more like, Oh, youÕve seen the stuff, itÕs meant something to you, and then, when I go back to work writing something, it helps that I did a Q-and-A somewhere, and people reminded me that they remember some movie or something connected with them. ItÕs easy for that to drift away, especially in a world where thereÕs so much content. You think, ThereÕs five hundred TV series right now. What is the point of being five hundred and one? 
Gary: Well, because five hundred and one could be Freaks and Geeks. 
Judd: ThatÕs why you have to leave the house to remind yourself. 
Gary: The first time we ever met was in a general [meeting], and I didnÕt have any ideas, and we just sat there, and I asked you questions about Freaks and Geeks, and you said that you had a cancellation and said, ÒLetÕs get everything in that we wanted to get in.Ó You made it seem like, out of necessity, you made it so good. 
Judd: It is true. There was some panic that there isnÕt going to be another eighteen of these, so What were we going to do in season three? Should we do it now? I was in the therapistÕs the other day, and the therapist is talking about my movies. HeÕs very aware of what I made. Sometimes he references it. So, he says, ÒJudd, you know you basically do the same thing every single time. You have somebody, and heÕs an insecure guy, and itÕs a coming-of-age story, and he has to learn how to become a man, and so, bad things happen, and he gets his ass kicked a little bit, and then, at the end, he has to come through for someone and be a man, right? ThatÕs what you do, right?Ó It was so tossed off, not meant to disrespect, but completely accurate. And I thought, IÕm so transparent. I thought I[Õd] tricked people into thinking all these movies were different, and it did make me realize, Yeah, I guess all these movies arenÕt what I consciously thought they were a lot of the time. 
IÕm aware that they are coming-of-age stories, but he described them as learning how to be a man, which I never thought any of my movies were about. HeÕs relating it to Funny People and This Is 40, and in a way, Trainwreck is about being an adult. 
Gary: And Knocked Up. 
Judd: ItÕs funny, and it made me think about how there are certain things that you have trouble with as a person, and in your heart you talk about it over and over and over again. And itÕs hard to be an adult. ItÕs hard to be a man. The world challenges you. Can you get your shit together and evolve and learn the lesson you need to learn? Then I noticed something that was at the end of almost every movie IÕve ever made and every TV show, almost down to the episode: I tend to write an ending where one character selflessly is present for the other character, who desperately needs it. ItÕs everywhere. You can name anything. What is the end of This Is 40? ItÕs Paul [Rudd] and Leslie [Mann] watching Ryan Adams together. We know she probably doesnÕt like his taste in music, and sheÕs just trying to support him and show him she loves him. At the end of Knocked Up, Seth [RogenÕs Ben] reads the baby book, and he drives [Katherine HeiglÕs character, Alison] home from the hospital. 
Gary: ThatÕs a man. 
Judd: And in Funny People, Adam [Sandler] writes jokes for Seth. The big star drops all his bullshit and is present for Seth. ItÕs always a selfless, giving gesture. So, then I thought, I must want that so bad: Every time I write anything, thatÕs what it is. What is the end of Freaks and Geeks? ItÕs the prettiest girl in school dancing with the nerd, and then Lindsay Weir dancing with the mentally challenged kid. ItÕs a double. And I thought, I must try to force everyone I deal with to do that ending. It must be a mantra. Can you be selfless? Can you be giving? 
Gary: But you also have turned that into your life. The other side is the secret that it actually feels really good. I wonder if thatÕs part of the evolution. It feels good to be selfless. I read this Twain quote that if you want to cheer yourself up, cheer somebody else up. And it works, man. IÕm having a tough day, so I email the Conan guy somebodyÕs video. I try to get him on Conan, and he gets on Conan. ItÕs more exciting for me than the last time I did it. It just feels great. Because I remember what it was like to get my first TV spot. You change the way you view yourself as a comedian, and so did your family, which is huge. Also, IÕve read everything Philip Roth has written, everything Kurt Vonnegut has written, saw every Judd Apatow movie, every Woody Allen movie, every Albert Brooks movie. They all work in the same themes and ideas. You read one Kurt Vonnegut book, itÕs not the same book, but itÕs the same types of people, the same relationships, the same exchanges, the same morals. Woody has become so problematic, I canÕt watch his shit anymore, but he was pretty much telling the same story every time. 
Judd: Even when you think youÕre making different things youÕre making the same thing. When you were a kid, when did you first notice stand-up comedy or think to yourself, ThereÕs a world where I could attempt that? 
Gary: Well, my brother had a job at the movie theater, and who knows how many nights a week he worked, but he would watch the movies. There were two movies over the few years that he worked there that he pretty much memorized: Blazing Saddles and Young Frankenstein, and he could do them word for word. He would come home and have the entire family in stitches, so I knew I loved to laugh. I knew I loved comedy and jokes, and then David Brenner was on either Mike Douglas or Merv Griffin, and I fell in love with this form. Bill on Freaks and Geeks sitting in front of the TV table watching Garry Shandling on Dinah Shore: That was my childhood. My mother would yell, ÒComedian on!Ó IÕd be in the other room or outside, and IÕd come running in. I would memorize their jokes and get laughs in class. To those kids, anything Bill Murray said was automatically funny, and you can get laughs repeating it at school. In first grade, I remember the teacher said, ÒWhat is a chick?Ó And [one] kid said, ÒA baby chicken,Ó and then, without raising my hand, I say, ÒOr a girl.Ó And she laughed, and everyone in the class laughed, and I was hooked. The dopamineÑIÕm sure that was coursing through my feelings. It happened over this past Christmas. I was with SadeÕs family, and there were older relatives, Southern Black relatives, and they were telling stories, and I was laughing, and then I said something, and everybody laughed, and I was like, Oh man, IÕm having the time of my life. This is fantastic. It wasnÕt in front of a real audience, but I had an audience, and I was in seventh heaven. And a lot of people say, ÒGulman canÕt play the Black rooms.Ó 
Judd: Where were you doing it when you started? Massachusetts? 
Gary: Boston. NickÕs Comedy Stop in Boston. The emcee of my first open mic was Billy Martin, who writes for Bill Maher, and after my set, he said, ÒThat was wonderful,Ó and I was like, Oh he thinks IÕm wonderful! So, I just kept doing it. I had a very fortunate experience, but you look back on your early stuff, and youÕre like, Oh my gosh, where did I have the delusion to think that I would ever be good at this? I remember when I did this state abbreviations joke, and people were very positive, and then Patton Oswalt wrote this glowing appreciation of it on Facebook, and I thought, All right, you canÕt get out of bed, you hate yourself, and you think youÕre talentless. But this man, who you idolize, thinks youÕre funny. That is something. And it helped a little bit, it really did. 
Judd: When I have a project and people send me nice emails or tweet something nice, I will screenshot it and put it in a file I call ÒSelf-Esteem Support.Ó 
Gary: I do that now. ItÕs a rainy-day file. ItÕs so helpful. I remember Nikki Glaser said that I was her favorite comic. I added it to a list. It was called ÒDonÕt Forget,Ó and it said, ÒYouÕre Nikki GlaserÕs favorite comedian.Ó I feel like itÕs important that people who I think are funny think that IÕm funny. 
Judd: Those moments and compliments can power you for years. 
Gary: Now that I realize it, I try to do that. IÕm not phony about it, but whenever I get the opportunity, I want to keep the young people, or the people who arenÕt getting the recognition they deserve, going. 
Judd: I remember Paul Feig got a letter from Steven Spielberg saying he liked Freaks and Geeks. And the letterÕs beautiful. IÕm so jealous; I wanted that letter so bad. So, I wrote Spielberg a funny letter just to get a response, and I thought it would trigger a praise letter, and at some point I spoke to his assistant, and she said, ÒI read the letter. I donÕt think Spielberg will get these jokes.Ó So, he didnÕt even read it; she just stopped it because she knew it would be ineffective. Then, years later, when Knocked Up came out, I get a call like, ÒSteven Spielberg trying to reach you on the phone.Ó 
Gary: Holy shit. 
Judd: So, I refused to take the call, and I communicated that I wasnÕt available, but itÕd be great if he wanted to send me a note. And he did! He sent me a note and said ÒI feel like Knocked Up is this generationÕs American Graffiti.Ó ThatÕs the one that gets me through some of the rough spots. But it took years of manipulation to get it. 
Gary: You handled it perfectly. You knew what you wanted, and you made it happen. 
Judd: I hate that there are emails now. Only Tom Hanks will still type a letter on a typewriter. 
Gary: But you can print those out and frame them. 

GAYLE KING 
May 2020 
Judd: I want to ask you about the work youÕve done at O Magazine, because I am a self-help freak. What is your relationship with that world? 
Gayle: When the magazine started twenty years ago, OprahÕs mission was to do a personal growth guide that met women where they were, rather than telling them how they can get thin thighs in thirty days, so that they feel good about themselves and not try to be somebody theyÕre not. ThatÕs where we started, and IÕm proud to say weÕre still there, to this day. ThatÕs still a core mission that resonates today, because I feel like there are a lot of us wounded people walking around the planet, searching, trying to figure it out, trying to heal ourselves and heal each other. 
Judd: ItÕs also a weird world now, because social media has exposed us to everybodyÕs point of view. A lot of people act out their emotional issues on social media channels, and many of them donÕt really think anybodyÕs reading it. 
Gayle: We read it and take it all in, too. You get a hundred good compliments, but then thereÕs that one negative one from someone who you donÕt even know, whose opinion you shouldnÕt even care about. The thing is, you can say stuff on social media with no accountability, and thatÕs not a good thing. 
Judd: All the social media algorithms reward our worst instincts. If you like one type of conspiratorial video on YouTube, the algorithm will take you to something even meaner, because thatÕs how they think theyÕre keeping peopleÕs attention. In a way, theyÕre training you to watch the worst of us. 
Gayle: We are rewarded for bad behavior in general. Look at the shows that are doing well now. On all these different franchises, youÕre rewarded for being your worst possible self. IÕm no Pollyanna, but that stuff about doing unto others as you would have them do unto you, treat people the way you want to be treatedÑthat stuff is real, and we need to figure out a way to get back to that without sounding outdated or out of touch. 
Judd: Do you feel like everything youÕve done your whole life has prepared you for this moment, where people are looking to you at a time of crisis for information and emotional support? 
Gayle: Listen, I majored in psychology. I wasnÕt going to do what IÕm doing nowÑI was going to be a child psychologist. Or I was going to be a lawyer, because I love listening to peopleÕs problems and giving unsolicited advice. I love that and still do. But when I was in college, I got a job at a TV station and just became hooked on television news. You donÕt even realize youÕre learning stuff when youÕre learning it. You go from one job to the next, and itÕs not like youÕre saying, Now IÕm going to learn this. And now IÕm going to learn that. 
Judd: How do you prepare for your interviews, especially when youÕre going to be talking to someone unpredictable or under a lot of scrutiny? 
Gayle: Talk about being preparedÑthat came into play for me for the R. Kelly interview, when he just went batshit crazy in the middle of it. That was the first interview he did after that documentary, and he was saying it wasnÕt true, that everybody was lying. He really wanted to go on air and tell what he says was his truth. At the interview, he went from zero to one hundred and ten, just like that. Had that happened to me earlier in my career, I would have been afraid, but I had had enough experience and enough preparation. IÕd watched his past interviews and noticed that when he gets upset and mad, he leaves, and IÕm just sitting there with my papers, thinking, IÕm not done. I know if I made a movement to stop him or tried to calm him down, he would have stormed off. So, I just sat down. I looked at him, looked at the chair, looked at him, looked at the chair. I let him know, IÕm not going anywhere, whatever youÕre doing, IÕm not going anywhere. And I think he saw that. He sat back down. And I was sort of shocked at that, because I was sure he was going to leave. But he also really wanted to tell his story, as he saw it. When that interview was over, he thanked me for allowing people to see his passion and his pain. He actually thought it went well. So, are you prepared for that? No. But I rule my life by intention, too. I believe any question can be asked, I just think that thereÕs a time and a place to ask it. I never want to do a ÒgotchaÓ interview. I never want to humiliate anybody or put anybody on the spot. That said, there are times that you have to ask difficult and uncomfortable questions. 
Judd: ThereÕs also such a long history of people like R. Kelly, who were terribly abused in their childhoods, and then, for whatever reasons, they hurt other people. 
Gayle: Hurt people hurt people. But I also think, at some point, you have to take responsibility for your actions. Like, I know youÕre hurt. Your childhood was messed up. But that was then, this is now, and you have to take responsibility for your actions. 
Judd: You came up in the Anchorman-like world of late seventiesÐera American journalism. A world ruled by the male chauvinist pig and where sexual harassment was pervasive in office spaces. How was that for you as a young person jumping into the business? 
Gayle: There was a time where a camera guy said something very inappropriate, something like, ÒIÕd like to take you in the car and lick you all over.Ó I was twenty-three, and I didnÕt know what to say. Now you know what to say, but back then, even though I knew it was wrong and out of line, I just sort of ignored it. But I havenÕt had any stories where I felt like I was compromised by someone in a powerful position. Knock on wood. 
Judd: YouÕve been at the epicenter of this all as a journalist, it seems to keep intersecting with your life. 
Gayle: It really does. IÕve struggled with some parts of the MeToo movement. I think [the] MeToo movement is important, but I also think that, if weÕre not careful, it could possibly backfire on women, and IÕm concerned about that. You canÕt paint everybody with the same brush. For me, IÕm more inclined to believe the woman, but just because she makes the accusation, it doesnÕt mean that a guy has to be totally destroyed without investigating what it is. I think we have to be careful with that. 
Judd: ItÕs especially complex when race enters into the discussion. I have a friend who was abused by Bill Cosby, so I was always very vocal about the fact that I thought he was guilty, and people would get very upset about it, because he knocked down walls in Hollywood for other Black performers. But at the same time, heÕs a violent criminal. 
Gayle: Both things can be true. ThatÕs the thing. ItÕs not an either-or; in some of these cases, both things can be true. 
Judd: People donÕt know what to do with that, because they want a person to be a symbol so badly, and you canÕt say, ÒThis legacy is great, but heÕs also the worst guy ever.Ó 
Gayle: In the Black community, there are so few symbols for us to look up to. So, if a Black person does something bad, IÕm like, Oh, God, oh, no. I donÕt know of any white person in America who goes, Oh, no, theyÕre white. Oh no. 
Judd: We do that to Jews, like Harvey Weinstein. We ask, Why did it have to be another Jew? I think we all have some elements of that in our own communities, where weÕre like, CanÕt we have a nice guy? When you first got interested in journalism, what was your goal? Because it seems like you start anchoring pretty quickly. 
Gayle: I just love breaking news. I remember when I was in a newsroom and there was a breaking news story, and I would see these people running around to get the news on, and IÕd just go, Whoa. IÕve been hooked ever since. I do thinkÑI think itÕs an honorable profession. And weÕre not a very big club. It may seem like it, but weÕre not a big club, so I take it very, very seriously. ItÕs gotten so out of whack now, where people are so siloed, and you have extreme points of view over here and extreme perspectives over there. I try to give you the straight facts and let you sort it out for yourself. But weÕre also human beings, with a point of view; weÕre not robots sitting there. 
Judd: What do people think the politics of your show is? Are you getting people from the left and the right? 
Gayle: I donÕt think people think weÕre either far left or far right. I think that they think we pretty much give you the facts. Though, the Trump administration doesnÕt really like anybody in the mainstream media, so weÕre all painted with that brush. 
Judd: There are so few places left that feel like the Walter CronkiteÐtype of show, but itÕs what you do every morning. It feels like there are a decent number of Fox viewers watching you. 
Gayle: IÕve heard that. IÕve heard people say, ÒI donÕt like your station, but I like you.Ó Do I say, ÒThank youÓ? 
Judd: Do you find the current climate engaging and fun as a journalist, or is there a part of you that wishes you were in one of those mellow periods of American history, so you could cover lighter fare? 
Gayle: I feel like I did that already. I like having a front-row seat to whateverÕs happening, whatever that is, but this pandemic has struck me in a different way because of the uncertainty of it all. When you had Katrina, when you had 9/11, when you have a mass shooting, when there is a disaster normally, you know how itÕs going to play out. ThereÕs a beginning, where weÕre all going to rally together to fix it, and then weÕre going to go back to where things were. But this is such a moving target, because thereÕs so much the experts donÕt know. ThatÕs whatÕs so scary about it. IÕm in New York, my son and daughter are in L.A., OprahÕs in California. I donÕt know when IÕll be able to see the people I care about the most. 
Judd: Being a part of that messaging that you and Oprah are sending to people about spirituality and how they should treat other peopleÑhow has that affected you in this moment? 
Gayle: I do think people are open to it. ItÕs not woo-woo, and itÕs not complicated. For example, I think meditation is way more popular now than ever before. People are trying to figure out a way to center themselves, and you hear anecdotally about people reaching out more than they ever have to family and friends. People are craving connection. And it puts into perspective what really matters. You can have these great jobs that make a lot of money and big, wonderful houses and all that good stuff, but at the end of the day, you have people who are dying alone in the hospital. 
Judd: Of the experts youÕve had onÑbecause I know youÕve interacted with them personallyÑwho are the ones you connect with the most? 
Gayle: I really encourage you to talk to Bishop T. D. Jakes. He is amazing. ItÕs not all religion; he just has such good common sense. Maya Angelou was a big touchstone in terms of really helping me navigate different things. I really miss her now, because she was always so wise. I can remember calling her about something. I go, ÒThey said da da da da daÉÓ And she goes, ÒStop it. Just say thank you.Ó I go, ÒWhat do I say thank you about?Ó She said, ÒWhining is so unbecoming. It lets them know thereÕs a victim in the neighborhood. So, just say thank you.Ó And she would always say, ÒPeople may not remember what you said or what you did, but they will always remember how you made them feel.Ó ThatÕs also very true. I miss her voice. 
Judd: These philosophies help me, because they force you to live in the present. Long-term thinking is rough when everythingÕs uncertain, but itÕs helpful to say to myself, Today IÕm talking to Gayle, and then IÕm going to have a burrito. Like, weÕre all on the verge of smoking pot with our children at this point. All walls are about to come down just to get through all this. 
Gayle: I know. I have never smoked pot, and Oprah has, and she goes, ÒWeÕve got to have Gayle smoke pot.Ó I just never have. I got drunk in high school, at the Christian Youth Leadership Conference, off of cheap keg beer, and I was one of the leaders. It made me so frigginÕ sick that, to this day, I donÕt drink. I remember, at the University of Maryland, sitting in a circle, and they would pass the marijuana cigarette around, although Oprah has said just the fact that I call it a Òmarijuana cigaretteÓ is a problem. 
Judd: What were your parents like? What was it like where you grew up? 
Gayle: I grew up in Turkey from first grade to sixth grade. My dad was an electronic engineer for the government, and my mom was a stay-at-home mom. I had a very idyllic childhood. WeÕd go on vacation to Paris and Rome and Greece. I thought thatÕs how everybody lived; I thought everybody traveled all the time. I was the oldest of four girls, but I always wanted a brother. My dad had diplomatic status in Turkey, and some people say my dad was in the CIA. I never thought that, but I am sort of curious now. They said a Black man in the sixties in Ankara had to have been in the CIA. He did have a room that we were not allowed to go into. 
Judd: Oh boy. 
Gayle: But it was because he was a ham operator, and it was his hobby. So, in the middle of the night, itÕd go, Didi didi di. He died when I was a freshman in college, and I was executor of the estate. I was going through his things, and I found a card that said, ÒScott King,Ó which was his name, ÒAccess to the White House, East Gate,Ó or something. So, it lends itself to that theory, but IÕve had no proof of that whatsoever. I was always told he was an electronic engineer. 
Judd: And did you ever consider using your journalistic skills to find out if he was in the CIA? 
Gayle: IÕve asked a couple of people. And people always say, ÒIÕll get back to you,Ó and nobody does. I did ask my mom, and she said, ÒNo, thatÕs just not 
true.Ó Then somebody said, ÒWell, maybe your mom didnÕt know.Ó 
Judd: You know what I think? I think your mom knewÑshe wasnÕt supposed to, but he told her anyway. And then your mom was good at keeping the secret. 
Gayle: They said it wasnÕt uncommon sometimes for the spouse not to know. He did travel a lot. So, I donÕt know. 
Judd: What is it like having access, in your personal life, to tons of fascinating people? 
Gayle: ItÕs so interesting, because you have perceptions of people, and then you meet them, and you go, Wow, you really are just like a lot of people. More people know their names, but they have the same insecurities, the same issues, which is why itÕs so refreshing when you meet somebody who youÕve admired and they turn out to be really terrific people. Like Tom and Rita. You meet them, and they are exactly who they appear. And then, sometimes, you meet someone else, and you think, Gosh, theyÕre really insecure. IÕm not saying itÕs all smoke and mirrors, but I find that just because somebody is famous doesnÕt mean they have their shit together. 
Judd: No, and a lot of them are amazing precisely because they donÕt have their shit together. Who have you met that had a personal impact on you or that you always wanted to meet? 
Gayle: I was in awe of Nelson Mandela. Meeting him was definitely a highlight, because I couldnÕt understand how someone could go through what he went through and not be bitter. Even behind closed doors, he was not bitter. Now I have a question for you, Judd. Would you agree with me that a sense of humor is a sign of intelligence? Because I think you have to be intelligent in order to be funny. 
Judd: Yes, I think most comedians are trying to make sense of the universe. And comedy is their way of expressing their search. 
Gayle: I like Dave Chappelle very much. I groveled to him for two years to come on my show. 
Judd: Did you get him? 
Gayle: Yes. He did an interview, and it was so good. I wanted Dave Chappelle and Bruce Springsteen, because I love Bruce SpringsteenÕs music. I love country music. I just love music, period, all different kinds. And I would keep asking and asking, because Bruce doesnÕt need to do an interview, ever, about anything. He doesnÕt. And he finally agreed for his thing on Western Stars, which is very good. I wanted Dave, and I wanted Bruce. Now itÕd be nice to talk to the Queen. But sheÕs not doing interviews with anyone. 
Judd: So, thatÕs the person you have left on your list? The Queen? 
Gayle: I like the Queen. I like Harry and Meghan. I think we have a different kind of pope now; IÕm curious about him. And IÕm fascinated, too, by Kim Jong-un, like, What makes him tick, really? I love pop culture. I love politics. ThatÕs why this job is so perfect for me, because you really get to do anything and talk to anyone. What other job do you get to do that in? 

GEORGE SHAPIRO 
June 2018 
Judd: Let me just ask about Jerry for a minute, because youÕve had such a long, fruitful relationship with him. How did you guys meet? 
George: ItÕs a very simple story. At the original room at the Comedy Store, there was a young guy who worked for us at the office, named Jim Canchola Ñhe was the one who spotted Jerry first. He said, ÒYou have to see this guy Jerry Seinfeld.Ó So, I went to see him, and then I brought [talent agent] Howard West, my partner, and we both loved him. He was so smart. When we first saw him, he would stand behind the microphone and wouldnÕt move three inches either way. Now heÕs sixty-three years old, and heÕs flying across the stage. 
Judd: What year was that? 
George: That was July fifth, 1980. 
Judd: WhatÕs interesting is he wasnÕt a regular at the Comedy Store, right? He didnÕt spend much time there. 
George: When he started out, he was there, and then there was this conflict with [Comedy Store co-owner] Mitzi Shore. Jerry believed there should be no boundaries with comedy, but Mitzi wanted people to just work at the Comedy Store and not at other placesÑnot at the Ice House, and especially not at the Improv. ItÕs so funny, because Garry Shandling was on one of JerryÕs Comedians in Cars Getting Coffee episodes, and at the last minute, they decided to stop off at the Comedy Store, because they [had] both worked there at the same time. They walk around, and theyÕre looking at all the pictures of the comedians that are up there. ThereÕs a great picture of Garry Shandling, and then GarryÕs looking around and asks, ÒWhereÕs your picture, Jerry?Ó And they couldnÕt find it, because Mitzi probably took it down. 
Judd: I remember, when I started working at the Improv in the late eighties, Jerry hadnÕt done a lot of acting work. He had been on the TV show Benson, and he got fired. 
George: He was fired off of Benson, and they didnÕt tell him. He was like a messenger boy for them, and then he would write jokes for Benson. He did three episodes and went to the table read, and they sat around the table, and someone came over and said, ÒExcuse me, you know youÕre not in the show anymore?Ó 
Judd: Did he get upset? 
George: He was kind of stunned, but luckily for Jerry, and the great gift of stand-up comedy, he went right on the road. He went right to Tempe, Arizona, at the Improv. He killed. 
Judd: At the time, all the comedians really looked up to Jerry and knew he was as great as you could be, but there was still a feeling that This is all Jerry does. HeÕs not an actor, and there was zero expectation that he was going to be anything but one of the great stand-ups. He felt like the least likely guy to do what he went on to do. It didnÕt feel like he was chasing that; he was all about the stage. 
George: Jerry was just emerging, getting a little publicity. He got this little article in People magazine, and then he was playing for the first time in New York City, at the Town Hall, his first theater. He only played clubs before that. Brandon Tartikoff was the president of NBC at the time, and I used to send Brandon tapes of Jerry, just to keep him up to date. Even though he was on NBC a lot, Brandon didnÕt see all the Tonight Shows and Letterman Shows that Jerry did. So, I sent him this simple little letter that said, ÒDear Brandon, Jerry Seinfeld is appearing at the Town Hall in New York doing his first theater date, and I predict that he will have a television series on NBC very soon.Ó No one came to see him, but it triggered a meeting, and Brandon said, ÒIf thereÕs anything you can think of that youÕd like to do, like a talk show or a variety show, you tell me.Ó So, Jerry responded with ÒAll I ever wanted was a meeting like this.Ó Then, three days later, he was doing stand-up in New York, at Catch a Rising Star, and Larry David was on the bill with him. So, Larry told him, ÒYou know, NBCÕs interested in you.Ó They were walking around to the supermarkets, ending up in a coffee shop, and Larry was saying, ÒThis should be the show. Two comedians talking to each other.Ó Then they decided that one of them should not be a comedian, and the George Costanza character should be a civilian type. So, they pitched it, and I always say, if thereÕs a stand-up comedian involved in the project, let him do the pitch. Not the producer, not the writer, because people are going to laugh, and even if they donÕt love the format, theyÕre going to know they felt good and laughed, and youÕll have a much better chance of getting a deal. ThatÕs what happened when Jerry pitched it. 
Judd: WhatÕs interesting about that is one might say that Larry David was failing at that moment in his career. He had been a cast member and a writer on the TV show Fridays, and it was canceled after two years. And he wasnÕt even successful as a stand-up. He didnÕt go on the Tonight Show or Letterman or almost any show as a stand-up. He didnÕt do things that would have signaled that he had the credibility to run the TV show with Jerry. ItÕs a famous example of a show that didnÕt take off for years. It just kept surviving and took off in the third season. 
George: The ratings were very low. In fact, the pilot got very bad reactions from the NBC research team. We all have the report framed in our bathrooms, Jerry and Larry and me. It says in bold print: Pilot performance weak. Audience didnÕt care about three guys going to a Laundromat and doing laundry, and people resented JerryÕs stand-up routine being interrupted by the story line. JerryÕs stand-up is why the show survived. He did stand-up at the beginning, middle, and end. So, I got the call from Brandon Tartikoff saying that itÕs not on the schedule; the comedy department passed on it. Then we spoke to [NBC executive] Rick Ludwin and asked, ÒCan we do something with the other department?Ó And Rick said he has two one-hour specials that he didnÕt order yet. He gave us an offer for four half-hour specials. It didnÕt do that well in the ratings, and we waited like a year, and then they ordered thirteen more. So, in two years, we did only eighteen episodes. 
Judd: How did it take off? 
George: This is exactly what happened: They were struggling from 1989 to Õ93. It was getting killed by Home Improvement and Jake and the Fatman. I always had nightmares of their fat ass burying us. During the summer reruns of 1993, the network put Seinfeld behind Cheers, and by the end of that summer, Seinfeld was out-rating Cheers in the last season, and then they got the nine oÕclock time slot. Then it ended up number one with seventy-five million viewers at the last episode. And a record on top of it. 
Judd: It sounds like you need somebody like Rick Ludwin to champion you. 
George: He was a champion. HeÕs the hero of Seinfeld, without a doubt. It never would have been on. They supported it when the ratings were very, very low. When they got the time slot, Larry David said, ÒI donÕt want those people. I donÕt need those people to watch the show.Ó 
Judd: Larry was always a guy who was willing to walk away. When they would give notes, Larry was like, ÒIÕm done, I quit.Ó IsnÕt that essential to making great TV? 
George: After doing the first four episodes, NBC gave us this note saying they would like Jerry to marry Julia Louis-Dreyfus (Elaine Benes). After they said that, we had a meeting in the parking lotÑme, Jerry, Howard West, and Larry. And LarryÑwho, as you pointed out, wasnÕt successful as a stand-up comic. He wasnÕt successful as a writer at the time. He said, ÒIÕm finished. I wonÕt do it that way. ItÕs not the balance we want. ItÕs not the show we want.Ó Because, then, Kramer and George would be superfluous. TheyÕd be dropping in and out as neighbors instead of being an essential part of the show, and thatÕs the show. ItÕs about four friends in New York City. ThatÕs the artist Larry is. He said, ÒThatÕs all IÕm going to do, because I donÕt think I have more than four shows in me anyway.Ó ThatÕs a quote from Larry David, ÒI donÕt think I have more than four shows in me anyway.Ó 
Judd: How were they as showrunners, Larry and Jerry? Because itÕs so much work, and back then, they were still doing twenty-two episodes [a season]. 
George: TheyÕre dedicated because of their stand-up background. TheyÕre dedicated to every word count and every single script. They sat opposite each other at Radford Studios, and they rewrote every script, every word. They acted it out; then they rewrote it. The minute they wrapped rehearsal, Jerry would go right back to the office, sit with Larry, and write everything. They were just so dedicated to the show. They didnÕt think about being tired. 
Judd: YouÕve worked with some very bold and experimental people who also had giant successes. People like Seinfeld, Andy Kaufman, and the Smothers Brothers were only similar to one another in terms of how different they were from everyone else. 
George: They were true to who they were. The Smothers Brothers had incredible pressure politically, and they kept doing the show. In fact, CBS was really interfering and wanted to take the editing part of the show from themÑthatÕs after the third season. I got a telegram picking up the Smothers for the fourth year, and I got the contractÉand then Tommy stole the tapes. CBS said, ÒThey violated the contract, and weÕre canceling the show.Ó They canceled the show after three years. 
Judd: You were their agent before they had the TV showÑor did you join up with them later? 
George: The funny thing is I was working on The Steve Allen Show in, like, Õ61, which brought me from New York to L.A. ThatÕs when I first saw the 
Smothers Brothers. 
Judd: I remember when Steve Allen had Lenny Bruce on. Did you ever go see Bruce in those days? 
George: When I was a kid, at William Morris, he used to play at this place called the Den, in the Duane Hotel in Manhattan. I used to see Lenny Bruce, and Nichols and May. I was in the mailroom at the time, when Lenny Bruce just got started, and IÕll never forget it. He was so funny then, and that was before he got into the politics and the fighting for the rights and the Constitution. He was one of my great heroes. 
Judd: What did people think was happening with him at that time? Was he edgy? 
George: He was edgy, but he was just funny. He just had great, funny material, like George Carlin. I worked with George Carlin, and in fact, I got George CarlinÕs first speaking role as an actor, as Marlo ThomasÕs agent on That Girl. 
Judd: Would you see Woody Allen in the Village in those days? 
George: Woody I knew from way back, from his work with Sid Caesar. I used to book him when he was doing stand-up. 
Judd: What was the booking job? 
George: I was a variety television agent, so I was always with the comedians when they did personal appearances. Woody Allen did the Greek Theatre. ThereÕs no comedian I have ever met in my life more nervous than Woody Allen. You meet in person, and before he goes on, heÕs bouncing off the walls and the ceiling. He was so nervous, but that energy went right on the stage. And he was hilarious. He was a very, very funny stand-up. 
Judd: You were in the mailroom, and then you were booking mainly comics and variety acts? 
George: YouÕre like a floating secretary when youÕre in the mailroom, so, when an agentÕs secretary or assistant is out, you fill in. You could work, and also it gives you a good overview of what you want to do, because I worked in the personal appearance department, a theater department, television, motion pictures, literaryÑonce, I floated into [talent manager] Colonel [Tom] ParkerÕs office. He would say, ÒGeorge, itÕs eleven-thirty. How much money did we make so far?Ó He had a fifty-fifty deal with Elvis, as his manager. 
Judd: Did you meet Elvis in those days? 
George: I met Elvis. Elvis was the first person in my life to call me Òsir.Ó I was twenty-four years old; he was like twenty-two years old, and it was at The Ed Sullivan Show. I was with him because I was with William Morris, helping out, and I said, ÒElvis, the press is ready for you now.Ó He goes, ÒYes, sir. IÕll be right there, sir.Ó So, I said, ÒYouÕre the first person to ever call me Ôsir.Õ Ó IÕll never forget that. In the Bronx, we donÕt use ÒsirÓ or ÒmaÕam,Ó like you do in Mississippi. 
Judd: Was that the first time he did Ed Sullivan? 
George: Yeah, and thatÕs my connection with him. ThatÕs a chapter of the book IÕm not writing: ÒElvis Presley Called Me ÔSir.Õ Ó 
Judd: Did you work with Buddy Hackett? 
George: When I first started in the mailroom, I had this one assignment on a Sunday, for a variety show called The Patrice Munsel Show, and I booked Buddy Hackett on the show. He goes into his dressing room, and IÕm with him, and he says, ÒThereÕs no telephone in my dressing room. IÕm not going to do the show unless I have a telephone.Ó So, I rush to AT&T, IÕm calling to get someone down, saying itÕs an emergency. Meanwhile, Buddy says heÕs going to go for a walk, and he says to me, ÒIf that phone isnÕt thereÉÓ And I figure thatÕs the end of my career. IÕm a very, very young junior agent. And then he comes in, the phoneÕs been set up, and he gets a dial tone, and he rips the phone out of the wall. So, I didnÕt like him then, and I had another confrontation with him, and I was going to hit him in the head with a cue stick. We were shooting pool. I hated him so much. And then I booked an act that one of my clients did, at the Sahara, and he coheadlined with Buddy Hackett. Buddy was so funny that night. I could not help but laugh my ass off. I fell off the chair laughing even though I hated him. 
Judd: I remember, there was a stand-up comedy competition at the Improv, and he was a judge. At the end, he went onstage and was supposed to read the ballots of the different judges and say who the winner was. Instead, he tore up all the ballots and said, ÒI donÕt think any of you guys are funny.Ó 
George: ThatÕs a perfect reflection of him. 
Judd: Tell me about the Smothers Brothers. How did that show get set up, and what was the evolution of it from being a variety show to being a very political show? 
George: They were just an innocent act: ÒMom always liked you bestÓ kind of comedy. They sang great, and they were very good musicians, and they were doing a lot of guest appearances. They were clients of William Morris. Phil Weltman, who was the head of our television department, was their agent. They were in a terrible time slot, but the public sort of gravitated to them and the ratings were pretty good. They were picked up, and they ended up a top-five show. They had great guest stars on, like Johnny Cash and Glen Campbell. Then they started doing political stuff, especially around the Vietnam War, which they were very opposed to. But they were always funny, and thatÕs why the public gravitated toward them. 
Judd: How did the network signal to them that they werenÕt happy with how much politics they were doing? 
George: They had meetings with them, but they did what they had to do. They just continued doing it, but also, in the second and third year, the show was a top-ten show. Then, toward the end of the third year, it fell off, to number forty. I have a feeling if it was still in the top five, the network wouldnÕt have canceled it. They were such a great staff. They had Steve Martin and Rob Reiner and Bob Einstein as young writers on the show. It was a tremendous, classic comedy show. 
Judd: Was there a real sense of urgency around the war where the Smothers Brothers said This is worth losing the show over? 
George: They never said that. They just did their job. ItÕs what they had to do, like when Larry David said, ÒIÕm not going to do Seinfeld.Ó The Smothers Brothers at least had an act that they traveled all over the world with, on tour. 
Judd: The fact that you had the instinct to be supportive of their creative vision above everything else goes against what you assume about most agents. Is that why you became a manager? 
George: I wanted to work with fewer people. Bernie Brillstein was my hero, because he blazed the trail for anyone who wanted to work as a manager. I worked with him at William Morris; he was a little ahead of me. I used to deliver mail for him, and he had an office with all these pictures in it. He had pictures of Kirk Douglas and Frank Sinatra and Sammy Davis, Jr., and Anne Bancroft and Sophia LorenÑand so I came back to the mailroom with my first delivery on my first day and said, ÒBernie Brillstein has to be the biggest agent in the world. He has all these people.Ó And they said, ÒNo. He left the mailroom three months ago. HeÕs in publicity. He sends the pictures out.Ó ThatÕs how nave I was. Anyway, Bernie was my hero, because he left, and he signed Jim Henson right away, and Lorne Michaels and [John] Belushi and [Dan] Aykroyd and Gilda Radner, and he started producing movies. In fact, he gave me my first opportunity to produce a movie, about a fat guy on a beach who feels out of place. It was called Summer Rental, with John Candy, and it was based on BernieÕs life, as he felt out of place as a fat guy on the beach. Like him, I wanted to get involved in producing and managing. When youÕre an agent, youÕre handling two hundred fifty to three hundred people, so thereÕs a good percentage of assholes within that group, and that was also part of the reason why I left. 
Judd: Did you find a common trait with the people who were a pain in the ass that explained why they were a pain in the ass? 
George: It was part of their personality, I think. 
Judd: Okay, what about someone you like? What do you love about Jerry Seinfeld as a person? 
George: HeÕs just a joy to be with. He knows how to have fun. I have this part of my brain that, even though IÕm quite old, has sort of stuck from when I was eight, nine, ten years old, and he has that, too. Going to a ball game with him is just an absolute joy. We talk about everything besides the hot dogs, and he always has two hot dogs and ice cream. 
Judd: Where did you first connect with Mel Brooks and Carl Reiner? 
George: I first connected with Carl when I was twelve years old. Carl was a corporal in the U.S. Army, about twenty-one years old, and my aunt Estelle was dating him. Then they got engaged, and she brought Carl over to meet the family, and I was all excited, because I was a kid, and I didnÕt have any relatives in the army. It was World War Two, and you were a hero if you had a relative in the army. So, I was very excited my aunt was going to marry a soldier. And I was so excited about seeing him, I just leaped on his back. And CarlÑyou know how sweet he is, right? Instead of throwing me down, or throwing me on the couch, he gave me a piggyback ride. He jumped up and down like a little horsey. And thatÕs how I first met him. 
Judd: Were you tracking the rise of Carl as a kid? 
George: Yes. After the army, he did this great Broadway show called Call Me MisterÑI think Buddy Hackett was in itÑand he was funny, and I said, Wow, is he talented. 
Judd: Is that when you decided you wanted to find a way to work in show business? 
George: Well, not really. Did you ever hear of a place called Tamiment, in the Pocono Mountains? It was like a resort, and I was there as a lifeguard, with my friend Howard and a couple of other Bronx boys. They had a playhouse that did weekly musical revues. Neil Simon and Danny Simon were the head writers. A very young, unknown Herb Ross was the choreographer; Barbara Cook was an ingnue and became a major singer there. They had all these brilliant young performers, and they put them in revues every week. ThatÕs when I really got connected to show business. You should see Herb Ross creating these dance numbers, and then these agents came up for the weekend, and theyÕd have dinner. IÕd give them a rowboat and a canoe. TheyÕd hang out with these beautiful singers and dancers. I said, ÒThis is your job?Ó So, when I got out of the army, thatÕs why I looked into show business, and I brought Howard [West] in after I got a job with William Morris. 
Judd: How did you know Howard? 
George: IÕve known Howard since we were eight years old. I met him in P.S. 80, in the Bronx. He was the new kid who came to the neighborhood, and I saw him sitting alone on the stoop, and I said, ÒCome on and play basketball with us.Ó Later on, I got him the job at William Morris, and we both worked there. I left first, and then I asked him to join me as a manager and producer. 
Judd: Who were the first clients you had as managers? 
George: We had [producer/actor] Dick Clair and [writer/producer/actress] Jenna McMahon, who worked for Carol Burnett. They created MamaÕs Family, Facts of Life, and a few other shows. Andy Kaufman was an early one. We had Carl [Reiner], and it was great having him as a client. We had a young comedian called Lonnie Shorr, from North Carolina. He worked a lot with Glen Campbell and did The Johnny Cash Show. And [director/screenwriter/producer] Bill Persky and [screenwriter/producer] Sam DenoffÑI got them a job on The Dick Van Dyke Show and then they created That Girl, and they did a lot of other great specials with Dick Van Dyke and Mary Tyler Moore. 
Judd: Who were your favorite stand-up clients who didnÕt become megastars? 
George: I loved Elayne Boosler. We produced a special with Elayne, and she was just so smart. I also loveÑand heÕs still working around nowÑSuli McCullough. 
Judd: Did you first see Andy at the Improv? 
George: My connection with Andy Kaufman initially was via Carl Reiner. He was in New York, working at Catch a Rising Star and the Improv, and he called me. Carl said, ÒGeorge, you have to get on a plane and fly to New York and see Andy Kaufman. I never saw anyone so original.Ó And Carl has total audio recall. He did his whole act [for me]. He did everything but sing like Elvis. But he did the whole thing, imitating President Carter, ÒHello, IÕm President Carter. President of the United States, thank you very much.Ó It was so funny. Coincidentally, Budd Friedman called me later that same day. He said heÕs flying in Andy Kaufman: ÒYou should take a look at him.Ó I said, ÒI want him already, just from what Carl told me.Ó And then I saw him at the Improv here. If youÕre a manager, Judd, and you see Andy Kaufman onstage, what would you think? Would you have any hesitation? 
Judd: Well, you think, What do I do with this? Where does this go? 
George: Exactly. So, my question was, Is he sane? Because he was so crazy onstage. I said, I have to have lunch with him, and then he was a real person who loved his grandma. He used to fly to New York and stop off in Florida to see Grandma LilyÑso, I liked him. But I loved his work. Totally loved his work. 
Judd: Did he think of it more as performance art? 
George: Oh, absolutely. He said, ÒIÕm not a comedian, IÕm a performer.Ó You hit it right on the head. 
Judd: Because heÕs trying to create moments. HeÕs not really thinking about laugh count. 
George: I tell this incredible story. Andy Kaufman had a bombing routine where he would purposely bomb. What comedian is going to do that? People are going to walk out of the comedy club. So, I had to negotiate with him. I said, ÒAndy, maybe fifty-five seconds, a minute, five at the most.Ó We had a negotiation for how short the bombing routine has to be. It came down to one minute, thirty-eight seconds. 
Judd: Did he ever talk about long-term career goals? 
George: No, he just was satisfying his heart as a performer. He was very good musically. Conga drums; he sang really well. He was a terrific performer. ThatÕs why, when Jim CarreyÑwho was already a star, because he did Ace VenturaÑwanted to play Andy so much in Man on the Moon, he did an audition tape for me. 
Judd: IÕm the person who videotaped it. 
George: Oh my God. YouÕre responsible for [his] getting the movie. You should get nominated for a Golden Globe Award. 
Judd: IÕm more responsible for just hitting Record on his video camera, but I was there with him. 
George: ThatÕs amazing. Andy Kaufman died in 1984. The Long Beach Museum of Art had this great tribute to Andy, after he died, and Jim came running over to me after the presentation, and he said, ÒI love Andy Kaufman.Ó And this was in Õ84, and like nine years before we talked about doing a movie about his life. I knew what a great impressionist Jim was, and a good actor. I said, ÒJim, my wish would be for you to play Andy Kaufman if we do a biopic on Andy Kaufman.Ó And it happened. I just loved that. 
Judd: And now thereÕs a documentary of Jim and Andy, which is about how Jim stayed in character the entire time. ItÕs a beautiful documentary. 
George: I saw that. I just canÕt tell you how emotional I was about it. I was laughing and crying, especially over how revealing it was about Jim. He wanted to lose Jim Carrey, is what he said. He said he lost himself completely in Andy Kaufman. Do you know the cast and crew never met Jim Carrey in that movie? He was Andy Kaufman when he was off-camera, the whole time. It was absolutely amazing. 
Judd: How was that for you to experience that? 
George: It was one of the best four months of my life, because I was with Andy again. Andy was back. He just nailed it. He got everything. 
Judd: And his family would spend time with Jim as Andy. 
George: Yeah. They all came in. We recreated Carnegie Hall in the Los Angeles Theatre, and Stanley Kaufman, his father, was walking down while Jim was onstage doing the conga drums and just singing. And the tears just streamed down his face. He saw Andy; he saw his son onstage. ThatÕs why the whole thing was a huge, emotional roller-coaster ride. 
Judd: People really felt very close to Andy as a person. But I think itÕs hard for people on the outside to understand what he was like when not performing. What was it about Andy? 
George: He was a regular guy who was very excited about his art. He turned down Taxi originally. I had to talk him into it, because he wanted to just create material. If youÕre doing a sitcom, it takes away from writing original material and doing it. I said, ÒThis could enhance the whole level of your career.Ó YouÕre talking about doing great theater dates, Carnegie Hall. Getting a salary from them could allow you to do what you want to do production-wise. So, he thinks about it, and he says, ÒIÕll do fourteen episodes, but they have to book Tony CliftonÓÑone of AndyÕs characters ÑÒfor four. Then IÕll do it, otherwise they can forget it.Ó So, I said okay and made this deal. I had to negotiate that Andy got much more money than Tony, because Tony wasnÕt well known. I also said, ÒThey have to have separate dressing rooms, business affairs, and separate parking places, and Andy has to meditate twenty minutes in the morning and twenty minutes in the afternoon.Ó But we worked out the deal. 
Judd: Can you tell me a little bit of what happened once Taxi comes out? 
George: ItÕs a big hit. High ratings. Andy is a smash, and itÕs time for Tony Clifton to do it, and so, they write in this really good character, Danny DeVitoÕs kid brother, who is a neÕer-do-well gambler and drinker. So, Tony Clifton comes down, and he rents a Winnebago bigger than Judd HirschÕs. A big trailer. He has two hookers with him, a blonde and a brunette. Then, when it comes down to the reading, he says, ÒI brought my friends with me. Is that okay?Ó So, he has one girl on each knee, and heÕs reading the script. He says, ÒOkay, okay, bullshit, bullshit, bullshit, my line, bullshit, bullshit, bullshit, bullshit, my line.Ó He reads the script, and he goes back to his trailer with the hookers. 
Judd: Were the other actors upset that heÕs doing this? 
George: TheyÕre upset. TheyÕre impatient with him. He goes back to his trailer, drinks Jack DanielÕs. Andy doesnÕt drink at all, ever. [Writer] Ed Weinberger calls me on, like, Thursday. He says, ÒItÕs not working out with Tony. First of all, heÕs not a good actor. He comes late. HeÕs not reliable. We just want to fire him.Ó I said, ÒWell, a part of his contract is that Andy has an out clause if you fire Tony.Ó And already the show took off like a hit with Latka as an important character. They said, ÒPlease, talk to him.Ó I said, ÒIÕll talk to him, but itÕs his call.Ó So, I talked to AndyÑand [as Latka, he] has [to wear] all this makeupÑand he says, ÒLet them fire me.Ó I think it was a little burdensome for him with all the makeup, to do it. 
Judd: Was he enjoying that the show was a hit at all? 
George: He loved that the show was a hit and that he got great reviews as Latka. But it still wasnÕt in his heart. He was much more interested in doing his own creativity. So, he said, ÒIÕll let them fire me only if they do it on run-through day, with the network and all the people from Paramount.Ó It was a hot show. All the people came down, the executives and the assistants. The place was full with ABC executives, and then they fire him. First, he comes down, and he gives gifts to everybody, and then they fire him, and he says, ÒWhat are you talking about? You canÕt fire me. WhereÕs Mr. Shapiro? George Shapiro? I have a contract.Ó So, he was resisting them. I brought a tape recorder, I recorded this, and that was in the movie [Jim and Andy]. I gave it to [producer/director/writer] Scott Alexander and [screenwriter] Larry Karaszewski. So, anyway, Judd Hirsch is getting pissed off; everyoneÕs pissed off. And then we get Security. ÒIÕm not going to let any of you guys see me in Las Vegas,Ó [Andy] tells the whole cast. And SecurityÕs dragging him off. A photographer for the Los Angeles Times was taking pictures when they were throwing him off. I told Andy IÕd meet him at a restaurant right around the corner from Paramount. He comes outÑhe had the makeup offÑ and he said, ÒGeorge, this is the greatest day of my life. This is the theater of the street. This is what I love more than anything.Ó And thatÕs him. 
Judd: He also loved wrestling. 
George: He did. He set up all that stuff with Jerry Lawler. I wasnÕt involved with that, except I was in Memphis when he did the Mid-South Coliseum, and he got the piledriver [move performed on him], and he was in the hospital for three days. ThereÕs probably some news footage of me with Andy, comforting him. In the hospital, I was holding AndyÕs hand, but I knew he was faking it. He had an injury when he was a teenager, which resulted in a little bit of tightness of the vertebrae in his neck, so it looked like that could have happened [in the ring that evening]. So, he stayed in the hospital for three days when he didnÕt have to. He wasnÕt hurt. And then he got out, and he did that Letterman show, and Jerry Lawler slapped him out of his seat while Andy had his neck brace on. He and LawlerÑthey staged that also. Smacking him off the screenÑeverything was staged. And then, when we were shooting Man on the Moon, Jim Carrey didnÕt get along with Jerry Lawler. Jim Carrey as himself didnÕt get along, and they pushed each other, and Lawler threw him down, and he was hurt. And he went to L.A. Hospital. Twelve years later, IÕm holding Jim CarreyÕs hand in the hospital. 
Judd: The movie [Man on the Moon] goes to a very emotional place when Andy gets sick. That must have been a horrible time. 
George: I was with Andy when they gave him the diagnosis. It was in December 1983. 
Judd: You were at the doctorÕs office with him? 
George: I was. When they came out, I was sitting with Andy. And they said, ÒYou have lung cancer. You have large-cell carcinoma,Ó which is kind of a rapidly growing cancer. So, the first thing Andy said was ÒListen, George, why donÕt you call David Letterman and the show. IÕll do the show like late December, or January, and David could ask me, ÔWhat did you get for Christmas, Andy?Õ and I can say, ÔI got cancer.Õ Ó That was his first thought after he was given that diagnosis. 
Judd: Where did he go from there? 
George: Then he was sick, and I got him a job hosting this Cyndi Lauper special that he presented. That was the last thing he did. And like in the movie, he went to India for this [psychic surgery] guy. He always had the feeling that he was going to be okay. He was thinking optimistically. Then he went out very quickly at Cedars, and I was there, and Robin Williams came over. Robin visited him a couple of times. Robin was very lovingÑthey were very loving toward each other. They had a really great relationship. It was very sad at the end. He was thirty-five years old. 
Judd: That was Õ84, and I was sixteen. When he passed, and Belushi passed, it was hard for a young comedy fan. I finally just want to ask, since youÕve worked with so many of the greats with these long careers, what is the secret to all these guys staying funny? 
George: Well, look at Mel Brooks. Two and a half months in Newcastle breaking in Young Frankenstein [the musical], and it got rave reviews. HeÕs ninety-one and a half. And look at Norman Lear. HeÕs ninety-five and a half, and heÕs working on [the] One Day at a Time [reboot]. If itÕs in your DNA and youÕre funny, you donÕt stop. 

HANNAH GADSBY 
September 2020 
Judd: Have you been enjoying your fame? Or the opposite? 
Hannah: I think that fame and celebrity add trauma. They have traumatic episodes. And I think itÕs difficult to process, because the trauma doesnÕt read as trauma, so you donÕt protect yourself from it, like when youÕre in a car accident, and afterward youÕre like, Fuck, I need to wear a neck brace for a bit. You can say after that accident, ÒIÕm not comfortable in cars,Ó and the world around you goes, ÒYeah, I can see that.Ó Whereas, it seems like nothing is bad with the type of success that leads to celebrity. But I think thatÕs incorrect. In the beginning stages of success, you have to navigate quite a large amount of trauma, because everything around your life changes. It doesnÕt change for the worse, but it changes in such a huge and impactful way that nothing about your life is the same anymore. And that is tantamount to trauma. You look at all these badly behaved boys, and women also; itÕs looked at as This is what being creative is. 
Judd: WhatÕs funny about you mentioning this is IÕve been seeing a new therapist for the last year or two. He sees a lot of creative people. And he sees the common issues that these people have, which is what youÕre talking about: No one has any compassion for you when youÕre successful. No one has any compassion for the amount of hatred you have to deal with, in addition to the love, and people donÕt have any compassion for the amount of stress creative people are under to do their work. 
Hannah: I donÕt think everyone on earth should feel compassion for you because youÕre in a position of privilege, but I think knowing that what youÕre going through requires some actual navigation and requires actual maturityÑthatÕs important. I experienced a little bit of success, and I would have changed my life quite dramatically, very quickly, but I was already old and tired. The kind of hate that I got, the ratio was the sameÑI just got more of it. I was doing festivals, and with every good review came a pummeling, and with every bad review came a pile-on. When real success hits, then more people are interested in us, but the ratio of hate stays the same. 
Judd: I was interested in your bit about this in your Netflix special Douglas, where you talk about how you like to snack on the hatred because it helps strengthen your immunity to it. We all have to decide what weÕre going to do in a world where we have so much access to everyone elseÕs opinion of our work. I remember back when we made The Cable Guy, on the way to the premiere, someone handed me two faxes, and they were the reviews from Newsweek and Time magazine. They both were the worst reviews you could imagine, and the night was destroyed. Back then, there were maybe ten or fifteen reviewers in the country whom you looked to in order to determine if critics liked what you did. You had no sense of the public at all. You would not get any feedback other than box-office sales, and maybe a letter here and there. But today, millions of people have a way to get to you through social media, and the internet forces us all to decide, Do we want to read anything? It sounds like you do read some of them. 
Hannah: I keep my finger on the pulse. I think thatÕs important when youÕre creating something for public consumption. Otherwise, IÕm apt to just fall into a void, and then what I create would have less relevance to anybody else on earth. I think of it as a conversation, where thereÕs a push and pull. You canÕt please everyone. My new line on that is, You canÕt be everyoneÕs cup of tea. ThatÕs just a glass of water. 
Judd: But some people arenÕt content with simply not drinking the tea; they also want to knock the cup out of your hands. 
Hannah: Their anger is fascinating. And I think most women who have a public-facing persona understand hatred. People just hate you; itÕs kind of incredible. ItÕs not always so clear-cut or obvious, but there is an element that, as a woman or anyone whoÕs different, reallyÑit just makes people angry. 
Judd: What do you think that is? IÕve noticed it when working with Lena Dunham, and IÕve also seen it with Pete Davidson. People seem deeply offended by him telling them how he feels. I wonder what that is? 
Hannah: I think your two examples, Lena and PeteÑthey have a distinct lack of boundaries. ItÕs very clear in their work and the way they think. People donÕt like it when other people donÕt have boundaries. But when you have a huge amount of influence, it becomes a two-way street. When people are online, they also respond to that with a distinct lack of boundaries. I have incredibly strong boundaries, so when I talk about these things, I do understand that I have Òmind blindness,Ó as they say. But that cuts both ways; so, I feel fine about it. There was a time when I would just be trying to understand what was wrong with me. 
Judd: What did you learn about yourself by going from a normal working comedy person to having an enormous amount of attention and success and criticism? 
Hannah: ThatÕs where I first understood just how profoundly incognizant I am to praise, but I actually think thatÕs a good thing. I often think about the amount of adulation that was surrounding me at the timeÑimagine if that was something I was actively seeking? I understand, in theory, how addictive or intoxicating that would feel, but for me, it just felt unsafe, and that is because I have autism and I have trauma, so I was just scrambling for the nearest safe space, which for me was creating a new work. Creating for me is a way of providing safety, and I chose to write a new stand-up special even though, honestly, I could have done anything. 
Judd: How was writing that special different from your earlier experience doing stand-up, when you werenÕt as well-known? 
Hannah: People were just like, ÒPlease, let me help you make the next thing you want to make.Ó For me, that was too unsafe, because I didnÕt understand how that industry works. IÕve been doing stand-up for a long time, so I understood how to construct a show and tour a show. So, even though things changedÑlike the amount of people that came to see my shows and the expectations around itÑthe core of it was the same. There was a little level of safety to that, but thatÕs a relational thing. And thatÕs autism. I donÕt have autism when IÕm on my own. The cracks appear as soon as youÕre trying to relate to the world. That is really whatÕs difficult about this interconnected world that we live in: ItÕs a really difficult space for people on the spectrum to navigate because everything is connected. When someone whoÕs neurotypical feels overwhelmed by the fact that everyone has access to them, then that is especially toxic for someone on the spectrum. Because the ability to retreat, which is something thatÕs incredibly important, is diminished. 
Judd: When I was young, and I went off after school, my parents didnÕt even know where I was. I talked to two people most of the time; sometimes weÕd visit two other people. We didnÕt have any awareness of who was having fun or who wasnÕt having fun, and we didnÕt feel judged by anybody. I think that the whole world doesnÕt know what to make of our interconnectedness. It feels like young people are really depressed and anxious from it. 
Hannah: I donÕt think weÕre biologically equipped to be in the world weÕve created. 
Judd: Did it change how you thought about why you were interested in comedy in the first place? 
Hannah: No, that was always fairly obvious to me. IÕd be stumbling and bumbling my way around the world and failing, but then I could reimagine my life and create something out of it. Hindsight was always my gift. But IÕd fail in the moment. I wasnÕt an active participant in life. I watched people. I was always watching what was going on around me, and so, I would see things that other people wouldnÕt. Before I ever did stand-up, I would do this kind of observation at a gathering. If you were to look at me from the outside, you would say sheÕs just a lump in the corner, but you donÕt understand whatÕs happening in my mind. ThatÕs whatÕs wrong with a lot of depictions of people on the spectrum, in literature and film and television: Those characters and those stories are being written largely by people who are witnessing autism and not experiencing it. 
Judd: I produced a special for Gary Gulman where he talked about his depression. He had just stopped doing stand-up for a couple years and moved back in with his mother and really had a hard time coming out of it. But we would get tens of thousands of messages from people who felt so much better hearing a story of hope and of how he climbed out of it. 
Hannah: I once did a show where I talked about coming off of antidepressants. This is before I was diagnosed correctly. ItÕs interesting how many people come out and say that they identify with that, and I feel like thatÕs why itÕs incredibly important to talk openly about it. But there is a flip side to that. People have very, very strong opinions about medication, and the amount of people who have told me that autism is dietary is wild. Or the people who told me IÕm too fat to be autistic. 
Judd: Were you on antidepressants before people knew you had autism? Was it a misdiagnosis? 
Hannah: ItÕs a comorbid situation, but what I have is periods of being overwhelmed, weÕll call it. A lot of times with autism, you will have a meltdown when you have trouble regulating situations. But meltdowns were not tolerated in someone like me. I was the youngest of five in my family, and a girlÑyou donÕt get to act up. So, I learned a coping mechanism where I just shut down as a way of stopping the input. 
Judd: Did you feel misunderstood by your family during your childhood? 
Hannah: Well, they also understand me better than most. I make sense to them; IÕve never been any different. In fact, I havenÕt changed much since I was five. I talk more, thatÕs it. I think itÕs more what girls are allowed to get away with, behavior-wise. IÕd withdraw, and I like habit and routine and quiet, but you learn pretty quickly that these arenÕt okay. It happens to boys and girls, but I think because girls are pressured to be more social, it happens for girls a little bit more. ThatÕs why girls are often diagnosed later. ItÕs called masking. 
Judd: Did you often feel like you had to ignore your own need to withdraw? 
Hannah: Yes. I would look at the behavior I was supposed to do, and even though it feels painful and unsafe, IÕd do it anyway, because itÕs what is expected. I was okay at it, because I grew up in a small town where there wasnÕt much change, and I understood my place in my family structure. But as soon as there was a small amount of change, IÕd flip out. 
Judd: It seems odd to then be drawn to the world of comedy, which is all about change. ThereÕs so much traveling and working with different people in different spaces. 
Hannah: I donÕt know how I got into comedy, to be really honest. Everything around comedy is incredibly painful for me. But being onstage makes perfect sense to me. 
Judd: When did you have an awareness that comedy was something that someone could do for a living? 
Hannah: It was quite late in life. I was in my late twenties, and my life was a wreck. I just couldnÕt get my shit together. I entered a national competition run by the Melbourne International Comedy Festival. I remember going, This just seems very much like a thing I can do. Now, to be fair, IÕd said that about a lot of things and I failed miserably, so there was no expectation that I would be good at this. ItÕs just like, This is a thing I could do. 
Judd: Did you study it as a kid the way you studied other things? Were you interested in stand-up comedians at all? 
Hannah: I was not obsessed in the comedians themselves, but IÕm very obsessed with mimicry. And a little-known fact about me is that I memorized Bill CosbyÑobviously, long before his abusive behavior was brought to light. 
Judd: We all did; thatÕs the hard part. 
Hannah: I had no real concept of Bill Cosby as a person. We had The Cosby Show, but we werenÕt allowed to watch it. It was on too late for me, I think. We just had a cassette tape of his comedy, and I would memorize it. But I didnÕt think about that in terms of like, This is something I could do, because it just wasnÕt in my wheelhouse. We didnÕt have any live theater where I was growing upÑwe didnÕt even have a cinema. Show business was not a thing. 
Judd: You were planting trees. Is that what you were doing around that time? 
Hannah: I say that I was a farm laborer, but in reality, I was an itinerant farmworker. I was basically working with ex-cons and international tourists and students who got trapped on a visa, and it basically meant indentured labor, and thatÕs pretty much it. That was where I was working, and no one could quite work out where I belonged, because I was not on parole, but was also from around there. 
Judd: Did you have a sense that life wasnÕt really working out? Were you depressed? 
Hannah: The thing about that industry is, you donÕt earn enough to live. YouÕre trapped as soon as you enter into it. ItÕs a really horrific cycle. IÕd be surprised to find out if the food on your plate is ethically produced. When I worked there, I was stuck planting trees. You get paid like four cents a seedling, and itÕs baking hot. You got a tray of seedlings, which would basically add up to around three dollars, and when you finish planting that tray, you call out. Then someone comes over and fills you up again. But sometimes you could be left waiting, because the guys who were supposed to fill you up donÕt like you. And I have autism. Often, I found that it took a while for people to like me. Eventually, IÕd get there. But you know, IÕm a weird presence. So, I would earn ten dollars a day. 
Judd: Did that motivate you to really work hard on those jokes? 
Hannah: I wasnÕt doing comedy at that stage. I was essentially homeless living in Australia in a tent, with no options. 
Judd: How did you finally escape? 
Hannah: I had an accident on the job. I got hurt pretty badly and had to get this really significant surgery. Then I went to Adelaide, where I lived with my sister for a few years, and it was during that time that I started doing comedy. For an older woman to start doing comedy like that, and start living the lifestyle of a comicÑbut it was still better than planting trees. I think the fact that my life was so dismal meant that I could navigate the dismal world that is being a working comic. 
Judd: And it put gas in your tank to just hang in there. 
Hannah: I couldnÕt have done it without my family. I lived relatively rent-free for the first six years. I lived above my brotherÕs fruit-and-vegetable shop, and then my sister let me live with her for a few years, until I got back onto my feet. 
Judd: Was it strange to be embraced in such a big way after a long struggle and after everything that youÕve been through? 
Hannah: This is the absence of confidence. If the world shat on you that much, you donÕt have confidence, but that also means you donÕt have anything to lose. When I stood onstage for the first time, I wasnÕt thinking, Gee, I hope these people like me. IÕm standing up there, and I assume they hate me. And thatÕs fine. ItÕs kind of easier. 
Judd: What happened when you realized that a fair amount of them really liked you? 
Hannah: Every time I was onstage, I was able to control the room. I was very good at that. I think I sat in a weird place for people, where I was unnerving enough but also not threatening. ThatÕs a perfect place to be in comedy: enough tension, but not enough to generate hostility. 
Judd: In Nanette, you talked about how, on some level, that didnÕt feel like a truthful place to stand. 
Hannah: I think thatÕs the only place to stand when youÕre first starting out. What became really difficult for me onstage was navigating what people expected of me and what I wanted to sayÑwhat I needed to say. ItÕs exciting when youÕre starting out, where youÕre working and creating. Then I think there comes a time when it plateaus and people expect a certain thing from you, but thatÕs a flattening out of who you are and how you could possibly grow. I donÕt think Nanette could have been a show I could have produced had I experienced success before Nanette. 
Judd: I do think that your success with Nanette, though, proves that your theory was wrong. Because the audience ultimately did want you to go deeper. 
Hannah: That was the one thing I agree with my criticsÑlike, no, it shouldnÕt have worked. It wasnÕt meant to work. It was meant to be a bomb. But thatÕs not what happened. So, we have to go with that evidence. 
Judd: Do you think that its success says anything about the role of comedy in the world we live in now? 
Hannah: That has everything to do with the kind of world weÕre living in now, where everyone is hyperconnected. People donÕt need comedians to show them how to be funny. Anytime you want to laugh, you can laugh. You can just watch cats doing dumb things on YouTube. Laughter is accessible at any point. So, the job of the comedian has to evolve. 
Judd: And you have to find your audience. I think thereÕs something about our interconnectedness today that makes a lot of people feel like theyÕre supposed to cross over to everybody. But it really is about finding the people who enjoy the way you express yourself. 
Hannah: Part of that has to do with how weird people are when they donÕt like something. Why do people get angry when they donÕt like something? Ultimately, they need to do some work on themselves. If a comedian makes me angry, I look at what it was about their joke that made me angry, and itÕs like, Oh itÕs because theyÕre amplifying toxic behavior and ideas. But I can pinpoint why that makes me angry. But the people who hated me, who I made angryÑthey were angry at a small number of things, and none of them makes sense. TheyÕre angry that IÕm a woman. TheyÕre angry that IÕm fat. TheyÕre angry that IÕm Australian. TheyÕre angry that I donÕt like being raped. 
Judd: Do you feel like weÕre experiencing a positive time of change? Are we just realizing how bad a pretty high percentage of people are, or are we just hearing the screams of the world as it slowly changes for the better? 
Hannah: It could be both. We have a fairly blas attitude toward history, where we look back and go, These things change. WhatÕs never actually spoken about is the devastation that is wrought with change. Colonialism is a good case in point. Oh, we ÒdiscoveredÓ the world. But actually people were decimated, cultures were decimated. I think that history repeats itself because we donÕt learn properly from history, and that has everything to do with the fact that we only listen to the history thatÕs written by the winners. TheyÕre hardly going to be self-reflective, like, ÒWell we could have done that better. We could have killed less people.Ó Right now thereÕs a lot of really intense energy floating, and that can only lead either to creativity or to destruction. History shows that it leads to both. WeÕre living in a time where itÕs going to be an incredibly creatively intense moment, but also probably a very destructive moment. I honestly think that the only antidote to this moment is to create. 
Judd: All we have is our choice of what to put into the world. We can make a choice to make the world a better place or take advantage of it and be toxic in 
some way and make it worse.
