Sometimes I make it less funny and less clever. But whatÕs most important to me is that it doesnÕt feel like a bunch of writers handed me a bunch of index cards that afternoon. 
Judd: [Do you] feel that the world has gotten way more intense and serious, as a comedian and interviewer? As a viewer, sometimes you feel for hosts, having to address the darkness of the world. I canÕt remember Carson ever having a night where he had to deal with some tragedy. It seems like itÕs a new skill: to be grounded and sincere and [to] emotionally connect with the audience. Was that a hard thing to learn? 
Jimmy: I donÕt think it was hard, but itÕs painful. I feel completely spent after a show like that. I dread having to talk about a school shooting or the death of a friend or anything that exposes my vulnerability. That is when IÕm least comfortable. But it also now seems to be necessary. IÕm not sure why, but thereÕs an expectation that you canÕt just tap-dance past some of this stuff. For me, if I feel like I have something to say that is different from what everyone else is saying, then IÕll want to say it. I donÕt feel like I need to say anything about everything, especially if another person basically said what I would have saidÑbut yes, it is very different now than it was. And the old way was easier, for sure. 
Judd: Is there a part of you that prays for a calmer political environment so you can just get back to being silly for a few years? 
Jimmy: I long for the days when the big issue that I would rant about night after night was how long the receipts are at CVS. That sort of thing is pure fun, without any of the baggage. I know that people appreciate it when we talk about serious things, but a lot of times, I feel like itÕs not what I signed up for. 
Judd: I remember, I was asked to go on The Tonight Show once, and I was the second guest. I get a call before the show, and they say, ÒCan you be the lead 
guest tonight? Bette Midler canceled because of the tragedy.Ó I was so dumb that I didnÕt ask what the tragedy was. I was just like, ThatÕs cool, I get to be the first guest. I assumed it was something that happened in Bette MidlerÕs family. But it was the day of one of those horrible shootings somewhere, a really bad one, and I find out literally while IÕm at The Tonight Show. Oh, thatÕs why she leftÑbecause itÕs almost inappropriate to be here talking about your movie. 
Jimmy: I donÕt think itÕs incumbent upon the guests to also weigh in, but I donÕt knowÑmaybe you would have gotten a ton of shit if that happened today. 
Judd: The only time IÕve ever hosted TV was on The Late Late Show, when they just let a bunch of people host the slot before James Corden started. I did a few nights, which was a really fun excuse to get Sandler and Shandling out there, and [stand-up] Maria Bamford, but I feel like I only got a taste of the daily stress of a talk show host. YouÕre basically shot out of a cannon every day. 
Jimmy: Everything seems hard until you do it every day for a year. I think about how getting the newspaper delivered to my house in the morning is itself a miracle. It involved all these people. Not only did they write all these storiesÑsome are long-lead stories, some were written last nightÑand then they send it to a printer, who hands it to a guy in a car, who then brings it to everyoneÕs house. That, to me, is more impressive than doing a television show every night, but I know, to those people who do it, itÕs not so impressive. 
Judd: At this point, youÕve built your machine, and the machine works great. What is the part that keeps it fun and engaging for you? 
Jimmy: It varies from day to day, but the reward is always the laughs. Not just on the show, but just the laughs throughout the day, and being so lucky to be around people who are funny at such a high level. I love it when I can sit in with the writers and just fuck around. ThatÕs the part of the show that I love. 
The deadlines are the things that kill me, and all the other stuff youÕre asked to do to promote the show that makes it hard. You never want to get in a situation where the show is your second job, but sometimes, it feels like it is. And because itÕs so familiar at this point, itÕs easy to give it less attention than it deserves. For me, ultimately, I fear going onstage and not being as good as I could be. Some nights, the audiences are bad, and they donÕt give you what you deserve. And some nights, theyÕre great: They give you more than you deserve. You have to remember that and enjoy the fun nights and move on after the ones that arenÕt as fun, and as you do the show more times, it does get easier. 
Judd: Where you differ from a lot of the other talk show guys is that there was a deep dysfunction or darkness in them that also made how they ran their ship dark. YouÕd hear from the Tonight Show writers in the Carson era that he would pick up their contracts every thirteen weeks, and he made them sweat every thirteen weeks that they were not going to get picked up. But it sounds like, for you, the joy of doing the show is building a machine that you like, that makes you happy, surrounded by people you really enjoying being around all day. That is, oddly, a unique experience. 
Jimmy: An advantage that I have that I donÕt think many of the older talk show hosts had is: I was once the writer. I was the morning disc jockey sidekick and producer, and I was fired a lot. One thing that I really disliked when I was an employee, on a TV show or a radio show, was when the host was moody, and I had to dance around that personÕs mood. So, I try my best not to be moody and to be consistent, so that I donÕt have a bunch of people having to guess. I donÕt always succeedÑI can be moody from time to time Ñbut I think itÕs a selfish way to go into the office every day, and I try not to do it. 
Judd: And your wife is working with you? YouÕre collaborators? 
Jimmy: She gets the worst of it, because thereÕs no hiding your moods from your wife. We talk about work at home; itÕs not limited to the workplace. IÕll vent frustrations to her, and she will do the same to me. People often ask me what itÕs like to work with your wife, and I think theyÕre expecting to hear something other than ÒItÕs great,Ó but it is pretty great, because I know that thereÕs at least one person who truly represents me. And even sometimes, when we disagree, I have to consider the distinct possibility that she might be right. 
Judd: What is the dynamic of your [wifeÕs and your] creative process and comedy writing together? 
Jimmy: She definitely knows my sensibilities as well or better than anyone, but she also reminds me of whatÕs important. Sometimes, I donÕt want to talk about something thatÕs uncomfortable. Sometimes, I would rather dodge it and give myself the night off from that kind of stress. But when she says to me, ÒYou need to talk about this,Ó or, ÒThis canÕt just be a footnote on the show tonight; this has to be significant,Ó I always know that sheÕs right. I donÕt necessarily like hearing it, but I know sheÕs right. 
Judd: And when youÕre working together and raising kids together, do you think that ultimately makes the balance and the sanity of life easier to accomplish, because youÕre in the ship together on everything? 
Jimmy: Having the same schedule is a great luxury. I have two older kids, who are twenty-nine and twenty-seven. I also have a six-year-old and a three-year.old. The first time around seems like a million years ago, but this time, IÕve learned a way of communicating that was totally alien to me, in which you listen to your children. And instead of immediately yelling at them, you explain why they shouldnÕt be doing something. These are all revelations to me, because itÕs not how it usually goes in my family. 
Judd: And what are your kids into; what are their interests? 
Jimmy: My oldest daughter, Katie, is an artist. She makes ceramics, and sheÕs very successfulÑshe has art shows all over the country and around the world. My son Kevin works at the show, and he also makes funny videos with his friends. I think he wants to do what I do, but it must be hard for a kid to say that out loud. But we finally reached the point where heÕs admitted itÑnot just to us, but to himself. So, thatÕs fun to watch, and I try to stay out of the way and let him do his own thing. Then, my younger daughter loves to draw, and sheÕs funny, and my younger son loves Spider-Man. 
Judd: My kids always talk about what it means being in the shadow of someone whoÕs accomplished something in the world they want to be in. It does, ultimately, bother them. I remember when I met [Ben] Stiller, he had a chip on his shoulder about his dad, Jerry, and felt like he had to succeed and he had to do better than Jerry, so people wouldnÕt think he was just Jerry StillerÕs son. It was the gas that made him work so hard in his career. But IÕm always trying to tell my kids that people are glad that Michael Douglas is famous. They donÕt go, ÒHe shouldnÕt be famous because we had Kirk.Ó Nobody cares. 
Jimmy: IÕm sure that they will eventually know that youÕre right. But I can imagine that being in that situation is not always so easy. Because you feel like the last thing, especially in comedy, you want to be is predictable, and thereÕs nothing more predictable than a celebrityÕs kid wanting to be in show business. 
Judd: I would say weÕre like cobblers, where itÕs natural for the cobblerÕs kids to make shoes. This is the family business. 
Jimmy: I do understand that hesitancy, though, and I think itÕs nice. I also find it helpful that there are people who are in a similar situation with their own children and would pull my kid aside and talk to them about it. Because I think itÕs a lot easier for the kids to talk about that stuff with someone other than their parents, but they also need to talk to somebody who knows whatÕs going on. 
Judd: ThatÕs the worst part, though. TheyÕll talk to Lena Dunham all day about it, and they wonÕt talk to me at all. 
Jimmy: ThatÕs the best part. 
Judd: When youÕre on your break with the show, what do you do with your time? 
Jimmy: I always have to be doing something. Where my attention turned to this past summer was toward organizing my life. IÕve spent the last three months trying to organize my office, which was a terrible mess, and I have this fear that I will lose something important, whether itÕs videos or some audio. I used to tape my family with a cassette [recorder], and I found this audiotape of my aunt Chippy and uncle Frank arguing. It is just hysterical: TheyÕre arguing over a bottle of ketchup. He canÕt figure out how to pour the ketchup out of the bottle, and she starts yelling at him, then she realizes he has the cap on and just starts screaming. And she has a great line. She goes, ÒFrank, youÕve gotta open it before it goddamn pours out.Ó And then she goes into this thing of, like, ÒHeÕs so stupid, heÕll never invent the lightbulb, heÕll never invent the airplane, and heÕs lucky they even let him ride on one.Ó That, to me, is just gold, and the idea that I had that in a box somewhere and I hadnÕt digitized it fills me with anxiety. So, IÕve been digitizing everything. I have all these magazines I was on the cover of, and I realized, No one wants any of this stuff. Maybe, if IÕm lucky, IÕll have a grandchild that gets a kick out of looking through it one eveningÑmaybe IÕll have a grandkid whoÕs a comedy nerd. But my kids donÕt care about this stuff. I donÕt know why itÕs so important to me, that I maintain this museum dedicated to honoring myself, but I do. 
Judd: I know my kids are not going to care about my three Paul Lynde autographs. 
Jimmy: You know whatÕs the saddest thing? When you think about those auctions that happen when some celebrity dies, and you get, like, Jerry LewisÕs pinky ring, and it goes for eight hundred and twelve dollars or something. ItÕs just like, Oh, no one in his family wanted any of this stuff. IsnÕt that sad? 
Judd: Steve Martin had a very funny article about having all this stuff and how he didnÕt want to put people through that after he died. So, he decided to get 
ahead of that by auctioning a lot of that stuff off, and I bought the puppet that he would do ventriloquism with from his fly. 
Jimmy: You mean the Great Flydini? For how much? 
Judd: I could have paid like six thousand dollars. 
Jimmy: What?! 
Judd: But I think it was for charity, for the Motion Picture and Television Fund. 
Jimmy: YouÕre lucky I didnÕt know about it. That would have cost you eight thousand. 
Judd: I always say to my kids, ÒThe second I die, just burn it all.Ó But then, when we were working on Funny People, I remembered how I used to videotape Sandler making phony phone calls, and I had, like, an hour and a half of him calling JerryÕs [Famous] Deli complaining about the roast beef in the voice of an old woman. And I was like, Yes! Hoarding pays off. 
Jimmy: IsnÕt that the best? I feel the same way, and I know that weÕre both wrong, but I still say, if thereÕs a one-in-a-million chance that IÕm going to need something, I want to make sure I have it. And you know what, both of us really need a young Judd Apatow or young Jimmy Kimmel to collect and treasure all our garbage. 

JOHN CANDY 
(Interviewed in 1984, backstage at the Lorne Michaels produced sketch show The New Show) 
Judd: Have you done any formal acting training? 
John: Only from a magazine ad that I once took out. That was only a twelve-week course, and it was only nine dollars, but I think it was a real course. But you know, it helped me out in my early days. 
Judd: This is your third show with The New Show. What keeps you coming back to it? 
John: Well, it wasnÕt the money. DonÕt get me wrong on this, it isnÕt the money. I had agreed that when we first got the show, that I would do three episodes over the thirteen that they were guaranteed for. 
Judd: What attracted you to the show in the first place? 
John: The people involved. It seemed like a good show with Lorne Michaels, and weÕll have Dave Thomas again. I thought I was going to be on the show with Catherine OÕHara, but she did three other ones that IÕm not in. But itÕs been varied. ItÕs been a lot of fun, and it has a good cast. 
Judd: YouÕve also been busy doing films, and it looks like Splash has been a hit. What do you think is the appeal? 
John: I think itÕs a nice family film in that thereÕs plenty for everybody to enjoy. You care about the people, and itÕs not real schmaltzy. IÕll attribute all that success to Ron Howard, because of his keen eye as a director and in the editing. ItÕs just a good production all around. This picture is a pleasant surprise to everybody. 
Judd: People seem to be especially drawn to your character in the film. Why do you think they like him so much? 
John: In the film, I play Freddie, the brother of the guy who falls in love with the mermaid, and IÕm kind of like the neÕer-do-well, the playboy, the guy with the devil-may-care attitude about life. And FreddieÕs a silly guy, heÕs got a good attitude. ItÕs a pretty harmless character, really. HeÕs just a good-time guy. People have met guys like him before. 
Judd: When youÕre playing racquetball in the movie, how many takes did you need to do to get the shot where you hit the ball and it bounces back and hits you in the head? 
John: Three takes. I was lucky. It was three takes. 
Judd: They must have had the whole day set up so that they could just keep trying till you got it. 
John: You got that right. They thought it was gonna be a long time. Not Johnny, though. It was three takes. 
Judd: Did you practice that? 
John: No, I was just real lucky. By rights, I should still be there hitting it. 
Judd: So, you signed a three-picture deal with Touchstone Films after this, is that right? 
John: It isnÕt signed yet, but yes, thatÕs pretty much what IÕm going to do. IÕm going to develop three movies for them, as well as write and perform in the movies. 
Judd: Did they approach you for this because of the success with Splash? 
John: Most likely, and IÕm glad they did. They seem like a very decent group of people who are bringing change to Disney, and with the Touchstone division specifically, theyÕre going to go for more PG-and R-rated movies. More mainstream American films, like Never Cry Wolf, which was basically a Touchstone film, although it had a Disney label on it. If the film is not like a Disney-type movie, theyÕll all be under Touchstone, and itÕs nice. YouÕre there from the beginning, which is also nice. This is brand-new, so youÕre right there when itÕs just starting to grow, which is exciting, as opposed to working with a major studio. Which, by the way, didnÕt bother to option any pictures. Touchstone were the only ones who did, so I guess thatÕs why I accepted them. 
Judd: What type of freedom did everybody on the set have? Did Disney have any control over anything? 
John: They didnÕt bother us at all. The only suggestions they made were after the picture was shot, and they were positive notes. I spoke to the producer Brian Grazer before I even started negotiating my deal with them and asked him what they were like to work with. And he said they were just great, they didnÕt bother you at all, they let you make your picture. Which is rare these days, for an executive to keep his hands out of it. 
Judd: What were some of the other movies that you did, other than Stripes and Vacation and Going Berserk? 
John: I did a lot of pictures in Canada. [The] Silent Partner, Fine LadyÑI was in a movie called [The] Clown Murders. I did another one called Tunnel Vision. And It Came from Hollywood, that short-lived film with Gilda [Radner], Dan [Aykroyd], and Cheech and Chong. It really didnÕt lastÑ though, actually, itÕs doing very well in cassette sales, I hear. But no one really came out to the theaters to see it. 
Judd: Well, it was a good film. It had all the conglomeration montages. 
John: Yeah, I thought it was funny. It was also funny that it didnÕt last long. We shot it all in two days, all our segments. 
Judd: What do you think about the fact that, when people walk into the theater and the movie starts playing and they see John Candy, everyone starts laughing? People hear your name, and they just start laughing. What do you think people like about you? 
John: It probably goes back to SCTV, because people associate me with that show, and I thought that was a pretty funny show. If we instill that kind of spirit into somebody, and theyÕre anticipating something good, thatÕs very nice. And we definitely were trying to do that. 
Judd: Will you be doing any more SCTV, like guest spots? 
John: Not with the TV show as it is now, no. This is the last year for them. I think theyÕre in the last six shows now. More than likely, IÕll be working with the group again, in pictures or television specials, I donÕt know. But I know weÕre all good friends and weÕll all work together again. 
Judd: When you see the movies that youÕre in, and other Second City people are in, it always winds up with you guys popping up all over. Why is that? 
John: Well, I guess you could call it nepotism. ThereÕs a core of usÑHarold Ramis, Bill Murray, Dan Aykroyd, John BelushiÑwho hire each other because we know what weÕre going to get, and you know youÕre going to get something real good. ThereÕs also a shorthand in working with each other, whereas, with some actors, you bring them in, and you donÕt know what youÕre gonna get. 
Judd: What do you think of the way that Second City got canceled by NBC right after receiving a lot of critical acclaim? 
John: ItÕs unfortunate. I guess NBC didnÕt feel like we were bringing in the numbers. 
Judd: What were your most memorable characters and skits? 
John: There are so many of them, and I just felt good about being a part of it, really. Each of the characters I didÑmeaning, the continuing characters and even some of the ones that you just do once or twiceÑwe were allowed to have so much control over these characters that we created lives for these people. We did it so many times that each is special to me. Yosh and Stan Shmenge, theyÕre brothers, and they have another life outside of that. Which is great. Which came into play with the show. Same with Doctor Tongue; Woody Tobias, Jr.; and Count Floyd. Their interactions with each other off-camera was just as interesting as what theyÕre doing on-cameraÑeven more so, actually. These two guys just bicker at each other all the time, but they still work with each other, they need each other. [Johnny] LaRue, Gil Fisher, all these charactersÑit was just nice to be able to do all of those, and we could do whatever we wanted. 
Judd: What do you think of the way the Bob and Doug McKenzie characters from Second City TV took off into movies and records? 
John: Man, that was great for them. Those two characters really caught on, and the album was such a big hit. 
Judd: How did the movie do? 
John: The movie didnÕt do too good. I donÕt know if a lot of people understood it. ItÕs coming out on cassette, I think soon. 
Judd: Are you interested in writing and starring in a movie? 
John: I think the only way IÕm going to get a lot of pictures is to do what I did in SCTV, which is write and perform. We know each other better than anyone else, and there havenÕt been that many offers coming from anywhere else. At least if we fail, we know that we did it on our own. 
Judd: You should have made a movie with you and Joe Flaherty, blowing people up. 
John: We talked about it. I donÕt know how it would last for ninety minutes, though. It kind of wears a little thin. 
Judd: You could blow people up and have the police chase you. 
John: I think with [Big] Jim McBob and Billy [Sol Hurok], it would be a little too much for two hours. 
Judd: Martin Short said that he thought that Second City would have done really well in America in a ten oÕclock time slot on a Friday night. Do you think thatÕs true? 
John: Absolutely, thatÕs what we always wanted. But we were told that thatÕs a sacred turf weÕd never get, that theyÕd never put a comedy show in there. 
Judd: But they did. 
John: They did. You know, they also said that theyÕd give us Sunday at seven oÕclock, opposite 60 Minutes. And if that were the case, we would have had to change the format of the show. We would have had to have a child psychologist working with us at all times, because itÕs prime access. Most of our characters would have had to change drastically: LaRueÕs drinking and smoking would be out; Bobby Bittman would be out. And they were actually asking us, ÒWell, maybe Bobby Bittman could have a cousinÉ,Ó and GeneÕs reply was ÒWell, what if Bobby Bittman doesnÕt have a cousin?Ó It was all very foolish. I mean, that was their excuse for saying, ÒWell, we gave them a shot. They didnÕt want it,Ó because there was a lot of heat on them. And when they dropped us, there were a lot of people going after them. There was no real reason to drop us from their format at all. 
Judd: TheyÕre out to make money. 
John: We just werenÕt in there with the ratings, but itÕs business. We didnÕt cut the numbers that they wanted, and thatÕs what itÕs all about these days. 
Judd: What about Big City Comedy? What type of experience was that where you had your own show? 
John: It was a learning experience: I learned what not to do in the business, and who not to work with a lot of the time. It was frightening. I was nave in that I thought I was going to have some control and some say on who the cast members were going to be, who was going to be writing the show, who was going to direct, and this and that. But the cast was good; we were fortunate that way. It was a good group of people. It just wasÑagain, they put us in the wrong time slot, seven oÕclock. 
Judd: When youÕre doing the introduction to the show, you seem a lot more comfortable doing Mr. Mambo than talking up there as yourself. Why was that? 
John: Well, IÕm not a stand-up comic. And thereÕs a real frenzied energy when youÕre on a show like that, that I had never experienced before. So, itÕs frustrating. IÕve stood in front of audiences many times before, but when youÕve got that kind of pressure onÑyou feel that pressure from these comics who are really trying, who want to make it. And this is their shot. 
Judd: How is that different from the Second City stage show, when you have all these people helping you out? 
John: ItÕs just a feeling, just a different energy, thatÕs all. We didnÕt have that killer instinct, where youÕre out there by yourself as a stand-up comic. ThereÕs a lot of pressure on themÑthey didnÕt want to blow their moment. I mean, this is a big time for them, and a lot of publicity. So, itÕs a lot on their shoulders. And theyÕve got to make it count. 

JOHN CLEESE 
September 2020 
Judd: Why did you decide that you wanted to write a book about creativity? 
John: IÕd been thinking about it for about thirty years. I got interested in creativity because I went to Cambridge and got into show business through the [Cambridge University] Footlights, but before that, I had always done science and law. I had no idea that I had any creative ability, and then, suddenly, I discovered I could write things that made people laugh. And I began to notice things happening. For instance, IÕd work very hard at night trying to write a sketch, and then, in the morning, IÕd sit down with a cup of coffee before I really got going, and the answer would be there within ninety seconds, and I couldnÕt even quite see what the difficulty was the previous evening, when IÕd been stuck. And I came to the conclusion that my mind was working on the problem when I was asleep. Then I began to realize it was working on the problem in my unconscious while I was awake, so I began to take notes of what the unconscious was capable of. The problem with the unconscious is that itÕs extraordinary what it can do, but you canÕt order it about. You canÕt hit it with a stick. You have to discover how to coax it. WhatÕs your process when you sit down? Do you do lots of sharpening of pencils, making of coffee, and all that sort of stuff? 
Judd: I like what you wrote about how boredom is essential. You have to allow yourself to be bored and, for me, I like to listen to music and try to let things bubble up. IÕm a big believer of what they call the vomit draft, where youÕre writing fastÑnot wondering why youÕre writing it, just babbling, just, Go. Then IÕll go, Is there anything of value in this thing I just babbled out? And a surprising number of times, there is. 
John: I read about experiments that have been done by a psychologist called [Donald] MacKinnon at Berkeley in the seventies. And I knew a psychology professor who said that the problem about writing about creativity is that itÕs so much to do with the unconscious. So, what you can say about it is pretty limited. But this guy MacKinnon was very interested in professionals, and particularly in architects, because they had to be very efficient as well as very creative, because bridges need to stay up. What he discovered when he compared the creative architects with the noncreative architects was that the important difference was that the creative architects could play, and I began to see that so much of creativity was just playing, which is what youÕve been describing, when you just sit down and start any old nonsense and see if it leads somewhere. 
Judd: I always think about the critical voice, how we all have that voice in our head telling us that weÕre terrible. And so much of being in play is to tell that voice to shut the hell up and have fun. 
John: As my psychologist friend pointed out, if youÕre sad, you have sad thoughts. If youÕre feeling angry, you have angry thoughts. So, how do you get in a creative mood? That involves, I think, a little bit of meditation. ItÕs the first time that you sit down and start to think, What am I going to think about? You think, I should have called Tom, I should have bought a birthday present for the cat. I havenÕt mended the roof. You have to wait for that to settle. Just like the Buddhists say: ItÕs like a glass of cloudy water; leave it there, and it will settle. After about twenty minutes, then those thoughtsÑI canÕt do this, IÕm no good, all that stuff youÕre talking aboutÑthat doesnÕt get in the way anymore. But it always does at the beginning. 
Judd: When youÕre young, itÕs different than when youÕve had success, because when youÕre young, I think youÕre a little delusional. For a lot of people, thereÕs a feeling when youÕre in your twenties that you can take over the world. 
John: I think Americans are more optimistic and positive than the British. I always had the feeling I wasnÕt going to be any good. What I discovered, particularly working with Graham Chapman, is that we could average fifteen to twenty minutes of good material a week. We might have a terrible day Monday and not a great Tuesday morning. Then, suddenly, after lunch on Tuesday, it would all start flowing until about Thursday afternoon. And then we dry up again. Once you know thereÕs an average amount that you can write just by sitting there, then when you normally get negative and think, IÕm no good at this, you just see it as part of the whole process. 
Judd: Mike Nichols once said that in order to have a good idea, you have to have a lot of ideas. I remember, when I was first starting out, there was this book by Anne Lamott, and she said she had a down/up theory, which is to get it down and then, later on, fix it up. 
John: ThatÕs exactly right. And sometimes, when you go into the critical phase, one of the best things you can do is get away from it for a bit, put it in a drawer for a week and then come back to it. When Connie Booth and I were writing Fawlty Towers, if we got stuck, weÕd just put the episode to one side and work on another one. Then, when you come back to it, two things happen. One, it becomes much clearer whatÕs good and what isnÕt good, because youÕve been away from it, and that gives you a certain objectivity. The other thing is, your unconscious has been working on it while youÕve been away, so you actually come up with ideas quite easily. 
Judd: IsnÕt it weird that your brain does that? 
John: IÕm fascinated by the idea of the two hemispheres. There is a book called the Master and His Emissary, by Iain McGilchrist. ItÕs the most interesting book IÕve ever read. He says that the interaction between those two hemispheres is extremely complicated, and theyÕre very interlinked, but you could say that they have distinct personalities. The left brain is the critical one that wants to know how to possess things and process them. It wants big things. Then the right brain is where the values come from. And I think we are going from one to the other. 
Judd: I also look at it from the simplest point of view, which is, I wake up from a dream and itÕll be so imaginative and so bizarre and emotional. I always find it so strange that, at night, my mind feels the need to create this little movie for me. What is that? And why in evolution would that happen? 
John: We need ideas, and the hard thing is getting out of the left brain. These days, we have phones calling all the time, and interruptions, which is exactly what keeps us stuck in the left brain. It stops us from being creative. ThatÕs why I believe you have to sit down somewhere where youÕre not going to be interrupted. Go sit in the park. One of my friends was reduced to locking himself in the loo because he was in a family of six. If youÕre a CEO, you just say to the secretary, ÒShut the door. DonÕt interrupt me unless the building is burning.Ó You create a quiet place where youÕre not going to be interrupted, where the phone isnÕt ringing, and thatÕs what enables you to sit there while the mind settles. 
Judd: I read somewhere that when you check your messages, that you cannot return to a fully creative state for about twenty minutes. 
John: I read twelve minutes. Interruption is the deadliest thing when youÕre trying to be creative. 
Judd: I wanted to ask you about spirituality, because there are some people who believe in the creative intelligence of the universe. Tommy from Cheech and Chong was on the radio, and he said itÕs almost like some people have got a hose, and they can hook it into the big pipe and get all the ideas. Like Jimi HendrixÑhe knew how to do it. Or Bob Dylan. Do you ever think of it in those terms? 
John: I donÕt, because I donÕt have experiences like that. But I think those people and Mozart and other geniuses do. I once saw Ian Holm do King Lear twice in the same week because it was so good. I went back, and I said to him afterward, ÒHow do you find the energy?Ó ItÕs just such a long play and so dramatic in an acting sense. And he said, ÒI donÕt know where the energy is coming from.Ó He said, ÒItÕs as though itÕs passing through me without touching the sides.Ó But IÕve never had an experience like that. I noticed on Python sometimes, when we got together after weÕd been writing on our own for a week, thereÕd be certain themes in everyoneÕs piece of writing, as though in some strange way weÕd been connected. I donÕt understand that. But then you discover that [Gottfried Wilhelm] Leibniz and Isaac Newton discovered calculus at almost exactly the same time. I think sometimes there are weird things going on. Do you have those experiences? 
Judd: I do, and itÕs always connected to a stupid dirty joke somehow. IÕll think of a dirty joke, and I literally feel like God gave it to me, because itÕll come from nowhere. I always think about you and Monty Python as having had a similar experience to the Beatles, in the sense of a group of people holed up together, all with very different talents that served each other, and even though everyone was paying attention and loved you so much, you found a way to continue to grow and change and push the limits. Did you guys feel that? 
John: I didnÕt realize the best team to build would be made of completely different abilities. Terry JonesÕs ideas for sketches were quite different from anything that Graham and I came up with; and Michael Palin, the same. At one stage, I tried to improve on that by actually saying, Instead of my writing with Graham, why donÕt I write once occasionally with Michael Palin, with Eric, because then weÕll come up with something thatÕs slightly different. But the force of habit overcame this. There were a small number of sketches that I wrote with Palin and Idle, and they were all, I thought, rather good. But we could have gone on longer and created more stuff together if weÕd broken up the writing parts. 
Judd: I was in college in 1985, and Graham Chapman was doing a college tour of America. He was showing clips of Monty Python and clips of the Dangerous Sports Club. It was really like an earlier version of Jackass, where these people would ride a bed down a ski slope or something. I was eighteen or nineteen years old, and I was one of the people in charge of the speakers at the University of Southern California. Graham gave his speech, and it was hilarious and wonderful, and then, afterward, a few of us went to go hang out with him and spent three or four hours chatting with him. It was a life-changing kind of night. He did something that really affected me, which is he was as interested in our stupid college lives as we were in his stories about getting hookers with [the Rolling StonesÕ] Keith Moon. He really was interested and was asking questions. We were all just idiots, and he was enjoying our company, but also telling us some of the funniest anecdotes weÕd ever heard. He was just a very special, kind man. 
John: He was extraordinary, and he was quite weird sometimes. I was talking to a theatrical producer whoÕd done a tour of his, and he said when Graham arrived in one city, he suddenly said he wanted six large salmonÑfresh salmon, untouched. And the guy thought he must be on some strange diet. All he did was he came out at the end of the show with these fish and threw them into the audience. And the promoters said it was the biggest dry-cleaning bill heÕs ever seen. ItÕs just a random thing that Graham would do. You have to be prepared to be random, because you donÕt know what your unconscious is going to throw up. The stuff that just makes people laugh and laugh and laugh is very often the pure, silliest stuff. Take the fish-slapping dance for Michael [Palin]Ñpeople can watch that again and again and again. ItÕs absolutely stupid, and it means nothing. But sometimes, these ideas just have a pureness. You donÕt know where they came from. 
Judd: When you were writing Monty Python, what kind of energy was it coming from? Was it coming from intelligence and confidence? Was it from anger? 
John: Graham and I soon discovered that when we wrote from anger, it wasnÕt funny. If we got angry about something, usually something about the Conservative Party, and we would try and write about it, it just wasnÕt funny. We had to let go of the anger, and then, maybe later, a few days later, we could be funny about it. It was as simple as: We would sit there, IÕd open up a thesaurus, and just read words to him. IÕd say, ÒCucumber.Ó And then IÕd say, ÒPlummet.Ó And I said ÒplummetÓ once, and Graham said, ÒWell, a sheep would plummet.Ó I said, ÒWhat are you talking about?Ó He said, ÒIf she tried to fly, she would plummet.Ó And that gave us a sketch, how there were sheep that were trying to learn to fly. 
Judd: ThatÕs how you get a dead-parrot sketch, I guess. 
John: That was more logical, because that was based on an experience Michael Palin had with a secondhand-car salesman. And we sat there for some time on it. We said, ÒWell, what could it be?Ó One of us said it could be an animal. That way, when you take it back to the pet shop, itÕs not working properly. Then we played around with what the animal would be. We go, ÒIt canÕt be a mouse, because itÕs too small and sweet, and people like cats and dogs too much.Ó So, then somebody said, ÒWhy not a parrot?Ó I said, ÒThatÕs great, because nobody likes parrots, so nobodyÕs going to feel sympathetic.Ó Then we wrote it about the parrot. I got the thesaurus back out and found all the synonyms for dead. 
Judd: It sounds like you guys were playing. 
John: DoesnÕt it? Einstein once said about science, if youÕre doing research and you knew what you were going to find out, it wouldnÕt be research. And thatÕs a nice thought. The main thing in creativity is you donÕt know where itÕs going. My experience in the film businessÑand I really want to know what you think about itÑis that when they commission a film, they want to see something immediate to know what theyÕre getting. Whereas they would get something much better if they just said, ÒGo away and come back in four months.Ó 
Judd: You definitely need people who understand your process. IÕve been lucky enough to work with the same film studio for fifteen years, and they just know the flow of how it happens. Creative ideas, even really good ones, are often a difficult sell. Do you have advice for presenting creative ideas to others? 
John: I think youÕve got to tell the story. You have to rehearse, and I think itÕs better to have a performer pitch the story to the executives. IÕm so pleased youÕve had a good experience with studios, Judd. But my experience has been largely that the executives simply donÕt know what theyÕre doing. It seems like, for you, the key is they trust you? 
Judd: Yes. ItÕs tough in the beginning, when they donÕt. When youÕre young and they donÕt trust you, then they want to be involved in your process. What was it like for you to have had the chance to do the reunion concerts with Python? 
John: It was a very strange experience, because we got into financial trouble. WeÕve always had hopeless managers, completely fucking hopeless managers. And we discovered we owed eight hundred thousand pounds in legal fees, because something had not been settled. We called in an old friend of ours from Cambridge who used to manage Queen, or still does manage Queen, and he said, ÒWhy donÕt you just do a show at The O2 [arena]?Ó So, we did it for money. Eric had a few days off, and he put a very good script together of all the sketches that we knew were going to work. When we read it, it was a wonderful experience, because we started to laugh, really laugh. And this is material thatÕs been around for forty years. But because we found it funny, we found that we were looking forward to doing the shows. Of course, the shows were very strange, because normally the people in the audience donÕt know the dialogue better than the actors. Very strange event, but very lovely. 
Judd: And how was it, as friends, to work together? People donÕt always remember that you guys toured America doing these shows at places like the Hollywood Bowl. You traveled like rock stars in that period, which must have been a strange thing for all of you. 
John: ItÕs very strange because comedians on the whole donÕt do that in the way that bands do. Why doesnÕt it happen with comedians? I donÕt really know. All I can say is it was the usual thing: Sometimes we got cross with each other, but we always thought the material was funny. And the sound of laughter is one of the loveliest sounds in the world. ItÕs hard to be very cross if youÕve just been in front of an audience whoÕd been laughing. 
Judd: What are your thoughts when you look back at the amount of stuff you all continued to do together? I think all of us wind up in groups of one kind or another. I noticed that in my world, many of the people that I thought would work together their whole lives have split up, or they get into a fight. And some people come back together; some people donÕt. 
John: I think we got together at exactly the right time. We went into the pitch meeting at the BBC, and the guy in charge of comedy, Michael Mills, said, ÒWell, what do you propose doing?Ó And we hadnÕt discussed it. So, we kind of ad-libbed a rather limp thing, and he asked us a couple of questions. ÒYou donÕt have music and guest artists?Ó We said, ÒWe never really thought about that.Ó And then he looked at us and shrugged and said, ÒGo away and make sense in program.Ó The most extraordinary thing. NobodyÕs ever commissioned a show before without knowing what they were getting. But the very fact that weÕd not given a concept meant that when we got down to it, we werenÕt trying to fit anything into an idea that weÕd had. And I think that was why it was so creative: because there was no sense that we had a mandate of This is what the showÕs got to be like. 
Judd: IsnÕt it strange when something that was funny a long time ago is equally funny now? DoesnÕt its timelessness surprise you? I do equate it to the Beatles. 
John: I was always envious of the rock musicians, because they made fifty times as much money as we did. I used to say to myself, TheyÕre lucky: If they write a good song, people can hear it again two minutes later. Whereas, if I tell you a joke and then tell it to you two minutes later, youÕre not going to laugh. Then something started to change: We suddenly realized that people were learning the sketches in the same way that people can quote Bob Dylan lyrics. Once, when I was onstage with Michael Palin, he broke me up and made me laugh, and I couldnÕt think what the next line was. I just said to the audience, ÒWhatÕs the next line?Ó and about sixty people shouted it out. 
Judd: How does that feel? How do you view that connection between them and your work? 
John: I always thought that Python was funny and that it was good for people to laugh. But I began to realize in the last ten years that we were doing a bit more than that, because people would come up to me after the show and shake me by the hand and say, ÒThanks for making me laugh all these years,Ó and thereÕs a tear in their eye. ItÕs meant something more than just laughter, and I think itÕs because Python is just saying, ÒLife is insane. This planet that weÕre on, itÕs ridiculous, nothing works, and nobody knows what theyÕre doing.Ó After about six shows, a friend of mine said, ÒWhat I love about Python is, after IÕve watched an episode, I simply cannot watch the news. I cannot take it seriously.Ó And I think thatÕs a really important thing that we do for people: say that we canÕt take this seriously. I think the turning point for me was when I went to Sarajevo for a film festival, and someone told me about how they had been under siege by the Serbians for four years. The Serbians were sitting up in the hills lobbing shells down, and people with telescopic sights were picking off people who were walking across the road. But in the evening, he found an underground garage, and they set up as a small cinema, and they used to go in there and laugh and watch lots of Monty Python. Other stuff, too, but they used to laugh especially at Monty Python, and what they said to me is, ÒWe came out feeling better.Ó Nothing had changed. Nothing about the shells had changed, but they came out feeling more optimistic about life, more able to go on. When I saw that the company has that effect, I started to realize itÕs more important than I used to think. 
Judd: I would assume thatÕs why people keep singing ÒAlways Look on the Bright Side of Life.Ó They need to hear that life is total madness. I had a similar experience. I was at a Mexican restaurant, and my waitress says to me, ÒI never say anything to people I recognize, but I want you to know that I was an interrogator in Iraq. We used to watch Superbad and Knocked Up at the end of hard days, to just relieve our pressure. And it really helped get us through. Thank you.Ó And I was so moved, and clearly that was important to 
her. But then I thought, Well, what did I do, give her more energy to interrogate and waterboard people? 
John: I think that the purpose of laughter, from an evolutionary point of view is to relax us after stress. Because once weÕre in the thrall of the left brain, weÕre worrying and planning and being resentful. And then, if we laugh, it simply moves us to the right brain, where thereÕs relaxation and a sense of meaning and all these kinds of things that you simply donÕt get from the left brain. 
Judd: ItÕs meant to help us relax after a hunt. It also relates to what people need to relax, because there are so many different types of comedy. Some people like gentle and harmless comedy, and other people want really dark comedy. They canÕt get off unless you say the worst thing ever, which has led us to all these debates about political correctness. I know you had that experience where they took down one of the episodes of Fawlty Towers. What was the detail in the episode that they had an issue with? 
John: We had the old Major [Gowen], who had been out there in the Indian Army or something like that, and he was explaining to his girlfriend the difference between these various insulting terms. Like, ÒWe call the Spanish dagos, and we call the Italians wops, and we call the Egyptians wogs,Ó and all these. He was explaining this in a very serious way, and of course the whole purpose of it was to make fun of the fact that he had these categories so clearly laid out in his mind. But people donÕt understand. You can make fun of things in two ways. You can either attack them or you can put the words that you donÕt like in the mouths of people that you then make to look very stupid. 
Judd: We need the ability to have characters who are allowed to make terrible mistakes and say terrible things. You canÕt take away that the world is filled with mean people or idiots, and we should be able to mock them in whatever way we need to. 
John: There is nothing funny about a perfect person. If you wrote a character who is really fine, intelligent, and thoughtful, and wiseÑno laughs there. An American once said to me, ÒYou show me a sitcom about Saint Francis of Assisi, and IÕll show you a bummer.Ó Comedy is about things going wrong and people going wrong. 
Judd: Tell me about your live performing now. I know you go out with Eric Idle and do some shows together sometimes. And you also go out by yourself. HowÕs that for you? And what do you try to accomplish in those shows? 
John: I just try to make people laugh. I used to like smaller theaters, and IÕve gotten used to places of two thousand, or sometimes two thousand five hundred. ItÕs a very simple way of making money. Because after the show, the tour manager goes backstage and talks to the guy in the box office. ItÕs all clear, you know what I mean? ThereÕs no studio coming out with huge expenses. ItÕs very straightforward. ThereÕs something very satisfactory in the pure transaction of standing in front of people and making them laugh. I played a show at the Acropolis in Athens last year and stood there on the stage, thinking, About two thousand years ago, people were standing exactly here and making the audience laugh. There was lovely sense of continuity or history about it. 
Judd: ItÕs amazing to be performing as a stand-up comedian at this point in your career. 
John: The Python show was a result of legal bills. My stand-up shows are really the result of my last divorce. 
Judd: If there was a common complaint about you that all of your wives had, what would it be? 
John: I used to be much more tense and uptight. Now IÕve gotten much more relaxed. IÕve also got a wife who I really like and love. I have a lovely group of friends. I donÕt feel I have anything much to prove now, and IÕm much less anxious. Somebody once said, ÒVery few things matter much, and most things donÕt matter at all.Ó And somehow that opinion covers my life. I donÕt expect things to go right. 
Judd: YouÕve been interested in psychology and your own behavior your whole life. What is the main wisdom that you have now that you may not have had forty years ago? 
John: The realization that you canÕt get things right most of the time. When you make mistakesÑsometimes a big mistake, sometimes small mistakesÑ you can usually correct them before very real damage is done. So, donÕt spend your whole time trying to not make mistakes. The English are like that. There was a speech in A Fish Called Wanda, when I said, ÒDo you think you know what itÕs like being English, going through life just trying to get everything right and not upset people?Ó Whereas, if you can just let go and say, Well, thereÕs always going to be some people who hate me anyway, that frees you up. And when youÕre trying to present an image to somebody that youÕre really a super person all the time and very kind and intelligent and that you earn more than you doÑall that ego stuff puts us under pressure and makes it much harder for us to actually be ourselves. 
Judd: How does that affect these friendships that youÕve had for fifty, sixty years, when you guys are all together and laughing and talking about your journey of creativity and humor? 
John: It totally depends on whether IÕm talking to my English friends or IÕm talking to my American friends, because I find my American friends are much more interested in the strange stuff. With my English friends, the conversations are very warm, but weÕre old-fashioned, and we probably talk about less controversial subjects. 
Judd: And what have you learned over the course of your life about how to be a good parent? 
John: I think itÕs the hardest thing in the world, because you never get any immediate feedback about whether youÕre getting it right. YouÕve got to try something and then, in fifteen yearsÕ time, you think, that worked or that didnÕt work. There have been difficult times with both of my daughters, and IÕm very, very happy now that the relationship with both of them is really warm and accepting. That, and a nice wife, and four good catsÑwell, what else would you want except a bit of money? 

JOHN MULANEY 
January 2018 
Judd: You were on Fallon recently, and you talked about going to a Steely Dan concert with your wife and Pete Davidson. 
John: IÕm a huge fan. IÕve always felt that people always say they donÕt like Steely Dan, but they donÕt even know what their music is like. IÕm like, ÒI think youÕre talking about the Doobie Brothers. YouÕre lumping it together with bands that itÕs not.Ó I talked to my wife before I went on Fallon, and said, ÒIÕm going to talk about when we all went to that Steely Dan concert with Pete Davidson, but IÕve never publicly talked about how much you hate them. What do you want me to say?Ó She said, ÒSay I hate them. I hate the music, I hate the lyrics, and I hate everything about them.Ó 
Judd: My wife really hates Steely Dan, too. ThatÕs one of the reasons why, in Knocked Up, Seth RogenÕs character goes off about how much he hates Steely Dan. ThereÕs a scene where weÕre sitting around at the table, and Seth says, ÒIf I have to listen to any more Steely Dan, I want you to cut my throat with an Al Jarreau LP.Ó I always felt bad that I insulted Steely Dan, whoÕs actually in my top-three bands. My wife doesnÕt like me talking about this, but she hates guitar solos. All guitar solos. If a guy is shredding, sheÕs like ÒI canÕt do it.Ó 
John: I remember, in junior high or high schoolÑand this is such a blanket statement, itÕs not fairÑgirls didnÕt like Kurt Vonnegut. And I was like, ÒYou donÕt like Slaughterhouse Five?Ó It was Phish, Kurt Vonnegut, Steely DanÉit was just these proud nerds of summer. 
Judd: Were you a nerd in high school? 
John: IÕm interested in how you define nerd. 
Judd: I always think of nerds in an admiring way. They were different. They usually werenÕt athletes. They liked weird stuff and took some shit for being different. 
John: I would say no, then. I was bad at sports, but basketball was so popular in Chicago that from ages nine to thirteen, I had to play basketball. And I was terrible. 
Judd: It was popular there because of Michael Jordan? 
John: Yes, and no one played football, because there wasnÕt room for football fields in the city. So, I had to deal with aggressive basketball schedules starting in fourth grade. By high school, I think I had enough defense mechanisms about the things I wasnÕt good at. 
Judd: In high school, did you have a sense of Òthings are working out for meÓ? 
John: Things were working out for me, but I couldnÕt wait to get out of Chicago. It was not a small town, but I still had the attitude of IÕve got to get out of here and go to New York. And like, This school is bullshit and small. 
Judd: What did your parents do? 
John: My dadÕs a lawyer, my momÕs a lawyer. They were sophisticatedÑwe saw a lot of theater. They went to the symphony. 
Judd: Did you know you were going to do something creative? 
John: Yes. And I was quite surprised with some of my friends who liked comedy when we were little, because IÕd be, like, ÒSo, when we grow up, weÕre all going to be in a comedy group like this, right?Ó and theyÕd be like, ÒWhat, why?Ó I would think, Why wouldnÕt we? DonÕt you want to do this professionally? I thought we were all going to be a sketch group. I even thought that with my college improv group at Georgetown. When we were graduating, I was like, ÒDo you want to be a duo?Ó And this guy I collaborated with a lot, and who I did a lot of scenes with, was like, ÒIÕm going to theological school,Ó and I was like, ÒThen why did we do all this?Ó 
Judd: Nick Kroll was at Georgetown, too, right? You eventually got him to do it. 
John: Kroll, I knew, was serious, but he was also four years older. To get face time with Nick was a big deal to me. He was already established in my mind as a wildly cooler older brother. 
Judd: Did you stay in touch after he left? 
John: Oh yeah. I bothered him a lot. I think we made a short film over the summer when I was between my junior and senior year. He and Mike Birbiglia were actually doing it, making it in the comedy world. I followed Birbiglia to a club, and I was like, Okay, he got eight hundred dollars, and I know his rent is, like, six hundred dollarsÉAnd the documentary, Comedian, about Seinfeld, came out around the same time, so that life became a thing that was no longer impenetrable. 
Judd: What have you learned from Seinfeld? Was he the number-one guy for you? 
John: I realize I donÕt say it enough, because I think of it as a given, like saying you like the Beatles. But his voice was in my consciousness so early. Him and Stern. Conan OÕBrien, too, especially, was a beacon for me. 
Judd: HeÕs an example of a smart, normal person whoÕs talented and funny. He proves that you donÕt have to come from a hellish situation to do something creative and great. 
John: It wasnÕt like, ÒOh, my dad died when I was two, and my mom had a heart condition, and the doctor was like, ÔYou better make her laugh.Õ Ó Which is some peopleÕs real story, but there was none of that for me. I definitely had a lot of embarrassment about being from a family that was well-off, and nothing other than personal problems that I had to deal with. My drug problems were basically fun until they werenÕt, and I took care of them rather quickly. 
Judd: So, were you guys doing stand-up and sketch comedy at the same time in college? 
John: We were in an improv group that we took very seriously, like long-form improv. We thought the Harold [improv structure] and all that was awesome. WeÕd come up to New York and go to the old Upright Citizens Brigade Theatre, on Twenty-Second Street, and we would do student shows where weÕd do forty minutes of improv games that audiences loved. Then weÕd do a Harold, and weÕd basically lose the audience. 
Judd: ThatÕs ballsy. 
John: Most people canÕt follow how a Harold actually works. If you donÕt know how it works, itÕs very confusing. And we were also sticking to the most regimented form of it. 
Judd: Were you doing theater in college? 
John: No, because once I could do comedy, I was like, I donÕt want to be in The Importance of Being Earnest. 
Judd: What was the dream in college? Was it to go write for Conan, or was it to start doing stand-up? 
John: I think the surprise to me was I didnÕt realize how much IÕd like stand-up. IÕd gotten so into Conan, The Simpsons, The Ben Stiller Show, SNLÑall that stuff, so I was like, Okay, thereÕs TV writing. I was like, Wow, Buck Henry had a cool career. 
Judd: ThatÕs such a nerd connection we have, as a guy who watched Buck Henry when SNL was airing in the seventies. Even as a little kid, at nine, I was attracted to Buck Henry more than Burt Reynolds hosting. 
John: IÕd seen a lot of those best of Õ75, best of Õ76 videos. Then I remember seeing him in The Graduate, and I remember seeing that he wrote The Graduate. So, he was a writer, but he also did things. Conan is a writer who then they brought on camera. 
Judd: ThatÕs such a specific track. When I was a kid, I wasnÕt aware that this was a career you could choose. Like: Can you do all these things? Can you write variety, can you write a movie? Can you do stand-up, can you act in something? 
John: Would you be as happy writing and seeing your joke on the Golden Globes as you would performing it? And kind of, yes. Because IÕm like, 
ThatÕs mine. 
Judd: My first job was writing the Grammys. And having Shandling do a joke or two. When somebody walked onstage and performed what I wrote backstage, I was so happy. I had no friends who wanted to do that. I was the only one who was willing to write jokes for other people. 
John: That reminds me of when I was at camp in Minnesota; it was called Camp Lincoln. It was on a lake, and the girlsÕ camp was through the woods. My brother was also at the camp, and he was older. He finished reading [Bob WoodwardÕs Belushi biography,] Wired, and he says to me, ÒRead this. YouÕd like this. ItÕs about John Belushi. Read it.Ó I remember readingÑat some point in the book, he says something like, ÒI was a sarcastic guy on the back wall whoÕd lob some good lines into the scene and then Belushi came in and he was like a tornado.Ó I remember thinking, at eleven, IÕll never be a tornado. IÕll definitely be the guy on the back wall lobbing in sarcastic comments. 
Judd: What does it feel like now when thereÕs an argument that you have evolved into the Belushi guy? 
John: I feel like itÕs so nice that everyoneÕs watching me do this. But I worry that in the back of their minds, they know IÕm just a writer and that someone could be saying this better. 
Judd: ThatÕs not how people think of you. The last few years of your career is a rare thing in comedy, where youÕre recognized purely on the quality of the work alone. 
John: ThatÕs very nice to hear. It is really nice when people are like, ÒI like this joke of yours,Ó or, ÒI like this story of yours,Ó the way theyÕd say, ÒI like this song.Ó I like that you heard the material first, then later connected that that was me. 
Judd: How did it feel to have already done so many different things in comedy, and then have your stand-up explode, and the Broadway play explode? 
John: Even on Broadway, I was like, Nick [Kroll]Õs an actor and a writer, and IÕm a writer, but IÕm the only one who can do this character. With Oh, Hello, IÕm like, I should do it because I donÕt want anyone to mess up the other characters. 
Judd: Would you say you saw any of this coming? 
John: The only time I felt it was when I saw the Comedian documentary, and at the end, SeinfeldÕs doing a two-thousand-something-person theater or something. I remember thinking, I want that. Like, I have a hunger, an ambition for that. And when I was onstage in St. Louis last fall, and it was like a three-thousand-seater, it was mid-show when I thought to myself, Well, this is bigger than anything I ever anticipated. WeÕre in St. Louis, and this is sold out. And I thought to myself, I have no need for this to get any bigger. This is it. Not in a ÒIÕm doneÓ way, but, like, Wow, this is the thing I fantasized about and it is actually happening. Wow, I get to do a theater. It doesnÕt go beyond that, I donÕt think. Or Bill Hader telling a reporter, ÒThe guy that writes those sketches is John Mulaney.Ó That meant as much to me as anything. 
Judd: Bill was always really good about that. 
John: He was extremely good. So, I had a rare case actually, now that I say it out loud. With Saturday Night Live, there was already some adulation, because people knew some of the things I was writing, mainly because Bill was extremely kind about sharing credit. 
Judd: In this moment, when you have more options, where does your brain go in terms of what you want to do next? What is that conversation like in your head? 
John: ThereÕs a natural self-protective feeling of, Go away for a while, so that people arenÕt sick of you. You know when the goods are gone. IÕm not going to be asked to host the Oscars, but letÕs say they did. IÕd be like, ÒI donÕt know if I have that this month.Ó It might actually be modesty, but itÕs probably not. ItÕs some instinct of, YouÕve been around too much. Back off. 
Judd: In stand-up, backing off still means writing. 
John: One issue I do have is I always need a specific role model, and as a pure stand-up, I look at someone like George Carlin, and IÕm like, ThatÕs the life of a king. However, I also want to be able to write a movie for Fred Armisen and Bill Hader, or I want to be a writer on Documentary Now! I guess you have to tell me, how do you also keep up with writing and learning about how to do stuff behind the camera? 
Judd: Did you see the Quincy Jones documentary? I feel like thatÕs a version of it. IÕm a trumpet player. Maybe I should also lead the band? Okay. Maybe I should be an arranger? Maybe IÕll produce other people? Maybe I should write stories for movies? Maybe I should produce the movies? Maybe I should produce a miniseries? ThereÕs that need to keep looking for spaces, creatively. 
John: IÕm not Quincy Jones, but I really identified with that. I feel like my generation was told that anyone would have wanted to be Johnny Carson, to be a talk show host, where thatÕs your job forever. But I think everyone my age is kind of like, correctly or incorrectly, Well, if you take that job, itÕs that for life. And thereÕs this weird hesitancy about a fucking golden job. 
Judd: My trajectory is slightly different because I started doing stand-up too young. I was seventeen when I started, but I left college by nineteen to do it full time. I lacked a vision or a style, I didnÕt really have that much charisma, and I was not about to reinvent the form like some of my friends were. When it came to stand-up, I thought, I donÕt think IÕm going to be doing what theyÕre doing. And that made me open to doing everything else. I could write RoseanneÕs act with her because I was giving up what my primary dream was, because I felt like the universe was telling me, ÒYou can do all this other stuff. Maybe youÕll write a movie.Ó 
John: Did you look at any of the very successful comicsÑlike Robert Klein, George Carlin, Tom DreesenÑand think, Maybe I could do that? 
Judd: It was Bill Maher for me, before Politically Incorrect. I thought, IÕm like a half-assed Bill Maher. I thought that would be the bar for me. But then I realized that when I sat with Jim Carrey kicking around jokes, and he did them, he would murder, and my confidence as a writer built. Was it like that for you, when you wrote sketches for Hader? 
John: Yeah, writing for Bill and Fred was like writing for Jimi Hendrix. 
Judd: I always enjoy peopleÕs later years at Saturday Night Live. Because I feel like in the early yearsÑand IÕve tracked this with a bunch of people, but most recently with Bill and with PeteÑtheyÕre trying to make a name for themselves, but then they start getting on too much, and they get a little burnt out. And there are a few years where they lose all anxiety about the show, and they hit a higher plane of fun. 
John: Where it dawns on you. ItÕs been either intimated to you or you have been explicitly told that you can perform on this live network show every week for as long as you want, and it suddenly dawns on everyone, like, ÒOh, we have this amazing platform.Ó 
Judd: ItÕs the same with stand-up when it all comes together, where you think 
IÕm not nervous on the stand-up stage anymore. I get what this is. IÕm enjoying it. When you were on SNL, what did you think about writing for the show, rather than performing on it? 
John: It sounds disingenuous, how much I try to convince people that I did not want to be in the cast because of how rewarding it was to be a writer. One of my biggest takeaways is that this is not something people will ever believe. If youÕre lucky enough to be there and feel the comfort and feel that you have a little bit of agency, that amount of freedom and fun was so great, and how much you learn about television is so insane that I find myself overexplaining when people ask, ÒWere you bummed you werenÕt in the cast?ÓÑto the point that it sounds like IÕm lying and IÕve just convinced myself it was a better existence. But I genuinely think, if not better, it was unparalleled. 
Judd: I believe everybody has a different experience. For some people, theyÕre fitting into the show machinery well, and so, their whole experience is very gratifying. For other people, itÕs the most painful time. 
John: I know. And I went in thinking it would be the most painful time ever. Because when I was hired, it was not in my plan. I was doing Best Week Ever a lot. I was doing stand-up, and itÕs kind of fun to be on TV every week. I remember this girl didnÕt want to go out with me, and I was taking the L train home to Brooklyn, and I thought to myself, At least IÕm on TV tonight. I was not covetous of Letterman or SNL, because I thought they were unhappy, cold environments. 
Judd: How did you get on SNL; was it a sketch packet? 
John: No. I auditioned. 
Judd: You auditioned to be in the cast and got hired as a writer? 
John: Yeah. Kroll and I were in L.A., pitching a movie with Tracy Morgan, who had left SNL already. This is the summer of 2008. I got a call to audition for the show, and since I was in L.A., they thought I lived in L.A., so they gave me a hotel. I lived in Brooklyn, but I took the hotel. And T. J. Miller, Nick Kroll, Bobby Moynihan, Donald Glover, Ellie KemperÑwe were all auditioning. 
Judd: ThatÕs a strong run of people. 
John: And I liked my audition. It was stand-up I was already doing that had some characters in it. I was like, IÕm not going to get this, but I think what IÕm putting up is funny, and IÕll get to tell the story of the one time I auditioned for Lorne Michaels. And I auditioned, and it went really well. Everyone beforehand was like, ÒThey donÕt laugh, just so you know,Ó and then they laughed a lot. Then Seth [Meyers] called me and hired me as a writer, and I remember thinking, I donÕt want this to end. It was so exciting to audition, I want this high to continue. IÕd already written for Important Things with Demetri Martin and a show for Comedy Central, but I did go into SNL being like, This is going to be hard. This is going to be a cold year. 
Judd: What is a highlight of Oh, Hello being on Broadway? 
John: Nights that itÕd be going great, IÕd just take it in, and this is, well, like IÕm on the moon. ItÕs working so well, and weÕre not supposed to be there. Quite literally. ThatÕs not modestyÑitÕs very hard to get a Broadway theater. 
Judd: Did you ever think thatÕs where it might ultimately land? 
John: The joke was always, ÒWhat are you going to do next?Ó and we said, ÒOh, Hello on Broadway.Ó Then we were like, We should do that. We were happy doing Off-Broadway while talking about how we should be on Broadway. Then, when we were given a Broadway theater, we knew it was fortunate, we knew the timing was great. But I donÕt think we fully appreciated how many shows with much bigger names than ours are in Connecticut or working out in DC and wonÕt get one of the super-theaters. I remember thinking, I genuinely donÕt know how we got here, and I also donÕt know, when we leave, if we can get back. 
Judd: IÕve felt that way, too. I had Freaks and Geeks, I had The Stiller Show, and you think itÕs crazy that it all has happened. The weird, nerdy kid who dreamed of hanging out with Rick Moranis. But then again, a lot of my career has been motivated by revenge, just to show people that they were wrong about something. You hated Freaks and Geeks? Then IÕm going to make Seth Rogen a star. But the main thing that I wanted to ask you about your style of joke writing, because it seems like youÕre doing two different things: YouÕre doing modern observational comedy, but then youÕre also doing very personal stories. You go deep. 
John: I always have. It is very funny for me to drop extremely personal bombs on people in what is otherwise an upbeat, ÒDonÕt you love entertainment?Ó tone. 
Judd: How did you land on, like, your general vibe and style? 
John: I think opening for Birbiglia for a couple of years, but especially over this one thirty-day stretch on a college tour. He did this campus tour, and it was thirty days on a bus. It was like a boot camp. 
Judd: Watching Mike and doing the show? 
John: I had a phase in the beginning where I so wanted to be a comedian, but I was not aggressively booking myself every night. I wanted to have already done it. I was like, Why arenÕt I already a comedian? I donÕt like this middle stuff. So, that stretch, with thirty straight days, and then followed by a dozen other daysÑyou have a show every night, sometimes two shows a night, and youÕre the emcee, and you have to be good. Which Mike actually said to me, and I still credit him for it. He was like, ÒIf I take you on this tour, by the way, you have to be good.Ó 
Judd: Now youÕre in a place where there are people who can do impressions of you. You have a distinct cadence in the way you express yourself, but how long did it take you to figure out who you are and what was funny about you? 
John: It is a presentation when youÕre a comedian. I wore a suit for the first time at [the] Helium [Comedy Club], in Philadelphia, in 2012. IÕd just been at the Laughing Skull [Lounge], in Atlanta, and I was wearing a plaid button-down shirt and jeans, and there were, like, four openers. We all were twenty-six, and we all dressed the same way. The audience was also wearing plaid button-down shirts and jeans, and I remember thinking onstage, This isnÕt right. So, I was like, I think IÕll wear a suit for this special. IÕd never worn one onstage, and I wore one to Helium. It sounds dumb, but that was it. IÕm now showing you what IÕve always wanted to be, which is Desi Arnaz. 
Judd: What does Desi Arnaz mean to you? 
John: On the surface level, that meant to me that you get to be home all day when you do the show at night. But I was like, ÒBoy, to do comedy in a suit and tie in New York City. If only.Ó And then I thought, Well, why donÕt you do that? 
Judd: ThatÕs a very Seinfeld thing, too. You suit up for the job, you take this seriously. Did anyone intimidate you as a comic when you first were dipping your toe in the scene? Who made you feel like, Wow, I donÕt know if I can ever be as good as that person? 
John: A lot of people. ThereÕs a guy at the Strip who had a long bit where he impersonated Willy Wonka yelling at the kids that got [into the] fizzy, lifting drinks. And I just remember it destroying at the Strip after IÕd eaten shit there. So, anyone who was doing well, it was just something like, Goddamn, how did they do that? How did they walk up and look like they were in charge? 
Judd: How many years did it take for you to feel confident up there? 
John: I would say IÕm still not fully confident yet. Lately, IÕve been like, Does this persona have to change? Do I want it to change? Will people get bored of it? Or is it like, ÒIÕll be here if you need me.Ó IÕve been reading Born to Run, Bruce SpringsteenÕs book, because IÕve been thinking about this a lot lately. IÕm getting past ÒTunnel of Love,Ó when heÕs very wealthy and heÕs at a loss, and he said, ÒI didnÕt want to be this rich man in a poor manÕs shirt.Ó 
Judd: The guy who could say, I think IÕm the person that needs to make that album about 9/11. Imagine if you really believe that in your soul. I think IÕm the person who can turn this into art and help people somehow, and he completely pulls it off. 
John: Or [SpringsteenÕs album] Wrecking Ball. That is a multimillionaire singing about the recession, and he means it. 
Judd: I have a pure technical question for you. 
John: Of course. 
Judd: IÕve seen you do shows where you come with your notebooks, because a lot of your bits are longer, and theyÕre intricately woven. IÕm always interested in how much time people spend at a typewriter or thinking through the whole thing. How much time in your act, when youÕre crafting a story, will you sit and try to take a run, on paper, at the whole story? 
John: IÕve almost never done that. When you do Live at Gotham or other TV things, they would ask for a full transcript and were writing out the jokes word by word in paragraph form. I remember, I was like, This is so not funny, seeing it written out. 
Judd: But do you go onstage and babble? If youÕre doing the Trump horse bit, how much do you have when you first get onstage? 
John: I at least have to have A and Z. 
Judd: What are the bits that youÕre most proud of? 
John: What was meaningful to me was the end of that New in Town special, which was a story about when I tried to get Xanax from a walk-in clinic across the street from the office that Pete Holmes and I shared. It was forty-five minutes of my life that was just humiliating, and then I collapsed because they had drawn blood and I hadnÕt eaten all day. Also, what I didnÕt say in that story is when I walked out of that clinic ashen from having fainted, I bumped into someone, and it was Elijah Wood. I went right onstage and told that story, and it did okay, but I was still literally sweaty from fainting. 
Judd: IÕve noticed that when IÕm writing other things, like a movie, I have to shut down the part of my brain thatÕs paying attention to things to see if they could be stand-up material. Is that a part of you thatÕs always on? 
John: I feel very uncomedic when IÕm dragging my bulldog down the street in thirty-degree weather, and she wants to walk, but I just need her to pee. Genuinely, IÕm sometimes amazed at how little a sense of humor I have about life. 

KEVIN HART 
May 2021 
Judd: People donÕt know our vast history. I met you when you were nineteen or twenty years old. Your manager, Dave Becky, said, ÒYou know who you need to know? Kevin Hart.Ó And youÕre just a young man. YouÕre a child. YouÕre not drinking age at this point. I was doing a television pilot called North Hollywood, about a bunch of people who live outside of Hollywood trying to make it in show business. It was Amy Poehler, you, Jason Segel, January Jones, Judge Reinhold, and in a small part, Adam McKay. And this was your first foray to Hollywood to do a TV pilot. 
Kevin: I remember when Dave first brought it up, and he was telling me, ÒLook, thereÕs a guy. HeÕs the hot guy in town right now. One of the best creators/producers. I feel like heÕs just the guy, and heÕs going to be the guy for a long time.Ó And I was at that point in my career where any information was the most amazing information that you can receive. If somebody chose an opportunity, everything was the opportunity. This is gonna be the one, this is gonna launch me. So, when he told me that he had a conversation with you, and it was about putting myself on tape for you to see, that became my moment to come to L.A. It ended up being the best thing ever. It was the best opportunity with the best group of comedic actors and actresses. And it came so long ago, but so early in my career, that I just wish it [had] worked out. But then, at the same time, IÕm glad it didnÕt. And not because of where I am now. IÕm glad because I stayed in your Rolodex, right? Our relationship continued. And you were very persistent in the opportunities that you were bringing to me. What I realized later on is that whatÕs going to be, will be, but if you surround yourself with the people who truly are about the work, then the work is going to find you, and youÕre going to find it. I became a fan of yours because you really did the work. You showed me the benefit of sitting down, creating, writing, and then seeing that material come to life. I got to watch you do that from the front seat. 
Judd: I saw it as my own personal failure that I knew every great person in comedy that deserved to be a star, put them in the same pilot, and I couldnÕt get it picked up. It was all single-camera, and there was this moment where Jason SegelÕs character had an audition, and he was nervous about it, and heÕs dry-heaving into the toilet. And while weÕre shooting it, because heÕs fake dry-heaving, he starts bleeding out of his nose. And itÕs gushing blood. I find it so funny, so we donÕt cut, and weÕre like, ÒLetÕs shoot this scene with real blood gushing out of his nose.Ó And then I hand it into ABC, thinking they would find this hilarious, and they did not find it funny. 
Kevin: Horrible. It was a horror film in there. 
Judd: And your persona on the show was a young actor who had money because he was on a beer commercial. 
Kevin: IÕll never forget it. A beer commercial. That was the best big beer commercial. 
Judd: And you kept saying, ÒIÕm going to be the next Chris Tucker because you canÕt understand him, but you can understand me.Ó And then you talked about how you wanted to be in the next Beverly Hills Cop, and itÕd be you and Eddie Murphy. And heÕd be like Scooby-Doo, and youÕd be like Scrappy-Doo.
