Kevin: It was so amazing, because itÕs so aligned with my trajectory, if you look at my success and where itÕs come from. Me saying, IÕm gonna be the next Chris Tucker, I want to be as big as Chris, and IÕm going to, and hereÕs why, it was really what I thought and believed would happen. And in that show, you gave me an opportunity to echo my real feelings. 
Judd: What was interesting for me is that, back then, you were fresh from New York and Philly, and you had this belief in yourself, which was comically gigantic. It made us laugh how sure you were that you were going to make it. 
Kevin: I truly believed I could accomplish that and get to these places, because I knew that once the opportunity came, there was no world where I was going to drop the ball. There was no place where I felt like, now that IÕve gotten an opportunity, IÕm not going to make the most or make the best of it. It was always, IÕm gonna knock this out of the park because IÕm ready and IÕm prepared. I know whatÕs going to happen once I get that opportunity. And once IÕm in position to go full steam ahead, IÕm not going to look back. 
Because I believed that I put the work in, and I believed that I was really good at my craft. That was a real feeling. And thatÕs what ended up happening. 
Judd: I remember that you would have outfits and call them sets, like, ÒYou like my set? IsnÕt this a good set?Ó And we found it funny that your pants and your shirt have been thought through in advance. As schlubby guys, we didnÕt understand that concept. But you lived with Jason Segel. I asked you to live with him, just so that you guys could figure out what your comedic rapport would be. 
Kevin: What I love about Jason and myself staying together is that, when it was presented, it was, ÒHey I want you guys to get to know each other, spend some time together before we start shooting.Ó Looking back at it now, I couldnÕt believe I had all of these people around me in the same space. I had no idea who I was around: I had Seth Rogen, I had Jason, I had James Franco, January Jones. We hung out in this genius comedy bubble without even realizing it. Jason was always creating, and he had bought a new camera. He was shooting sketches before sketches were a thing to shoot, before social media, before YouTube. Jason was just shooting and directing his own sketches. And we were doing them from the house, because he was excited about the new camera that he bought. That was not only the space that I was in, but where I learned what would ultimately help further my career. I thought, This is what the people who want to get here do, Kevin. YouÕve got to learn to do this as well. And thatÕs why Jason and I hit it off. We had a great relationship, man. I still owe him money, Judd. 
Judd: IÕm sure you do. WerenÕt you saying that since you had nothing to offer him in the relationship, you would make him breakfast? 
Kevin: Yeah, I made him breakfast. Pancakes, eggs. Jason would always say, ÒKevin, you are so nice.Ó And I was thinking, Well, thatÕs all I can be, Jason. I have nothing else to do. I got no money to give you. So, I need to be a nice guy and show you that I appreciate this. And when I finally got some money, I remember writing a check, and I had, like, a great presentation. I was giving him the check to say, ÒThank you for letting me stay there and covering these things.Ó And he wouldnÕt take my money. 
Judd: When youÕre young, and you get your first show, you really feel like, This is it, IÕm about to make some money. My life is about to change. They tell you how much youÕre going to make every week when you get a TV show, when you sign the contractÑsay, twenty grand an episode. But then it doesnÕt happen; the pilot doesnÕt get picked up. Then youÕre back in the clubs. What was that like? 
Kevin: ItÕs tough, because you get thrown a vision of what the life is, or what it can be. And then you go, Wow, itÕs over. It didnÕt get picked up. Now I have to go backward and go back to the road. Back to the life of the struggling comedian. And thatÕs a hard transfer. ItÕs not until you get a firm understanding of the business that youÕre able to process the back-and-forth. 
Judd: Was that a depressing period? 
Kevin: Yeah. But I got up, and I was like, This is it. This is where I need to be. And when the show wasnÕt picked up, I went back home to the East Coast, but I was like, I canÕt come back here after seeing what I saw there. So, I made the decision to move to Los Angeles, even though there wasnÕt a plan in place. There wasnÕt a blueprint laid out of how I was going to pay these bills or where the money was going to come from. I had a little bit of money from that pilot and a little bit of money from a deal that I got at Montreal, and I was like, IÕm bored and IÕm moving to Los Angeles. IÕll figure it out. I put all my eggs in that basket. And thank God, I figured it out. But I just said, I canÕt see what I saw and not chase that. 
Judd: There are people who are really funny, and then there are certain people who will just sit alone in a room and write a pilot or write a movie, and they have a vision to take it to the next level. 
Kevin: I think you need to get a nice balance of the two, and I definitely developed that at a younger age. As a result, I was able to constantly stay in a good headspace. I never got rocked too far this way or too far that way. I stayed in a space of positivity, and itÕs because I saw high levels of negativity early on in my career. 
Judd: I feel bad when things donÕt happen. IÕm very empathetic, to a fault, for the life experience of actors and actresses and how the success or failures of projects affect their lives. I donÕt just move on, I have a kind of Jewish guilt about it, where I always think, Damn, it didnÕt work for them. So, I always was looking for ways to work with you at that time, and to not have destroyed your life. I felt that way with all the Freaks and Geeks kids, because a lot of them relocated for that show. Seth moved here from Canada. It changed their lives, and then, suddenly, the show is gone. But then we worked together again on The 40-Year-Old Virgin. And you came in and did a scene for that movie, too. 
Kevin: That was a defining moment for our relationship. ThereÕs this expectation that youÕll get an opportunity because you just know someone, and with The 40-Year-Old Virgin, I auditioned for the part that Romany Malco got. I was like, Wow, this is the one. Judd is going to give me this part. IÕm going to knock this audition out. I remember when Romany got it, I was like, Dang. I lost out. Shit, I really wanted it. But I instantly understood that Romany was probably better for that part than me. I couldnÕt wait to see RomanyÕs performance, because thereÕs something IÕm either going to learn or see, but ultimately, IÕm going to know that he had something that I didnÕt have. Later on, you guys are about to go to production and film, and you just call me randomly to do a scene. That made everything okay, and this is why the positive mindset has always worked for me, because things always worked themselves out. That scene ended up doing so much for me that it was ridiculous. Romany Malco destroyed that part in 40-Year-Old Virgin. He was a more polished actor, and he had things that I didnÕt have yet at that young point of my career. I said, I need to take some acting classes. I need to make sure that IÕm ready for these opportunities when they come. But this all came from the school of Judd. It all came from being underneath that umbrella that you knew you held for all of us. 
Judd: That scene was also such a natural space for you to be yourself and show the world, ÒThis is how Kevin Hart works in a movie.Ó We did a DVD of the extended version of the movie, and I added like seventeen minutes, which you really shouldnÕt do. But there were so many funny things that were too short. So, I extended your fight with Romany by, like, another minute, and most people remember that version of it. 
Kevin: ThatÕs the way it floats around in social media. 
Judd: I was reading your book, and you talked about when you would go into auditions, and you used to think, It doesnÕt matter if I get this or not. IÕm going to be so appealing that even if I donÕt get it, these people are going to like me, and itÕs going to pay off at some point. I think for most young actors and actresses, those auditions are so terrifying. They want it so badly that they melt down in the room a fair amount of the time. 
Kevin: YouÕre going to get an opportunity to be brought up in conversation again, and itÕs your choice as to how that conversation goes. Some people approach every audition as a possible job, and thatÕs great; you should. But my approach was, I want to be the best that I can be. IÕm going to show them who I am. I want to be likable, and I want to give a good read. And regardless of whether or not I got that part, when I leave, theyÕre going to go, ÒThat guy was cool, I like that guy.Ó ThatÕs it for me, because eventually, when you see me again, youÕre going to remember that moment, and that moment is going to act as a motivating factor to make the next moment happen. Sometimes, you got to lay that groundwork, and it can be a lot. You might have to do a shitload of undercoats before you actually apply the paint job, but those undercoats are going to make that paint job pop. ThatÕs how I approached my auditions, and thatÕs how I approach my career to this day: IÕm still the people person, IÕm still the guy who will shake all the hands. And thatÕs worked for me; itÕs been very beneficial thus far. 
Judd: I remember when we first met, you talked about knowing Jay-Z a little bit. What was that relationship like? YouÕve said before that he inspired you to not just think of yourself as an actor trying to get a gig, but as more an actor trying to build a business. You were the first person I met that had that type of a mentor. 
Kevin: Hova, heÕs my guy. HeÕs who I go to when I have real questions, or when IÕve got an idea and IÕm having trouble figuring out how to make it a reality. I remember our conversations back then were about where he was versus where he was going to go, and there was never a moment where he looked at himself and said, This is all good right here, IÕm great. It was always the mindset of the greatest, always the mindset of taking over, of changing the way this genre of music is identified. It was always the mindset of CEO instead of coworker. I somehow managed to duplicate his mindset and his persistence. When youÕve got a good mentor or adviser, youÕre able to dissect and process their wisdom, and use it how you feel it best fits or suits you. And thatÕs what he does for me until this day. 
Judd: You made a choice to be that mentor to anybody who wants to read your books or listen to you talk. You encourage them to think that they can be the greatest, they can be the next Jay-Z or Walt Disney. 
Kevin: I donÕt think we give enough information to each other, and I truly believe in sharing information. For us, as older people, weÕve got to equip these younger people to be better than us, to be able to surpass what weÕve done. The best way for the world to get better is to give the people who are coming up behind us an opportunity to make it better. I donÕt take that lightly. 
Judd: You have one of those rare minds that allows you to switch gears to funny Kevin, or creative Kevin, and you can also switch to inspirational or business mode. I find the reason why a lot of comedians donÕt get very far is because they might be hilarious, but they do not have that business acumen. ItÕs rare that someone can switch from comedy to work on a clothing line, or self-help books. Most people canÕt do both. I find when I have business calls, my comedy mind shuts down. How do you look at that for yourself in terms of how you organize your day, or your mind? 
Kevin: For me, the best thing is the merging of those distinct worlds. I try my best to create a complete circleÑall of those things have to connect somehow. My business has to complement the things underneath it, which ultimately should be driven by my personality. Regardless of the venture, regardless of the project, regardless of the task at hand, my personality should never disappear, because whatever it is that IÕm offering, I should be offering it as myself. Merging those things so that IÕm not always tugging and warring with myself has seemed to work. 
Judd: As someone whoÕs so busy and takes so much on, what has the experience of COVID, of this Òchill-outÓ moment weÕve all been forced into Ñwhat has that been like for you? 
Kevin: ItÕs amazingly positive. IÕm happy that I get to spend more time with my kids, and it makes you understand and appreciate the value of quality time. You think you know your kids, your wife, and your household, but when youÕre able to be there every single day, all day, you discover more. And you love more; you love harder. Yes, I still love my job, and IÕm looking forward to getting back to doing what I love, but IÕm never going to overlook the high level of love that was developed through this time, when we were forced to sit down and all be together. It came right on time for myself, my wife, and my kids. So, IÕm extremely thankful. 

LIN-MANUEL MIRANDA 
August 2020 
Judd: My daughter Iris wanted you to know that she sang ÒA Very Good Day,Ó from Working: The Musical, in high school last year. 
Lin-Manuel: ItÕs a very heartbreaking song. The songs that I wrote for that were kind of my In the Heights methadone, because I spent my twenties writing In the Heights. I started writing it when I was nineteen years old, for a college project, and then we opened on Broadway when I was twenty-eight. IÕll never make a leap that big again. I went from substitute teacher to Broadway composer. I donÕt have another leap that big in me. 
Judd: Is that how you were making money, as a substitute teacher? 
Lin-Manuel: I started teaching seventh-grade English at my old high school. They offered me a full-time position, and I got scared of the Mr. HollandÕs Opus version of my life, because I really liked it. I enjoy teaching. But I said, No, I can make rent by subbing, and then I have more time to write. 
Judd: Were you going out on acting auditions and trying to have that life as well? 
Lin-Manuel: I went on lots of voiceover auditions and acting auditions, and the only thing I booked was The Sopranos, as a bellboy; I say ÒI donÕt knowÓ twice. IÕm so green you can see me look down at my mark. Watch it if you get a chance, because now people are like ÒLin-ManuelÕs cameo on The Sopranos!Ó It wasnÕt a cameo. I wasnÕt even in the union yet. 
Judd: Who were you in the scene with? 
Lin-Manuel: Gandolfini and Paulie. My one story about Gandolfini was that he stayed and did his sides even though it was the end of the night. He had no need to do that. He stayed and did the scene for the scared-shitless Puerto Rican kid in the bellhop outfit. The other thing I remember was that he sat on the steps of his trailer the whole time. He was never locked up in this trailer; he was just out, and people would talk to him. 
Judd: ItÕs a powerful lesson as a young person. ÒOh, you respect everybody, you care about the work, youÕre not playing star.Ó ItÕs weirdly meaningful to observe one of the greats on a set. YouÕre also the leader of a giant crew when you do these shows. When I worked as a producer on The Ben Stiller Show, I didnÕt know how to do it at all. I thought, IÕm so young. His whole crew is older than me. They must hate my guts. I was just a terrified boss, and I would read, like, business books in my office, like [The] Seven Habits of Highly Effective People. I didnÕt really know how to be a leader of a hundred people. How was that, for you to realize thatÕs your role? 
Lin-Manuel: I think the biggest gift I had is [theater director] Tommy Kail, because IÕve gone through it twice with him. With Heights, we did so many readings and workshops with the same actors over and over, for no money, in the basement of the bookshop, that it was very easy for the actors to see me as just a cast member, and I disappear for rewrites. I was also such a kid that we were all kind of in it together, and because IÕm the writer but I donÕt run a department, I donÕt really hire or fire people. I weigh in on casting, but itÕs TommyÕs show. So, I had the best position, that I got to play with everybody. I do have to say it was different on Hamilton. I think I was expecting it to be more ÒHey, itÕs just me, IÕm one of you guys,Ó but because Hamilton became this thing, I felt a little more isolated. I was isolated by the praise. The cast was fantastic and lovely, but it wasnÕt the same. We were all strapped to this rocket ship together, hanging on for dear life. 
Judd: I always remember the first time we metÑthis was maybe five days before the official opening of Hamilton on Broadway. That energy right before had that feeling of ItÕs working. In a few days people will see it, but weÕre still scared shitless. What is it like for you right before? Are you in a meltdown state? Are you just riding it? 
Lin-Manuel: YouÕre just trying to stay on top of it. The weird thing about the show was that it solved its own problems. The two and a half hours where youÕre performing is so intense that it actually forces you to shut out the world. The thing that is causing you the most anxiety is your moment of Zen, because if you space out onstage, the turntable will throw you into the pit, or you will get hit with a chair, or a dancer will kick you in the face. These are all things that happened. The nerves are around the whole two and a half hours youÕre doing it, because itÕs a meal. ItÕs a fourteen-course meal. You live the whole life. ItÕs a joy, and it takes everything out of you to do it, and that, I think, is also what kept us sane and kept our energy, because you also have to pace yourself for another two and a half hours living life. 
Judd: As a comedian, you can change all the words in any moment, so thereÕs this tension of, Am I going to run the act or completely bail and go into free-flow mode, or do both? Whereas, you do improvÑand you do rapÑand you cannot miss a beat. 
Lin-Manuel: I find that the tracks in my brainÑthe one that knows all the words to Hamilton and the one that is open and receptive and listening to suggestionsÑare really different. So, the few times IÕve had to improvise within Hamilton, I feel like I lose a year off my life. We did a performance in Puerto Rico, and there was a young lady in the fourth row, and she was filming, and this is a performance that was just for the university students. So, IÕm coming down the steps in that opening number, and I just see this, and IÕm like, All right, IÕm not gonna say shit. ItÕll probably go away after the opening number. Then, I come around with Aaron Burr, into the bar, and itÕs still there, and IÕm getting distracted because I also know if it were any other show, thereÕd be a moment where I could go offstage and tell the stage manager, ÒHey, someoneÕs filming in the fourth row.Ó ThatÕs how I handle it ninety-nine percent of the time, but Hamilton doesnÕt leave the stage. 
Judd: So, you go full Patti LuPone. 
Lin-Manuel: But I donÕt want to stop the show, so I think up, ÒI got a scholarship to KingÕs College / I probably shouldnÕt brag but, dang I amaze and astonish / Hey, lady filming in the fourth row, please stop it / I got a holler just to be heard / Every word I drop knowledgeÉÓ and I just kept going. I was so covered in sweat just having to think of the line while the show was going on, whereas in the freestyle show, the whole thing is just being open and saying whatever comes into your head. On Hamilton, it was really hard switching gears. 
Judd: Jay Leno always talks about when heÕs onstage and heÕs running his set, sometimes his brain can split so much that he can count how many people are in the comedy club while having a good set. It almost becomes a meditation. 
Lin-Manuel: That happens over the course of the run. Camera spotting as an example, especially with the [Richard] Rodgers Theatre. ItÕs a very steeply raked house; I can see nineteen rows back. IÕll be doing the show, and IÕll tell the stage manager, ÒTenth row, five seats in.Ó 
Judd: Or Bryan Cranston is there that night. 
Lin-Manuel: ThatÕs the shit that bugs you out. I like to know beforehand, and IÕm not limited to the odd Tom HanksÑitÕs like, ÒYouÕre Robert Goulet,Ó or, ÒYouÕre Tony Randall.Ó So, IÕd like to know in advance. The hardest one was early at the Public: Busta Rhymes, front row. Honestly, no oneÕs opinion means more to me than Busta RhymesÕs. Everyone else can go fuck themselves. I was like, DonÕt look at Busta Rhymes. DonÕt look at Busta Rhymes. And heÕs a big guy, and heÕs in the front row. So, I looked up to not look at him, and Mandy Patinkin was sitting above, who you could argue is the Busta Rhymes of musical theater. 
Judd: In my house, he is. Do you feel that your improv skills, your freestyle skills, affect your ability to get into flow as a playwright and as a songwriter? 
Lin-Manuel: For me, freestyle is the ultimate version of being present. And what you want in your characters, when theyÕre talking to each other, is to be present, speaking in a way that feels spontaneous and feels real. So, absolutely, I think itÕs made my writing better because IÕm always chasing the most honest impulse. ItÕs also the ultimate excuse against writerÕs block Ñyou canÕt lie to yourself and say, I have writerÕs block, when youÕve just done a ninety-minute show based on the verb gesticulate, or whatever the fuck the main audience said that night. You made a show out of nothing last night, so get to the page and donÕt lie to yourself. It cuts out my internal bullshit and excuses not to write, because I know that if IÕm in the right setting, I can just generate it. 
Judd: But you also make the choice to mainly be a songwriter who writes alone. 
Lin-Manuel: I always see the freestyling as exactly the opposite muscle group, because it is collaborative. ItÕs also your first best impulse. ThereÕs no time to be like, Wait, I have a better punch line. ItÕs gone. TrainÕs left. It allows you to honor that first impulse in a real way. Then I think they feed each other. It was really ideal during Hamilton, because I knew the moment I had that idea that it was going to take fucking forever to finish it. I was like, Oh, I have to now go do homework and learn the social studies I never learned and do research, which I didnÕt have to do on In the Heights, or any project to that extent. 
Judd: Did you like that world before, or did you just happen to stumble upon something that caught your interest? 
Lin-Manuel: I like biographies. I like dipping into someone elseÕs life. I think the only other really big historical biography IÕd read prior to HamiltonÕs was Team of Rivals. But even thatÑit was more the conceit than the historical biography, like, Oh, this guy hired the people who opposed him. 
Judd: So, it wasnÕt like, ÒLin loves Founding Fathers books.Ó 
Lin-Manuel: On the contrary, none of those stories were interesting to me. They were just iconography. What got the book off the shelf and into my hands was that I remembered that IÕd written a paper in eleventh grade, and I knew [HamiltonÕs] son died in a duel, and then he died in a duel in the same place three years later. I knew that more as a piece of trivia than anything else, and I was just like, How do you not see that coming? It felt like the most avoidable mistake of all time. 
Judd: I tried to read the Ron Chernow bookÑI didnÕt read all of itÑbut what kept coming up was how religious Hamilton was. A lot of it was this faith that God wouldnÕt do these things to him. 
Lin-Manuel: ItÕs interesting, because itÕs in his early writings as a teenager and then in his later writings, after heÕs experienced loss and death. I think itÕs something that was really drummed into him as a kid, and then he went and lived his life and came back to it. Which I think is how a lot of people experience religionÑall right, hereÕs the things youÕre supposed to learn, then you live your life and shit happens, and as youÕre on the back nine, you start, like, going, Oh, IÕm finding myself praying. I guess all that scaffolding my parents put in, itÕs still there. 
Judd: How many years was Hamilton from the first thought to Broadway? 
Lin-Manuel: I picked up the book in summer 2008. And then we opened summer 2015. 
Judd: When youÕre in an eight-year run of work, what makes you not lose your touch with what it is? Because this is your second show, right? 
Lin-Manuel: I also co-wrote Bring It On: The Musical. That was the miracle baby, because it was two composing teams, so it came together really quickly, and it was really fun and good. 
Judd: ItÕs a strange trajectory, for your first show to win the Tony, and then for Hamilton to be the same, but way bigger in terms of success. What do you think prepared you to not lose it both then and after? Because nothing has changed in terms of your work. It hasnÕt gone off the rails. What about your life or how you were raised allowed you to be able to handle this? 
Lin-Manuel: A couple of things. I think if the Hamilton-level success had happened on Heights, IÕd be a lot more fucked up, because I didnÕt really have my shit figured out yet. Vanessa, who is now my wife, and I were dating, but I donÕt know if the tsunami of attention would have hit me in the same way. By the time Hamilton happened, I was married and had my first kid. He was born two weeks before rehearsal started. 
Judd: Trying to get sleep is your main concern. 
Lin-Manuel: IÕm trying to get sleep. IÕm going home. IÕm going to the show, and IÕm eating by five-thirty P.M., so I donÕt burp during any of my reps. All of the stuff I learned on Heights. I used to try to do a matinee of Heights on a muffin and a cup of coffee, and then IÕm like, Why am I lightheaded? Because I didnÕt know anything about nutrition or maintaining my bodyÑI learned all that stuff on the first show, like, Okay, I become a monk for the time IÕm doing this run. This is the food that gets me through the show, and this is what I need to sleep. So, all of the other noise and the things that derail folks, like getting invited to the party and the famous people saying, ÒWe want to do this,Ó I had to say no to ninety percent of it, because I had a show the next day, or I had to sleep eight hours and I knew I was gonna wake up twice and change diapers. My family really saved my ass, because I think thatÕs how you lose it. 
Judd: I think IÕve seen the show five times, and it was fun to watch how it morphed. The first time I saw it, it hadnÕt opened officially. The next time I saw it, it was giant. It was more of a rock concert. Now theyÕre applauding when the song starts. 
Lin-Manuel: And then the audience starts knowing the words, which puts a different kind of pressure. Because you know youÕre gonna see that tweet about the line you fucked up that night. 
Judd: I think we saw one show where we thought, Lin looks fried. ItÕs still greatÑmost people donÕt noticeÑbut it was maybe eight months into the run. I went again the last week before you all left, and it seemed like the cast was luxuriating and enjoying the moment and present. Like, WeÕre probably not going to do this again. I felt the energy explode again. Is that what it felt like to you? 
Lin-Manuel: IÕm grateful that the streaming version that most of the world is seeing was like a week and a half before we left, because we know what weÕre doing. We know itÕs finite. So, itÕs the energy of savoring it, but itÕs also really dialed in. ThereÕs lots of puppy energy in previews, like, Let me try everything! I always laugh when I see dancers in the workshop space trying really crazy shit. I wander behind them, like, ÒEight shows a week, eight shows a week, eight shows a week.Ó I know youÕre showing Andy how many spins you can do, but youÕre going to have to do this a lot of times. 
Judd: I remember going to see The Book of Mormon very early and watching Josh Gad going crazy. Then, after six months, he could barely walk. His back might have been completely out. I remember him telling me right at the beginning of the run, ÒThis is a nightmare for me. Because, in my mind, I get really bored. ItÕs almost unbearable to do it so many times.Ó 
Lin-Manuel: ItÕs a hell of a thing. I can really stay lasered in, and Tommy makes fun of me because IÕll tell him the same story fifty times, including one memorable time where he was like, ÒThat happened to me. I told you that.Ó But he also said, ÒThatÕs why youÕre cut out for theater, because youÕll tell it like itÕs the first time.Ó 
Judd: Did you find that there was a giant adjustment, when the show ended, in terms of getting your work done? When people win an Oscar, they tend to make fewer movies after, because on some level they overthink it. One of the most important pieces of advice I ever got was from Ron Howard. He just said, ÒI donÕt look at any of my movies as more important than any other movie IÕve made. I just think thereÕs an art gallery with posters of all the movies I made, and I hope some of them are good.Ó ThatÕs how he framed it. YouÕre working with him now, right? 
Lin-Manuel: HeÕs so level-headed. The theater version of that advice is how [Harold] Prince would always say, ÒStart working on your next thing the day after opening night.Ó A couple of things happened after Hamilton, or during the end. I donÕt make any decisions without my wife. I canÕt be like, ÒIÕm going to do this, honey.Ó ThereÕs no world in which that happens. But the thing you need to know is my wifeÕs mom is Austrian, and her dad is Dominican, and she was born in Sweden. If it were up to her, we would live in every country in the world for a year until we die. I wrote a whole show about how I donÕt want to leave the block where my apartment is and where I can see the George Washington Bridge. So, thatÕs a big difference, and when that call came to do Mary Poppins Returns, it was exciting because it was, Oh, I can work for Rob Marshall and see how musical films work. It was not so much Mary Poppins as seeing the director of Chicago do what he does, and we get to live in London for seven months, with a kid whoÕs young enough that itÕs fine. So, that was the next big move. It was the balanceÑ whatÕs right for my family and What do I want to learn next? Because to go right to work on a new musical made no sense. Rodgers and Hammerstein, after Oklahoma opened, were like, ÒWe canÕt write another musical with ourselves; this thingÕs a fucking hit!Ó So, they went into producing. 
Judd: How did that affect the In the Heights movie? And youÕre doing tick, tickÉBoom! [the Jonathan Larson biopic]. 
Lin-Manuel: Those are the film schools I didnÕt pursue. I went to Wesleyan to study film, and then I fell into theater because it was a much more affordable option. I was not gonna be able to afford a senior film that gets into a festival. Whereas, with theater, you put up three hundred dollars for the lights and the costumes, and you can make as much theater as you want. I remember, from your first book, you talked about how your parentsÕ divorce was the thing that made you be like, No oneÕs minding the store. I gotta get my shit together. My version of that in college was I keenly felt my parentsÕ financial burden. Their thing was, ÒOur present to you is weÕre gonna work our ass off.Ó My dad quit his nonprofit job and, like, went into for-profit stuff, and my mom took on more patientsÑsheÕs a psychologistÑso that IÕd have as little debt as possible getting out of school. My sister had to do an extra semester of college, and when she came home and was like, ÒIÕm not going to graduate in four years,Ó I remember my mother bursting into tears at the table. I wrote a ton in college, because I was like, I canÕt just leave with a degree that says ÒTheaterÓ on it. That doesnÕt mean anything. I was just like, IÕm gonna leave with three scripts. My version of your epiphany was, My parents are killing themselves so I can be here. I felt that really keenly. 
Judd: I had the opposite of that. I needed six grand for tuition for the third semester of my sophomore year, and both my mom and my dad, who were divorced at the time, did not have six grand. So, I quit college. 
Lin-Manuel: So much of the plot of In the Heights is about that financial pressure and how no one preps you to be an adult on your own, and the culture shock if you go from being the star in your neighborhood to being one of many. All the stuff Nina goes throughÑitÕs all from that anxiety. 
Judd: The strange thing, as parents, is we realize that the thing that motivated us was this neurotic terror, so we have this insane, workaholic energy. Now we have kids, and we want them to have that fire, so how do you fuck them up in the right way? 
Lin-Manuel: The thing that I worry most about as a parent is empathy. To understand that the things you have are hard to come by for so many people, to have empathy for that and to understand and recognize the enormous privilege. Putting yourself in other peopleÕs shoesÑto me, thatÕs the magic bullet of existence. ItÕs what we do as writers: put ourselves in someone elseÕs life and then try to speak honestly from that place. ThatÕs the trickiest and hardest gift to give your kid. Have you ever read a book called My Name Is Asher Lev, by Chaim Potok? It was a book I was assigned in eighth grade, and itÕs actually the first musical I wrote. ItÕs the reason I started writing musicals, because we had to teach three chapters on it, and I decided to write a song for each chapter. Like, bad Les Mis a cappella. The whole point of the book is: This kid, whoÕs incredibly smart, so his father goes, ÒI donÕt know how he will understand the suffering of other people, so IÕm going to raise him in absolute silence.Ó ItÕs the most fucked-up thing, and it works. The kid is keenly aware of suffering and keenly aware of pain in himself and in others because his dad froze them out, and my terrible eighth-grade version was: ÒNow I can hear the silenceÉÓ And my eighth-grade teacher, whoÕs kind of the guy who changed my life, was like, ÒYou should be writing plays and not bullshitting in the back of my class.Ó 
Judd: IsnÕt that amazing? All it takes sometimes is just one person. 
Lin-Manuel: One person who is not a family member. We had a student-run theater group called Brick Prison, and he was like, ÒI donÕt know if Brick PrisonÕs ever done a musical. You should write one.Ó And that was it. 
Judd: An English teacher in eleventh grade for me. This teacher, who was not cool but a nice ladyÑand you would never think that would be the person that puts some seed into my head. 
Lin-Manuel: ItÕs about being seen, right? You hope that you are a little clever, or you hope that someone else sees it. 
Judd: I know, when I speak to a film class, IÕm not going to reach everyone. But in ten years, two of those kids are going to walk up to me randomly on the street having done something pretty cool, and theyÕll remember something I said. WhatÕs the main thing you think about as youÕre trying to communicate to a class of kids? 
Lin-Manuel: ThatÕs the reason IÕve only ever done two graduation speeches, because they take it out of me so hardcore. I remember being as hungover and terrified as IÕve ever been on my graduation day and knowing I really havenÕt packed enough, and IÕve got twenty-four hours to get my shit and fill an already marijuana-filled U-Haul truck with actual things and move to New York. I really try to put myself back there: WhatÕs real and whatÕs actionable? What can I say that will break through the fog of these kidsÕ self-regard? TheyÕve got their families behind them. I remember having my grandparents when they were still alive. My momÕs parents didnÕt show. 
TheyÕre from Jersey. My momÕs parents lived in New Jersey, and my dadÕs friends lived in Puerto Rico, but the Puerto Rico ones were the ones that showed up for everything. The other ones couldnÕt come from East Brunswick. 
Judd: Traffic that day. 
Lin-Manuel: Both the Wesleyan and the UPenn speech, I remember them really well. I was in the library the night before, until four in the morning, at Wesleyan, being like, I canÕt believe IÕm writing another fucking paper for Wesleyan, and at the last minute. ThatÕs how it felt to me. IÕm really pulling from a deep place. For UPenn, I felt, You have to dig personal if itÕs going to break through to this audience. Because theyÕre going through their own shit. 
Judd: You had a great story in that speech about dating someone and having to break up with them, and feeling guilty because you realized, through her lens, youÕre just the idiot she had to get out of the way to find the right guy and be happy. 
Lin-Manuel: She was studying abroad in the Dominican Republic. I wrote In the Heights while she was away, and when she came home, I couldnÕt stop cracking my shoulder. Like to the point where I was getting pain in my shoulder and my neck. It was a nervous tic. My mom looked up back doctors in New York magazine and took me to some guy who was one of those seers. I remember he had thick glasses and he looked at my shoulder for a second, and he went, ÒWell, thereÕs nothing wrong with your shoulder or your back, but there will be because you keep clicking it. You have a nervous tic. Is there anything stressful in your life?Ó And I burst into tears in his office that I broke up with my girlfriend that night and I went into therapy the following week. In my head, I was a good guy, and good guys donÕt break up with their significant others while one of them goes off to study abroad. The story I was telling myself was being physically rejected by my body. The punch line is she started dating a guy who was friends of ours who lived on her hall, like sophomore year, and I was not the Jim and we were not the Jim and Pam. I was Roy. 
Judd: Yeah, you were the bullet dodged. 
Lin-Manuel: I was totally the bullet she dodged. 
Judd: I realized that I was that way where I never broke up with a girl ever. I would ride it out till she ran away screaming for the hills. And I would never ever think I could break up with someone. I didnÕt have the self-esteem to go, IÕm unhappy. This isnÕt right. I just couldnÕt believe anyone was there. And then I realized that thatÕs what I was doing. And this beautiful woman liked me, but I didnÕt think I liked her enough. But I was terrified to just tell her. IÕd never done that. And then one day, she bought me a pair of pants. And I was like, I gotta end this because now sheÕs so sweet and sheÕs buying me pants. And so, I told her I couldnÕt do it anymore. I think I may have said, ÒI donÕt think youÕre the future Mrs. Apatow.Ó I think I said that, which is awful. And then she said, ÒI canÕt believe youÕre dumping me.Ó 
Lin-Manuel: It reminds me of your Steve Martin story, one of my favorite stories. ÒIÕm sorry. I didnÕt realize I was talking to the Judd Apatow.Ó 
Judd: I was in a meeting with Steve Martin and someone asked me to tell that story. And I told it. And at the end, they asked Steve if that was how he remembered it. And he said, ÒIn my memory, I knocked on JuddÕs door.Ó 
Lin-Manuel: ThatÕs a nice coda. 
Judd: Who are your mentors? I saw that you translated West Side Story with Stephen Sondheim. Who were the people for you that gave you some time and had a real effect on you? 
Lin-Manuel: It was a couple of folks. My interaction with Sondheim is interesting, because it was the second brush with him. The first one: I was a kid, and I was directing West Side Story at my high school. We got him to come speak to our cast, and the only reason that happened was because [lyricist] John WeidmanÕs daughter, Laura, was a good friend. Weidman wrote Assassins with [Sondheim], and Pacific Overtures. 
Judd: You had an in. 
Lin-Manuel: We had an in. [Sondheim] came and spoke to us on a lunch break. I had changed some of the things to the movie version, because I liked them better, which youÕre not allowed to do. But I did a girl version of ÒAmerica,Ó and I remember one of our cast members asked [Sondheim], ÒWhich do you prefer, the stage or the movie version?Ó He was like, ÒOh, girl version is better in every way.Ó Then, the other thing I remember, was he talked us through the creation of the Prologue from West Side Story, and he discussed all these lyrics heÕd written with Leonard Bernstein. Like, originally, Riff was named ÒRiffÓ because he came in holding a trumpet and started singing these snatches of lyrics to us. As you and I know, there are no lyrics in the Prologue, so weeks of [SondheimÕs] work went into the wastepaper basket. That was my first glimpse that these are not stone tablets from on high, these musicals. You revere these people, and hereÕs Steve telling us, ÒYeah, I really wanted them to say, ÔFuck you,Õ but they wouldnÕt let me, so itÕs Ôcrap.Õ Ó Giving us process. 
Judd: ThatÕs a real guy. A guy in a room with the pencil. 
Lin-Manuel: ItÕs real the same way you became a kid reporter to talk to these folks. We got him on a lunch break. 
Judd: Like, thereÕs a person who does this, and IÕm a person, and I guess that I seem a little like them. Right? 
Lin-Manuel: Right. Then my second brush with someone in this worldÑone of my best friends in college, his dad was the cocreator of AinÕt MisbehavinÕ. Those were Fats Waller songs, but he wrote some additional lyrics, and he was also a lyricist. He gave me two pieces of adviceÑwell, he actually gave me money for my senior thesis and recording it, but he also said, ÒJoin the Drama Guild as soon as you graduate college, because they help writers, and it doesnÕt cost that much, but if someone says, ÔI want to produce your show,Õ you can go to the Guild and go, ÔWhat do I do?Õ And two, get the Clement Wood rhyming dictionary.Ó He said, ÒYouÕre good, you have rhyming; you donÕt really need it unless youÕre stuck. But heÕs got a great essay at the beginning about what rhyming is and what it isnÕt.Ó Then, the other piece of advice he gave me, when he saw my senior thesis, was like, ÒThese are a bunch of songs, but it can sing and not have to be a song.Ó Look at Hamilton Ñthat advice bore fruit in a huge way. You donÕt just throw a song at every moment. Sometimes people sing, but it doesnÕt have to be an ABAB chorus. So, those were nuggets that my friendÕs dad gave me, and then, when I met Sondheim laterÑthis is later, with In the HeightsÑI told him about that West Side Story lunch meeting, and he said, ÒOh, thatÕs the one where I sang the Prologue lyrics.Ó 
Judd: Incredible that he remembers that. HeÕs a steel trap. 
Lin-Manuel: Insane mind. He was one of the first people I told about Hamilton, because he asked what I was working on next. At that point, I just had the one song, and he threw his head back and laughed and said, ÒThat is perfect. No one will expect that from you.Ó And that laugh was enough to power three years of writing. 
Judd: I had that once, when I was starting out. I introduced Jerry Seinfeld at the Improv. I was the emcee, and you try to slip in a couple of jokes before you bring him on, and he came onstage. He just looked over at me and went, ÒThat was funny.Ó And I knew he doesnÕt give that up for people. Two years on ÒThatÕs funny.Ó 
Lin-Manuel: ItÕs the way writers talk about living high off a personal note on a rejection letter. 
Judd: What about having the movie out there now? In the last month, more people have seen Hamilton than have seen it in all the time since it came out. Suddenly, millions and millions of people seeing it. 
Lin-Manuel: ItÕs great. I mean, itÕs a weird dj vu. Everything is louderÑthe people who love it love it; the people who hate it hate it. What IÕm most grateful for is that one of the biggest digs against Hamilton was that it was so hard to get to see itÑeven though we grew five companies as fast as we could out of a desire for people to see itÑbut now thatÕs gone. The person who bragged, ÒI saw the original cast!Ó well, so did this guy for six ninety-nine. 
Judd: I thought they did an amazing job with it, as someone whoÕs seen it a lot. And I really enjoyed watching the performances up close, to see your acting and Leslie [Odom, Jr.]Õs actingÑtight. 
Lin-Manuel: ThereÕs a reason all of them have gone on to become movie stars. They are comfortable with the stage, and theyÕre comfortable with the cameras an inch away. ItÕs the same performance. ThatÕs just a testament to how incredible that original company was. They really were. But also Tommy [Kail, the director of the filmed version of Hamilton] had four years. He would edit it, and then put it in a drawer for a year, and then look at it again. We had the foresight to make it ourselves and finance it ourselves at the last minute, before the principals started to leave. 
Judd: People never understand how hard it is to decide when to be tight and when to be wider, when editing something like that. 
Lin-Manuel: There are some sections of that where we are so fucking sweaty, where weÕd joke about the gift of going wide. I was like, ÒThank you for going wide in that moment, Tommy, because IÕm covered in sweat and I have the weirdest look on my face.Ó 
Judd: ÒWhereÕs the CGI sweat-removal guy?Ó The show came out during such a political moment. Suddenly, the world had changed, and everyone is looking for new ways to interpret work and decide if it fits what they think is appropriate. But I saw one interview you did where you said they didnÕt talk about slavery that much in the musical because they didnÕt talk about slavery enough back then. Like, ÒIÕd be lying if I put it in there.Ó 
Lin-Manuel: I wrote that third cabinet battle and put it on the mixtape. It was incredibly cathartic to write Hamilton being like, ÒWashington, you motherfucker.Ó I had to write it, because I was struggling with it. But then you get to the end of the song, and itÕs like, None of these guys are doing enough. Can we afford four minutes that might make the audience feel better but [that] doesnÕt move the story and doesnÕt really accurately reflect any debate that these guys were having about this, because they literally put a provision in the Constitution saying weÕre not discussing slavery till 1808? So, it was something, like so many things in the showÑwhat do you put into two and half hours of a musical? 
Judd: Because youÕre saying, Do I change the vibe of this to be really disappointed in these people? 
Lin-Manuel: ItÕs the live ammo of playing with real lives, right? I donÕt think Hamilton comes off as a saint in our show. Pretty relentless and flawed motherfucker, but when it comes to that question, specifically of slavery, none of them did enough. So, how much time can we meaningfully spend on it when thereÕs no other boot that drops? The weird thing about the show is that because it deals with the origins of our country, people who are rah-rah about our country are rah-rah about the show, and people who go, ÒWeÕve never been represented,Ó go, ÒIÕm represented.Ó The big thesis, if there is any, is that when it comes to talking about America itself, itÕs the same contradictions. WeÕre having the same fucking fights, and that can be a source of comfort, or that can be a source of dismay, depending on your mood that day. 
Judd: There was an Obama-era sense of change: This is getting better. Then, suddenly, someone ripped it all away and revealed, ItÕs fucked. 
Lin-Manuel: I think a lot of people who wrote about the show said, ÒThis is the Obama-era show.Ó ThereÕs a Black president here, and thereÕs a Black president onstage, very one-to-one. I was as stunned and depressed by TrumpÕs election in 2016 as anybody else. I saw this show go from the show that the president likes to the show that the president attacks. Then people came out to support the show because Trump hates the show, but also the death threats. Weirdly, the show contains that. Hamilton comes to power; he falls from power. HeÕs outlived by all his enemiesÑthey all become 
president.Ó[*] 

Judd: ThereÕs a line that I felt appropriately summed up this entire debate: that the country is a Òwork-in-progress.Ó 
Lin-Manuel: ThatÕs the big thesis. I think itÕs in the end of act one, where they say the Constitution is a mess, and itÕs full of contradictions, and it needs amendments. The ideals have never been enacted. Life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness has never been true for all Americans, ever. When the thesis is ancient, unchanged from the 1700s until now, itÕs gonna resonate. Different things are gonna pop. The Trump/King George thingÑÒI will kill your friends and family to remind you of my loveÓÑis a terrifyingly resonant line from the guy saying, ÒMaybe herd immunity is not so bad.Ó 
Judd: What is your daily writing routine? How do you carve out your time, and where do you do it, and how do you get focused? 
Lin-Manuel: IÕm pretty deadline-based. I wrote those graduation speeches the night before. ThatÕs one of the benefits of working with Tommy [Kail]: He just sets a meeting every week. So, if the meetings are on Friday, IÕm writing Thursday night. The tricky thing is carving out time. The work-life balance is a universal thing. My parents were both workaholics, so I want to be as present as possible. Yet, the thing you learned when you grew upÑyouÕre like, Oh, my dad loved his job. He wasnÕt like, ÒI wish I could be home with my kids all the time.Ó He really likes his job. 
Judd: ThereÕs a certain guilt for enjoying your work. Because if you come home and go, ÒThis was the best day of my life!Ó your familyÕs like, ÒWeÕre here.Ó 
Lin-Manuel: You cracked it nicely: You made movies with your family. 
Judd: WeÕve enjoyed doing that. And that was part of it. I needed the collaborator with Leslie [Mann], so we could both go deep and talk about each other from a different point of view. But also, we had something to think about together for years. 
Lin-Manuel: Most people just get a house. 
SKIP 
NOTES 

* 
Except Burr 

LULU WANG 
August 2020 
Judd: What do you do to let go and try to access your creativity during the pandemic? 
Lulu: Gardening has been helping, because it allows me to be present in a moment and try not to think about all the things we canÕt do. I try reading books. I donÕt know if you are dealing with this, but I signed up for a bunch of projects before the pandemic hit, and now I have to execute them. But when I go to execute them, IÕm like, Does anyone care about this? All the drama that was interesting before feels completely out of context. IÕve had to find ways to reassess or reposition those narratives so that theyÕre relevant without making them about COVID. 
Judd: I have always had trouble believing that anybody cared about anything I had to say, but right now, with everything thatÕs been going on, itÕs especially easy to go, Does anybody want to see this? Is this what we should be talking about right now? But then, at night, we turn on the TV, and we spend forty minutes trying to find the right show or movie. I realize that people must still need that stuff, because IÕm watching 90-Day Fianc for the third time today. 
Lulu: I just finished watching Indian Matchmaking, which I binged in about two and a half days. And I thought it was great. Some people will criticize it and say that it might be playing into stereotypes about Indian culture, but I have friends who live that reality, and it is interesting coming from a more traditional culture. Chinese culture is very different, but still, I see why you might have to think practically and honestly rather than just sentimentally about your life choices. ItÕs like, yes, we have to talk about money, we have to talk about class, we have to talk about colorism. What does the world value? How does that position you in the world? We can pretend that romance is just romance. But the show plays with that. And also itÕs just really fun. 
Judd: I feel like people have a much bigger hunger to see those stories like The Farewell than the studios want to acknowledge. ThereÕs always been a bias in how studios pick projects, what they think will make money. Was it hard to get financing? 
Lulu: Very hard. ItÕs funny to answer this question now, because I think that our culture has definitely shifted since the pandemic. This is a global pandemic; weÕre looking at the world as a global culture and how weÕre dealing with it as the world. Even before the pandemic, I think that Hollywood has shifted directions. But when I was trying to get financing for The Farewell, between 2015 to 2018, all of the studios kept comparing it to either My Big Fat Greek Wedding or The Joy Luck Club. Of course, those are the only films those studios could compare it to because thatÕs all most people knew of ethnic comedy. So, I got a lot of notes along the lines of, ÒMaybe somebody would give you more if you did X, Y, and Z,Ó trying to push it in a different direction. It wasnÕt until I did an episode of This American Life and really grounded that story in reality that producers responded to it. 
Judd: You had a script for The Farewell before This American Life? 
Lulu: I have so many versions of the script. Before going on This American Life, I had some version of the movie that we saw, but it had been through so many iterations because of the influence that I got from the outside, where people kept saying, ÒCan you make it broader? Can Billi have a white American boyfriend that she brings home? Can she be the bride?Ó I would try these things because, as a filmmaker, you think, Maybe theyÕre right. And itÕs like you said: We already struggle with that doubt of Who cares about what I have to say? But when I wanted to make The Farewell, people saw this script for a movie about an eighty-year-old grandma, whoÕs Chinese, and were like, ÒWhat are the stakes? SheÕs eighty. So, she has cancer. SheÕs gonna die at some point.Ó As if we donÕt all have grandmas or somebody older who we love, right? And the script was bilingual, which was another issue. I tried to write that studio-friendly version of the script, but it just didnÕt click. It was a compromise between my vision and theirs, so it was neither here nor there. I had to just scrap all of those versions and start from the beginning. 
Judd: How did the This American Life piece happen? 
Lulu: Neil Drumming is a producer at This American Life, and he was just about to start that job when we met. HeÕs a filmmaker as well, and we met at this film festival in New York, where I was screening my short film Touch. He said to me that he saw his role at This American Life, as one of the few people of color, to bring underrepresented voices and stories that we havenÕt heard before. He said, ÒDo you have any stories that Hollywood is not letting you tell? Can you bring that to me?Ó And obviously, I had been pitching this film about my grandma for years, and nobody would let me make it. I immediately thought it would be perfect for This American Life, because I had even toyed with the idea of writing it as a short story and sending it to The New Yorker, so I had a very rough draft of that and sent it over, and thatÕs how it got set up. 
Judd: Did the process of crafting the radio version of the story help you find its tone? 
Lulu: Absolutely, because itÕs a fact-based show, so you canÕt make stuff up. Normally, you walk into a room to pitch a movie, and immediately youÕre trying to make it more dramatic and more interesting. But with This American Life, itÕs like, ÒOkay, what happened? And how did that make you feel? And then what did your mom say? And then what did she do?Ó There were certain questions that they asked, and I said, ÒI actually donÕt know why she said that. LetÕs go talk to her.Ó So, we interviewed her. Neil and I flew to Atlanta, where my parents live, and we spent a weekend with them and interviewed them. And those pieces of that interview ended up in the show. 
We interviewed my great aunt, who was the instigator of the lie about my grandmaÕs diagnosis, and allowed her to have a voice. You saw all these different perspectives. And we could argue about how I felt, and: ÒHow did you feel back then? Did you think that I was a threat to telling her?Ó The process was all about finding the drama in what exists, finding the shape, finding the main character. Finding the narrative drive without making something up is a really great exercise, because youÕre shaping it rather than resorting to easy answers. We donÕt want the easy solution. WeÕre trying to get to some exploration of some truth. 
Judd: There are so many different people and voices that you have to contend with during the process of making a movie, and sometimes that can make you lose sight of that sense of exploration. 
Lulu: I got really lucky having the experience at This American Life, where it was all about protecting the story, protecting what I wanted. And then meeting producer Chris Weitz, who in the first meeting that we had said, ÒI see my role as the person whoÕs going to protect your voice from whoever finances the movie.Ó I think a lot of filmmakers wonder, How do I know that this is going to be a company that is going to protect my voice if I donÕt get final cut? So, Chris actually said to me, ÒI will fight so that we all share creative control.Ó And we all shared final cut. 
Judd: ThatÕs a very hard thing to get, and it really is everything. It reduces your level of terror, because you think, No one is allowed to make me screw this up. 
Lulu: Totally. And in the end, no one had to use that clause. How to protect oneÕs vision is the question I get the most from young filmmakers. I remember hearing Ava DuVernay give a keynote speech many years ago, where she talked about something she called Òthe cloak of desperation,Ó and how she realized that she was wearing a cloak of desperation whenever she would walk into pitch meetings, and it stinks from a mile away. NobodyÕs going to want to work with you. But how do you remove that cloak when you actually are desperate to get a movie made? For me, yes, itÕs important to remove that cloak of desperation when youÕre pitching, but then, once you get the yes, once you have people who are interested in working with you, you have to ask yourself, Is every yes a good yes? When youÕre desperate, both to make money to pay the rent and also to move your career forward, how can you say no to an opportunity? I think people will often rush into these partnerships without really vetting them beforehand. They feel like theyÕre the one being vetted. Oh my God, somebody said yes and was willing to give me money. IÕll do anything. But that can be tricky, because if you get into a situation where it ends up not being your voice, and your name is on it, then what? Where do you go from there, if itÕs not representative of what you want to make? 
Judd: I remember when we set up Freaks and Geeks at NBC, at the time there was no head of NBC. That person had just stepped down. The higher-up person, who wasnÕt as creatively involved, green-lit the show. In the middle of us working on it, NBC hired a new head, who didnÕt really get what we were doing. So, when notes would come down, we were always like, Do you take notes from someone who isnÕt even excited about it? Years later, we did a documentary about the show, and they interviewed that person, and even after all the acclaim for the show, he still said he thinks he was right. I thought that was funny. How do you avoid people who ultimately are going to intrude and say, ÒI donÕt like this casting,Ó or, ÒAdd this person to itÓ? 
Lulu: Intrusion is possible at every step. I was just talking to a friend about this, who had been trying to get her film made for so many years, and the financers didnÕt let her do the casting or have a say in who was controlling the casting. I have another friend who went through this with his first feature film, where they wouldnÕt let him hire the department heads. He couldnÕt bring his own DP [director of photography], and itÕs his first feature film. I canÕt imagine making The Farewell without my team, because thatÕs who you turn to when everyone is doubting your creative vision. 
Judd: It took me decades to find the people who understand what I do. In the beginning, you donÕt always have that luxury. The Big Sick was a five.million-dollar movie, and a lot of that had to do with the fact that we really wanted a partner that would let us make the movie that Kumail and [screenwriter] Emily [V. Gordon] and [director] Michael Showalter wanted to make. And Film Nation were great partners, because they believed in the team and let us make the film. Most of our other movies cost anywhere from twenty to forty million, so that was the concession that had to be made. 
Lulu: The Farewell was supposed to be a one-point-five-million-dollar movie, because I think thereÕs sort of this unfounded belief that itÕs cheaper to shoot in China. But we ended up making it for three million, which is a pretty substantial budget for an indie film. IÕm curious: Do you see your role more and more as one that shepherds newer voices and protects themÑsort of in the way that Chris did for me in The Farewell? 
Judd: On some level, I felt like my early years were difficult for reasons similar to what youÕve described. I didnÕt have that productive collaboration most of the time. So, I think itÕs great to work with someone whoÕs just getting started, and save them from the pain I had. I want to save you from everything that broke my heart. I donÕt want you to even know how brutal this business can be. I want you to be clear-headed, so that all your energy is going into your creativity. When I met Lena Dunham, when we were starting Girls, the first thing I said is, ÒI want you to know you have final say on everything with the show.Ó I wanted her to feel fully empowered. I think that everything comes out better when you do that, because when people get watered down, it gets weird. 
Lulu: I think one of the best questions that IÕve ever been asked was just, ÒWhy is this important to you?Ó My editor and I work this way, where heÕll make me defend scenes and frames. HeÕll ask me, ÒWhy do you want this to be forty frames instead of three? Why canÕt we just get out of this moment?Ó And if IÕm like, ÒBecause itÕs pretty,Ó then thatÕs not enough of a defense. When I worked with Chris [Weitz], it was never like, ÒThis sucks, why donÕt you just take it out?Ó You have to assume that the creative put it there for a reason. That reason may not be coming across, or maybe the execution isnÕt working, but itÕs worthwhile to dig and find out why itÕs there. And you might land somewhere really unexpected, and come up with a better solution of how to communicate this idea. Chris was often asking me that, and just being able to talk out loud about it made what I was trying to do clearer to me, even if it wasnÕt working on the page. I donÕt think most people experience that kind of creative collaboration during development. ItÕs often just like, ÒWe donÕt like this, can you remove it?Ó 
Judd: Can you talk a little about why this particular story was so important to you? 
Lulu: After I made my first feature, Posthumous, I was really focused on making my next movie something that I could stand behind and say, ÒYes, this represents my voice.Ó I love to tell stories in my daily lifeÑwhen I talk to friends and people I feel comfortable with, I have a particular sensibility, where itÕs darkness and humor, all combined. I wanted to learn this skill of putting that into a film, which is really a lot of different elements. ItÕs about putting it on the script so people can read it and get that. ItÕs putting it on the screen through camerawork and lighting, and then itÕs casting, and then itÕs working with your actors to find that right balance of not being too big, but not being too little. When this story happened in my real life, and I told my friends about it, it was like, ÒOh, this is so classic Lulu. This is such a classic thing that would happen to your crazy family.Ó In telling the story over cocktails, people would be laughing, people would be cryingÑso, I knew I had a good story. The challenge for me as a filmmaker is, How do I get that into a film? And if I can do that successfully, then itÕs going to work, because I know that itÕs working. The story itself isnÕt the problem. 
Judd: Did you feel like it was important that your way of looking at the world would get across to the viewer? 
Lulu: It kept me up at night. Since I had already made my first feature, and I knew that I wasnÕt going to get that many more opportunities to really establish my voice and show what I could do, I felt like even if I had to make this movie for very little money, it had to be a film that I could completely control creatively, so I could really balance the tone that I wanted. I would describe my vision to people where theyÕre like, ÒIs this funny or is it sad?Ó IÕm like, ÒItÕs both, just like life.Ó And theyÕre like, ÒWell, do you want the audience to cry or laugh?Ó IÕm like, ÒBoth. I want them to be laughing as theyÕre sobbing, because thatÕs how I felt through this entire wedding, funeral, reunion, goodbye, whatever.Ó 
Judd: I had the most joyful experience watching the movie. I just thought, This is everything I love about movies. This is a way of presenting humanity that I so related to. And we had a history, which I didnÕt really remember, which is that that you worked on Pineapple Express briefly? 
Lulu: And you fired me. 
Judd: Well, not me; another person fired you. We wonÕt call them out. What happened? 
Lulu: Pineapple Express was my second job in the industry ever, and I was a terrible assistant. I didnÕt know how to make coffee, and ultimately, I didnÕt understand studio hierarchy. David Gordon Green was the director, and he comes from the indie world. So, he and I were just always like, ÒLet me see your film. Let me do this. Let me do that. And let me help you.Ó ThatÕs probably not what youÕre supposed to do on a studio set. Lots of lines were crossed as far as the hierarchy. But David was incredibly supportive, and that was one of the great things that came out of that experience, where he said, ÒIf you want to be a filmmaker, donÕt work on a film set. Get the experience, and then go and figure out your story. Because if you do this for ten years, youÕre not going to have any stories to tell.Ó He worked as a janitor and worked like three different day jobs and then lived under somebodyÕs stairwell and wrote George Washington. That was actually his advice to me. He said that it was okay if I was a bad assistant and didnÕt want to work on a set. So, I left and started writing scripts. And then, right before I went off to shoot Posthumous, I saw you at an award ceremony, and I said, ÒHey, Judd, you guys fired me. But guess what? IÕm about to go make my first film.Ó 
Judd: And I think I said that to get fired was the best thing that ever happened to you. 
Lulu: You said, ÒCongratulations, thatÕs a great badge of honor.Ó 
Judd: ThatÕs a funny thing about the business. These moments that feel so terrible can spin you off into a completely different journey. I remember, when I was at USC, John Milius came and spoke, and he just said to the cinema class, ÒQuit. Quit college right now. Go live your life. Otherwise, youÕre going to have nothing to write about.Ó And thatÕs hard advice. 
Lulu: When I came to L.A., my parents were so terrified. They were like, ÒYou donÕt know a single person in the industry. We have no money. We canÕt help you. How are you going to do this?Ó And I didnÕt go to film school, so it felt like the question was: How do you break in? ItÕs a legitimate question; you have to find a way to break in. I thought I broke in by being on that set, but I wasnÕt there in the capacity that I wanted to be. So, now I think, anytime that youÕre learning or gathering stories, great. ThereÕs a lot of different ways to sustain a living while youÕre able to write. And set life is really hard. I had done another movie before I worked on the set of Rush Hour 3, so I went from one movie to another, and it was, like, sixteen-hour days. I was just exhausted and wondered, When am I ever going to have time to be creative or be a writer and have stories to tell? 
Judd: I think when you have those experiencesÑlike when you were talking to David Gordon Green and heÕs excited for youÑit makes you go, Oh I can get in this club. I can figure this out. 
Lulu: I think most people know a novelist or a journalist, but not many people know directors. ThereÕs something about directing that feels like itÕs put on a pedestal and itÕs this inaccessible thing. Particularly for me and my parents, it was like, ThereÕs nobody that looks like me doing that. So, thatÕs like a whole other step. When I met David Gordon Green, it meant so much to me that heÕs like, ÒMy assistant said you made a short film and itÕs pretty good. Can I 
watch it?Ó And IÕm like, ÒOh, my God, this is the coolest thing.Ó So, I just went up to Video Village. It was like, ÒHereÕs a DVD.Ó Judd: And thenÉ? Lulu: I got fired. 

MARGARET CHO 
August 2020 
Judd: Did you ever do that stand-up show The Half Hour Comedy Hour? 
Margaret: Yeah, I remember Janeane Garofalo couldnÕt take the microphone out of the stand, because she didnÕt remember how to. So, she was just holding it and walking around with it, sort of like itÕs a compass or something. It was just so hard to take the mic out of the stand. 
Judd: That show was a big deal. I was living with Adam Sandler at the time, in the Valley. I lay out in the sun and got a tan for it. IÕve never been tan in anything, ever. And my set was so stupid and pointless. 
Margaret: I noticed that our voices are very different now. The way that we speak is very different. 
Judd: Yes, I had a Long Island accent back then. ItÕs a little shocking. I remember starting out at a bunch of comedy clubs in Long IslandÑChuckles in Mineola, GovernorÕs in Levittown, and the East Side Comedy Club in Huntington, which was one of the first clubs on Long Island. I washed dishes there in high school because I wanted to see comedy. But then I realized, IÕm in the kitchen, I canÕt see anything. Then I became a busboy, so I could be in the room. And thatÕs where Rosie OÕDonnell was starting out, and Eddie Murphy still came in a few times. He was twenty-one years old and exploding the comedy scene with his brilliance. What club did you get your start in? 
Margaret: Probably the Holy City Zoo in San Francisco. I lived across the street from it so I could emcee there. I would go there every night after I would do shows everywhere else, and just watch everyone. And I could do a set there anytime anybody dropped out. It was really fun, too, because that was the one club that when we were all young, everybody lived close by. So, one block away was the apartment where Patton Oswalt and Blaine Capatch and Brian Posehn lived. And then, on the corner, was the Toy Boat caf, where Jeff Garlin and Robin Williams would always stop by. I think Ron Lynch lived about ten blocks away, in a house where Alex Reid lived at first, and then it was Dana Gould, and then Jim Earl also lived thereÑall of these comics lived there at some point. Did you ever live in one of those houses? 
Judd: I used to visit one of those houses deep in the Valley when I was an eighteen-year-old at USC. The house was a total crash pad, and youÕd go there, and Joel Hodgson would be there, or Rich Hall. There was an amazing crew of hilarious people there, and I couldnÕt believe they would let me hang out. I was just a nerd wanting to be a comic. I used to host at the Improv, but it was like Ellen [DeGeneres] and Paula Poundstone and Leno and Seinfeld and [Paul] Reiser. It was star-studded and terrifying. And then you would hear about San Francisco, and it always sounded way more creative and fun. But I never spent much time up there. I just remember opening up for Jim Carrey at CobbÕs, when he was on In Living Color. 
Margaret: I was there for that. He was so great. 
Judd: That was before he got really big, and even In Living Color had only been on a few months, and he just started doing the road a little bit. But that scene in San FranciscoÑI was very jealous of that scene. It seemed mysterious at the time, and now IÕm friends with so many people who started there. It must have been amazing to have landed there at that moment. 
Margaret: We were just doing drugs. We were just stupid and were doing stupid open mics. What I loved about it was, in the beginning, the Other I was still open, which was the really good club. YouÕd get to go see Paula Poundstone there, and she would do these shows where she wouldnÕt really do any jokes. She would just fuck around with the audience the whole time, which is so funny and so crazy. She still does that. And that, I think, was amazing. Or you could be at the Zoo and watch Jeremy Kramer and Robin Williams on one side of the stage making fun of Perry Kurtz trying to do a set on the other side of the stage. The best part of comedy was before any comics were tied to any kind of family obligations, before everybody was married or had kids. We could spend all of our time watching each other and making fun of each other. That was a good era for comedy, because nobody was really successful or anything. After a certain amount of time, people start to get successful, and then thereÕs a real sense of bitterness that creeps in. And success is actually pretty meaningless in the end. When you look at how much it affects things, and how it affects you inside, itÕs weird. Also, the people who are really talented often never get whatÕs due. Some of the people I thought were the funniest never quite achieved the success I thought that they would. Or they didnÕt want to. And thatÕs fine. 
Judd: Because thereÕs talent and thereÕs ambition, and not everybody has every part of it. Some just get the business side of things, while other people are geniuses, but they have no idea how to interact with the business side of comedy. 
Margaret: I mean, everybody has a particular genius, and thereÕs very few that have everything. But there are some people that are always my perennial favorites, that I love to watch. I always love Andy Dick. I love him. He always makes me laugh, even really mundane, stupid things like hanging up a payphone. 
Judd: Someone like Andy is a good example, because heÕs also been in his share of trouble. You feel for them, because you know their story, but as we get older, there seems to be more and more of those situations, especially in this era when the MeToo movement has revealed so much about people, people youÕve known forever, and youÕre like, How do I file this? What does my relationship with them become in the aftermath of their transgressions? 
Margaret: There are a lot more opportunities for bad behavior to be witnessed now. Before, we didnÕt have smartphones, so we didnÕt have as much accountability. The only time anybody would get in trouble for anything is if it was reported, or you got arrested or something like that. Now itÕs constant accountability for everything, which is betterÑitÕs safer for people. At the same time, if you are going through a hard time and you want to talk about it, going public with that kind of personal difficulty is now seen as a new kind of strength. Our society values a whole different set of strengths, where bravery is now about opening up. Vulnerability becomes very powerful in this era of accountability. ItÕs a different landscape from the hypermasculine world of the nineties. The comedy scene that you and I came up in is very different from what weÕre looking at today. 
Judd: So much of what is happening now is stuff that youÕve been talking about for decades: diversity, MeToo, childhood sexual abuse. This is stuff that you started hitting head-on, way before other people were talking about it. 
Margaret: People are starting to be more aware of these issues, but you also want people to find their way through it and find a purposeful way to talk about it, because I donÕt have all the answers. People think that IÕm the expert because IÕve been vocal about these issues in the past. I was interviewing this author about a traumatic experience, for example, and it was really hard, and I felt like I didnÕt do the right thing. When I talk about sexual trauma with people, I feel like I should be equipped to do it in the right way. But IÕm not sometimes, just like everyone else. 
Judd: Part of the problem is generational. When we grew up, no one talked about anything. But I look at my kids, and I see all their friends, and theyÕre very open about all their pain, all their trauma. I think about all the people I went to high school with in the eighties and how no one talked about their issues. We might have talked about our parents getting divorced, but there wasnÕt one person who came out or talked about their depression or their eating disorders. It was a very different era. 
Margaret: Everything was really shut down. There was that movie Ordinary People, with Timothy Hutton and Mary Tyler Moore, and itÕs all about how they canÕt talk about anything. ThereÕs one point [when] Timothy Hutton goes and hugs Mary Tyler Moore, and sheÕs just so brittle, itÕs like sheÕs going to break. ThatÕs our generation: Nobody can talk about anything. 
Judd: Why do you think you were able to make the leap to being an open, expressive, creative person? When did you realize that comedy was a place where you could find your voice? 
Margaret: IÕve always just really loved the art form. And I got lucky because my dad is a comedy and a movie fan, so he would let me watch movies that I probably shouldnÕt have seen, like Jaws. We also saw A Clockwork Orange together, which is very adult. We also loved movies like Blazing Saddles and Young FrankensteinÑall the Mel Brooks movies, which were just hugely important to me. 
Judd: It sounds like your family exposed you to all sorts of good stuff. 
Margaret: They were big laughers. They didnÕt approve of the Three Stooges, though; they were too violent. But my parents were, overall, very permissive about television, because they werenÕt home a lot, so they were glad that I could be entertained by something. We got a VCR pretty early on, and we would watch Richard Pryor, which was also very adult for a kid to watch. I got to watch SNL, and Joan Rivers hosted one week, and that changed everything, because I wanted to be a comedian after I saw her. Watching Andy Kaufman and Steve Martin host SNL was also so influential to me, and they were the comics who made me want to be a comedian. I was obsessed with SCTV and SNL. Really, really obsessed with SCTV more than anything, and mostly Eugene Levy. 
Judd: IÕm still obsessed with Eugene Levy. 
Margaret: Me, too. So, when I started doing comedy, it was a very natural thing, and I just never thought to stop. 
Judd: You also starred in your own sitcom, All-American Girl. 
Margaret: That was an interesting experience. IÕm glad that I went through it, and it would be interesting to try it again, even though I donÕt know exactly what that would look like now. I like the format of, like, multi-cams. I like the tradition of itÑI love an old-school Norman LearÐstyle show. I like the audience, the live energy. ItÕs really special. ThereÕs a real science to making it work, like trying to figure out the right combination of people and finding the right network.
