But it also depends on the context, the moment in history, that would allow you to successfully execute your vision. Even though the sitcom has reemerged in recent years, thereÕs still something traditional about it that I think is ready for true reinvention, where it can be fresh, but at the same time, very traditional. I like Taxi because Taxi had a film look to it, but it still was a multi-cam, so there was a melding of formats, and there was also a melancholy as well. IÕm not sure what it is, but I think it has something to do with the immigrant story of love. 
Judd: ItÕs Judd HirschÕs existentialism. It has to do with the things that the characters are not getting from their lives. They drive other people around, but they donÕt go anywhere themselves. We could update that to Uber drivers, but Uber drivers are not centered anywhere. 
Margaret: ItÕs like, what do we do when we donÕt do anything? Where do we go when we donÕt go anywhere? 
Judd: Where do you feel like you stand now, in terms of what youÕre doing and what you want to be doing? 
Margaret: IÕve always liked the way that Greg Proops would frame it. He said that I was going to be a lot like Ella Fitzgerald: Somebody who gets better with age. Somebody youÕd go and see at the Venetian on a night out because theyÕre a class act to go see. 
Judd: When you look back at all your sets over the years, how do you see your comedy evolving? 
Margaret: I think I hit a high point around 2000. I was really great in 2003, and then itÕs gone up and down since then. Sometimes, youÕre like, Oh, my God, I was amazing. Or, sometimes, IÕll go back and look at my past work, and although it was great, IÕll still think, Oh, that was terrible. I havenÕt done a show since the beginning of March, so thatÕs weird. I donÕt know about you, but if I donÕt do a show every week or so, I get really freaked out. 
Judd: I didnÕt do stand-up for twenty-two years, and then I started doing it again in 2014. I realized I really missed it, because this is the fun part of comedy, not sitting in the editing room all day long with my editor for years. All the fun is that interaction with people. 
Margaret: That energy that you get from performance is very vital, and I didnÕt realize how much I got from it, because it was such a part of my daily life. ItÕs like working out. I didnÕt realize how much I missed going to the gymÑ which is unfortunate, because I complain about it all the time. 
Judd: When people talk about how much your work means to them, what do they say to you? 
Margaret: ItÕs different for different people. But itÕs mostly that it was the first time that theyÕve seen somebody who looks like them talk about things they understood, that theyÕve personally seen the experiences I talked about. ThatÕs really meaningful. Other Asian American comedians say that I inspired them to do comedy, which is really awesome. 
Judd: Because you didnÕt have that person as you were coming up? 
Margaret: I did, in a sense, because I had people like Joan Rivers, who, even though sheÕs not Asian, is still somebody I looked up to and was like, I want to be like that. So, I did have somebody that I could really admire. But the fact that there are other Asian comedians who really look to me for that, I would say thatÕs my greatest achievement. 
Judd: I just thought whoever is funny is funny. I didnÕt really have any thought about representation. Only when I started working with people like Lena Dunham did I realize how meaningful it was for people to see her on television. You donÕt often see a woman who is so comfortable with every aspect of herself, comfortable with herself sexually, and whoÕs a writer, and a director, and a producer. There are other people whoÕve been able to break down that wall, as I saw when I worked with Kumail Nanjiani on The Big Sick. I donÕt think I put much thought into it, other than, like, KumailÕs hilarious. It was almost after the fact that you realize the significance of a movie or a show that breaks boundaries in terms of whatÕs represented in Hollywood. 
Margaret: ItÕs that lack of visibility which is the hardest thing to even quantify. When the problem is invisibility, it doesnÕt even come to mind as discrimination, because itÕs not even there, so itÕs hard to feel it. 
Judd: I remember, when we were trying to sell The Big SickÑwe worked on it for years, the script was great, but it was hard to get someone to finance it. Most studios didnÕt believe it would sell to the masses, but it did. They were stuck in an old way of thinking. 
Margaret: ThereÕs a deep-seated racism that is not perceived as racism. ItÕs this idea that if itÕs only about a certain race of people, then itÕs only applicable to that particular race or that niche audience. But thatÕs not true, because we all want to see stories of all kinds. 
Judd: Or the idea that men wonÕt go to a movie about women. But itÕs not true. I know from working on Bridesmaids, where maybe fifty-five percent of the viewers were womenÑit wasnÕt that skewed. 
Margaret: All we want to see are good movies, and nobody really cares about what it is or who it is. Race, gender, sexualityÑall of those things matter less than the quality of the stories being told and how well theyÕre being told. 
Judd: Do you consider yourself political when it comes to these things? 
Margaret: I think itÕs about inclusion. I think itÕs about seeing ourselves in the way that we are in the world. ItÕs fairness, itÕs equality in a very plain way. 
ItÕs not so much political for me, although itÕs seen as a political thing by others. ItÕs more important than ever because of the way that COVID-19 has caused this surge of racism toward Asian Americans, but in general, I just want to make it where we can tell our stories or see ourselves in the same way that everybody else does. 
Judd: Was there a comedian who inspired you? 
Margaret: Greg ProopsÑnobody is better. He is so funny, and heÕs always been that funny and smart. His mind works in a way that I still canÕt really figure out. I love Patton Oswalt. I loved Mitch Hedberg, and loved working with him. He was really special. Paula Poundstone, of course. SheÕs probably my favorite. 
Judd: WhatÕs the history of your love affair with tattoos? 
Margaret: Growing up, my parents owned a gay bookstore, and all of the people that worked at my parentsÕ bookstore had full-body tattoos from Ed Hardy, who made the famous T-shirts that are worn by the Jersey Shore people. They all were getting full-body tattoos in the eighties. So, when I was a kid, I knew that was going to be my destiny. When I was in my thirties, I went to get my first Ed Hardy: very large stomach and back tattoos. That was the beginning, but I knew that I was going to get my full body tattooed eventually. That was the plan from when I was a kid. I hadnÕt gotten around to it till my thirties because I thought I was going to be an actor. But now IÕm almost completely tattooed. 
Judd: How does it feel now that youÕre almost done? 
Margaret: I donÕt think IÕm going to get any more, because there are enough now that I could still probably cover them if IÕm in a suit or something. ThatÕs probably good enough. If you have them all the way up to your neck and your wrist, people just know you have tattoos. You canÕt really hide them. I want to try to maybe hide them sometimes. 
Judd: How did your family come to own a gay bookstore? 
Margaret: They wanted to have a bookstore like City Lights, because my father is an author. In the seventies, one became available that he could buy, and it happened to be a gay bookstore. So, we had a gay bookstore in the seventies, and thatÕs where I grew up. It was a great place to grow up and a great place to emerge from. There was a gay comedy club upstairs from the gay bookstore, called the Rose and Pistol. I did some of my first sets there. 
Judd: From those sets above your parentsÕ store until now, how have you seen the comedy scene change over the course of your career? 
Margaret: I think comedy now is so much more sophisticated and grounded in a truth that is more profound, and I feel like we as a generation have really elevated the entire game. ItÕs all of us, weÕve had to push it all forward. There are different philosophies around it now. Even the whole idea of alternative comedy: that we could just take away microphones and do comedy without a microphone. Or without shoes on. There was this whole movement of doing sketch comedy, doing these shows, where we could be out of the construct of a comedy club. Comedy audiences are just a better judge of whatÕs funny now, and I think itÕs up to us to keep that bar high. 

MINDY KALING 
May 2020 
Mindy: I mentioned to you that I subscribed to MasterClass because I wanted to see your class. After I watched yours, since I had that subscription, I watched David SedarisÕs, and I was excited to see that he talks a lot about how he works. IÕm obsessed with process. I would read a book that was just about peopleÕs workday. In his master class, David Sedaris talks about how he works from, I think, ten A.M. to one P.M., every single day, seven days a week, and I thought that schedule sounded really good. 
Judd: I used to be someone who wrote at night, and I loved writing from, like, ten P.M. till two in the morning, and I havenÕt been able to do that for twenty-two years now. 
Mindy: Are you working on something right now? 
Judd: I produce a lot of things that I like, to be a part of other peopleÕs writing process. But I donÕt very often have an idea for a screenplay I want to write. Someone once said that at some point, you mine yourself completely, and then it all turns to mining other people or your imagination. 
Mindy: I loved your master class. Your anecdotes are great, but your advice is so practical, so attainable. IÕm so mad that IÕm forty and IÕm only learning this now, which is that any great comedy story is actually a drama that you can make funnyÑas in, it has to have real emotional stakes for all the characters, not just the leads. Otherwise, they become bad secondary characters. Everyone has to have a story, and it has to work as a dramatic story. I would have saved myself countless, countless hours if I had known that earlier, even going back to when I was at The Office, approaching an episode of TV that way. I thought that was so wise. 
Judd: When I worked for Shandling I just got the sense that every scene that he was either writing or approving was a gem. That was the word that kept coming into my head, like, Oh, heÕs trying to create these little gems. So, anytime we switch spaces to another character, another problem, even exposition, heÕs finding some way to have a great beginning, middle, and end to every moment. Making every moment special in some way. 
Mindy: That has a lot to do with laziness, which no one really talks about. YouÕll hear a joke in a movie or a show, and it sort of seems funny, but it also seems like no one on set or while they were writing the screenplay took the time to ask if it was funny, or if it was just three quarters of the way there? In comedy, itÕs not about like getting things three quarters of the way there. You have to get everything one hundred percent all the time. ItÕs backbreaking perfectionism, which is maybe why you only write movies every five years. 
Judd: When I was on set, it helped me to imagine that I was in the editing room, so that I was always thinking, If I hate this, why will I hate it? Will I wish he had given me more joke options? Would I wish he got me a dramatic version instead of just a goofy version? And because IÕm so neurotic, I usually try to give myself all of it, so in the editing room, I have options and can modulate it to my liking. 
Mindy: When I watch your movies, the experience feels like youÕre seeing the greatest hits of the scene, like youÕve done the work of culling it. And in addition to being funny, your movies often feel very personal and dramatic, because I know that working with Amy Schumer and Seth Rogen and Pete Davidson on their movies, they were tapping into something that was really true for them. 
Judd: Now, Mindy, we have a shared history which we never talk about. YouÕre in The 40-Year-Old Virgin, and that was your first movie. How do 
you look back on that moment and where you were in your life at that time, way back in the early aughts? 
Mindy: It feels like I had this great opportunity and I blew it, because I didnÕt know what it was like to be on camera. I only shot for two days, but I have two really vivid memories of that movie. One was that I was sitting across from Paul Rudd, who, for the record, is ten years older than me but now looks fifteen years younger than me, so that I donÕt love. But I was sitting across from Paul Rudd, and I was supposed to be talking to him, but I started looking into the camera. And I remember, at one point, you had to come over and really nicely tell me, ÒHey, you canÕt look into the camera.Ó And I didnÕt even know I had been doing it. I was just so nervousÑI couldnÕt believe that IÕd been picked to do this with all these funny people. The second memory was in Video Village, and it was you and Seth and Paul and Elizabeth Banks, and you all had such a great rapport. I didnÕt want to seem like I was hovering and trying to laugh along, so I sat a couple of rows back and watched you guys. I love the movie, but whenever that scene comes up, I fast-forward through it. 
Judd: You scored every single time in the movie, and my memory is only of you being hilarious and great. But I guess the only thing you had done on camera before was the first season of The Office, which was like a half season? 
Mindy: And I probably had just four or five lines on camera, so The 40-Year-Old Virgin was a huge experience for me. I also remember that I didnÕt know youÕre supposed to hire a stylist for the movie premiere. I had a frenemy at The Office, and I asked this person, ÒHey, what do you wear to a premiere?Ó And they said to me, ÒOh, just wear jeans and a T-shirt. ItÕs L.A. so nobody really gets dressed up for these things.Ó I was like, Okay, cool, because I didnÕt want to seem thirsty wearing a gown while everyone was dressed down. So, I showed up in a tank top and jeans with some earrings, and everyone there looked so goodÑthey had been styled to perfectionÑand I was like, IÕve been fucking duped by this frenemy of mine. That also felt like a missed opportunity, and then after that, I became really into fashion. 
Judd: I also did not know that you paid people to help you dress for Hollywood events, because usually the studio pays, so you donÕt dress like an idiot to things. But in all these early pictures, IÕm wearing a collar that is sticking out over the suit, and everything is wrong about how IÕm dressed. What was your path into comedy? 
Mindy: IÕm so inhibited compared to someone like Andy Kaufman or Robin Williams. And I think that when youÕre inhibited and shy, and you really care what people think about you, those people just seem incredible to you. I loved, loved watching them. I donÕt know if this is the way you were as a kid, but I couldnÕt tell the difference between watching an Albert Brooks movie and a Gilbert Gottfried stand-up special. I wasnÕt a snob; if you were trying to do something in comedy, it was worthy of me watching it. And I grew up in that time where Comedy Central would do random stand-up shows with little clips. I didnÕt have a favorite; I just loved how everyone was trying something with it. Then, as I got older, I got really into Kids in the Hall, to the point that IÕd seen this one episode five times and written out the specific sketch, because I wanted to know why itÕs funny. Then that evolved into my really loving Saturday Night Live. Stand-up was less exciting to me, although I was still in awe of it. 
Judd: I did that, too, for Saturday Night Live, where I would write the sketches out and try to understand why it was funny. ItÕs a specific kind of comedy nerd that goes that far. I know Bill Hader also talked about recording Saturday Night Live on audio, to hear it and try to understand it. When did it become a job for you? When did you think, I could have a career in this? 
Mindy: When I was a teenager, ConanÕs first show was really big. I remember I would tape it, and I would freeze-frame on the list of writers. I was like, So, this is something you can do. At that point, I think he pretty much had all guys who were writing for him, but I thought, That must be heaven. I didnÕt know what that was actually likeÑthere werenÕt books that existed as they do now, telling you what that would be like as a job. I just remember the credits on Conan, and the credits on SNL. It wasnÕt like two people, it was like twelve, fifteen, twenty people wrote it. I thought, Maybe I have a shot. But this was pre-Twitter, pre-Instagram, so you couldnÕt just tweet at Colin Jost and be like, ÒHow do you do what you do?Ó 
Judd: Do you feel relieved that social media wasnÕt around when you were younger? 
Mindy: The thing IÕm relieved about is I thought I was funny, and I did have a good sense of humor, but it wasnÕt until I was on The Office, when I was around all these other funny people in a writersÕ room, where I realized, 
Those kinds of jokes are kind of hacky, and these observations have already been made. I was already twenty-four when I felt like I was starting to become more sophisticated. I just think that, as a confident sixteen-year-old kid who was a comedy nerd, I probably would have been tweeting Lorne Michaels really lame things, and it would have ruined my career. 
Judd: And people would still be able to find them today. 
Mindy: IÕm so happy thereÕs no physical evidence of me on the internet from before IÕm twenty-four. 
Judd: I always say to my kids, ÒIf you want to find something mean on the internet, youÕre going to find it.Ó You really need to have some discipline to not go online and look for that. 
Mindy: I have a show that came out on Netflix about three weeks ago, and I think it was well reviewed, and it felt like a lot of people were watching it. It has an all-Indian cast, and IÕd never done that before. I was riding this wave of all this positive feedback, and the tweets I was getting were so nice, so I just kept looking. I should have stopped looking, because I found a website that was written by a group of anonymous Indian and South Asian writers in 
L.A. and Hollywood, and every fifth tweet was about how much I sucked and how I donÕt do anything for Indian people. I was like, I just put this show out; what are you talking about? I spent so much emotional energy on that. I spent a night writing a rebuttal, but I realized I shouldnÕt tweet it because theyÕll never be happy. IÕm forty, so I still havenÕt learned this lesson. I couldnÕt just stop at the people saying nice things. I had to look and find the criticism. 
Judd: As comedians, weÕre always looking for the two people in the room who arenÕt laughing. I tell everyone I work with, twenty percent of the world hates everything. If you look at the movies that have won Best Picture for the last twenty years, I probably hate twenty percent of them. And if you search [for] Òthe Dalai LamaÓ on Twitter, twenty percent of the tweets will be hateful. No oneÕs exempt from that hate, but it doesnÕt stop it from being really painful. How has working on Never Have I Ever been otherwise? 
Mindy: ItÕs really fun. I found that with some other comedy shows with diverse characters, a lot of times you feel like youÕre getting a civics lesson. Like theyÕre putting in these diverse characters just to show that theyÕre a woke show, and itÕs not actually funny and it doesnÕt help the story. What was cool about this show is we spent so much time casting it that we were able to find truly hilarious people. YouÕve done this with a lot of young comedy peopleÑ maybe they didnÕt quite have the chops yet, but you still saw something in them. We were able to find this immensely funny cast of actors of color and then got them acting lessons and got them experience, well before we started doing the show. There wasnÕt the phenomenon where white characters are funny, and the characters of color have the setup lines because they couldnÕt find a funny young Latin actor or something like that. It took a lot of hard work, but I think thatÕs why people are responding to it. 
Judd: When we did Freaks and Geeks, one of the things we decided to do was to assume that all the great people didnÕt have agents because they didnÕt think they could be in show business, so we did open-call auditions. We said, ÒAnyone can come.Ó We went to Vancouver and Chicago and New York and Toronto. We just let any kid show up. If we find an interesting kid, we will teach them how to act. 
Mindy: Freaks and Geeks came up constantly when we were doing this. ItÕs the gold standard for teen shows. TheyÕre all stars. TheyÕre all so funny and completely different. I didnÕt know you went to these open casting calls in Vancouver and everything. That must have taken a really long time. 
Judd: I was just like, Where are all the weird kids like me? Well, they wouldnÕt have an agent. They wouldnÕt think they could be in show business. They wouldnÕt think they could act. They wouldnÕt think they looked like an actor or an actress. So, letÕs just open it up to them. But the key thing is, even though I felt like a weirdo and an outsider, I still felt represented on television; I saw people like me. So, the idea that I could get on TV seemed possible. How was it for you, not being able to see people who looked like you on television? 
Mindy: This might just be a defect in my personality, but I just thought, Even though I donÕt look like these white actors and actresses, IÕll still be accepted in Hollywood. I donÕt know why I felt that way, but that confidence followed me through my careerÑthe belief that sensibility and a sense of humor are much more important than the way we look. I donÕt know why I thought that, but I just did. I feel really lucky that I have that personality defect, because I think a lot of people donÕt. There are a lot of people who looked at TV and comedy at that time, who are Asian or Black, and just thought, I really like it, IÕm a big fan, but how would I explain this to my parents? For whatever reason, with my family, they just saw my determination. They got used to the idea that that was my passion, so they got on board. But not that many people are lucky enough to have that with their parents. The one thing youÕve talked about, too, is how if youÕre not obsessed, itÕs not going to happen. And I was obsessed as a kid. If youÕre someone who just thinks that show business is kind of cool, but there are a couple other things you want to do, and you donÕt want to stay late, you donÕt want to work hard, then itÕs just not going to happen. There are far more people who are less talented but who are obsessed who will make it than people who are super talented but donÕt have the patience to put in the time. 
Judd: The hardest worker has the best chance of winning. One of the things I always tell people is that most writing in Hollywood is terrible. ItÕs a funny thing about the industry, which is that there might be a hundred thousand people trying to get the writing jobs, but thereÕs still probably only about eighty people who are actually great. And if you worked really hard to be one of those eighty, you donÕt have to worry about getting work. ItÕs so obvious, and thereÕs not too many of them. Even on the greatest shows, you might have twelve writers, and maybe three of them arenÕt doing much. 
Mindy: I canÕt believe you say three. IÕve worked on shows that people love and would look around the room and think that four people were doing all the work, and the rest are just there. 
Judd: You show that in your movie Late Night. I was laughing at how it was castÑthat all-white writersÕ room is so realÑbut also I thought, Oh man, IÕm one of those guys. IÕve been in those rooms. Most talk shows didnÕt have a single female writer until five years ago. 
Mindy: I learned so much about the late-night writersÕ room world. The first Black woman wasnÕt hired on a late-night talk show until 2016. It was on SethÕs show. These are shows that are set in New York City, and they couldnÕt find a Black woman to write on a late-night TV show in New York City until 2016? I was lucky because, when I was in New York prepping the movie with Emma Thompson, I asked all the women on all the New York shows if they could come to the table read and tell me about their experiences. I think that the late-night rooms have an edge and a competitiveness, whereas thereÕs a clear hierarchy in sitcom writersÕ roomsÑ thereÕs a staff writer, a story editor, a supervising producer, and so onÑso people arenÕt really trying to one-up each other. ItÕs counterintuitive, but I think people do really well when you know where you are in the ladder. You know when you should be in line to get food. If youÕre a staff writer, youÕre not going to be first in line for dinner. You have to wait. And itÕs good. I think I do well when I know what the hierarchies are. But I think in a room where everyoneÕs a writer, like for those late-night shows, whether youÕre a fifty-two-year-old guy or a twenty-two-year-old just out of college, that would feel like itÕd be more of a struggle. 
Judd: I also think that with a talk show staff, theyÕre not writing anything thatÕs emotional. As a result, itÕs a different kind of vibe. Because a sitcom staff is talking about emotions and feelings and love and caring, and thereÕs none of that in a talk show. 
Mindy: That is such a good point. ItÕs all about one character, which is the host of the show, which is more likely than not a man making funny jokes. Whereas, in sitcoms, if itÕs a good sitcom, you learn a lesson. ItÕs impossible on a sitcom to not have that emotional stuff come up. 
In writing late-night shows, thereÕs an emphasis on a kind of boy humor, which is really cerebral, well-worded and -crafted jokes. A lot of my friends who come from the LampoonÑthat sort of humor is put on a high pedestal. Whereas being really in touch with your emotions, or having a character say, Hey, what scares me? WhereÕs my place in the world? That stuff is considered not funny and sort of lame. 
Judd: IsnÕt that part of the arc of what we learn over a long career? That we all get into it, and weÕre just trying to be funny, but we donÕt really know ourselves yet. We donÕt realize how much of comedy is about deep personal exploration. 
Mindy: What I love about your movies is that theyÕre so often about someone who is stuck at a crossroads, where they have to decide whether theyÕre going to keep going down the path that theyÕre already on or switch directions. A central question for your characters is Am I going to be brave enough to try and change? And if you talk about it like that, that doesnÕt sound like a comedy premise; itÕs a really intense drama premise. 
Judd: ItÕs funny because they say that most filmmakers or writers write the same story every time, even if they think theyÕre doing something different. I noticed that with my work, and IÕm trying to not be ashamed of it. It must be just what I want to work through, because even when I think IÕm doing something so different, it ends up still being about that stuckness that you described. What has writing and telling stories revealed to you about yourself? 
Mindy: It feels like IÕm writing something that I cannot see, and I felt this way especially with Late Night and Never Have I Ever. I write something because I feel like I canÕt see it unless I do it. With Late Night, I wanted to see Emma ThompsonÑwho is one of the greatest comedy actors of all time, and gorgeousÑnot in a period movie, where sheÕs playing a grieving grandmother. I wanted to see her be funny and sexy in New York City with a high-powered job. And with Never Have I Ever, I wanted to see a corny, dorky Indian American girl who isnÕt a quiet side character. So, lately, IÕm writing what I want to see. But I think what you said about telling a story that will somehow heal meÑthatÕs a deeper and more honest answer, I just hadnÕt thought of it. So, now thatÕs going to be my answer for my master class. 

MORT SAHL 
August 2017 
Judd: How are you, Mort? 
Mort: Good, man. 
Judd: I watched your Periscope last night. And it was fucking hysterical. 
Mort: Thank you for looking. 
Judd: Oh, my gosh. You were tearing it up. 
Mort: Well, we turned the corner there. You know, weÕve been doing it for a year. And you can feel it now. 
Judd: I really enjoyed watching it. But I want to go back to the beginning with you and track your career a bit. You wanted to be a comedy writer first, and then, when that wasnÕt working out, you became a performer. But when you were in high school, were you interested in comedy? 
Mort: No, no. You know, when I was in high school, the war was on and I was interested inÑRoosevelt was the president and I wanted to be a paratrooper and I wanted to get the girl. I was in the movies. All that stuff. When I came out of the military, I didnÕt fit in anywhere. I liked jazz a lot and hung around the Kelly Band and eventually, when the singers would take a break, I would get up onstage. I wasnÕt an instant hit. Everybody wondered what I was trying to prove and it took a long time. And then I followed a girl up here to Berkeley andÑromance was always in the way. She said to me, ÒIf you can do this, weÕll have a better life. If you canÕt, weÕll have a terrible life and youÕll blame me because you canÕt do it.Ó 
Judd: Yeah. 
Mort: So she sent me over to Hungry I. It was a folk club. I got up to do it and it was a disaster for a long time. Long silences. Finally, I threw the suit away and got the sweater and began to talk personally, and thatÕs when I realized there was a constituency out there that isnÕt heard from. I donÕt think it would have happened in another town. 
Judd: The owner of the Hungry I believed in you even when you hadnÕt figured it out yet? 
Mort: That was Enrique. He used to run the sound system. At first, he tried to fire me, but then he was the one who said: Take your tie off, take your coat off. And I began to go against authority. You know, McCarthy was around then. Nixon was around then. 
Judd: Good targets. 
Mort: Yeah. And I began to talk about the blacklist of the writers. That was the first real breakthrough. 
Judd: What were the jokes? 
Mort: They had just sent the ten writers to prison, Trumbo among them. And the joke was, every time the Russians put an American in jail, we put an American in jail. That was the breakthrough. 
Judd: ThatÕs funny. Were there other political comedians at the time? 
Mort: Not that I remember. Nobody. And that was one of my first disappointments with the Jewish people. I thought they were the guys who were gonna speak up! They didnÕt. They were doing what they considered commercial. 
Judd: Who was the hot comedian in the clubs when you were first starting to do political comedy? 
Mort: They were all guys in suits. Paul Gray. And of course, Gleason. Danny Thomas. And most of them worked what they call blue, which is the bedroom. Now itÕs the bathroom. So again, it was a fight. You know: ÒItÕs too intellectual. Nobody will understand it.Ó Finally I went to New York and did the Blue Angel with Jonathan Winters, and Pat Weaver came in. He was a great man. He said, ÒNo, everybody will understand it.Ó I did The Steve Allen Show while I was there and Steve was a saint. 
Judd: He supported all the great smart comedians. 
Mort: The best. The best guy of all time. And then of course everybody wanted to come to the Hungary I, so I brought them all out. I brought Woody out here. I brought Cosby out here. Cosby, that great American. WhoÕs carrying the quaaludes, you know? 
Judd: I hear it was a lifetime hobby. 
Mort: Oh yeah. Forever. 
Judd: What do you make of, like, a guy who is almost like a pedophile priest and presented himself as this pure person? 
Mort: Have you ever known any entertainers who couldnÕt get a date after the job? 
Judd: Exactly. 
Mort: IÕm in love. The rock guys didnÕt have any problems finding a girl. 
Judd: Well, I guess it wasnÕt about that. 
Mort: HeÕs about him. 
Judd: Yeah. 
Mort: When I went to work for Jim Garrison in New Orleans, Cosby was eating dinner in my house with servants. Eating dinner. I had gotten him the television show. I had gotten him the Playboy jazz festival. I had gotten him into the Hungry I. And he stood up and he said, ÒWell, where youÕre going, I canÕt go. IÕve got a wife and kids.Ó And he left the food. 
Judd: Because he didnÕt want to support your investigation into the Kennedy assassination? 
Mort: Actually, nobody did. There was a tremendous break there. Everybody said I was crazy, which is easier than recognizing a national malady. 
Judd: After all these years, where are your thoughts about the assassination? 
Mort: Exactly where we were. You know, I was a credentialed investigator in the office and IÕve got a plaque here from the four DAs. It says, ÒTo the best friend Jack Kennedy ever had.Ó And Garrison said to me, ÒToo bad heÕll never know it.Ó But as I told the audience last night, the CIA killed the president. We took a few bumps along the way, and Garrison said to me, ÒIf you go public with that, theyÕre gonna say youÕre paranoid.Ó But we had a lot of problems. They dropped LSD on me. The wheels came off my car. Garrison was chased by Cubans in the menÕs room of the United terminal at LAX. ThatÕs a long way from the stage of the Hungry I. 
Judd: What do you think is the main piece of information that the public ignores about the Kennedy assassination? 
Mort: That the president did not want to proceed in Vietnam. He fought with [Curtis] LeMay and the generals. In other words, he double-crossed them. They thought they elected a fascist. 
Judd: Let me ask you a question, Mort. Do you think, when they assassinate the president, is it because on some level they think theyÕre fighting the communists? Or is it for financial gain? 
Mort: No, itÕs strictly buying guns. Just like Cheney did. Just like Obama. They donÕt care whether they win. They just want it to be perpetual. Even though it sounds absurd for a comedian to be opposing the military industrial complex, I didnÕt know why everybody else didnÕt want to. 
Judd: If you were a religious leader, what would you preach? What have you learned about people and what would you say to people about how they should live their lives? 
Mort: Well, you should be in love if you can possibly work it out. You shouldnÕt be by yourself. You should stand up for what this country is. JeffersonÕs America. The place that my grandfather came from Russia. 
Judd: So it all comes down toÑI mean even through all of the attacking of politicsÉ 
Mort: People using each other. 
Judd: Who do you think has explored that territory well? 
Mort: Jonathan Winters. HeÕs a total individual. He was a goy, a Republican, didnÕt curse, but had no mercy about exposing the manipulators. A total original. 
Judd: Who made you laugh the most? 
Mort: Herb Shriner. Remember him? 
Judd: Absolutely. 
Mort: I liked Herb Shriner. I thought The Fred Allen Show was good. But nobody really busted me up because they all thought the audience was dumb and you had to be kind of commercial. IÕm not sure the audience is dumb. I think itÕs a rationale for not giving them anything. 
Judd: What are your favorite movies? 
Mort: Hardly any now. ThereÕs nothing. First of all these guysÑtheyÕre filmmakers. TheyÕre not guys like Richard Brooks, or Robert Aldridge. Look at why we won. We were the good guys. And what did we win? We won the girl. WhoÕs the girl now? I mean itÕs all very pessimistic. 
Judd: Did you ever hear about Bill Hicks? 
Mort: Yeah, I did. Judd: Did you enjoy any of his work? Mort: No. I thought it was on the nose. In other words, weÕre noble and theyÕre 
the bad guys. It was on the nose. You know, I was a great friend of LennyÕs. Judd: Lenny Bruce? Mort: Yeah. And heÕs totally misinterpreted. He liked girls and jazz and dope. 
He said to me, ÒI donÕt know how a bright guy like you would think thereÕs a difference between the two parties. TheyÕre all hustlers.Ó 
Judd: What did you think of Lenny BruceÕs politics? Mort: He didnÕt have any. I knew him very well and he had none. I thought he was far from profound. But they talk about him like heÕs Jesus. 
Judd: What do you think thatÕs about? Mort: He took a chance and got crucified. But nothing he did was profound. I 
want to emphasize that. Nothing. He was all right. By the way, the audiences used to walk out on him. It was kind of rank. Now itÕs rank and it isnÕt funny. Judd: Does it blow your mind a little bit that you seem to have interacted with 
everybody? 
Mort: Well, thatÕs the miracle. ThatÕs the miracle of AmericaÑthat you can get up with an idea and you can do that. Judd: You were friends with both Kennedy and Reagan. What were the 
similarities and differences between them? 
Mort: Kennedy was highly intellectualized and a little bit skeptical. And he had his trouble with me Õcause he said, ÒYou really love Adlai Stevenson. YouÕre working here but you really love Stevenson.Ó He was hoping eventually IÕd come to love him. He was very very subtle, you know. Reagan was straight up. And he knew audiences. Before I did my show at the White House he said to me, ÒTheyÕre not gonna laugh. DonÕt get discouraged. TheyÕre gonna look at me as an authority figure and when I laugh, they will laugh. ItÕs gonna be fine.Ó And he had one other thing on his side: His wife really loved him. Nancy Reagan once told me that Gable said to her, ÒNo matter how well he does at Warner Bros. or as governor, be at that door when he comes home for dinner because thatÕs what we all dream about.Ó 
Judd: What did you think of him as a politician? 
Mort: Well, the fascists had their fun. Look at Grenada. Look at the Contras. 
Judd: Yeah. 
Mort: The other side was doing it all: Jim Baker and the Bush side. I knew him pretty well, too. As a matter of fact, when George Bush ran, Jerry Weintraub threw a party for him in Malibu and I was the entertainment. And at the end of that show, we were waiting for the car parkers to bring the cars around. Carson and I made up, because Carson had thrown me off over the Garrison stuff and didnÕt talk to me all those years. 
Judd: Did Carson apologize to you? 
Mort: No, no, he was very withdrawn, as you remember. 
Judd: But he really hurt you, though. 
Mort: It was like ten years of not being able to make the rent. 
Judd: Carson was another one of those guys that presented himself in a certain way but was a really dark guy. 
Mort: Oh yeah. I mean, antisocial out there in Malibu. He came from game shows. Remember, he never worked live like we did. 
Judd: A big drinker, right? 
Mort: And everything else. 
Judd: Do you think that all of those guysÑlike the Rat Pack, and Carson, and Jerry LewisÑwere they just on a rampage of bad behavior? What would they do if there was a YouTube back then? 
Mort: Lewis was crazy. 
Judd: Were they a bunch of good guys or a bunch of just narcissistic lunatics? 
Mort: Sinatra kept romance alive in this country for fifty years. He was talented. But when I got on the wrong side he disappeared on me, too. 
Judd: And he had his problems with the Kennedys as well. 
Mort: Uh, yeah. We went bananas when Jack wouldnÕt stay at the house. Because the old man wouldnÕt let him. So he went to Bing CrosbyÕs house. And by the way, Crosby and Bob Hope split up over Kennedy. 
Judd: Really? How so? 
Mort: Because they were both Republicans. Crosby was a Catholic on top of it. But heÕs gonna vote for the Irish Catholic. So they split. So did Poitier and Belafonte. And afterward, there wasnÕt anybody around except me, the enemy. 
Judd: Do you generally have a good feeling about people? YouÕre pointing out all of these problems and hypocrisies, but overall: WhatÕs your feeling about human nature? 
Mort: I think itÕs gonna be like ItÕs a Wonderful Life. I think weÕre all gonna rally to Jimmy Stewart by the third act. 

NATHAN FIELDER 
January 2020 
Judd: What makes you laugh? 
Nathan: I laugh a lot when IÕm uncomfortable. And I put a high value on comedy that gets me to laugh out loud when IÕm sitting alone. 
Judd: WhatÕs an example of that? 
Nathan: Curb Your Enthusiasm was a show that consistently made me do that. Also, a lot of dumb stuff on YouTube, like, people falling and farting. 
Judd: ThatÕs always the funniest. 
Nathan: I know. And thatÕs the thing: No matter what you do, you canÕt compete with comedy like that. Even though we had stuff we planned or wanted to do [on Nathan for You], we were always chasing the unquantifiable moments that would emotionally make you laugh and you donÕt know why. Those moments are so hard to find. I donÕt get that feeling with scripted things as much. You have to do a bunch of stuff and just hope they happen. IÕm not really trying to make any political statement, or trying to provoke in that way. 
Judd: Are the people who end up on your show generally happy with how theyÕre presented? 
Nathan: WeÕve actually never had anyone who, after the show aired, has come back and been like, ÒYou guys tricked me,Ó or, ÒIÕm upset with how this went.Ó Their main worry is how theyÕre going to be portrayed, but weÕve actually edited out the hateful stuff people have said, because we donÕt want the audience to hate them. 
Judd: When I watch the show, what I tuned in to the most is the loneliness. ThereÕs a lot of material that feels like itÕs about your characterÕs loneliness, or people trying to reach out and connect. So, underneath the laughs is something thatÕs really powerful. You are captivated by it because, deep down, you hope they donÕt go bankrupt or fail in some way. 
Nathan: The recession of 2008 was the first economic collapse IÕd experienced in my lifetime. My parents had been through something like it before, but I really thought the world was going to end. I read so much about the housing crisis, and I came to the conclusion that running a successful business is about finding that legal loophole that no one has patched up yet and exploiting that for profit. When that loophole gets patched up, theyÕll find the new one. It made you feel like anyone whoÕs not at these big companies that are doing this really have no chance. 
Judd: Because they donÕt know how to do it. 
Nathan: Or theyÕre put in a place where they have no ability to win, no matter what. There are so many confusing messages, and people are telling you, ÒHere, you can do this. HereÕs a loan.Ó Everything was based around tricking people. So, when I met these business owners, I felt that as long as what they were doing was legal and it makes money, then thereÕs no reason not to do it. You can tell that a lot of these people donÕt necessarily think itÕs right for them, but at the same time, they realize that this is just the world that they exist in, and if they donÕt embrace these practices, they might be left behind. You come to see how unnatural or how disconnected from real emotions the business world is, and I think it makes it hard for people to connect, in general. 
Judd: Did you find that most of the people who came to you had to be particularly gullible or ignorant? Or did you find that a range of people fell for it? 
Nathan: A lot of people watch my show and think, Oh, if I was in that situation, I would just laugh or I would say, ÒThatÕs absurd.Ó But I donÕt really think thatÕs the case. First, thereÕs a lot of pressure when there are cameras around. And at the same time, given the right sales pitch, most people would agree to do stuff to save or make money. Then it just keeps escalating and escalating to the point that thereÕs no way to bail. 
Judd: Do they know itÕs candid comedy, or do they really believe that itÕs sincere? 
Nathan: We try to avoid people who ask a lot of questions, and we try to find people who seem to be open to an experience outside of their day-to-day existence. We definitely donÕt say itÕs a comedy show when we book people or anything like that. As time goes on, people do get a sense that thereÕs like something a little off about this, but also businesses become so absurd with their marketing that I was always able to justify it as something that would get attention. We walked the line between comedy and marketing. 
Judd: What was the pitch for the show? 
Nathan: I initially pitched this show as me being a consumer advocate. Then we turned in a draft of that version, and I donÕt think it worked quite as well. ItÕs hard when youÕre attacking businesses, because unless youÕre doing all big businesses, you donÕt want to say anything bad about someoneÕs small business. I just have a hard time criticizing, or punching down, that way. 
Judd: When did you discover what it was? 
Nathan: At some point, Comedy Central said it just didnÕt feel big enough. At first we were like, ThatÕs so annoying. They obviously donÕt get it. And then we thought, What if we just sold people, like, poo? We had the idea of poo frozen yogurt as almost a joke. The more we thought about that concept, though, we started to realize that that could work, and that Comedy Central was right. We had already figured out we wanted to help businesses, but then they pushed us to come up with bigger ideas that would anchor the show, and then I could still do all the more subtle, human moments that I liked. And it was great. Comedy Central saved me from poverty. 
Judd: How did you develop your persona on the show? 
Nathan: I first started doing this stuff with real people in Canada. I got hired to do work on a show called This Hour Has 22 Minutes, which is kind of like The Daily Show, where I was a correspondent who did these consumer advocate pieces. I noticed they would screen the show in front of a live audience. I had stuff in there that I thought was funny, but people would also laugh at stuff that I didnÕt intend to be jokes. There was something about how I was acting that people were finding funny. I generally have a fairly flat demeanor, and I just kind of leaned into that. 
Judd: You realized that you were part of the joke. 
Nathan: I was going over the top with trying to be funny, but then people were just like, ÒWell, you as a human are so sad and pathetic that we find that funny.Ó With time, I leaned more and more into that with the show. I didnÕt say anything not truthful about myself. I use my real name, my real background. A lot of interactions are structured in a way where itÕs not awkward. When youÕre talking about something business-related, you can interact comfortably. But the second you start to have a personal conversation, it gets really weird, because thatÕs not the reason those people are there. The subtext was always that this whole show is born from my desire to just be around people. IÕm trying in little ways to create friendships, to see if people actually like me as a person, or if theyÕre just there for this other stuff. People come up with extremely roundabout ways to accomplish very simple things, because no one teaches you how to be social. I got a business degree and learned all sorts of things, but no one is teaching you how to be close to other people. ItÕs one of the saddest things to say. 
Judd: IÕm so interested in why this kind of comedy works. Why am I enjoying this? I think thatÕs why that format interested people so much. 
Nathan: The series finale of the show was this feature-length episode, almost like a movie. And there was this Bill Gates impersonator we met in the second seasonÑa seventy-eight-year-old man whoÕs also named BillÑand in between the times that we shot with him, he would come around the office and talk about this woman he dated when he was twenty, who he really regrets not marrying and had recently been trying to track down. That was just in the background for so long, and we were like, ÒWell, maybe we can incorporate this into the show in some way.Ó Of course, we didnÕt know how she would react to him and what we would end up with for the episode. As things go on, youÕre like, Okay, what can we do with this material? How can we adapt? Sometimes itÕs not till the edit where I really realize what somethingÕs about. I would like to think I had amazing foresight, but a lot of the time we just plan broad strokes. 
Judd: And that could have taken a much darker turn, or just had no ending. 
Nathan: We try to think through various ways that it could play out, like, ÒOkay, if this happens, whatÕs our plan? If that happens, whatÕs our plan?Ó I can think through all those scenarios, but oftentimes what happens is something very different than what we expect. And my weird instinct is to feel like I planned poorly, rather than just realize that life canÕt be controlled. 
Judd: And in the end, when he found her, she was kind to him. 
Nathan: She was. I mean, sheÕs married, and she has grandchildren. I think, in his head, Bill thought that she still wanted to be with him, and he was very much living as his twenty-year-old self, even though heÕs in this old-man body. I really got caught up in that whole thing while we were filming, and it also made me think about my own life, because Bill had been all about his career and prioritized his career over his personal life. He moved from Arkansas to L.A. to be an actor. ThatÕs why that relationship with the woman [had] ended. A lot of itÕs unclear, since I canÕt get a clear story of everything from him. IÕve tried to think a lot about what about him makes me laugh so much. He just has this rhythm and cadence that really gets me; heÕs almost the embodiment of something that I donÕt understand why itÕs funny, or why itÕs interesting. 
Judd: What did he make of it afterward? 
Nathan: For his whole life, he wanted to become an actor. And he never quite had a career of it. But when the episode screened, we did a big premiere with, like, fifteen hundred people, and at the end, everyone gave Bill this big standing ovation. I think he was really touched by it, and I donÕt know if he sees it the same way I do, but I think it brings him back to those momentsÑ of her and him, and his regret. And I think he was happy with it. 
Judd: What made you want to end the show? 
Nathan: I felt like I had cornered myself into something that I couldnÕt really keep doing. Basically, every season of that show, I thought, This is the last. We just got so lucky every year. Outsiders are like, ÒOh, this is great. Can you do more?Ó But itÕs very hard for an episode to work out. There needs to be a lot of things that happen, and that took a lot out of me, because IÕm performing this version thatÕs twenty percent my real self, and I had to get my personality back after each season. We started to feel the constraints of it being a show about business, where the ideas that we were most excited about as time went on were the nonbusiness ones. And if it was going to become a show that wasnÕt related to business at all, I was like, Well, why keep doing it as this show? I also wanted to end it because I had this fear I was going to screw it up. 
Judd: This is the type of thing where, if you ever had an idea, you can always bring the character back. 
Nathan: If IÕm doing more stuff like this, I think itÕll be me and some other type of environment. But I was watching your Garry Shandling documentary around the time we did the last episode, and what really resonated with me was when Garry talked about how he was always just trying to find projects that would allow him to be closer to himself. That really stuck with me. I think, over time, you start getting more comfortable as yourself when youÕre doing comedy, and then you home in on who you are. That was my experience with the show, where I felt that, as time went on, it was harder for me to be further from that self. 
Judd: Shandling always said that itÕs just so hard for people to connect, to open up, and be real and love each other. He said that all stories are obstacles to love. ThatÕs why itÕs fascinating to hear how you didnÕt necessarily know that your show was pulling you toward a certain direction, and then it ends with this episode thatÕs beautiful and compassionate toward this strange man. It becomes as universal as it ever could be, because on some level, weÕre all Bill. 
Nathan: A lot of people have said that theyÕve made changes in their lives after watching that episode, because they realize they donÕt want to work their whole lives to accomplish this one goal and [then], in their old age, find that they had left all this stuff behind that they actually cared about. 
Judd: But youÕre feeling your way through that, as you do it. YouÕre not philosophizing as itÕs happening. 
Nathan: I really just started with like, Oh, poo is funny, how can we do a poo joke? And then, from there, a world evolves around that joke. 
Judd: I remember that episode, where this one guy is on a date at a fast-food restaurant. And you were feeding him lines to say on the date, through an earpiece. 
Nathan: That was Solomon. Basically, Comedy Central came to me and was like, ÒQuiznos wants to do product placement in your show.Ó I had already been talking to Solomon about his love life, so we were already planning on doing something with him. And when we told Comedy Central that, they were like, ÒCan you do his date at a Quiznos?Ó I was like, ÒCan the Quiznos rep be in the show?Ó They agreed; so we did it. [Solomon] was trying to make the date work, but he also had to say these Quiznos taglines, like ÒMmm, toasty.Ó 
Judd: But what was weirdly powerful was that his date seemed very warm and positive with him. YouÕre enjoying the comedy of the moment, but itÕs not cruel. ItÕs kind of the opposite. ItÕs about acceptance. 
Nathan: People do think that IÕm trying to take people down a notch or make fun of them, but really, itÕs not about the other person or me. The joke is in the space between people, where weÕre struggling to be on the same page. I really believe itÕs impossible for two people to be on the same page. When I was younger, social anxiety was the number one stress for me. As a high school student, I was into magic, which obviously is something you do to try to break the ice with people. There are always these excuses to talk to people Ñyou can never just go to someone and say, ÒDo you mind if we have a conversation right now? IÕd like to talk to you,Ó because thatÕs weird. So, you need a trick to talk to someone, you need an excuse. 
Judd: ThatÕs why people become the funny person in school. 
Nathan: ThatÕs probably part of it. Most of high school was just awful for me, but then, in college, I went in the opposite direction, where rather than trying to hide all my insecurities, I leaned into them more. I would try to exude confidence I didnÕt have, and it was really awkwardÑpeople could see through me. But I was just like, All right, however people see me is how I am. And that was a turning point. Because then, no one has anything on you. 
Judd: I find that part of it fascinating, where we find ourselves and drop our fears. And then the world says, ÒThis is great. I love that.Ó ThatÕs the lesson. WeÕre all trying to learn in some way that weÕre okay. 
Nathan: That was also a large part of the show, where no one is saying what theyÕre thinking. ThatÕs the heart of the problem. Without ever saying what youÕre thinking, youÕre always just playing a game, and youÕre never able to really form connections. 
Judd: Shandling would always say that, in Los Angeles, everyoneÕs wearing a mask. No one says what theyÕre thinking, no one speaks the truth. When they do, itÕs a huge deal. 
Nathan: ThatÕs true. Even now, thereÕs this underlying stress in my chest about talking about myself. IÕm terrified of being on camera, that IÕm going to humiliate myself all the time. ThatÕs a real motivator for making sure somethingÕs good. 
Judd: I feel that way about work. The thing I like about making things is that anything you made in the past has no bearing on whether your new thing will work. That fear of humiliation is always terrifying, but it wakes you up and makes you want to work hard. But then I started having anxiety when I was watching TV, because I was burnt out, and I felt like I was bored of stories and that IÕd seen them all before. 
Nathan: ThatÕs another interesting thing about how the show developed. I was very aware that comedians had done shows with real people before. I was aware of the Sacha Baron Cohen stuff, The Daily Show, et cetera. So, it was almost a process of elimination, where itÕs like, ÒWell, we canÕt do that, canÕt do that, canÕt do thatÉÓ We couldnÕt do any joke that felt like it could be on those other shows. 
Judd: What were the ones you thought came together perfectly? What are your moments of pride now? 
Nathan: My favorite moments are the ones that went so differently than what we planned. Where we scrambled to figure it out. ThatÕs what I love about it: inexplicable moments between people that are somehow funny but donÕt follow any of the normal rules of why somethingÕs funny. There was that one episode that was about a gas station rebate, but to claim the rebate, customers had to drop it off in person at the top of a mountain. But then, when they got there, the rebate box was hidden, and people stayed overnight. That was the first one we did that was really elaborate. But then, I think, once we did that Dumb Starbucks one, we realized [that] just skirting around the law actually gives you a lot of story to work with. Like, How do we make this legal? 
Judd: WhatÕs next for you? I know youÕre directing and producing, but how do you approach your creative career? 
Nathan: IÕve always tried not to have goals. Instead, I make sure I like what IÕm doing, and then once I stop liking it, I stop. But I have a couple of shows for HBO, one IÕm producing and then one IÕm going to direct and act in. It has elements of Nathan for You. It has a real-people element to it, but itÕs a different thing. IÕm also doing something with the Safdie brothers [Joshua and Benjamin]. IÕm trying to make more things, and just have more things going on, and IÕm doing better at it. 
Judd: ItÕs funny. When youÕre open, things just present themselves. 
Nathan: When I first moved to Toronto and started doing comedy, I had this video camera, and whenever I would have an idea, IÕd just go out and do it. I would shoot it in a day, with no planning. There was something so great about being able to execute an idea just like that. When things go on for a long period of time, it sucks out some of that energy a little bit, but I try to hold on to that. I guess thatÕs the appeal of the reality-based stuff. It keeps that sense of Well, anything could happen any day. 

PETE DAVIDSON 
February 2020 
Judd: People might not expect it of you, but you have a great love of film and a great education in film. I know, because the whole time we made The King of Staten Island, you would always say to me, ÒAfter work today, letÕs go to where I live,Ó which is in the basement of your motherÕs house, where you had built a man cave. And you would always say, ÒTonight, weÕre going to watch movies.Ó And then I always would not go. 
Pete: You never went. 
Judd: I didnÕt really need to see [The] King of Comedy at two A.M. on a shooting night. 
Pete: On mushrooms with me and my homes. Steve Buscemi came to the cave. The cinematographer Bob Elswit came twice. He had a really great time. He signed the wall. 
Judd: Your grandfather was the guy that got you interested in movies? 
Pete: His father owned a movie theater back when thatÕs all that existed, so he grew up watching every single movie ever. And he had to babysit me all the time, so he made me watch a bunch of movies, his favorites. So, I know, like, Ernest Borgnine and all those old guys from the forties. When my grandfather got a camera, we started making our own movies. And my mom got really mad, because we would make a version of Mission: Impossible where I would jump off of shit, or come down from the ceiling in my cowboy boots. I was, like, six years old. I was in constant danger, and [my grandfather] would go, ÒWhat? WeÕre making movies here!Ó I did that till I was about nine or ten, and then I got my own camera. Then me and my cousin and my friends started making movies like Jackass. I think every kid does that in high school. We would also try to make some sketches. 
Judd: What was the most dangerous Jackass stunt that you did? 
Pete: It was all done in Staten Island, so thereÕs not a lot you could work with. But we jumped through a thorn bush in our underwear. We went right through it, and we were like, ÒThis is some shit. If only the Jackass boys could see us.Ó 
Judd: Now, with [The] King of Staten Island, how was it for you to make a movie without your grandfather? 
Pete: It was cool to do, because now I have something to show him. He was always showing me what we made, and now I got to show him what I made. HeÕs in it, and he arguably gets the biggest laugh in the entire movie. 
Judd: How was it having him on the set? 
Pete: You really got the best of Steve Davidson, for sure. It was great. ItÕs fun watching him on set because he didnÕt know that one scene takes eight to twelve hours sometimes. HeÕd be like, ÒPeter, if you move any slower, this picture will be done in 2030.Ó It was just adorable for him to be on set and see how things are actually done. 
Judd: He felt I was abusing him, I think. 
Pete: Halfway through, he went, ÒThis director that you got over here, Mr. Apatow. I donÕt know what he wants from me. IÕm very sweaty, and my ParkinsonÕs is about to kick in.Ó And youÕd be like, ÒAnd action!Ó 
Judd: WhatÕs it like for you now that the movieÕs over? What was your hope for it versus what it became? 
Pete: I wanted all the people in it to crush more than anything. We just have such a good group of people. IÕm lucky all my friends are hilarious. I was so glad that we got to put them in a place where they could hit home runs, and they all did. 
Judd: Do you remember when we first met? I was working on Trainwreck, and Amy Schumer was talking to me about you. IÕd asked her, ÒWhoÕs funny? Who should we consider putting in the movie?Ó She said, ÒYou need to see this Pete Davidson kid. ItÕs crazy how funny he is at nineteen.Ó So, I said, ÒLetÕs put him in this little scene in the hospital, just so we can say we were the first people to put him in something.Ó 
Pete: I was so happy that it wasnÕt cut. That day was so crazy. IÕd never shot anything before, so I didnÕt know that you get there and then you just sit in your trailer. I didnÕt bring much stuff with me, or deodorant. I was just sitting in there and started smelling like shit, because itÕs the middle of summer in Manhattan. Then theyÕre like, ÒYouÕre wearing a tank top in this scene.Ó And I was like, Fuck. I was super in my head about stinking up the Apatow set that entire time. 
Judd: My monitor was far away, so I didnÕt notice. 
Pete: I was very lucky. But it was so much fun. I got to improv with Bill Hader, the year he left SNL. It was just super crazy. 
Judd: Then Bill recommended you to Lorne, and I bumped into Lorne and talked about you. Then, suddenly, youÕre on SNL. Afterward, we talked about making a movie together. 
Pete: I remember we tried to write one script, but our writing wasnÕt there yet. Then there was another idea that we had that was floating between us for a little bit. Then we wanted to do something where we could get my mom involved, but there wasnÕt any heart to it. In the end, we combined these two ideas. 
Judd: In the beginning, you wanted to write something about a guy whose mom hasnÕt dated in a long time and about how you wanted your mom to be happy and to be in a relationship. I remember, in our conversations, I kept saying, ÒBut wouldnÕt you not want her to be in a relationship?Ó How did you feel when we started kicking the script around, and it became more and more personal? 
Pete: IÕve always been pretty open, but I think itÕs much easier to talk about your past than it is to make a movie about it, because youÕre doing a version of yourself, so you donÕt want to be disappointed in yourself while youÕre watching yourself. That part was weird. 
Judd: Did it feel strange to write a fictional version of your life and your family? 
Pete: It was pretty accurate. We just switched jobs and the thing that I was aspiring toward. The hard part was being in the firehouse, because I donÕt go to the firehouse. I had to hang out with those boys again. The only time I would ever stop at a firehouse was if I ever had to go to the bathroom. I would be like, ÒHey, my dad died in 9/11. Can I take a shit in your bathroom?Ó And theyÕre like, ÒYeah, heÕs a hero. Right this way.Ó 
Judd: How was that for you, having your dadÕs friends as extras and as actors in the movie? 
Pete: It meant as much to them as it meant to me, because I just wanted to show my appreciation, and we also wanted the movie to be as authentic and real as possible. We all had the same mission, and it was great. We all just want to make this as real as possible. 
Judd: YouÕve told me how youÕve spent a lot of your life avoiding all these people. I remember, there was a day where we have a scene in a bar, and we decided to have a lot of your dadÕs friends in the bar. And before the scene, you were worried about it. But on the day of, you seemed to like having them all around. 
Pete: It was really nerve-wracking at first, because we get to deal with emotions all the time through watching movies and making stuff, and we heal through that. But these guys havenÕt seen anything in twenty years, and they havenÕt seen me in all that time, so all of this is just a fucking straight shot to them. I was really worried about them. But then, having them there made me more emotional and made me want to do a better job and put me more in the zone. So, I think it helped a lot. 
Judd: All those guys really loved your dad. 
Pete: They meet for drinks on his birthday every year. They listen to Creedence, his favorite band, and they do this every year. Every time they cough or anything, everybody just stops for a second, because they were all down there doing Ground Zero duty. ItÕs a real brotherhood. And I wanted people to see that these guys arenÕt just fucking around. ThereÕs this common misconception that they are just sitting around and that there arenÕt that many fires. Do you have any idea what a fucking fire is? I think we really showed that, when the bell rings, itÕs time to go. And thereÕs a chance that not everyone will come back. 
Judd: ThereÕs a scene in the movie that was a hard scene to shoot, where, for the first time, your character goes with them on a ride-along and watches them put out an apartment building thatÕs on fire. That was one of the harder days for you. 
Pete: Even though it was fake and all set up, it was still just, like, ÒWow.Ó This is what theyÕre doing five days out of the week. ItÕs them, leaving everything behind just to go in and save people. It was really seeing that for the first time in person and on camera. That was a lot. 
Judd: In one of the first conversations that we had, as we were trying to figure out the emotional life of the character, you talked about how you felt like firemen shouldnÕt have kids. You told me that thatÕs something that you felt as a young person. 
Pete: I did feel that way, and this movie made me feel differently toward it, because you get to hear the other perspective. Previously, I just shut out that other side. I remember being at a Christmas party at the firehouse when I was five or six, and everybodyÕs having a good time, and then, all of a sudden, that bell rings. And then all the kids are just sitting there, and youÕre like, 
Wow, I hope they come back. 
Judd: So, your reaction to losing your dad was Why does he have this job? Why would he have taken a job this risky? 
Pete: There was a little bit of anger. When he died, I wondered if he [had been] doing this or that, would he still have died? My parents [had] just got divorced, and he moved out, so his work schedule changed. So, I would ask, Would he even have been working that day? Is it my fault? Should he have called in sick that day? ThereÕs a million scenarios that you play in your head. You drive yourself nuts. I was just seven years old. YouÕre not supposed to learn what death is until youÕre in high school. I was literally trying to be, like, a detective. Then IÕd watch TV, and certain people give their answers of what happened that day. I should have been watching cartoons. I didnÕt understand anything that was going on. 
Judd: Seven is a hard age for that to happen. If you were five, youÕre almost too young to even realize whatÕs going on. Seven is just old enough to be really confused. 
Pete: YouÕre just starting to be a person at that age. It was so weird. But it would have been worse if it happened now or when I was a teenager, because of the accessibility to drugs and stuff. It would have been so bad. I already wanted to kill myself when I was seven. If I wanted to do that when I was seventeen and had the access, it would have been a wrap for sure. 
Judd: What were you like when you were five or six, before your dad died? 
Pete: I was bouncing off the walls. I was a big Jim Carrey and Adam Sandler fan, so I was always walking around being weird. For the most part, we were just a normal Staten Island family, where there was a lot of love, but couldnÕt stay together. Parents got divorced, split time with the kids. It was pretty chill. I had a very chill childhood up until that day. My parents got divorced six months to a year before 9/11, and thatÕs when I was starting to understand, Oh, so heÕs not going to be in this house anymore. I was still just getting used to not seeing him at home every day, and then I didnÕt see him at all. 
Judd: When my parents got divorced when I was a kid, that screwed me up enough to do the things that IÕve done. It made me want to be creative and to live with a chip on my shoulder. 
Pete: It fuels you. You almost think, If I kick ass, maybe theyÕll get back together. ThereÕs just so many things you think as a kid. 
Judd: You were telling me that the week before 9/11, you switched schools? 
Pete: It was a Catholic school. I was there for a week and was not making many friends, and then 9/11 happened. NobodyÕs nice to the new kid, but after that happened, everyone would try to be nice, but kids donÕt know how they are coming across. So, they would say to me, ÒHey, I heard your dad died. You want to be friends?Ó And IÕd be like, ÒNo.Ó 
Judd: You also sent me a video of you, where you were playing football. ItÕs two weeks after 9/11. And the coach gives this really bizarre speech, about how we need to respect Pete, but also heÕs berating the parents for getting too emotional about the games, and using 9/11 as a way to convince them that theyÕre getting too crazy when they root for their kids. 
Pete: He made it about him, which is my favorite thing. Nobody knew how to deal with it. And because it was New York, there are cameras everywhere. So, if IÕm playing football, thereÕs going to be people there. Everybody wants the 9/11 familyÕs opinion for that little blip on the news. I met the president when I was seven. He gave me a medal. I just went, ÒThanks, Mr. President.Ó And he said, ÒStay in college.Ó That was it. 
Judd: Where was this? 
Pete: This was at the White House, on the front lawn. They gave everybody the Medal of Valor. We all got to receive the medal on behalf of our lost loved one. And I got to shake hands with George Bush. It was just weird. I watch clips of it now, and IÕm like, Oh fuck, thatÕs horrible. I canÕt believe this is what they thought would be helpful. And then my sister, who was five years old, fainted, because it was like three hundred degrees outside, and Laura Bush carried her into the White House. 
Judd: At the time, did you think this was all weird? 
Pete: I didnÕt get it at all. I was like, Wow, my dad must have been some guy. ThatÕs all I thought. This guy must have been the coolest if IÕm shaking hands with the president. But I couldnÕt really grieve on my own. People were always inserting themselves. ThatÕs the New York way of dealing with things. ItÕs like, your dadÕs dead. Got a PS3 with the new Madden, though. Ultimately, itÕs just distraction, and then you never deal with it. 
Judd: YouÕve said that it was just such a bizarre aftermath. My mom died eleven years ago, and no one ever mentions it after that first week. 
Pete: 9/11 is mentioned on a weekly basis, in comparison. 
Judd: Does it feel like you can never get over it because it just never goes away? 
Pete: IÕve still never fully dealt with it. I still donÕt remember all that went on. When I was little, I blocked a lot of it out. I think it made it impossible for me to grieve, because there was such a fucking spectacle. But comedy saved me. It gives me a purpose and stuff to do. The first time I was ever happy was going to an open mic and just trying out a joke, and it got a little bit of a giggle. That feeling was the best. 
Judd: You were sixteen when you started doing stand-up, which is crazy. In some way, you developed a dark sense of humor from just having to deal with all of this. 
Pete: That, and I had no friends. I ripped my hair out all the time from anxiety, so I was a bald sixteen-year-old with braces, acne, pores everywhereÑso, I just thought, What do I have to lose? Then I started doing stand-up, hanging with older dudes and meeting people who were troubled like I was, but they had normal lives and girlfriends. I found this whole other outlet and this group of people that kind of saved my life. It wasnÕt in Staten Island, so I had to take the ferry. It felt like, IÕm leaving the island to go hang out with people who get it. 
Judd: Your mom had to make a deal with you that she would allow you to go to Manhattan by yourself as a child if you agreed to stay in school. 
Pete: My senior year at public school in Staten Island, you would get out of school at eleven A.M., and my mom was like, ÒThatÕs not happening, youÕre coming over to the school where I work, and you get out at three P.M.Ó So, I was like, ÒI just wonÕt show up to school,Ó and she was like, ÒYou can do stand-up as long you get up in the morning and go to school.Ó 
Judd: So, she let you roam the city alone. 
Pete: I was in weird bars at four in the morning when I was sixteen years old. 
Judd: You found this group of comedians who are accepting of a super weird, partially bald, depressed child. Did you feel like you found a world of acceptance or parental figures? 
Pete: I could tell these people how I feel. Because all the open mics were just people trying stuff out, so it felt like an AA meeting for me, but with jokes. Then, if you get a little giggle out of telling a joke about your dadÕs death, it became a nice, healing moment. And no one knows who the fuck you are, which is great. No one from Staten Island was there; it was all random people. 
Judd: Do you feel like [The King of Staten Island] is an extension of that in some way? 
Pete: The movie is my love letter to my mom. ItÕs my trying to end that part of my life, because she and I maybe got to a place where we can finally let go a little bit. And thereÕs art to it. 
Judd: What does your mom think about what the movie was saying to her? 
Pete: I think she got it. My momÕs been going out since then. I donÕt know if sheÕs going out on dates, but sheÕs like, ÒIÕm going to hang out with friends. IÕll be back at like two, three in the morning.Ó The last month or so, sheÕs been really social, so I think she got the point. The movie was me saying, ÒI donÕt want you to blow your life for me.Ó ThatÕs pretty much where it is. And itÕs also saying, you know, ÒI made a movie, so you should go date now.Ó 
Judd: And you and your grandfatherÑI would assume after making all those movies with you, it must blow his mind a little bit to see this. 
Pete: As soon as he gets paid for it, heÕs going to be thrilled. ThatÕs a real text from him. I wish I was joking. 
Judd: What do you think your dad would make of this movie? 
Pete: HeÕd be thrilled; he would get a kick out of it. He gets a kick out of shit like this. The last movie we saw together was Rush Hour 2. He loved comedy, and I think he would be thrilled, because some of his buddies are in the movie. ItÕs really real and full circle. He liked real stuff. 
Judd: Your dadÕs friends all talked about how funny he was. They would say, ÒHe was kind of like Pete.Ó He was the funny guy at the fire station. 
Pete: He was always hilarious. He was the guy that would show up late, and people would be like, ÒYou fuckinÕ asshole.Ó Like, nobody would be mad or anything like that. He was just the life of the party. 
Judd: You said you were really happy when you learned that your dad drank and partied and did coke back then. Because it freed you from the idea that he was perfect. 
Pete: It gave me a little insight into the divorce. It made me realize that he had his own issues; he had problems just like everybody else. But it also made me realize that even with all that, his morals were still intact, and none of that prevented him from being a hero. It made me feel good on two different levels. 
Judd: We put in that dedication to your dad at the end of the film, and I think when you see that photo of you and your dad, you realize that even though thatÕs a fictional character, itÕs a real thing that happened and that [that] little kid in that picture grew up and made that movie. He ended up okay. Now, Bill BurrÕs someone youÕve known since you were first trying to be a comedian. You met him when you were really little. 
Pete: I met him when I was sixteen at Carolines and I just was like, ÒHey, IÕm trying to start stand-up. Do you have any advice for me?Ó And he just said, ÒDonÕt sign anything.Ó I thought that was hysterical. HeÕs like, ÒIf they want to work with you, theyÕll work with you.Ó And then my mom took me to [Atlantic City] for my seventeenth birthday to see Bill Burr tour with Jim Norton and Jim Breuer, Artie Lange, and I ran into him there, and I just kept saying hello. Eventually, I started doing stand-up, and we started meshing a little bit, and then I got him to bang my mom in a movie. 
Judd: It came full circle. Would you say that Bill was a mentor for you growing up? 
Pete: Yes. Anything he says, I donÕt take lightly. He has a deep knowledge about the game. HeÕs been doing stand-up for thirty years, so heÕs the shit. Having him play my father in the movie was amazing, and he really cares about the acting. 
Judd: How do you prepare to act in a movie like this when youÕre playing a fictional version of yourself? 
Pete: You just try to believe the other guyÕs story, which is a little different. HeÕs a tattoo artist; his friends are complete scumbags. HeÕs selling weed and pills and stuff. He doesnÕt really have a lot of options. He doesnÕt have a safety net. HeÕs still with his mom. You just put yourself in that position. I had to put myself back as an eighteen-year-old. This was the version of me if I didnÕt get into stand-up or escape to the city. IÕd probably be robbing pharmacies with my boys and getting more tattoos. 
Judd: The opening of the movie is you driving in your car and closing your eyes. That was something you told me happened years ago in your life.
