Pete: There was a time in my life where I wasnÕt trying to kill myself, but I was testing it. You close your eyes for fifteen seconds, and if you die, itÕs supposed to happen. Or, just, like, putting yourself in a place where you canÕt be in control. I did that a bunch. I thought, I canÕt hurt myself because thatÕs not fair to my familyÑthat was my rule. But I could accidentally get hurt, and then I thought my mom and my sister could live with that, if it was just a bad accident. ThatÕs how fucking psycho I was. 
Judd: Does it feel different now that youÕre twenty-six? 
Pete: ItÕs starting to get a little easier. Your twenties suck. Growing up is really hard. ItÕs really awful. Especially in front of the world. 
Judd: LetÕs talk about your friends in the movie. 
Pete: In Staten Island, I hung out with guys who were like three, four years older than me. They introduced me to weed, and we used to smoke weed all the time. And then they started moving on to harder drugs, selling them and pushing around. Luckily, I realized I had to get out of there. So, my mom helped me find an apartment in Brooklyn, and I decided that I was going to go full into comedy. And I never spoke to those people again. 
Judd: That probably saved your life. How is your relationship with Staten Island now? 
Pete: Much like my public relationships. TheyÕre really good for three months, and then, all of a sudden, right down the clogger. I love Staten Island to death. I still live there. I just have a lot of issues with the way itÕs starting to change. TheyÕre starting to get a little blue over there, and are more aware of the socioeconomic crises, but in many ways, theyÕre still stuck in the eighties or nineties. ItÕs like an old suburb. Staten Island is the sister to Long Island, where itÕs just for families, and if you want to be more fast-paced, you go to Brooklyn or Manhattan. 
Judd: Do people like you on Staten Island? 
Pete: I think itÕs fifty-fifty. I think some people like me, and they get that anything I say about Staten Island comes from a loving place and [that] I genuinely just want it to be better there. The other fifty percent are just like, ÒFuck you, dude. I hate you.Ó 
Judd: How is the movie different from the experience of making SNL every week? 
Pete: With the movie, I had a little bit of control for the first time, and I really liked that. I appreciated that people would listen to my ideas. At SNL, itÕs a little more competitive. 
Judd: Is it intense to be on a TV show thatÕs part of the national conversation every week? 
Pete: Yeah, especially when you donÕt want to be a part of the national conversation. When youÕre trying so hard to be quiet. 
Judd: Yet, on the show, youÕll talk about going into rehab. Why do you share that on the show? 
Pete: I donÕt like people to be surprised about me; I like people knowing things. So, if they see me being sad or having issues, theyÕre like, Oh, I know. 
Rather than being in the dark about whatÕs wrong with me. Also, IÕm not the greatest sketch guy there. There are fifteen others on the show who are the greatest sketch guys. The best that I could bring to the show is to talk about my personal life, or to do stand-up at the [ÒWeekend] UpdateÓ desk. 
Judd: YouÕve talked about how, when you have a father whoÕs a hero, in a lot of ways it makes you afraid to take risks, because you think, IÕll never be able to do what he did. 
Pete: I also just donÕt want to insult the Davidson name. ThereÕs not much higher you could go after giving your life to save others. It always instilled this fear in me. DonÕt ruin the family name, because itÕs all weÕve got. ThatÕs just a very Staten IslandÐtype of motto to live by. DonÕt ruin your fucking name. 
Judd: I know when we put the dedication to your dad at the end of the movie, it made you uncomfortable. 
Pete: I get why itÕs there. ItÕs just a dedication, like before certain Law and Order episodes. But it makes me feel weird. 
Judd: You donÕt want people to think you want sympathy? 
Pete: Yeah, IÕm not looking for sympathy at all. IÕm just hoping you enjoy the movie and that you could maybe relate to it in a certain way. 
Judd: I think, when you see the dedication, you realize that even though thatÕs a fictional character, itÕs a real thing that happened. And that little kid in that picture grew up and made that movie. He was able to make that movie andÑ 
Pete: ThatÕs our way of saying heÕs okay. 

RAMY YOUSSEF 
September 2019 
Judd: Your show Ramy has been getting a lot of attention, and it seems like everything has happened very quickly for you. Does it feel that way, or does it feel like itÕs happened at the right time? 
Ramy: ItÕs interesting, because I moved to L.A. with a job, but it was just so under the radar that no one knew that I was on a show. I did this Nick at Nite sitcom, [See Dad Run,] for three seasons. Otherwise, I wasnÕt really getting cast as an actor, because I was kind of a difficult type: I wasnÕt quite the lead; I wasnÕt quite the best friend; I wasnÕt quite ethnic enough; I had an Arab name, but not the vibe that theyÕre looking for. But all that did was just keep driving me into stand-up, which I had only done a handful of times before I moved to L.A. And then I came out here, and all my sketch comedy partners were in New York, and I realized, Okay, I really want to hit stand-up hard. By the time I got into development on my show, IÕd been doing stand-up for about six years, which is still not that long. But it certainly feels like itÕs been coalescing as it should. Although, I still canÕt believe all this is happening. 
Judd: Do you feel like Hollywood has changed in those eight years since you got the Nick at Nite sitcom? Was there suddenly a demand for more diversity? 
Ramy: A couple of years before I even sold my show, I remember thinking, Oh man, whoÕs done this before? A show about anyone who is practicing Islam? There must be someone, but I donÕt even know them, and thereÕs no real connection that I have to them. I knew I wanted to make something in that 
vein. I didnÕt know what it would be, but I remember going into my management company at the time and telling them, ÒI want to do this story about a Muslim family,Ó and they asked me, ÒWell, what if they lived next door to a white family?Ó or ÒWhat if itÕs one kid, and heÕs kind of, like, displaced?Ó It was all really like, ÒHow do we make this something that a non-Muslim would be interested in?Ó Now they need to be as Muslim as possible, like, ÒLetÕs show the whole thing in Arabic, and if youÕre Egyptian, letÕs have the whole thing with hieroglyphics subtitles.Ó People are so down to go down that path now, and everyone is really sensitive about representation. 
Judd: ItÕs a funny moment in comedy and television, because thereÕs finally a demand to have more diversity in the writing rooms and in TV shows. ItÕs always hard to know how much of that is sincere and how much of it is business. How do you perceive it these days, as someone whoÕs in the clubs and hiring people for your show? 
Ramy: ItÕs interesting. ItÕs almost like being like a white writer in Hollywood now is like if you were a writer of any minority before, which is, you have to be the best. IÕve seen it even in ways that IÕve constructed my shows. ThereÕs nothing wrong with this on a certain level, but a lot of the time, you just make stuff with the friends that youÕre with. And a lot of the time, your friends are like you. So, you kind of have this club that gets developed where a lot of the people whoÕve been in it, who are friends, who are making things, are white guys. And thatÕs fine. I donÕt really think thereÕs a problem with that. I think that now itÕs this thing where there is this desire to have more diverse voices, I think that can really make you stronger. With the amount of streaming services we have, and the amount of outlets to make things, itÕs just more interesting to have these new stories. A big part of it is thereÕs this pressure to tell other stories, and maybe some people are afraid of being called out on Twitter or something like that, and that motivates why they would do something. But for me, none of that is really a motivator as much as ÒWhat is the story?Ó WeÕre now decades and decades into audiovisual storytelling. How can we start talking about things that we havenÕt yet? 
Judd: You also need people to understand what youÕre writing about. ItÕs like when we did Freaks and Geeks, I hired Jon Kasdan to be on the writing staff, because he was eighteen, and I thought, HeÕll remember high school. We hired Seth to be a writer at Undeclared because he was the age of someone in college, and we just thought that would somehow add something to the soup. Do you think writers and comics can grow from this moment? 
Ramy: Overall, the demand for more diversity changes what people are looking for, and I think it can be really positive. But there are probably going to be some growing pains as to why people are doing what theyÕre doing. That needs to be examined, and I donÕt think someone can just be in a situation because they check a box. I donÕt think thatÕs fair to that person, either. The thing that IÕm afraid of is that we hire people just because theyÕre minorities, because if someone doesnÕt get the opportunity to get rejected for a job, they donÕt get the opportunity to grow. You grow most as an artist from being rejected, so I think there needs to be this healthy balance of just making sure that weÕre really doing due diligence in meeting the right people and making sure that theyÕre getting a chance to be read and getting a chance to audition. The main responsibility, to me, is to make sure youÕre casting the net in a genuine way. And then you really do have to make the hiring choice that you feel will inform your storytelling most accurately and to the highest ability. 
Judd: When I started out, I didnÕt see goofy, nerdy, not-classically attractive people on television. And on some level, I thought, IÕm not represented here. IÕm curious to hear how it felt for you, as a kid, to be completely unrepresented in the culture for so long? 
Ramy: On one level, the only cultural reference point to being Arab was Aladdin, and that was it. Or else, it was in the news. So, you were either a cartoon or a villain. ThereÕs nothing really human there. And then I feel like I have a grounded spiritual practiceÑthatÕs another thing thatÕs either very villainized or cartoony. ItÕs hard to find an honest expression of religion that isnÕt caricatured. I think with both of those things, it was very much feeling like, Yeah, thereÕs not really anything that looks like me, and I think thatÕs when you have actual violence happening to certain communities, because of the misunderstanding that proliferates. You look at the spike of hate crimes against anyone whoÕs perceived to be Muslim or brown in this countryÑit spikes at various points in the political cycle, when itÕs weaponized. When people know so little about you that you can be weaponized every time there is an election, thatÕs a problem. ThatÕs a problem that I felt personally. When youÕre making things, or if youÕre a network that wants to green-light something, I think you look at that political reality and youÕre like, Television is about having a conversation. HereÕs a group of people weÕre talking about, but weÕre not talking with. And so, why not make a show so we can talk with the people who are very much being talked about. 
Judd: Who are your influences? 
Ramy: I donÕt know if IÕve told you this, but Knocked Up, Superbad, 40-Year-Old VirginÑthatÕs the shit I watched in high school. I graduated in 2009, so when I really got into making things, my thing was always like, man, I love Apatow movies, and I love Tarantino movies. I love how slanted Tarantino and the Coen brothers are, how weird those worlds are, and I love how relatable Apatow leads are. So, when I got into writing, I was like, I want to put in the likeable Apatow lead that I feel like I am, even though IÕm a Muslim kid. I watch Michael Cera, I watch Seth [Rogen], and IÕm like, Fuck, I relate to this dude. And I want to throw that into the weird Tarantino-y world. That was always my approach to what excited me about writing and what I was into. All of that stuff came out when I was really getting the love for comedy in high school. 
Judd: IÕm someone who has been in similar situations, in the sense of having to run a show, to be the boss, without much experience. How did you know how to do this? 
Ramy: A lot of running the show has been on the fly, and IÕve learned a lot from season one versus season two. I wasnÕt the showrunner on season one. IÕve learned what I want a room to look like based on trying to adapt what I like about stand-up. Because so much of stand-up is you go to a show and you try some jokes, you see how the crowd reacts, and then you walk around feeling sad for a while. Then you go back, and you try it out again. I kind of wanted to set up a writersÕ room like that. Instead of being in the room from nine A.M. to seven P.M. and eating lunch together and doing all this stuff, I want to get together for short bursts of time. So, we were meeting for just three or four hours at a time, not much longer than that. And that was me experimenting, trying to make it feel like other processes that I knew. IÕve just been trying to embrace a lack of convention and trying to find a recipe that can fully utilize how I think, which feels important for a show thatÕs semiautobiographical. 
Judd: That episode where you take your friend, played by Steve Way, to hook up with a girl, and she turns out to be sixteen years old and drunk. He has muscular dystrophy, and he says, ÒI donÕt have a lot of options.Ó I thought it was a really daring episode. How did you guys conceive that story line, and what were you trying to do? 
Ramy: That was actually something that I had talked about with Steve for years, about how he could get away with anything because, as a society, we kind of put people who are disabled onto a pedestal, and we assume that they must be good. I think that the characters on my show, and in anything that I would want to make, are spiritually inspirational. That might look like faith, but, really, what it means is just that theyÕre trying to do the right thing, and theyÕre trying to be themselves. ThatÕs really at the core of where theyÕre at. They want to be good, and theyÕre trying to grapple with their ego and their desires and whatÕs Òright.Ó That is such a small goal, but it can really expand into so much comedy if thatÕs all your character wants. As a writer, you can then just dress it up with like, This person is trying to do the right thing but also grapple with their desires. LetÕs enter the most fucked-up situation that we can. We just got excited by the prospect of testing audiencesÕ morality and what their threshold is in terms of acceptance. For us, as creators, how do we draw the line in a really firm way where it couldnÕt go either wayÑwe want to be on the right side of it, but we also really want to show the wrong side of it and show how tempting that might be. Anything that seems fucked up? Our job is to make whoeverÕs watching it, if they were that person, think, I might have done the same thing. I might have fucked up the same way. How can we put the audience in that place? And so, that was the scenario that episode was born out [of]. It emerged from the question ÒWhat are the lines we can push?Ó 
Judd: How do you relate that to the current climate in stand-up, where everyone is debating what is appropriate? 
Ramy: I think there are two parts to this. I wish that, as a society, we could demarcate between the honest thought processes we all have and something being presented as a challenge or as a fuck-you. Because, for me, the best comedy is someone walking you through the honest thoughts that hit them, even if those thoughts are messy. We should be able to share those messy thoughts, because if we act like theyÕre not there, then weÕll never actually accept the group that needs to be accepted, that might feel like theyÕre being attacked. So, I think we need to draw this line between ÒWhen are we thinking something out together as a society?Ó and ÒWhen are we attacking as a society?Ó ThereÕs a version of any show that can come from a thoughtful place, and you can really pull off anything. I donÕt think anythingÕs untouchable. I do think that certain subjects are much more of a tightrope walk than others, and the reason they are is because at the other end of them are communities who feel like, There is violence involved in me being misunderstood. So, if you are a trans person who is hearing about trans people being murdered all the time because thatÕs your community and thatÕs what is being talked about, itÕs not that you canÕt hear a trans joke; you actually want to hear one. You want to hear one that is actually in line with helping and with understanding whatÕs going on, even if that joke encompasses the lack of understanding people have. I think some people will still find that funny. IÕve done trans jokes, and they donÕt fall in this place where everyoneÕs upset. I mean, some people are sometimes, but I find that if itÕs rooted in something thoughtful, that will get you really far. ThatÕs one part of it. The other part of it thatÕs important to me is this idea of what we should be expecting from comedy. I think that from growing up watching The Daily Show, my generation is very much at this place of ÒComedy should be facts.Ó Because when people watched Jon Stewart on The Daily Show, there was this thing that I heard people say all the time, which was, ÒI donÕt get my news from the news. I get it from The Daily Show.Ó Which is kind of fucked up, because now youÕre putting comedy in this position to tell you whatÕs going on. And it puts this unfair weight on comedians to feel like, Am I supposed to be telling the truth? Is that what this is? Is this supposed to be educational? I think thatÕs wrong. Comedy doesnÕt have to be right. It doesnÕt have to be a fact. Comedy is about someone just being honest about what they think, and that should look messy and can look messy. By no means should you get your news and facts from comedy, because none of us went to college. 
Judd: I think there are a lot of comedians now who see their performances as being about kicking the tires on whatÕs happening in the world and having fun with the audience, discussing issues and how the culture is changing. They are just trying to toss it around. But sometimes itÕs in a dark way, sometimes in a hurtful way. Sometimes thereÕs a sloppiness to it. 
Ramy: I think itÕs our job to find a personal connection to whatever weÕre talking about. Otherwise, it feels like youÕre adding fuel to a random fire, because itÕs already lit. I think thatÕs whatÕs infuriating to people. There are a lot of people who feel like theyÕre edgy and boundary-pushing, but theyÕre really just being opportunistic, because they donÕt have a unique point of view on the subject thatÕs being debated. They donÕt actually have a connection to this issue that actually needs to be talked about. TheyÕre just doing it to do it. 
Judd: Do you think thereÕs still room for roast humor out there? 
Ramy: I think what captivates people about roast humor is that everyoneÕs on the same stage, and they get to say something back. We get to say these nasty things, making fun of someone for anything, but then that person gets to respond. ThereÕs an even playing field with a roast, even though itÕs not something IÕve ever done. Nor do I think IÕd be very good at roast humor. But the thing I like about it, when itÕs good, is that people are really going there, but then the other person whoÕs being made fun of gets to go there, too. 
Judd: Sometimes, I wonder if comedy is in a precarious place right now, or if you just have all these comedians who are saying it is, but then theyÕre selling out arenas. I donÕt know anyone whose career has really ended who was doing normal comedy. 
Ramy: No oneÕs really been silent. Even Chappelle was talking about critics and the media, and he was talking about it in, like, his fourth special in two years. So, heÕs getting to say whatever he wants to say, and I think heÕs very funny. There seem to be very few instances where a comedian is actually being wronged by the crowd. Ultimately, I think a comedianÕs job is to be super aware of the climate weÕre in. Maybe the level of difficulty has gotten a little harder, but if youÕre a comic, that should be fun. That should be what youÕre aiming for. 
Judd: So many people are working from a place of neurosis and anxiety and depression, but you seem so grounded and thoughtful and centeredÑwhy is that? 
Ramy: I appreciate that. I have my anxieties, I have my bouts of feeling depressed, all those things, like we all do. But, obviously, IÕve made this show about a Muslim family. I donÕt make it because itÕs a cool filter to sell a show. ItÕs just who I am, and itÕs a big part of my life. When I think about my goals, the highest thing for me is spiritual actualization of who I want to be and who I know IÕm not yet. ThatÕs what IÕm really struggling for. And, genuinely, anything under it is cool, but I think itÕs important that your goal is to do something that will challenge you until the day that you die. I donÕt think IÕm ever going to reach the goal that I have for who I am as a human. ThereÕs almost, like, teeth in that for me. I never want to feel like my show or a stand-up set or a special rules my life and my sense of self. I give everything to what I do, but itÕs not the thing thatÕs going to tell me who I am. And I think thatÕs the only thing that keeps me from going crazy. 
Because, like you said, a lot of this stuff has happened very fast for me. I think that the only thing thatÕs helped me with that speed is that I know thereÕs something bigger than this, and so, IÕm just going to go along for the ride and try to maintain my humanity. 
Judd: Can you explain how your sense of purpose as a creative person is connected to your religion and how you look at the purpose of your life? 
Ramy: ThereÕs this Islamic saying: The purpose of life is to know God, and the only way to know God is to know each other. I really feel thereÕs something at the core of comedy that can help us know each other in a unique way. That, to me, is tied in with any spiritual path, and certainly with mine. 
Judd: Is it heartbreaking when a lot of people in our country have an impression of your religion that is so the opposite of how you see it? 
Ramy: ItÕs heartbreaking mostly because it has real-life consequences that hurt people. ItÕs tough growing up, because when youÕre an adult, you have your rationalizations, and youÕre like, ÒThereÕs this idea against Muslims because itÕs part of a larger tactic of dehumanizing people in order to justify certain political or military actionsÉÓ You can rationalize the shit out of it when youÕre an adult. But when youÕre a kid, youÕre just scared. I had such a fearful childhood, where I was actually afraid of myself and afraid of my family because I believed things I heard, and then, in turn, [I] had doubts about who I was. That is something that I donÕt want to happen for kids growing up. I want kids, regardless of what they are, to be proud of what they are. 
Judd: What do you think this show has done to help change that misrepresentation of your culture, and what would you say is the mission statement of your show? 
Ramy: The mission statement is super simple: Expand the conversation. Even if not a ton of people are watching the show, for me the goal is: How do we extend the conversation, even if itÕs just on an industry level? A real reaction to my show, from industry people, is that it is really specific. It was about a Muslim Egyptian guy in New Jersey, and we were so zeroed in on what we were doing, that I think we could do a show about a Black Muslim woman in Michigan and it would feel completely different. The thing that excited me is I think it expanded the palate; it expanded the appetite for comedy and the different types of stories we can get into. I keep hearing from people all over the world who are watching the show in areas where itÕs not even distributed or officially available. I was kind of blown away when I saw that our show was being pirated and that people were putting subtitles in their languages. We didnÕt even do that. It was just out there with other languages subbed on it. ItÕs so amazing that someone would care enough to make sure that people understood the show in their language and feel connected to those scenes. 
Judd: With The Big Sick, I always thought the reason people didnÕt think the movie would do well is probably the reason why it will do well. Most studios who turned it down just thought, No one wants to see this community. 
Ramy: They look at it like toothpaste or something, right? Like, thereÕs not enough Chinese people, or thereÕs not enough Muslim people, to form a real audience for this. TheyÕre coming at it from this marketing standpoint of product-per-seller, like thatÕs the only group of people who would buy this thing. But whatÕs really cool to me is when you ground a film or a show in a character that everyone can relate to. So, when you put a particular cultural filter on it, itÕs just a filter. The core of it is what everyoneÕs feeling. 
Judd: What does your family make of your show? 
Ramy: ItÕs been amazing for my family, because so much of what happens in the show are conversations that I feel our community has a hard time having. A lot of them around sex. I wanted to make something that would help us open up conversations that I donÕt think we have in a clear way. The conversations this family is having on the showÑsome of them, I never have with my own family. People reached out to me and were like, ÒYour show helps me have this conversation that I never have with my dad,Ó and IÕm like, ÒYeah, me, too.Ó I use my show to talk to my dad, and thatÕs kind of blowing my mind. ThatÕs so much cooler than anything else that has come of it. My family feels closer to me because theyÕre seeing this story thatÕs not fully autobiographical or anything, but theyÕre seeing my point of view. My show and my special came out within a month of each other, and my parents had never seen any stand-up that I did outside of a five-minute set that I did on Colbert. 
Judd: Why is that? Because that was the case with Aziz [Ansari], and Hasan Minhaj also told me that his parents had never seen him perform in person. Is it something specific about stand-up that is uncomfortable for your parents, and maybe for the Muslim community more broadly? 
Ramy: I donÕt know if itÕs stand-up specifically. In my case, I really care about what my parents think, because I love them and I care about their opinions, and I didnÕt want to have their opinion influence what I wanted to talk about onstage. I didnÕt want them to see my stand-up because I didnÕt want them to say no to it. ItÕs better to just do it and apologize later, rather than ask for permission and have them say no. I wanted to protect this incubator, and I didnÕt want them to see it, because their silence, or even their laughter, was just going to influence me too much. That was the reason for me. 
Judd: And what becomes the purpose of your stand-up when youÕre putting so much of your creativity into the show? 
Ramy: They kind of feed off each other. The first season of my show was based on all the stand-up IÕd ever done. Pretty much every episode had a scene that was based on my stand-up, or at least the subject matter. Now IÕve been in the process of making a TV show, so IÕm doing less stand-up. The interesting thing for the show is the characters are alive now. TheyÕre like these people we made, and they can grow into their own story lines. They donÕt need my stand-up as much. 
Judd: As you approach the next season, what are your ambitions for the future of the show? 
Ramy: To continue to remain nervous about what weÕre putting out there. The more you do something, the more you get a little desensitized, and I want to just make sure that IÕm continuing to dig into the things that are making me uncomfortable. ItÕs not about being insatiable. ItÕs about stripping away at things that bother me and making sure thatÕs in the forefront of what weÕre making. We put out the first season and realized that there was certain backlash, but for the most part, everything was more than okay. ThatÕs exciting, because that encourages me to dig even deeper and know that everything is going to be fine. Like, just go for it. 

ROGER DALTREY 
October 2018 
Judd: How exciting is this? IÕve waited since I was ten years old to do this. 
Roger: What? Sit on this stage? 
Judd: To grill you in this way. 
Roger: Hold on. I just got to adjust the hearing aids. YouÕre too loud; itÕs like being back with Entwistle. ThatÕs better. 
Judd: You know, IÕve got ringing in my ears as well. I went to a Lou Reed concert, and my ears rang, and they havenÕt stopped for the last twenty-five years. And I saw him somewhere, and I said to him, ÒI saw you, and my ears have rung for twenty-five years,Ó thinking heÕd laugh, and he just looked at me. 
Roger: Yeah, that was Lou. 
Judd: He was not amused. The book [Thanks a Lot, Mr. Kibblewhite] is fantastic. It was really fun to hear your point of view. Did you enjoy looking back and writing it? 
Roger: I didnÕt know if I had a good book in me, and all I wanted was a good book. If it hadnÕt been a good book, I wouldnÕt have gone to a publisher with it. So, I worked with a friend whoÕs a journalist. He had followed me around for the day, and he wrote this article in The Sunday Times, and IÕm having Sunday breakfast, and Heather, my wife, falls on the floor laughing. IÕm like, ÒWhatÕs the matter?Ó She says, ÒIÕm just reading this article on you by this guy called Matt Rudd, and he totally gets you.Ó It was indeed a very funny article. 
Judd: What was his take on you? 
Roger: IÕm completely mad. I wonÕt have an iPhone, so I still have my old steam phone, and it was taking me ten minutes to do a three-line text. But thatÕs me. I just refuse to have an iPhone. I find them sucking up your life like a vacuum cleaner, and it disappears into this thing. People are spending their lives looking down instead of up. 
Judd: And they found a way to suck all of your royalties away. 
Roger: Well, yeah, the internet. IÕve never been very pro. ItÕs useful in its ways, but it seems to be bringing out a lot of bad in people. 
Judd: Me at two in the morning. 
Roger: But Matt got it. I just said to Matt, ÒWould you like to work with me to see if I have a book in me?Ó He did interview after interview after interview for about four years. He even did an interview with me when I was in the hospital with meningitis, when I didnÕt think I was coming back. He thought, You better get one last one in. He wrote the interviews down exactly how I said them, and then, of course, speech isnÕt grammar, so I had to write it over and rejig it. You have to give it a thread. And because I did it the way I did it, I could insist on the title [Thanks a Lot Mr. Kibblewhite,] which the publishers fought against. I donÕt know why, but they seem to think every biography just needs to be called the name of the person with the face of the person on the cover. 
Judd: What I found so fascinating in the beginning of the book is how you grew up in postÐWorld War Two England, what that experience was like and how it shaped you. 
Roger: One of the hard things to do through our whole career is to get Americans to have any idea of what we grew up in. My mother started to go into labor on the twenty-ninth of February, 1944. In the area we lived, which was West London, there were lots of arms factories, and by now, Hitler had designed the V-One bomb, which used to just come over, boom, boom, boom, boom, making this thudding noise, which would then stop, and then, before you knew it, thereÕd be two tons of high explosives falling on your head. So, my mother went into labor during one of those raids. And she was determined I wouldnÕt be born on the twenty-ninth of FebruaryÑso, well done, Mom! She had waited till after the air raid was over. 
Judd: Because she didnÕt want you born on a leap day. I like that during World War Two, thatÕs what your mom was thinking about. 
Roger: SheÕs strong, and thatÕs what they were like. Just imagine what our parents went through. My dad was in the army. He got off the landing craft on D-day, [was] immediately blown up by a motor bomb, and fortunately survived. He had the shrapnel in him until the day he died. But they were terribly traumatized by the war. YouÕve got to remember, your 9/11 event, how it traumatized you, how you sat there in that hopelessness. That feeling in the gut? Our parentsÑPete [Townshend]Õs parents and my parents and John [Entwistle]Õs parentsÑthey were going through that kind of event every night for four, five years, because we were being bombed for that long. YouÕd go on down into the shelter, and youÕd come up, and maybe your house wasnÕt there. My mother came out of the shelter, and both ends of the street had disappeared. Just gone, flattened. So, you can only just imagine what that must have done to the way they were in their lives. And it was only later on in my life [that] I realized that they were probably shell-shocked for their whole life. Every time there was a thunderstorm, my mom would scream like a banshee. Run under the kitchen table like she was back in the bombing. 
Judd: How did that change how they parented you or how they felt about you running around? 
Roger: It created a kind of silent, incredible loving, but there was a distance I found with my parents, and a lot of people I talked to found it with theirs. 
WeÕve all come to the conclusion theyÕd all been through the same trauma. When we grew up, we had very little food. During the war, it was rationed, as you know. A pound and a quarter of meat was the rations for a family of four for a week. 
Judd: I just had that at the Palm earlier today. 
Roger: Exactly. That was a family of fourÕs meat for a week during the war. And when we got to 1945, we then had to share that ration that we had with the German people, who were in even worse straits than we were. In 1945, half of our loaf of white bread was made of chalk, to pad it out. ThatÕs why a lot of my generation are very short. IÕve always blamed Hitler for my legs, because these fucking legs wouldnÕt stop a pig in a passage. 
Judd: But it seems like one running idea throughout the entire book is how much you appreciated your success and how responsible you were to keep it going even during difficult times. You just had this work ethic that came from growing up like that. 
Roger: People say we were poor. We were not poor; we were incredibly wealthy, because the war brought families very, very close together, and they were big family units in those days. The workforce was far less mobile than it is today. And one thing that got people through was singing, which Hitler could never work out. The more he bombed us, the more we sung. The louder we sung. There was singing everywhere. Every factory youÕd go past, the workers would be singing. The builders on the street would be singing. It gave us this ambition and this willingness to strive for what you wanted. I couldnÕt afford to buy my first guitar, so I made it. Keith Richards made his first guitar. Quite a few other people from our generation made their first instruments, because to buy one was impossible. It was a terrible guitar but it worked. As long as youÕve got six strings and you learn to tune it, the intonation of it is not really important until you get up high. And all we needed in those days to play the music that inspired, the music of Leadbelly and all those other early American blues singersÑyou could learn the three chords that you could use to play most of those songs. So, immediately, you could start a band. You get a tea chest, which was a two-foot-six box of plywood with an open button, you put a string through the middle and a broomstick. But a good player could make that sound like a double bass. IÕm serious. Every street had a band, with people singing. It taught us to do things for ourselves and not have it given to us. 
Judd: DoesnÕt that seem like a miracle [that] it all worked out? 
Roger: ItÕs extraordinary when you look at the bands that came out of that period. Elvis and Little Richard and Jerry Lee Lewis and all those great guys would come in to us through our ten minutes that the BBC allowed us, because this music was dangerous. ItÕd be hidden away on a Saturday night or Sunday night somewhere. But weÕd managed to find it, and of course, when everybody saw Elvis, even my ageÑand I was about ten or elevenÑ immediately, it was going out to get the soap to slick the hair back. We couldnÕt afford Brylcreem or any of those hair products, but the soap worked wonders. You could slick your hair back, and it would stay there, and we all thought we all looked like Elvis with that soapy hair. Until it rained. But then we saw this guy called Lonnie Donegan, and he was singing all this Leadbelly and early America folk songs in a way that was primal. And he was really square, because he used to sing in a dinner jacket and a bow tie. But it was the way he sang it. 
Judd: How did he sing it? Give us a little Lonnie Donegan. 
Roger: HeÕd start a song: Ham and eggs, pork and beans / Could had more pork, but that cook was so mean. Kind of chain gang songs, but then heÕd get halfway through it, and heÕd start wailing. Often heÕd sling his head back and heÕd wail, and it touched me in a way that Elvis didnÕt. Elvis looked great, but Lonnie had a primal thing in him. I thought, IÕm going to do this. This is good. 
Judd: When you first thought, IÕm going to do this, did you have any real vision it was possible to get outside of your small town doing it? 
Roger: No, no. You do what you do, and I got up onstage at the local youth club, did my version of ÒHound DogÓ and ÒMidnight Special,Ó and suddenly, there was a row of girls down in front! I was eleven years old. I didnÕt quite understand what that was, but you understand that thereÕs something about singing that attracts friends. I always felt more comfortable on the stage than in the audience, so that was the start of it. Then I started a skiffle band, like every street had. And later on, when I started working in a factory, I made my first electric guitar, and going home from work one night, I bumped into a guy whoÕd made his first bass guitar, and that was John Entwistle. I managed to talk him into joining my band by telling him that we were getting paid. We had been paid once, and not a lot, but we had been paid. It wasnÕt quite a lie, but it wasnÕt quite the truth. He started to play with me, and I thought, This guy is great. At that time, we had a rhythm guitarist who was not very good. I was the lead guitarist, and the rhythm guitarist had the ampÑthe one amp that John and me and the rhythm guitarist had to go through. 
Judd: So, he had to stay. 
Roger: So, we couldnÕt afford to get rid of the rhythm guitarist. But after six weeks of putting up with this, John said to me, ÒI donÕt know whether I can go along with Reg playing this rhythm guitar. IÕve got a friend that would suit us much better. Do you mind if I bring him along and give him a try?Ó So, I had a serious talk with Reg. And to his credit, Reg loaned us the amp and gave up the rhythm guitar. Which was a relief. 
Judd: Because he was sure you were going nowhere. 
Roger: Then John brought this guy along who IÕd seen at school, because you could hardly hide him in a crowd of a thousand, or even a hundred thousand. Because, in those days, we were all incredibly skinnyÑand I donÕt mean it as an insult, but in those days, Pete really was a nose on a stick. I love Pete. I love Pete. I think heÕs got a fabulous head. HeÕs grown into his nose. IÕm just trying to give you a picture of why you could not miss him. Anyway, John brings this guy along with another handmade guitar, but it had a machine-made neck. Where the body had gone, IÕd never bothered to ask. And it was Pete Townshend. And immediately, it was quite obvious that this was a budding genius. He was playing chord shapes that weÕd never seen before, not even the professional guys on the TV. He was playing all these diminished things; I donÕt know what theyÕre called now, even. These shapes that you could only go, What is he doing? And his rhythm action was so different than anything else, and I think that came because, in the trad [Irish folk] band, he was playing banjo. Pete joined the band, so there we are the three of us. IÕm playing lead guitar, PeteÕs on rhythm guitar, JohnÕs on bass guitar, and my oldest friend from the first day at school, Harry Wilson, is on the drums. 
Judd: How old are you at this time? 
Roger: I think just turned sixteen, and they were just turning fifteen. 
Judd: Was Pete popular in high school? 
Roger: Because IÕm small, I was bullied at the grammar school, quite badly bullied. IÕm sure Pete, because of the way he looked, didnÕt have an easy time, either. At that age, all boysÕ schools can be quite cruel. I broke my jaw when I was about eight years old. I was playing on a building site. We used to play on bomb sites. I remember finding the tail end of a bomb once. YouÕve never seen ten kids run faster in your whole life. There were no safety things. A bricklayer had left one of his lines out for the night, and over I went and smashed my jaw. I didnÕt realize it was even broken until about a week later. I was kind of a funny-looking blokeÑstill amÑbut then, a really funny-looking bloke. And they gave me some very cruel names. So, that was our years, and we would just progress as this band playing pop music of the day, playing clubs and pubs. But then we got lucky. Betty Townshend, PeteÕs mother, was instrumental to the success of the band in the early days. She managed to get us gigs, and an agent who got us better places to play. 
Judd: They were in the music industry, his parents? 
Roger: She was a singer in a band, and PeteÕs father was the saxophonist in a RAF band called the Squadronaires during the war, so a very accomplished musician. Betty Townshend managed to get us an agent, who then booked us into an officersÕ club for the American army in London, called Douglas House. We used to play there every Sunday afternoon, and of course we came into contact with all these GIs. They would start requesting music that weÕd never heard before, stuff like the blues, like Johnny Cash and Chuck Berry, all this material, and youÕd be expected the next week to play at least one of them. So, that started our musical journey into other music than what was in the Hit Parade at the time. We had another singer with us at that time, who used to think he was Cliff Richard, and he used to do all the Cliff Richard songs, and I used to sing all the other songs. I would do the Roy Orbison. I would do the Johnny Cash. When I look back, it was probably only about a year. And then we started to get into the blues, which Pete heard from his friend at art school. A guy called Tom Rat, dear friend of ours, had an amazing collection of John Lee Hooker and HowlinÕ Wolf, Jimmy ReedÑ all those people that we were introduced to. Pete wanted to immediately become a blues band. And I thought, Well, if we do that, overnight weÕll lose our audience. 
Judd: They didnÕt want to be depressed. 
Roger: But, slowly but surely, we became a blues band, just like the Rolling Stones. Then we found a manager, and he thought we had something special. By this time, we found this strange drummer. I canÕt tell you how strange he was. They donÕt come any more strange then Keith Moon. ItÕs impossible to describe to anyone who didnÕt know him and didnÕt spend lot of time with him, through the good times and the bad, what Keith Moon was like. He was an extraordinary character. And when he joined the band and started playing with us that first night, that first audition, I knew that something big would happen. There was something that locked together. Pete, John, and myself had it. But we could never find that missing drummer, that missing piece. But when Keith joined, he was the key that started the engine. And justÑpoofÑit was perfect. 
Judd: So, he comes in and he auditions at the end of a gig? 
Roger: No, he came halfway through. 
Judd: So, in the middle of the show youÕre like, ÒYou want to do one with us?Ó 
Roger: In the middle of the show, he came up and said, ÒI can play much better than that drummer you got.Ó He was a cocky little son of aÉWe didnÕt hire him that night, but we thought we better try him out in rehearsal. It was quite obvious that he was going to be the one. He said he was never asked to join the band. Perhaps he was, perhaps he wasnÕt. We didnÕt need to ask him. We invited him to the rehearsal and said, ÒWould you like to do the next gig?Ó So, I reckon thatÕs pretty much being invited to join the band. IÕm trying to make a film of him at the moment, but IÕm determined not to make a biopic. Keith Moon deserved a proper film. Because KeithÕs personality was so enormous. Every facet of his personality was enormous. He could be the most generous, the most mean, the most caring, the most uncaring, the most loving, the most hateful, the most spiteful. 
Judd: How did you learn that? 
Roger: He was wonderful guy, and he was the funniest man that IÕve ever met in my life. He was one of the few people in the world that could make Peter Sellers laugh. Keith could have you belly-laughing until you had to leave the room because you were aching so much from laughing. I donÕt know if you have ever had that in your life, but IÕm not kidding you, it really hurts when you canÕt laugh anymore and you start to cramp up. When Keith would get on a soliloquy, heÕd go off, and he had a vocabulary way beyond his education, and he could pull it out of the air with subjects that went off of tangents, absolutely extraordinary stuff. 
Judd: Where did his personality come from? What do you think was the root of what made him that type of guy? 
Roger: Depends on what drug he was on. Apparently, his mom said he was a normal child. Dear Kit MoonÑwhat a sweetheart she is. I doubt if Keith was ever normal. What a one-off. If I ever do allow my picture to ever be made of him, I guarantee you itÕll be something youÕll not expect him of, and worth your while. 
Judd: What was that scene like in London in Õ62, Õ63, right when you had your first singles, and the Beatles were around, and the Rolling Stones and the Kinks? Was it a community? Did you guys do shows together? Was there a camaraderie? 
Roger: I mean the BeatlesÕ ÒLove Me DoÓ was the record that really was the ignition of the swinging sixties. And then, of course, the Stones came with ÒCome On,Ó Chuck BerryÕs song, and they looked very different than the Beatles. But then this movement started up which this new manager we got talked us into becoming the figureheads for, which was the Mod movement in London, and they all rode scooters and dressed in trendy, trendy clothes. Of course, I was really a teddy boy, which was not even a rocker. Teddy boys wore Edwardian dress: long, draped, finger-tipped jackets, velvet collars, kind of smart. I liked the Mod thing when it started, because that was smart, too. It very quickly turned into a kind of mess, but in the early days, it was smart. Anyway, Pete and me took this long-haired Rolling StonesÐalike band into the barbershop, having walked in as the Who, we walked out of the barbershop as the Mod band called the High Numbers, with these very short haircuts, looking very different indeed, but of course, inside exactly the same. 
Judd: So, he created an image for you guys in the beginning. And then you were doing your first singles? 
Roger: Pete wrote ÒI CanÕt Explain,Ó which was a kind of tribute to the Kinks. You got to remember, in the process leading up to this period, weÕd supported every one of those bands. WeÕd supported the Beatles in Blackpool, weÕd supported the Rolling Stones a few times, weÕd supported the Kinks a few times, and every band that was out there, Gerry and the Pacemakers, you name it. Dusty Springfield. WeÕd been on the same stage, but as the support act. So, we were cutting our teeth in a pretty good way, but then, once Pete wrote ÒI CanÕt Explain,Ó it was quite obvious that he was writing songs from a different perspective. They were writing music for girls; Pete was writing music for blokes. Rock and roll was music to make love to, but rock, which the Who was doing and still does, was music to fight to. Pete understood that. So, heÕs writing these songs for My Generation, and itÕs got an arrogance about it that isnÕt there in rock and roll. And the beat is on the one. 
Judd: It was very angsty at a time when music wasnÕt angsty. He was talking about being a teenager. 
Roger: I recognized his talent very early on, and I was just happy to be the guy that was lucky enough to be in that position on the stage with these songs to present to an audience for the first time. How lucky was I? 
Judd: What do you make of the songwriting when heÕs writing songs like ÒPictures of LilyÓ and ÒIÕm a BoyÓ? It was very different than what anyone was writing. 
Roger: ThatÕs when I had to really get to grips with Pete. Prior to that, itÕd been easy. It was blues, it was aggressive, and I could sing it in much more of my natural voice, from a point of view that I understood really easily. Then he presented ÒHappy Jack,Ó and I donÕt get where this is coming from. So, I had to learn then to really gain an empathy for PeteÕs interior motive for writing these songs, and what he was going through inside, and feeling. So, I had to kind of climb into Pete in a way. 
Judd: What was he going through? What do you think it was that he was struggling with that made him write that type of music at the time? 
Roger: I just think he had this imagination that was so wild. If you read anything about Pete, you know he had quite a lot of abuse when he was young, and like I say, I think he was probably bullied at school. He is incredibly intelligent. He has a mind that goes off at tangents all the time, twenty-five thoughts at once. And heÕs incredibly vulnerable at times, but he likes to put that in right in front of himself, which is incredibly courageous. 
So, for things like ÒIÕm a Boy,Ó I had to climb inside that, and I really struggled to find the voice. But when I listen back to it now, itÕs kind of interesting, because thereÕs a haunted quality about it. You really do feel that youÕre this boy going, ÒBut Mom, IÕm a boy, not a girl.Ó And thereÕs a quality to my voice that was obviously mentally trying to tune in to where Pete was when he wrote that. Then Tommy and ÒI Can See for MilesÓ and those things happened, and that gave me the vehicle to develop the voice that I ended up with. 
Judd: What I thought was interesting was that you were the only person in the band who wasnÕt imbibing and partying and drinking and drugging. You were the person keeping the trains [running] on time. In the book, you talk about flushing their stash down the toilet. And they fired you. Then, when you join the band again, after they took you back, you say that they were mean to you for years as a result of this. 
Roger: It was really a feud between myself and Keith and John. John had a side to him that could be quite spiteful. He had this side of him that was dark. The reason I flushed their stash was that we did that first tour of Europe, and they managed to get ahold of this bag of amphetamine tablets. I tried them early on in our career, when we were doing two shows a night. We worked from eight to eleven at nightÑthis is after a day in the factory, by the wayÑ and then from two in the morning to five in the morning the next day. So, it was quite a long day, and I tried doing it just to help me stay awake, and all it did was dry me up, and I couldnÕt sing. All I ever wanted to do was be a good singer and deliver a good show. IÕll tell you the reason for that: I went to see Cliff Richard and the Shadows at the Chiswick Empire, and I remember how many Saturday mornings I had to work to pay for those tickets. And if Cliff Richards and the Shadows had been shit that night, God help them. I really realized then that, as the person on the stage, you owe it to an audience to turn up. You do not ever dial it in. And what these pills were doing, because the band could take them, they were starting to speed the music up, and they were starting to make the music louder. And in those days, we had no monitors, so it was very rarely I heard myself singing. By the end of about five or six shows in Europe, they were not only taking one pill before the show, they were taking I donÕt know how many. We ended up at a show where I could not get the words of the songs in. It was ridiculous, and it was so loud. IÕve spent four years managing the chemistry of this band, and IÕm listening to this stuff falling apart at the seams, and I think, This has to stop, because theyÕre far too good for this. So, while theyÕre busy gleefully smashing up the equipment at the end of the show, I go off the stage and I find KeithÕs stash in his suitcase and flush it. He comes off the stage, immediately looking for the stash, which is now gone. And he says, ÒWhereÕs my stash gone?Ó And I say, ÒWell, itÕs down the toilet.Ó And he attacked me with a tambourine. Which sounds very gentleÉ 
Judd: ItÕs like razors, right? 
Roger: He didnÕt attack me with the skin of the tambourine. He attacked me slashing with the bells. Needless to say, he didnÕt get very far. I was thrown out of the band for fighting, and I was out of the band for about four weeks. And they did a few shows without me. 
Judd: Who sang in those weeks? 
Roger: I donÕt know. I donÕt really care. IÕve never asked Pete about it. IÕve never talked to them about it. I really didnÕt give a shit. 
Judd: You were talking about starting your own band during that time. 
Roger: That was how it was then. I felt, Well IÕve started this band. IÕll start another one. By now, I knew I could sing. WeÕd had a hit record, IÕd a following, I knew my voice was doing something, and if it was to be in a band that had potential but weÕre throwing it all away because of this silly bag of amphetamine, I donÕt want to be in that band anyway. Anyway, after a month, the management comes knocking at the door, and theyÕd had a talk with the band and told the band, ÒYouÕre not working without Roger. ItÕs just not happening.Ó Apparently theyÕd been booed off. That might not be true, but IÕve heard that. They said, ÒWeÕve talked to the boys, and theyÕve agreed to take you back if you promise not to fight.Ó I said, ÒIÕll tell you what. IÕll go back, and IÕll promise not to fight again, but they have to promise not to do any drugs before a show. I donÕt care what they do after the show, but they go onstage straight.Ó And they lived up to their half of it, and I lived up to my part of it, but for those two or three years after thatÑbecause Keith and John knew that I had promised not to fight, but they knew my red mist and how it could come downÑthey would do everything they could to antagonize me. I used to just think of myself as a duck, and I would just let it roll off my back. 
Judd: Did it make you feel isolated in the band, that they want to party like that? 
Roger: IÕve been isolated all my life. It didnÕt bother me. IÕve always wanted to be the dog and never one of the sheep. So, it never bothered me. 
Judd: Were they bonded in a way that you werenÕt with them? 
Roger: Once the drugs kicked in, I felt isolated, because I didnÕt do any of it. I got to be very good friends with Owsley Stanley, the maker of LSD, Purple Haze, and all that. Great chemist. He said to me, ÒRoger, whatever you do, never take any chemical drugs. They will not agree with you.Ó I got on great with Owsley, and for some reason or the other, because he was so respected by everybodyÑthe Grateful Dead worshipped OwsleyÑI listened to him. He said, ÒJust smoke the pot. That will never hurt you. HereÕs a present.Ó And he gave me this joint that was about this long, and it was wrapped in the Stars and Stripes. I said goodbye to him, and the next day, we traveled down to Monterey, where all the others after the show dropped acid and were tripping, while I spent the night smoking this mentholated Stars-and-Stripes thing that Owsley had given me. And it was wonderful. I woke up in the morning, and I was fresh as a daisy and straight. The others were somewhere in outer space, looking at everything, and I had to get them back to the UK all the way from Monterey, through the San Francisco Airport and New York, and then get them through immigration when theyÕre all like this. Oh God. You got no idea what itÕs like to be on a plane with them. Pete spent his whole time looking at my caftan tan coat, but apparently, according to Pete, thatÕs what led him to write ÒI Can See for Miles.Ó He looked like he could. 
Judd: It also seems like it would be hard to conceive of Tommy if you didnÕt do that. 
Roger: What that whole journey did for him was it turned him off drugs, and he got into Meher Baba, into religion, really deeply into it. Pete never does anything by halves. His work ethic is extraordinary. He got into Meher Baba and went completely antidrug for a very long period of time. Right up until after Keith MoonÕs death. So, that was the most creative and the most joyous part of the Who as the originals, with Keith in the band. That part of our lives was the most fantastic time. 
Judd: Can you talk about a press conference where you guys brought in all the music journalists to tell them about Tommy and then played part of Tommy? Can you tell me that story? 
Roger: It sounded ridiculous. WeÕd written a rock opera, and itÕs about a deaf, dumb, and blind boy who plays a pinball machine, and of course, [the reporters are] just getting drunker and drunker in a tiny little club in the West End of London. They all seemed very disinterested in the spiel. So, we thought, LetÕs just play the music. And we played most of Tommy, and we flattened them, and they went out with their heads spinning, and the reviews they gave us were incredible. So, it just shows, you can do all the talking in the world, but if you can play a good piece of music well, youÕll move mountains. 
Judd: Do you remember the first time you heard what the premise of it was? 
Roger: The premise was a very simple idea: What would life be like if all you experienced were vibrations? I thought that was very interesting, because music is vibration. And this thingÕs carried through PeteÕs life and quite a few of his things, like [the rock opera] LifehouseÑthatÕs the theory that if you ever find the true spark that sparked up the universe and the cosmos, it will be a music note. That to me is magical, and it was the same thing with Tommy. So, we slowly but surely pieced these songs together, and Pete would go home, write demos, and bring them in two days later. Kit Lambert was instrumental to the narrative of Tommy. He wrote a film script. It was very much like the stage play. And it was just a magical period where we were free to rule our own lives creatively. Unlike the music business today, which is kind of ruled by the accountants and the lawyers. 
Judd: You were taking gigantic creative swings. With the Lifehouse project, some people said he was almost envisioning some version of the internet. 
Roger: He had this idea of the grid rather than the web. It was the search for this note, the meaning of life, and it was incredibly futuristic. ItÕs almost like weÕre moving into Lifehouse at the moment, with virtual reality and all that stuff. Pete had written about experience suits and things like this. 
Judd: Where did he get that from? Were other people talking about that, or did he conceive of it? 
Roger: Other people werenÕt. But instead of him just writing the music and it being whatever story you wanted in your head, heÕd written this film script about this vibration. And Pete is not a film script writer. ThatÕs a different skill. So, none of us could get our heads around it. And weÕd have meeting after meeting after meeting, and itÕd usually end up with us all very, very drunk trying to understand it, and Pete getting very frustrated. But in the end, he had the fabulous songs, the wonderful songs, which we experimented with. We did some live stuff at the Young Vic in London, where weÕd play all afternoon with people just coming in off the street. No charge. We just thought, Can we find this thing that PeteÕs looking for? We did that for two or three weeks and then decided, ÒLetÕs go out and do a few shows with it,Ó and we did a few live shows. WeÕre experimenting with the tape machine, putting all the synthesizers on ÒBaba OÕRiley,Ó all that stuff. It went down incredibly well with audiences. ThatÕs the only album weÕve really ever recorded where we had the songs quite a long time before we got in the studio. 
Judd: Was it considered a disappointment that you couldnÕt figure out how to put the whole thing together like Tommy? Because the album it became, WhoÕs Next, is that the biggest of all the albums? 
Roger: TommyÕs the biggest of all them. WhoÕs Next is a strange album. It should have been a double album and never quite made it. Of course, individual songs on that albumÑÒBaba OÕRiley,Ó ÒWonÕt Get Fooled Again,Ó ÒBehind Blue EyesÓÑtheyÕve become kind of legendary. But as a continual piece, itÕs not as strong as either Tommy or Quadrophenia. I donÕt think, anyway, but fans will argue with you anyway, just for the hell of it. 
Judd: YouÕve told this story before, in the early days, about PeteÕs guitar smashing into the ceiling, and he seemed embarrassed as the reason why he started smashing the guitar. 
Roger: YouÕd have to ask him about that, but from my perspective, over my left shoulder, it was kind of a weird moment, but then it was an explosion of anger. It was a Rickenbacker as well. My heart was bleeding. I thought about all those hours it took me to make my first guitars, and there was this Rickenbacker heÕs destroying. I could have repaired it, it wouldÕve been all right! But the effect it had on the audience was extraordinary, and of course, Kit Lambert, who was then our manager, recognized it to being the key to us getting noticed. It was like how to write your name large on a wall and get people to see it. 
Judd: Did they ever get bored of doing it? 
Roger: We got very bored of doing it. I got more bored of the fact that no one ever wrote about the noise and only ever wrote about the visual. Because the noise, for me, and the sound that it created, this whole auto-destructive art thing, the noise was the important thing. Incredible. The guitar used to scream like an animal being sacrificed. It used to howl and scream. MoonÕs drums would thunder. EntwistleÕs bass [was] going double, and [the] feedback. And I used to do a thing with KeithÕs cymbals on the microphone. The sound was just too immense. Never once did they ever write about the sound. All they ever wrote about was ÒThey smashed their instruments,Ó and I found that very dull. 
Judd: And it was expensive. 
Roger: It was very expensive. 
Judd: One recurring thing in the book that I was surprised by is, in every chapter you talk about going on tour, they would break the guitars, they would trash the hotel rooms, you would every once in a while be arrested for stuff they did, and then you would come home and find out youÕd lost money on the entire tour. 
Roger: It was really extraordinary. Because thereÕs something about having a hit record. Immediately, all your friends overnight think youÕre a millionaire. Of course, weÕd had hit records for six years; people thought we were millionaires. We didnÕt have any money. We did a tour in 1971. We came home from it, and our accountant said, ÒYouÕve done great this year, youÕve done really great: You started the year with a debt of six hundred fifty-five thousand pounds,Ó he said. ÒAnd now IÕm happy to tell you itÕs down to six hundred thirty-two thousand.Ó And youÕd go, Well, this is mad. This is how stupid it all was. Because youÕve got to remember that kind of money now would probably be twenty million dollars. Of course, Keith Moon, hearing this news, immediately stripped bollock naked and laid his balls on the table. 
Judd: What was that meant to signify? What was his message? 
Roger: IÕm thinking it was his fuck-you message to the accountant. IÕll leave this to say that his balls were upside down, because he was standing on his head on the chair. 
Judd: YouÕve said when youÕre twirling it around onstage, the mic sometimes flies into the crowd. 
Roger: The few times, the lead is broken. I tape them on really well, to make sure it canÕt come off from the plug. But thereÕs two times the leadÕs broken, 
and where the mic was going, I wonder if itÕs come down yet. Because when itÕs spinning, it must be going probably a hundred miles an hour. 
Judd: People are injured right now somewhere. 
Roger: IÕve been waiting for lawsuits. 
Judd: In this period youÕve been doing a bunch of tours, big triumph tours and the Super Bowl and the amazing performance at the [Concert for New York City]. What does it feel like in this era for you? 
Roger: WeÕre having the time of our lives, Pete and I. For the first time for a long time, since Keith died, the band feels like itÕs doing the music in the right way. WeÕre progressing. It can never be the same. We can never have the energy of our youth, but the music is still there, and that was what was always important to me, and I know thatÕs what was always important to Pete. Every time we play those songs, Keith and John are there with us. The noise they made in their life is still echoing, and every time we play the music, we ground it again. So, theyÕve never left me when IÕm onstage; theyÕre still there in my head. They always will be. 
Judd: I saw the Quadrophenia show a few times, and it really was remarkable to see you do the entire album. 
Roger: I love doing Quadrophenia, and itÕs a tough one to sing. It is a tough one to sing for any singer. ThereÕs been a few tribute nights to the Who where guest singers come up, and they all come up to me afterward and say, ÒHow do you do it?Ó And I realize thereÕs something about my voice that doesnÕt sound very high, but the songs are incredibly high. They are up in the high Bs and the high Cs, some of the notes. But I just love it. I just love TownshendÕs music. I think he is one of the most important rock composers, or any popular music composers, of the twentieth century. In my opinion, he should be out there with the Lennons and McCartneys. If not as popular, but in the quality of his music. He writes in a classical manner. ThereÕs something else going on in PeteÕs songs that is nowhere else out there. 
Judd: As a teenager, Quadrophenia was the kind of album that got you through that period of your life. Listening to songs like ÒIÕm One,Ó which we used in Freaks and Geeks for a sequence. And I talked to Eddie Vedder about itÑthat was the record where we felt like somebody understood what was happening. 
Roger: PeteÕs genius is that. It still happens today. The audience, when we play Quadrophenia, goes from eight years old to eighty. And it speaks for every generation going through. Now, that is pure and utter genius. 
Judd: How was it doing the Super Bowl? 
Roger: I hated it. 
Judd: Is it terrifying? 
Roger: ItÕs not terrifying. YouÕre completely out of control of anything. And the dressing room smells of jock strap, as you probably know. Then the stage has to be set up. And of course, you have to do a recording of it, in case they lose transmission to the stage, because everythingÕs done so quickly. So, you have to do a recording of it first, and you also have to sing it and do it live, but you never quite know what one theyÕre using. And you think, Why? It is a big honor, I understand that, but the Who arenÕt really up for medleys. I did my best putting that piece together. It was left to me to do it. I thought it gave an indication of the kind of stuff that weÕve done through our whole career. Although I donÕt like medleys generally, I think it flowed as a single piece. 
Judd: How about the [Concert for New York City]? Because that really was a remarkable performance. 
Roger: That was a tough cookie. Just emotionally for everybody, for the Western world, surely. It was traumatic. Wonderful but sad. All your emotions were amplified a thousand, maybe a million times. 
Judd: You talk in the book also about when you had meningitis, and you looked back on your life and you were happy with it. 
Roger: Well, you could hardly be sad with it, could you? From when I got kicked out of school to where I was laying in that hospital bed, really thinking I was going to die. They couldnÕt find out what was wrong with me. I was getting worse and worse and worse. I had tubes coming out of everywhere. The pain was horrendous. And I just thought about my life, and I was in wonder of where itÕs taken me and what IÕve done and how lucky IÕve been with my family. All the things that IÕve achieved. And I just thought, Ya know, Rog. It doesnÕt matter if thereÕs no more. Because I was calling people up saying goodbye. The only thing I could work is a finger to push a Skype button. And I really didnÕt think IÕd make it. So, I laid there and I thought about it. ItÕs all right, you wonÕt leave anyone in trouble, let go. And I donÕt know if they had given me a drug or what, but an incredible peace came over me, a calm like I canÕt describe. There were no lights at the end of tunnels, there was none of that, but my brain did have a thought: Why fear? And I thought about how everybody views life as an exit, and I thought, If youÕre on the other side of that door, Roger, itÕs an entrance. So, now, every time I meet anyone that feels theyÕre on their way there, I say, ÒHead for the entrance.Ó 

SACHA BARON COHEN 
January 2019 
Judd: How has it been returning to television with Who Is America? 
Sacha: Bizarre, because this is the first time IÕve shot something simultaneous to releasing it. IÕd be filming, and then I decided to reedit while I was filming. I looked at the show at three oÕclock in the morning the day before I left Morocco, which is where weÕd been filming, and realized I got it all wrong. So I started reordering and reorganizing everything. 
Judd: I bet Showtime loved that. TheyÕre like, ÒWe already did closed-captioning!Ó 
Sacha: Something IÕve noticed with all corporations is thereÕs a point where something is just Ògood enough.Ó TheyÕve done the budget, theyÕve worked out roughly what itÕs going to do for them, and the difference from its being good and very good, or very good and excellent, isnÕt really worth the hassle. They just go, ÒOkay, this movieÕs going to make sixty million. If itÕs ten percent better, itÕs going to make sixty-two million.Ó 
Judd: They did it on Superbad. We knew the movie was good, but it needed more support from the studio, so we called Amy Pascal at Sony, and we said, ÒYou have to throw a lot more money at this. WeÕll put out the first five minutes of the movie on the internet, just so people can get the sense of it.Ó And she did that. 
Sacha: Our experience was the opposite in that they wanted to launch a traditional long marketing campaign. And I said no. ÒI want no marketing 
campaign. I donÕt want you to announce the show.Ó Every two months, Showtime would say, ÒSo, weÕre going to announce it now, right?Ó And I said, ÒNo, no, no. WeÕre going to drop the show.Ó 
Judd: Like a Beyonc album? 
Sacha: Yeah, but what I didnÕt tell them was that I didnÕt mind whether the show was a success or not. My aim was that the show was as good as it could be. And I knew that if we did a normal publicity campaign, [the real] people [in the film] like Roy Moore and Joe Arpaio would sue or try and get an injunction. Or they would start getting calls from the White House. 
Judd: They would have too much time to find ways to fuck with the show. 
Sacha: They could get a court order or start suing and could basically shut it down. And part of the appeal for the show was we were getting powerful people. And once they start suing, the networks start thinking, Wait a minute, is this worth it? Do we really need Bernie Sanders? But the network was actually great, because they kept it secret, which is quite incredible, considering who we got. And secondly, they were tough when there were objections. We shopped this show around a bit, and one of the conditions for a channel buying it was that I could speak to the head of the channel and ask them, ÒIf you get a phone call from Washington saying we want this thing pulled, are you going to say yes or no?Ó And David Nevins at Showtime got on the phone and said, ÒIÕll stand by you.Ó 
Judd: And did that call from Washington eventually come? 
Sacha: Yes. WeÕre in DC, our first interview is with Bernie Sanders, and IÕm doing this character, Billy Wayne, whoÕs a conspiracy theorist. ItÕs my first time as this character, so IÕm not deep in character; the accentÕs not quite there. We sit down, and itÕs chaotic. We know weÕve only got an hour with Bernie Sanders. HeÕs late. And he gets pissed off within five minutes, when he realized that he was with this idiot. He didnÕt think it was part of a prank; 
he just thought, This is not the show that you told me that weÕre going to make. 
Judd: What did you say it was going to be about? 
Sacha: I canÕt remember. We said it was for Showtime, about great political minds. 
Judd: And then everyone says yes because they wanted to be on the show? 
Sacha: Yes. So, with Bernie Sanders, he comes out of it, and his team are really good. They immediately call up CBS, who owns Showtime, and they go, ÒWhat the hell is this? WeÕre going to the press with this. Who was this?Ó And the good thing was CBS didnÕt know what it was. So, they asked Showtime. They go, ÒDo you know anything about this show?Ó Showtime said, ÒYeah, weÕre making it; itÕs legitimate.Ó SandersÕs people said, ÒWe donÕt know. These could be terrorists, and if you donÕt pull the interview weÕre going to go to Congress and get a congressional hearing on this terrorist group thatÕs going around DC.Ó His team is tough. Sanders didnÕt know whether it was a right-wing show trying to destroy him or what. 
Judd: And itÕs funny, because youÕre getting to see a side of politicians that most people donÕt see, which is not even necessarily what youÕre putting on the show, but their protectors. 
Sacha: Bear in mind, this our first morning in DC, and I have to survive there for two weeks. 
Judd: In that character? 
Sacha: We did three characters there. During those two weeks, no one in DC can know that IÕm there. But right away, Bernie Sanders already thinks somethingÕs up. If he goes to the press, everythingÕs destroyed. We wouldnÕt be able to get all the rest of the guys that we wanted. 
Judd: What was your philosophy on what you wanted to accomplish by goofing on the Ted Koppels of the world? 
Sacha: We were thinking in the writersÕ room, ÒWhatÕs the modern Ali G?Ó Like, whoÕs the modern stupid interviewer? And we thought, Actually, those guys are the conspiracy theorists. Because of the internet, they have as much status now as respected journalists. ItÕs absurd that Ted Koppel, one of the most respected journalists in America, should sit with one of the worst journalists. This character is basically the worst possible journalist in America. But because of the internet, he can possibly be on Breitbart. 
Judd: ItÕs not shocking that [that] guy would have a showÑand a show that people would want to be seen on, too. 
Sacha: Well, Ted Koppel didnÕt. We had the simple philosophy that this character, the interviewer, is a guy who believes every lie that Trump has said. So, Trump says he had the biggest inauguration crowd ever? This guyÕs going to prove it. And heÕs got a bunch of fake evidence, and itÕs pretty bad faked evidence. And Ted Koppel had enough integrity to say, ÒYouÕre an idiot; youÕre spreading lies. IÕm not going to sit for this anymore. IÕm leaving.Ó There are different styles of comedy that we were looking at, and I think some of the reviewers missed that. They said, ÒOh, this one failed with Ted Koppel because he didnÕt get his ass out and scream, ÔAmerica!Õ like another guy on the show.Ó 
Judd: And then you have the rich character whoÕs like Trump. 
Sacha: Gio Monaldo. Guys like Trump have their Roger Stones, the facilitators who let evil stuff happen. Weinstein had that as well. They go, If Harvey Weinstein wants to rape somebody, how can I get the girl that he wants, because itÕs going to be good for my career. The idea was to have a look at what evil things people will do out of greed. And it was all done by hidden camera. We had one interview, in Las Vegas, where we made Gio worse than Weinstein. We went, ÒLetÕs say this guy has sex with children, and weÕre going to get a concierge, one of those guys that help rich people in Vegas, and see how long it will take him to walk out of the room once he realizes that the rich guy in front of him has had sex with an eight-year-old boy. How many seconds is it going to take to get up and go, ÔIÕm out of here and IÕm calling the policeÕ?Ó The shocking thing we found out was that this concierge that we spoke to not only didnÕt leave the room; he helped the rich guy solve his problem. My character said, ÒThereÕs this eight-year-old boy, heÕs victimizing me; heÕs threatening me. He sent me these Snapchat messages that he wants tickets to the Criss Angel concert, the bastard!Ó The concierge goes, ÒWe can get the Criss Angel tickets, not a problem.Ó Then I go, ÒCanÕt we get rid of the boy? Can we murder the kid?Ó And he goes, ÒUnfortunately, we canÕt do that in America.Ó By the end, heÕs helping me fix the problem of the boy. And I go, ÒThank you for helping fix the problem. I want to have fun tonight. Can you get me someone for me to play around with tonight?Ó And he goes, ÒAll right. How old?Ó I go, ÒUnder bar mitzvah, but above eight.Ó And heÕs like, ÒOkay. I know somebody who can get you something for tonight.Ó 
Judd: Wow, and that didnÕt air. 
Sacha: It didnÕt air. And itÕs incredible. ItÕs journalism rather than comedy. We ended up turning over the footage to the FBI. 
Judd: At some point, thereÕs no humor in it at all. ItÕs just pure art. ItÕs horrific. Because you think, Oh my God, these evil networks exist. In this world of the super-rich, they can have anything. 
Sacha: Exactly. But what was happening, at least for this conciergeÑI think the FBI looked into it and thought he probably didnÕt have a network. He probably was just trying to get some money out of me. These guys think theyÕre going to be in with a billionaire. When we were researching The Dictator, I spent a bit of time in the United Arab Emirates, and there was a philosophy there of like, ÒWe can get any actress we want to fly over, and weÕll have sex with them, and weÕll send a private jet and five million dollars and send them back.Ó ThereÕs always been this network of people with incredible money.
