Tig: Of course, I hope people like me. I like being liked. But I also havenÕt ever lived or died by it. My mother, whenever there was a problem at school or with friends, if people didnÕt like how I dressed, she was just like, ÒTell him to go to hell. Tell everyone to go to hell if they donÕt like you. You look adorable. YouÕre the coolest person I know.Ó And I was like, All right. Obviously, I still had to show up to school and have my hard moments, but having that in my head really informed me and helped me in those moments when IÕve taken these bigger risks. Speaking out at times when itÕs not safe and taking leapsÑI think thatÕs all very much tied to my mother. 
Judd: The thing that I like about comedy is you can make the worst thing have a value. I think most people donÕt have that ability when bad things happen. They just feel fucking awful. And in our business, we see the best of people, and we also see the worst of people. Comedy feels like it got divided in the last few years, starting with all the Louis C.K. stuff. This idea, that I can say anything I want to say no matter how hurtful it is because itÕs just a joke. ThereÕs a real division in comedy with how people are dealing with the ideas of freedom of speech, or how jokes hurt people. ThatÕs been pretty heartbreaking to watch, too. 
Tig: I have not made friends through speaking out. I mean, IÕve made new friends, and IÕve strengthened certain relationships, but thereÕs certainly a feeling I get from people just being absolutely baffled that I would speak out against somebody, not just in the comedy community, but somebody that elevated me. ThatÕs, again, that moment where IÕm happy to shake loose from you. And I am somebody that believes in redemption. I just am not somebody that believes that these peopleÕs redemption rates higher than the support of victims. I will always go back to that. Why is this the conversation, and why are they not the conversation? I canÕt get beyond that. I donÕt understand how people are left in the dust as they are as victims. But I feel the divide. 
Judd: I remember, I went to a benefit at the rape treatment center, and all the victims of Bill Cosby were there. The courage it took for all those people to speak up and the damage to their psyche to be called liars for so long, to not have their experiences validated. 
Tig: Or to be told, ÒWhy did you go over there? Why didnÕt you leave the room? Why didnÕt you hang up the phone? Why? Why? Why? Why? Why?Ó I really donÕt understand. And I donÕt want to understand. When I say IÕm happy to lose friends or fans over it, itÕs not that IÕm happy to lose friends, itÕs just that itÕs such a divide, where I can be friendly and I can be cordial, but itÕs a hard line for me. 
Judd: I got upset with LouisÕs Parkland jokes and spoke out a bunch about it. It just seemed like the weirdest target. And the number of comedians who are furious at me for saying ÒThatÕs really fucked up.Ó IÕm not saying, ÒHe should never work again; letÕs boycott this.Ó But youÕre allowed to tell people their jokes are fucked up. 
Tig: Your opinion is just as much free speech as his. It does feel like the comedy community is a microcosm of the country. 
Judd: We didnÕt realize that, right? We didnÕt think that, at a comedy club, it would be the exact same divide. 
Tig: I have a person that is an old friend in the comedy world, one of the closest people IÕve ever been to in my life, who said he is not in support of the Black Lives Matter movement. He was like, ÒMy brotherÕs a cop, and somebody threw tomatoes at my brother,Ó and IÕm like, ÒOh. Okay. Somebody threw tomatoes at your brother.Ó I just donÕt have time for it. IÕm still working. I still have friends. I still have a family. IÕm healthy. IÕm thriving. 
SKIP 
NOTES 

* 
Dave Tribble is a comedy booker. 

WHITNEY CUMMINGS 
September 2019 
Judd: What was your family situation like growing up? I feel like youÕre like me, in that you dealt with family stress by becoming a hyper-achiever. 
Whitney: Yes. Do you have siblings? 
Judd: I have an older brother and a younger sister. 
Whitney: I was the youngest. For me, I definitely knew at a very early age that I was a mistake. And I instinctively had some kind of Darwinian survival instinct of You better earn your place, because no one wanted you. 
Judd: Did your parents have money? 
Whitney: No, but I thought we had money. You know how you always think everything is perfect until you go to another house? I remember going to a friendÕs house for the first time and opening the fridge and being like, Oh! WhatÕs all this stuff? This looks like a grocery store. 
Judd: Twinkies?! 
Whitney: Totally. It was very Òkeeping up with the JonesesÓ in my family. We had nice stuff. We had nice cars. Judd: But no food. Whitney: No food, and the electric bill wasnÕt paid. Judd: My mom was like that. She would ask me for money, and then she 
would go lease a Mercedes. I would say, ÒWhy donÕt you lease a Camry, and then youÕd have money to eat?Ó 
Whitney: Or go to the doctor. 
Judd: Or go to the doctor! And sheÕd say, ÒBecause IÕm not an animal.Ó So, where did you grow up? 
Whitney: Born and raised in DC. I think I also was born into an alcoholic home. 
Judd: Both of your parents? 
Whitney: No. My dad was not. ThereÕs alcoholics, and then thereÕs codependents. There was a lot of ÒLetÕs pretend everything is fine when itÕs not.Ó Which, until very recently, was what I thought comedy was. 
Judd: You thought that was the definition of comedy? 
Whitney: Like, we alchemize very painful things and put them in pretty presents and make them digestible. My definition of what funny is has changed, but that is definitely a version of comedyÑwhere, if we donÕt laugh, weÕre going to cry. In my family, I always had a hard time discerning when people were laughing or arguing because it would seamlessly go from one to the other. Our parents would be fighting and screaming, and then, two seconds later they would be laughing. And I donÕt know if thatÕs alcoholism or maybe some mental illness that I will never understand, but I just remember learning at an early age that anger and laughter are very close. TheyÕre a millimeter away from each other. Maybe the same neurological response. The pendulum always swung, and it was like the angrier people were, the more they were laughing. I remember sitting at the Thanksgiving table as a kid, and everybody hated each other. You could feel the tension, but nobody was just, say, like ÒHey, you owe me fifty dollars, man. Pay me back.Ó It was always these passive aggressive roast jokes, like, ÒWell, maybe if Marcy didnÕt get divorced six times, we wouldnÕt have that problem!Ó And then everyone would laugh. ItÕs how we communicated. 
Judd: What did your dad do? 
Whitney: I still donÕt know. It was high-risk venture capital. I just remember he would come home with a nice car, and then, three days later, it was gone. 
Judd: What do you think it really was? 
Whitney: I donÕt know, but he taught me that if you are funny and charming, you can get away with anything. 
Judd: So, he was a salesman of some kind? 
Whitney: High-risk loans. People that couldnÕt get loans from banks would go to him. As a kid, I had people come to our door saying, ÒWhereÕs Bill? WhereÕs Bill?Ó IÕm like, ÒBill? WhoÕs Bill? No, he doesnÕt live here.Ó They were looking for my dad. His name was not Bill. So, I would just charm them and try to make them laugh, and they would go away. 
Judd: ItÕs fascinating that you grew up and you still donÕt know. Usually thereÕs a moment when you crack the code on what was really happening. 
Whitney: He just passed. We had to move the funeral last minute, because people were showing up, trying to take pictures of me, which I was very flattered by, but we didnÕt have a priest, and my uncle had to improvise. So, we were already laughing at a funeralÑthatÕs magic. If someone pitched it to you as a story, youÕd say it was too broad. It would never happen. My uncleÕs improvising a funeral, like, ÒHear ye, hear ye.Ó We have no idea what weÕre doing. Basically, it turned into ÒEveryone just go around and tell a story about him.Ó And everyoneÕs telling stories, like, ÒWhen he was like in the navyÉÓ And IÕm like, ÒHe wasnÕt in the navy.Ó He apparently told a different story to everyone. No one could corroborate what the truth was. I remember, as a kid, thereÕd be some Armenian menÕs store in DC, and heÕd tell me, ÒGo in there; get whatever you want.Ó And the manager would say, ÒWhatever you want!Ó And IÕd think, ÒI donÕt need any menÕs suits.Ó Like, howÉ? 
Judd: Did it feel like mobster stuff? 
Whitney: It definitely felt under the table. 
Judd: Were they ÒassociatesÓ? 
Whitney: There would be guys living in our house for a couple of months. As I grew up, everything felt weird to me. Parties felt weird, college felt weird. And then I walked into the Comedy StoreÑit was like a bunch of weird degeneratesÑand I thought, This feels right. I feel at home. 
Judd: And what about your mom? 
Whitney: She worked in fashion. She worked in public relations for BloomingdaleÕs. She would bring me to work, and it was all about what youÕre wearing and your makeup and your lip color. My mother could put makeup on without looking in a mirror, while she was talking to you. 
Judd: But she had a normal job. 
Whitney: Yeah. She worked incredibly hard. She was funny, but by accident. She was always like, ÒWhere are my glasses?Ó and her glasses would be on her head. She was a flaky, funny, hot mess. But also ambitious. I donÕt love the word strong because sometimes it implies suffering through things needlessly. But she was very engaging, very social. She needed to laugh. I could feel it was a need. You know when people laugh just a little too hard at something thatÕs not that funny? 
Judd: What were their parents like? What led your parents to be like they are? 
Whitney: ThatÕs a great question. IÕve been on that quest, getting to the forgiveness of what we didnÕt get as kids. To be able to go, ÒWell, our parents didnÕt get what they needed, either.Ó And thatÕs really helped me have compassion. My dadÕs mother died young. It can do a number on people, to not have a maternal figure all of a sudden, especially in the 1940s and Õ50s, when that role was so important. Right now, parents can be women or men. But back then, mothers were their only source of permission to be vulnerable. 
My grandfather was in the war. He was very stern and all about work. He injured himself all the time and didnÕt tell anyone. Very stoic. Vulnerability is not in our vernacular. I think my dad definitely inherited that. 
Judd: And your momÕs parents? 
Whitney: I donÕt know that much about them. SheÕs from Texas, and her dad died young, and her mother had, like, a cantaloupe farm or something but was very enigmatic. There was an absence of expressing love, though IÕm sure there was love there. My dad used to hug me sideways. There wasnÕt a lot of eye contact, so I think, as a result, I developed this deep need to be seen and loved. 
Judd: You donÕt hear many comedians say, ÒMy parents were solid as a rock.Ó 
Whitney: Exactly. WeÕre all in therapy. But my therapist did tell me I probably had something called infant-maternal disruption, which is when you donÕt get enough eye contact from your mother in the first couple years of your life. 
Judd: I do a bit about that. 
Whitney: No way. 
Judd: I was dating Leslie, and I was maybe twenty-six. And she goes, ÒWhen I talk, where are you looking?Ó And I go, ÒAt your mouth.Ó And sheÕs like ÒWhy?Ó And I go, ÒSo, I can understand you and hear you.Ó And sheÕs like, ÒYou know youÕre supposed to look people in the eye.Ó And I said, ÒIÕve never heard that before.Ó 
Whitney: Wow. I used to look at the side of someoneÕs ear. I had to relearn eye contact as an adult. 
Judd: My mom was always looking around, a nervous person, and I think that just wires you to feel anxiety for the rest of your life. 
Whitney: Human beings would rather suffer physical abuse than neglect, because at least with physical abuse, youÕre acknowledging that they exist. 
For the first year, children donÕt know that theyÕre separate from their mother yet. So, when theyÕre not being looked at, they donÕt feel they exist. And I think a lot of stand-up is I exist, right? 
Judd: When I do stand-up, I have a very strong desire to never do it again. 
Whitney: Is it shame? 
Judd: ItÕs Shut up. No one cares. 
Whitney: No one cares. YouÕre a phony. Why do you think you deserve to be looked at? Even though youÕre doing a service to people. YouÕre healing people. 
Judd: I didnÕt do stand-up from the time I met my wife, in 1995, until a couple of years ago. 
Whitney: You wanted her to stay with you. 
Judd: Exactly. So, she didnÕt know me as a stand-up comedian. When I said I wanted to do it again, she wanted to know why. She asked me, ÒIs it something people do because theyÕre insecure orÑ?Ó 
Whitney: Too secure? 
Judd: Yeah. Is it a positive thing or a negative thing? 
Whitney: ItÕs interesting. Whenever I get in that mode of You needy, desperate narcissist. Who do you think you are? I remind myself that itÕs only five thousand people. There are six billion in the world. IÕm not really taking up that many peopleÕs time. 
Judd: I thought you were going to say, Five thousand people paid to come here to see me. Why do I doubt them? 
Whitney: Now IÕm insulting them instead of myself. But yes, that inner monologue starts at a very early age. I also think I developed an adrenaline addiction, which I had never really heard anyone talk about. In the womb, epigenetic imprinting apparently occurs if your mother produced a lot of cortisol and adrenaline because of stress. My parents fought when my mom was pregnant, for sure. ItÕs like a baby being addicted to drugs when theyÕre born. I think I was addicted to adrenaline at a very young age. 
Judd: I think I need to go to your therapist. 
Whitney: From a very early age, I was always creating dramatic situationsÑ like putting glasses on top of each other and going, ÒAre they going to fall?Ó Or hiding things and shoplifting and keeping secrets and engaging with dangerous people. I think stand-up sort of fit that bill for me. ItÕs scary, IÕm terrified, IÕm producing adrenaline. IÕm going to small towns with weird drunk men. This is perfect. I think it checked all the boxes in terms of my various addictions and wounds. And then, creating that shame cycle of I donÕt belong here, I donÕt deserve to be here, which goes back, I think, to how I felt as a child who believed she was born by accident. 
Judd: And a daily, or nightly, chance to prove you do belong there. 
Whitney: IÕm fascinated by comics that keep doing it, because thereÕs so many reasons to quit. You are bad for the first couple years, legitimately bad. The only way to get good is to suck publicly. 
Judd: ItÕs the only job where you have to do it for people when youÕre bad, and they have to pay to watch it. 
Whitney: ItÕs like paying to watch someone go to the gym to get in shape. 
Judd: Do you think it ultimately has been a positive for you, doing stand-up? 
Whitney: I started doing stand-up when Myspace was happening. ThatÕs how I first started hustling for spots. It was around 2004, and I was hustling, hustling, hustling. And it was working until Twitter. Then came all that feedback. 
Judd: YouÕd start reading what people said about you. 
Whitney: It was like, This was working until I found a way to hurt myself with it. It was just weapons out there. 
Judd: Just knowing that, at any moment, if you want to hear someone say something negative about you, itÕs there. 
Whitney: I can corroborate my inner monologue. IÕve had to work incredibly hard at EMDR [eye movement desensitization and reprocessing] and trauma therapy. IÕm trying everything, so I can understand that the kind of person that needs to call someone the N-word, or call me a cunt, or say I am a hag, or whateverÑtheyÕre in pain. The same reason I go onstage to get attention from strangers, they have to try to get that from me. TheyÕre getting their adrenaline fix. ItÕs interesting. When you asked me ÒDid stand-up work for you?Ó I guess it did work, because I started to monetize my pain and my failure. I started getting rewards and started paying my bills because of my mistakes and all the bad things that have happened to me. I remember, after the first special I did, I got a car. I had never had my own car before. I was renting a Kia and hustling. Anyway, I got a hybrid Lexus, and it was the first nice thing I ever bought for myself. I needed a good car, because I was always driving to open mics like, Am I going to make it? One day, I filled it up with diesel fuel. The hybrid. I just jammed the nozzle in. I literally thought the green nozzle meant, like, itÕs green! Good for the environment! Hybrid, green nozzle! It was like a seven-thousand-dollar mistake. I totally trashed my car. Then I wrote a bit about it, and people laughed. So, now I have this muscle that whenever something bad happens, IÕm like, Oh, IÕm going to get a good bit out of this. This is how I survive. 
Judd: What was the trigger to go into comedy? 
Whitney: The first time I understood comedy, I was, like, twelve. I was alone a lot as a kid, and I had to keep myself busy, so I was always creating. I was so bored, I would write out interviews and then answer questions in different characters. I was very lonely as a kid, but I did watch a lot of TV. I watched Roseanne, and I remember thinking, This is healing me somehow. I feel better for this half hour every week. TheyÕre poor and theyÕre talking about being poor, and weÕre not allowed to talk about that. It was almost like a boy looking at Playboy. Oh my God, theyÕre saying they eat out of cans whereas we pretend like we donÕt, you know. But when I was twelve, I was at a yard sale, and I picked up a book by Paul Reiser called Couplehood. I remember reading it and just being like, Oh my God. ThatÕs how I think. I see the negative in everything, too! He was complaining about mundane things. As a kid, really benign things would drive me crazy. I remember a doily somewhere and being so annoyed that it didnÕt fit right. I would obsess over it. I will obsess over the packaging of something being inefficient for too long. Anyway, after Couplehood, I read a book called The Beauty Myth, by Naomi Wolf. ThereÕs a lot of people who have debunked it since, but she was pointing out how the standard of beauty oppresses women. And this just broke my world open. The idea of rejecting a social construct: I was hooked on that idea. For a while, I thought journalism was the solution. 
Judd: IÕm going to write a story about this. 
Whitney: And then everyoneÕs going to listen! Statistics, boring statisticsÑ thatÕs how you get people to wake up! I interned at a local NBC station, and, actually, local news is probably accidentally funnier than anything we could ever script. 
Judd: ThatÕs Anchorman. 
Whitney: Exactly. And so, one day, they let me read at the anchor desk to make a tape, when audition reels were a thing. I got up there, and I was reading the news. And local news, as you know, is so sensational and just upsetting. There was a kid who had been kidnapped. They showed a picture of him. When somethingÕs too dark, I have to make a joke. ThatÕs how I cope. So, they showed a picture of the kid, and I made a joke; I couldnÕt help it. I was like, ÒWho would want to kidnap him?Ó And I just found myself making jokes. Not good ones. But I realized, serious is just too painful. I was making jokes in a news station about a child who might have been dead. 
Judd: ItÕs like George Carlin saying, ÒI root for disasters. I root for churches to burn down. I watch the Indy 500, and I want the cars in the stands.Ó 
Whitney: In hockey, I want the fight. 
Judd: So, when were you aware of stand-up as an option? 
Whitney: People always told me I was funny. I wasnÕt trying to be funny, but when I would tell stories, I would think, Why is everyone laughing? Why wonÕt anyone take me seriously? And then I did that show PunkÕd on MTV. 
Judd: HowÕd you end up on PunkÕd? 
Whitney: I auditioned after the first season. There must have been like four hundred people there. It was one of those cattle calls, and my parking meter was about to run out, and I literally couldnÕt afford to get a ticket; I had that little money. So, I was like, I have to get out of here, and I think one of the producers saw me leaving, and he was like, ÒWhere are you going?Ó And I was like, ÒI canÕt wait for this.Ó There was something about me not wanting it that was attractiveÑwhich I donÕt know when IÕm going to learn that lesson for realÑand he called me in, and he said, ÒIÕm just going to insult you for as long as I can.Ó I had seen people coming out of this audition crying. I was just like, ÒOh, I grew up in a verbally abusive household. LetÕs do this.Ó 
Judd: But why was that the premise? 
Whitney: I think the premise was: LetÕs see how much emotional fortitude you have, because the pranks are so long and stressful, and you canÕt crack. 
Judd: And is he just insulting you, or is he making you pretend youÕre someone else and heÕs someone else? 
Whitney: It was me. I just remember being in there for a couple hours, and at the end of it, they asked me to do PunkÕd, which was not a comedy show. The whole point was: Do not approach it like a comedy, because as soon as you try to be funny, the celebrities are going to know that something is up. 
You canÕt have empathy. You have to just let these famous people cry. I mean, it was awful. 
Judd: Did you do it as long as they wanted you to? Or, at some point, were you like, I canÕt do this anymore. 
Whitney: I needed health care so badly that I didnÕt care. 
Judd: So, by that time, you donÕt think youÕre going to do journalism anymore, right? YouÕre kind of drifting to some sort of hosting role? 
Whitney: I studied theater in college. I did A DollÕs House in college. I thought I was going to be a serious actress, and I guess thought I was going to do that when I came out here. Hosting was like my waitressing. I was just trying to make money. 
Judd: But you thought you were going to act at that point? 
Whitney: It was acting, journalism, hosting. 
Judd: Did you think, I donÕt know which one of these is going to hit, so IÕm just going to spread some bets on the table? 
Whitney: Yes. I needed money. After PunkÕd, I did a show called Best Week Ever, on VH1. It was a bunch of comedians. ItÕs how every respectable comic starts: insulting talented people who have made it. Completely innocent celebrities who are just doing the best they can. It was Paul Scheer and Jessi Klein and Michael Ian Black. 
Judd: Killers. 
Whitney: Killers! And we were all making two hundred dollars to make fun of celebrities. All of a sudden, they started using me a lot, and I would write jokes, and the jokes were pretty good. And then other comics started asking me to write jokes for them. 
Judd: Who did you write for? 
Whitney: I canÕt tell. If they tell, fine, but I promised them. I want to tell so bad, because some of them still owe me money. 
Judd: How much did you get paid per joke? 
Whitney: I got fifty dollars a page. 
Judd: A page? 
Whitney: That was before I understood how much jokes were worth. 
Judd: You got ripped off. 
Whitney: Yes, very. Four or five years later, I was probably charging four hundred dollars a page. But then I wouldnÕt get paid that money. If you charge fifty dollars a page, you actually get paid. You charge four hundred, youÕre never getting paid. 
Judd: When you heard about stand-up, were you scared to make that leap? 
Whitney: This might be clinical narcissism, this might be God, this might be metaphysical, this might be me lying to myself, but when someone said, ÒYou know, you should really do stand-up,Ó I was like, ÒOh, yeah. IÕm a stand-up. ThatÕs what I am.Ó It was like I found something I had been looking for. I had found my keys. The Sundance Channel did this thing called the Festival Dailies, which was coverage of the film festival, and I had gotten a correspondent gig on that. Jay Mohr was hosting it, and Jay is Jay: He likes to poke peopleÕs weaknesses. ItÕs not my favorite quality in a person, but he finds what youÕre insecure about and just goes after it. ItÕs actually kind of a gift, and I was able to come back at him, and he told [producer] Barry Katz, ÒIÕve never seen a girl be able to do that. Normally they just cry.Ó And I think I did cry later, but it was a delayed response. But then Barry Katz took a meeting with me, and I was like, ÒIÕm a stand-up, and I would like you to represent me.Ó He goes, ÒDo you have a tape?Ó And I was like, ÒNo, I havenÕt even done it yet.Ó He said to me, ÒAfter you kill ten times in a row, letÕs talk.Ó And so, I had my goal. I actually did well for my first time ever. I was like, this is my drug. I knew it right away. And then I bombed for, like, two years. First night was great, and then I spent two years chasing that first time. 
Judd: I started in L.A., too. I moved here to go to college in Õ85, and I would just do weird one-nighters at, like, Temecula. And then I started booking Sammy ShoreÕs comedy club in Marina del Rey. It was a comedy club in a fish restaurant. It had a little room in the back, and I would book it, and in return for booking it, the owner would let me do a spot. 
Whitney: I remember there was one gig in Lucky Strike, the bowling alley on Highland. And then SportsmenÕs Lodge was my other spot. On Friday and Saturday nights, the show would start at five. ItÕs still light out. YouÕre doing stand-up to the waiters while cars are zooming by. You would have to yell over the cars. 
Judd: And then you got in at the Store, or was it at the Improv? 
Whitney: The Comedy Store. I couldnÕt get a spot at the Improv, because it was always booked. I went every Sunday and Monday, when there was an open mic. You had to go sign up as early as three. YouÕd wait in line with homeless people. I remember, there was a guy who wore a dollhouse on his head. 
Judd: Was [the owner] still Mitzi [Shore then]? 
Whitney: It was Mitzi. I did that for about a year and a half. YouÕd wait and then come back, and theyÕd tell you if you got three minutes or not. And then youÕd wait for three hours to bomb for three minutes. Something happens to your fight-or-flight response when youÕre up there on that stage. The way itÕs litÑand I know Mitzi lit it this way on purposeÑyou see David LettermanÕs name, and something would just power down in me. Something froze inside me up there, and I would just go bomb, bomb, bomb, bomb. IÕd wait in the hallway and try to get comics to like me, which doesnÕt work, by the way. The harder you try with comics, the more they hate you. ItÕs the best spot in the world to get better, but I didnÕt understand that at the time. And then, one night, Mitzi happened to show up without my knowing she was there. 
Judd: ThatÕs the best audition. 
Whitney: I was bombing so hard, but I didnÕt know she was there, so I was at least having fun bombing. When I came offstage, she was like, ÒYou remind me of Roseanne,Ó and I was like, I think thatÕs a good thing? 
Judd: What year was that? 
Whitney: It must have been 2005. 
Judd: ThatÕs right when [Mitzi] got sick. 
Whitney: She was sick already. There was just something so amazing about watching all these caustic, larger-than-life comedians that I look up to, and then she comes in, and theyÕre all just kissing the ring. She is a prophet. The people that she has handpickedÑthere was something magical about it. The Comedy Store used to be where you go to bomb. It was like the gym. Lifting paint cans and working out the kinks and getting to the truth of who you are. I remember the night that I broke onstage. I would sometimes get the one A.M. spot, which ends up being a two A.M. spot, where you follow Don Barris. YouÕre just cleaning up a mess. YouÕre performing, and the lights are on, and everyoneÕs looking for their stuff, and youÕre just bombing. I remember I got cheated on, and I went down to the Comedy Store, and did my spot, and I was just bombing. I started crying. There was this woman in the audience, and I started talking to her. I was like, ÒI donÕt know if I should text him back.Ó I just had a meltdown. I started telling the truth, and people started responding and laughing, and I was like, Oh, they donÕt want you to pretend to be funny. They just want to hear your vulnerabilities. I thought doing stand-up was about going up and pretending youÕre really strong and put together and sharp. 
Judd: ItÕs like a eureka moment. Garry Shandling used to always say, ÒYouÕre trying to get to your core.Ó 
Whitney: The armor came off. Something happened when I stopped caring what they thought. ThatÕs when they started liking it. ItÕs like how Chris Rock says, ÒIf youÕre bombing, slow down.Ó DonÕt speed up, donÕt try harder. ThatÕs the worst thing you can do. 
Judd: Steve CarellÑthe first time he did Jimmy Kimmel, promoting 40-Year-Old Virgin, he was sweating more than Albert Brooks on Broadcast News. But he didnÕt acknowledge it, and the place was getting more and more tense. And then, finally, he made some joke about how much he was sweating, and the place laughed so hard, and Jimmy Kimmel said, ÒThank God you mentioned it.Ó And then the whole thing worked. So, after all that, you did roasts for a little bit? 
Whitney: The first one was Joan Rivers, my idol. I had a joke that was like, ÒJoan, I loved you in The Wrestler.Ó It was rough. ÒJoan, youÕre so old, your vagina has a separate entrance for Black dicks.Ó I was like, ÒBy the way, I love you so much. IÕm so sorry about this.Ó It was such a conflict of interest, and I realized [that] comedy, even the meanest jokes, is how we show love to each other. IÕm not saying itÕs healthy. 
Judd: And she enjoyed it? 
Whitney: She enjoyed it. 
Judd: I never want to be roasted. I donÕt want to know what the joke of me is. 
Whitney: IÕm curious. ThereÕs something liberating about it. 
Judd: The idea of everyone telling me the joke that IÕm worried about when I leave a room. I donÕt want to know what that is. Ever. 
Whitney: It hurts, it definitely hurts. I remember the one with Carrot Top. He got emotional because heÕs sort of sequestered. When youÕre around comics a lot, youÕre more used to it. 
Judd: HeÕs just with his friends in Vegas. 
Whitney: All of a sudden, theyÕre making plastic surgery jokes about his face, and I donÕt think anyone does that on a regular basis, so it was heartbreaking to watch. Roasts were a tricky balance for me. After a while, I was like, This hurts too much. I canÕt do this anymore. 
Judd: Do you think itÕs different for women, the experience of opening up onstage? 
Whitney: I still donÕt have a good answer about women in comedy and sexism. ThereÕs a comic, Kevin Christy, who I take on the road with me whenever I can. One night, someone charged the stage at me. I did some joke aboutÑitÕs a generalization, obviouslyÑhow all guys have a jar of coins. ItÕs a dumb observation. I said it, and people laughed; I guess it resonated. And one guy just snapped. He goes, ÒThatÕs so we can pay for your shit!Ó I obviously stepped on a land mine, and Kevin said something to me later. He was like, ÒI think something happens with you, and I think itÕs probably just a female thing in general, where for men, you become their wife, their mother that didnÕt breast-feed.Ó ItÕs funny until forty-five minutes in, when they decide, A woman is just yelling at me at this point. 
Judd: A woman whoÕs strong and who might be wittier and smarter than me, who I donÕt want to make me feel bad about myself. 
Whitney: Is it emasculating? 
Judd: ThereÕs something about exposing yourself or being comfortable showing your flaws. 
Whitney: ItÕs triggering, showing your humanity. Being comfortable with yourself sexually or your body. How dare you not have shame? ThereÕs a tribalism when someone is not acquiescing to the norms of the tribe. ThereÕs something frustrating about a nonconformist, you know? We [humans] seek sameness on a very deep, tribal level. If that person is similar to me, they will protect me. 
Judd: And people want to find a way to make certain people be quiet. 
Whitney: Yes. ÒYouÕre disrupting the status quo.Ó Stop. And ÒI want to fuck you and I canÕt.Ó 
Judd: Well, thatÕs a whole different part of the problem. 
Whitney: Honestly, I was way more scared of women at the beginning than I was of men. I was such a jealous, scared person who saw a lot of cheating growing up, and I wouldnÕt even make eye contact with men. I just wanted the women to like me. I would talk about dating stuff, and I remember couples would get in fights in the audience. Because IÕd be like, ÒAnd this guy cheated on me, and this is how he lied, and he sent this text,Ó and I would see the girl [look at her boyfriend and] be like, ÒYouÕve done that.Ó I would have guys come up to me all the time and be like ÒWhat the fuck? Why did you do that?Ó 
Judd: ÒWhy did you give away my secret?Ó 
Whitney: Totally! ÒI was just trying to take this girl on a date, and now weÕre fucking arguing!Ó It would happen all the time. Maybe itÕs just the areas IÕm walking around in. But I think itÕs that when people donÕt have shame, itÕs very triggering to people who do, because shame is what keeps tribes controlled. 
Judd: Amy Schumer has a funny line, where she is talking about being famous, and she understands why people turn on you a little bit when you get famous. She goes, ÒI have to admit, I have mixed feelings about Taylor Swift.Ó In some way, itÕs as simple as that. 
Whitney: Comedy is an extension of schadenfreude. Watch me suffer for your enjoyment. ThatÕs really what it is. Our job is to go into these areas people are uncomfortable talking about: money, race, sex. WeÕre talking about really incendiary ideas that piss people off and [that] they have been avoiding for a reason. The worst set IÕve ever had was in Vegas. It was one of the scariest moments IÕve experienced as an adult. I go back to my hotel, and IÕm about to go to sleep, whenÑbang bang bang!Ñon my door. Five guys yelling, ÒYou know what you need? Some dick!Ó 
Judd: How did they know where your room is? 
Whitney: I had walked back to my room. I was with security, but they followed me back. And I was terrified. It was a tribe of drunk men telling me that, because I was successful, I needed to get fucked or humbled. So, that was a very interesting reaction. This is a huge generalization, but men, at least these men, they donÕt like the idea of not being needed. 
Judd: Or [of] the other person being stronger. 
Whitney: Or having money. Money, to me, is the newest taboo, if you want to make someone uncomfortable. If I say to you, ÒHey, how many times a week do you have sex?Ó itÕs like, meh. But if I say, ÒHow much money do you have?Ó 
Judd: Wait, itÕs supposed to be Òa weekÓ? 
Whitney: I have sex with my money twice a week. 
Judd: Do you want to do another TV show? 
Whitney: I do. I just want to do it right, if thereÕs such a thing. IÕve been such a paralyzing perfectionist the last couple of years, and I have so much fear about what happened with my first show. I get that there are worse things than having your own TV show, but that trap of having your name on something and it not being good enoughÑitÕs your identity. 
Judd: So, it paralyzes you about doing the next one? 
Whitney: I think it did. IÕm working on my next special, because stand-up doesnÕt hurt you like TV. 
Judd: It takes so much energy to just get into the fucking business, where youÕre hustling and happy to do prank shows and be a host. But then, when things go well, itÕs so easy to get paralyzed, because now youÕve created some idea of what it is to be successful. 
Whitney: I have an insecurity in that I want everyone to know that IÕm turning stuff down, because I donÕt want them to think IÕm not being asked. ItÕs literally the high school playground. 
Judd: You want to feel like youÕre still in the game and for them to know youÕre still in the game. Shandling always talked about how itÕs okay to not work. Like, you exist when youÕre not on TV, and you have to have something to say, or you shouldnÕt be saying it. 
Whitney: ÒYou exist when youÕre not on TVÓ is the most heartbreaking concept. WeÕre all five, you know? My dad passed away, and I just was like, 
Okay, the person whose approval I wanted is gone. Can I stop now? 

WHOOPI GOLDBERG 
September 2020 
Whoopi: When I think of all the things that I had planned to do in 2020, I see that they all went to hell in a handbasket. 
Judd: ItÕs a little bit like weÕre all having this pause to go, What is my life? WhatÕs the value of it? 
Whoopi: ThatÕs kind of why everything is happening, isnÕt it? Now that people are paying attention, the little stuff that used to roll off your back now becomes a boulder. You notice things. And I think itÕs exciting to see people having to really stand up and take stock of where theyÕre at. Many of my friends would call me in the early days of the Black Lives Matter protests and marches and would ask me if I thought they were privileged, and if I was resentful of them. I would usually respond by telling them, ÒListen, youÕre a pretty decent person, but IÕm not with you when you treat people poorly. You may treat me well, but if you think youÕve acted poorly toward other people, then you need to make a change.Ó When my granddaughter and I had a big talk about these things, she said, ÒIÕm so glad youÕre woke, Granny.Ó I said to her, ÒWhat do you mean ÔwokeÕ? IÕve never been asleep. You just woke up, but when youÕre a kid who comes through the sixties and seventies and eighties, you fought for the vote, you fought for the right to decide what you do with your body. Those rights werenÕt a given. So, you had to be awake for all of it, to make sure that you werenÕt losing sight of the fight.Ó When I said that to her, she said, ÒI never thought of it that way.Ó And I thought, Ah, to be in my twenties again, as opposed to my mid-sixties. 
Judd: You were born in Õ55. ThatÕs almost the perfect starting point to see what was achieved in each decade, and what was forgotten. Some of us are only realizing these things now, because of our privilege. I notice that when we try to hire diverse directors or writers, that the pipeline isnÕt even there. No oneÕs been giving them the smaller break to get to the place where you can give them this bigger break. The pipeline hasnÕt been working. 
Whoopi: Because thereÕs no Rolodex for that. ThereÕs a dearth of diversity thatÕs been everywhere for a very long time. So, you have to go person by person, incident by incident, to figure out what you need, until we get to the place where itÕs just people. Because, at some point, people are going to have to deal with the fact that there is no blueprint for this. If you want to fix the ÒOscarsSoWhiteÓ problem, youÕve got to get to the casting directors, youÕve got to get to the people who are writing the movies. There is more to it than just, Oh, there are no Black people. ThereÕs a whole bunch more of us than there were, say, between the years of 1940, when Hattie McDaniel got her Oscar, to the time that Sidney Poitier got his Oscar, in 1964. ThatÕs a whole empty space of people who could have been here. ItÕs empty of the Anna May Wongs, who were there acting and trying to do their thing. Meanwhile, people were painting white people up and saying, ÒWell, hereÕs Mickey Rooney.Ó 
Judd: Playing an Asian man. 
Whoopi: ItÕs not that IÕm mad that youÕre playing the Asian guy. ItÕs not that IÕm mad that youÕre playing the Black guy. But there are Black people. There are Asian people. TheyÕre here. So, give them a shot. Maybe you might find a couple that can act, or design clothes, or do hair, or create things, or make the space more interesting. But all of these things seem to be changing. ItÕs slow, but I hear a shift coming. And I like it. 
Judd: The first time I was around you, I was working at the Comic Relief charity event. I was probably seventeen years old. IÕd heard about the event, and as a comedy fan, I just said, ÒIs there anything I can do to help you? IÕd work for free.Ó My job was to put together benefits at comedy clubs, but I was able to watch those rehearsals for the main shows, and part of my job was to get everybody to sign the poster for the show, so I got to walk up to every legend in the world to say, ÒCan you sign these fifty posters?Ó And I would get like eight minutes with each person, which, as a seventeen-year.old, was life-changing. When you look back at all of that, what was the Comic Relief experience like for you? How did you get involved? 
Whoopi: I got a phone call from my agent, and they said, ÒChris Albrecht wants to know if you want to be part of this telethon that theyÕre doing. ItÕs for the homeless, and itÕs with Billy Crystal and Robin Williams.Ó Now, IÕd known Robin for quite some time. He used to come down to San Diego, where I was living and performing years before, and worked at the Comedy Store. But I was crazy about Billy Crystal and really wanted to meet him. I said, ÒWhatever you want. IÕm there.Ó And, suddenly, I was among these legends, and I thought, What the fuck am I doing here? IÕm not a stand-up comedian. I tell ridiculously long stories that are funny at times. I donÕt fit here. And one day, Billy sat me down and said, ÒIÕve seen your shows; you do fit here. And the three of us are going to go out, and weÕre going to find a way to help the homeless.Ó And then, of course, RobinÕs like, ÒYou feel Mr. Happy? He loves you, too.Ó So, Billy and Robin became my big brothers, and from that moment on, they would call and check on me. They both talked me up to [producer] Gilbert Cates, and thatÕs how I got to do the Academy Awards. I canÕt even describe how much I love them. I was a bit of an anomaly when I got there. Nobody really knew what to do with me. They were like, ÒYouÕre Black, right?Ó And I go, ÒYeah, IÕve been Black the whole time.Ó Richard Pryor once said to me, ÒListen, they donÕt get it. TheyÕre not going to get you, just know that. You look different. You donÕt look like youÕre trying to be anything but what you are. But as long as you are you, youÕll be fine.Ó 
Judd: YouÕre such a quintessential New Yorker. How did you end up in San Diego? 
Whoopi: I grew up in Chelsea in New York, and we had the Hudson Guild Theater, where my mom was the theater manager. IÕd go and hang out and help her and talk to these acting troupes that came through. And one of the guys says, ÒIÕm getting ready to go home to California; my wife just had a baby. We have a four-year-old, and IÕm gonna need a nanny.Ó I had a one.year-old at the time, and I said, ÒIÕll be the nanny.Ó And my mother said, ÒWhat are you doing?Ó I said, ÒWell, IÕve always wanted to go to California. We donÕt have any money. So, IÕm going to see what this is. And IÕm taking my daughter, Alex, with me.Ó ThatÕs how I got to San Diego, where we stayed for eight or nine years. 
Judd: Did you know that you were trying to get to California to ultimately be a part of entertainment and performing? Did you have that dream? 
Whoopi: I always was a performer. My mother was like, ÒYou can do this if you want to. But you should remember that itÕs going to be a little harder for you, because you donÕt look like other people.Ó But I always thought, I have a different kind of thing I want to do. So, when I got to San Diego and ended up at the San Diego Rep, I got to do lots of different things. We created a theater. I joined a troupe of people who were doing improv, which is really my joy. Years later, I met a guy who lived in San Francisco, and I thought, What do you think, kids? Should we try San Francisco? Later, my daughter and I are driving around San Francisco to buy shoesÑsheÕs around eight or nineÑand weÕre listening to Alice Walker read The Color Purple on NPR in our van, and we pull over because weÕre so mesmerized by the story. So, weÕre sitting in the van at the Bay of Oakland, listening to her read these chapters. And because IÕm a visualist, I can see everything sheÕs reading. Afterward, I write a letter to her, via her publisher, saying, ÒMy name is Whoopi Goldberg, and IÕm a performer. And if they make a movie of your book, I will play the dirt on the floor.Ó I give her my address in New York, which is my motherÕs address, and when I finally get to New York to do a monologue festival, my mother says, ÒOh, a letter came for you about two weeks ago.Ó I look at it, and the return address says ÒAlice Walker.Ó I open it, and she says, basically, ÒDear Whoopi, I live in Oakland. IÕve seen your shows. I know exactly what you do. Quincy Jones and several other people are trying to put this together to make the movie. IÕve already sent your paperwork. I already sent everything I know about you.Ó 
Judd: Wow. 
Whoopi: Then I go to the festival, which no one had been coming to those first couple of days, and suddenly, Bette Midler is one of the twelve people in the audience. Then a couple of days later, thereÕs forty-five people in the audience. And a couple of days later, thereÕs a hundred people in the audience, and then a critic for The [New York] Times came, Mel Gussow, and if I had been his girlfriend, I could not have gotten a better review. 
Judd: Were you doing a full night of monologues in San Francisco, and you brought a full night to New York? Or do you have to assemble it for New York? 
Whoopi: I always had fifteen that I could choose from, because I wrote them all myself. I used each piece as an audition piece. I thought, IÕm going to just write some monologues and see if anybody knows that these arenÕt written by somebody else. 
Judd: When did you realize you could perform the monologues and not just audition with them? Where did you go? 
Whoopi: ThereÕs a place in Berkeley, California, called the Cellar. There was also a little theater called the Rose Caf in San Francisco. You could go and do shows at the Fillmore occasionally. I learned a lot of stuff because I was around a lot of dancers and a lot of performance artists. And then AIDS hit and cleaned out a whole bunch of folks, and that started a long fight to just get respect for our friends, whose roommates would sometimes kick them out and leave them in the streets in their dementia. It was a wild and scary time, because you didnÕt know how you got it. 
Judd: Did that influence the types of monologues you were writing? It seems like, in the beginning, youÕre writing them just to showcase your talent. Did you become more aware of what you were trying to say and the purpose of it? 
Whoopi: One of my friends at the time said, ÒCan you write a piece about somebody like me?Ó And I said, ÒI donÕt know.Ó And he said, ÒBut you can do anything. YouÕre an actor. Why wonÕt you do this?Ó At the same time, I had just come back from Amsterdam, where I visited the Anne Frank House. It was such a life-changing experience, I wanted to tell somebody what happened to me and how it felt. So, I thought, Well, fuck, maybe I can just write something. So, I wrote it through the eyes of a junkie, because I knew that life quite well. And once I wrote that, I thought, Maybe I can do something else. So, I wrote a piece about a girl who is physically different, but in her dreams, she thinks of herself as completely straight, and she moves onstage like a dancer. Suddenly, those two stories joined with my outrage at people who were throwing their children out because they got pregnant, which led me to write a piece about this surfer girl who gets pregnant. She goes to the church to confess, and the priest throws her out. So, she goes home to tell her mother, and her mother throws her out. So, she basically lives at the beach, and she took a hanger and did what she needed to do. But onstage, I would untwist the hanger. I made people very uncomfortable. And then she talks about the fact that she canÕt have children now, but sheÕs still a great surfer. So, that became the sort of shows that I did, and that became The Spook Show that I did on Broadway. 
Judd: ItÕs always interesting to look at that early stage of a personÕs career, when you have no sense of what you should not be able to pull off. ItÕs almost like youÕre delusional when youÕre young. 
Whoopi: I got very lucky. And I continue to have the luckiest career on the planet. Because thereÕs no way, looking like I did and sounding like I did, that anything should have gone in the direction that it did. I just am who I am. 
Judd: I think of the idea of representation, that people see you up there and go, Oh, thereÕs a way to do this. Mindy Kaling said she just never had the mindset that she wouldnÕt be accepted. Did you feel that way? 
Whoopi: When I was in my early teens, my best friend, Rosie, and I were going to go see Romeo and Juliet. I was a bit of a freak back then. I had big Afro puffs and bell bottoms and was just a hippie girl. When Rosie came to get me, she looked at me and she said, ÒAre you gonna change?Ó And I was like, ÒNo, why?Ó And she said, ÒBecause you just look dirty.Ó I said, ÒBut IÕm not dirty. Smell me. IÕm clean.Ó She said, ÒIf you donÕt change your clothes, IÕm not going with you,Ó and I said, ÒWell, IÕm not changing my clothes,Ó so she left. And I said to my mother, ÒAm I wrong?Ó And my mother said, ÒDo you think youÕre wrong?Ó I said, ÒNo, I donÕt think itÕs fair that I should change. I didnÕt ask her to change.Ó And my mother said, ÒWell, thatÕs a sign of independent thinking. And if youÕre comfortable the way you are, then youÕre fine. If you are not comfortable, then maybe you have to change.Ó I said, ÒIÕm comfortable.Ó And she said, ÒWell, thatÕs the answer to the question.Ó And that cemented the idea that I could make decisions for myself, and sometimes theyÕre right, and sometimes theyÕre wrong, but theyÕre mine. 
Judd: Your mom just sounds like such an incredible person. 
Whoopi: She was. 
Judd: What do you think was the secret to her being such a great parent? 
Whoopi: My mother thought she was a freak, too. So, she raised us like she was raised: as freaky kids. 
Judd: Proudly. 
Whoopi: She was different from folks in the neighborhood. Her interests were different, and she was interested in the greater experience of the world. We were raised so we should know what foreign cuisines taste like, in case we got to go to Ireland or to China. So, she fed us bok choy and things because it was inexpensive. She created stuff with nothing. And I loved what my mother did for us for Christmas. Eight days out from Christmas, we would wake up, and on our windows there were stencils of SantaÕs face and snowmen and all kinds of stuff. And we didnÕt know who did it. Every night, something would happen. The tree would show up. And then the next night, half of the tree was done. And then the night after, the other half of the tree was done. So, that took us up to Christmas Eve, and there was nothing under the tree. My brother and I would look everywhere for the presents, and there was nothing. But on Christmas morning, there would be a bicycle, thereÕd be a Lionel train set for me, thereÕd be board games like Sorry and Candy Land. My mother just made it magical. 
Judd: It sounds like she worked really hard for you guys. 
Whoopi: She worked her ass off, and she paid bills and did all that stuff that parents are supposed to do. And she did it with an eye to making us understand that when youÕre a kid, things are supposed to be magical. But when I turned twelve or thirteen, she had a nervous breakdown. She was gone for about two years, at Bellevue. Later, when I was an adult, and my mother was talking about that time, she said, ÒI didnÕt know who you kids were, and I did not want to go back. I just went along with whatever you all were telling me, and I learned about who you are.Ó In those days, if you got put away, your husband could decide what treatments you got. So, they did all kinds of electroshock shit on her, and her thing was, ÒI will never go to another doctor in my life.Ó She died when she was eighty-five and never went back to another doctor. 
Judd: What was her diagnosis? 
Whoopi: I donÕt know. I just came home from school one day, and she was crazy. But she was present enough to tell me to go get help from one of the neighbors, which I did, and the neighbor got hold of the hospital. But IÕll never forget them taking her down into the elevator, the elevator stopping on different floors, people getting in the elevator and looking at us, and then watching them take her down the stairs into the ambulance. In those days, your kids couldnÕt go to the hospital with you. So, she went into the ambulance, and she was gone. 
Judd: You didnÕt see her for years? 
Whoopi: For about two years. 
Judd: And what about your dad? 
Whoopi: My dad and cousins came and took care of us so we didnÕt have to move. But itÕs very crazy when your dad comes out of the blue, and you donÕt really know him, and you donÕt know if you like him. 
Judd: What did your dad do? 
Whoopi: My dad was part of the Jewel Box Revue for a while. It was a group of male performers who dressed as women and would imitate people. They would get booked at bar mitzvahs and all kinds of great things. So, he did that for a while. He worked in the Diamond District for a while. He was a preacher. He was just really interesting. And I believe he was a very happy gay man. 
Judd: So, he wasnÕt really around until that moment? Did you get closer with him after he returned? 
Whoopi: He was there, but he didnÕt really want to be there. He knew that there wasnÕt a lot of choice. But you know, when I got famous, Bob, [my father,] told the National Enquirer that I wasnÕt paying enough attention to him. 
Judd: That always helps. 
Whoopi: Always. And then he passed away, and the Enquirer went down to take pictures of him in his coffin and to talk about the fact that I wasnÕt down there to bury my own father. My brother was. So, yeah, I have had a very interesting life. 
Judd: IÕm interested in Mike Nichols stepping into your life. To have this man from another generation step into your work process and somehow understand it. What did he have to tell you at that moment? 
Whoopi: It will probably make more sense if I tell you how it happened. It was back when I was doing The Spook Show, where I do all my characters, and Mike was in town and came to see me perform. I think I started with Fontaine, who is the junkie who talks about traveling on the plane to Amsterdam and then going to the Anne Frank House. And Mike had been on the very last ship out of Germany, before the Nazis just shut everything down. So, the Anne Frank story took him and twisted him up. When I get offstage, my mother is sitting backstage, and she says, ÒI have to tell you something, Mike Nichols is here. He came to see the show.Ó I said, ÒI canÕt believe it, fuck fuck fuck. Oh my God, are you sure?Ó And then thereÕs a knock on the door, and itÕs him. HeÕs standing right there. And his eyes are wet. He comes in and says, ÒI have to tell you, that story about being at the Anne Frank House just knocked me out. Do you have plans, would you consider doing Broadway?Ó And I said, ÒYeah, okay.Ó He said, ÒAre you not sure?Ó I said, ÒNo, no, IÕm sure, Mr. Nichols, but I donÕt want to suck. And then you get a bad mark because I was no good.Ó He said, ÒMiss Goldberg, if you suck, IÕll be there to unsuck you.Ó So, I went back to Berkeley, and about a month later, the phone rings, and it was Mike. He said, ÒDo you remember when I asked you if you would be interested in doing Broadway? Well, I found a theater for us.Ó So, we made the plan, and once IÕm in New York, he goes, ÒDo you want to go to lunch with some friends of mine?Ó So, we walk into this restaurant, and as IÕm walking, IÕm thinking, Is that Paul Simon? Is that Steve Martin? Oh, my God. Carl Reiner, oh my God. IÕm walking very coolly, but inside, IÕm dying. And thatÕs what he would do to me. 
Judd: Was he desensitizing you to it? To get you comfortable with being part of Broadway and that pressure? 
Whoopi: Yes, and what he thought was coming. Remember, IÕve had this amazing review from Mel Gussow, and Mike uses that as part of the advertising for the show, so weÕre sold out. We hadnÕt even started yet. And on opening night, Ruth Gordon is there. Garson Kanin and Diana RossÑall these people. IÕm like, What the fuck? What is happening here? And they like it. They like the show. So, it was kind of spectacular. 
Judd: Were you able to tune in and be present and enjoy the moment? It can be hard when youÕre trying to focus on the work and not melt down. 
Whoopi: At the opening night party, my mother was all teeth. We were both standing there with these big smiles. And Paul Simon was at the party and came over, and he said, ÒI want you to enjoy everything thatÕs coming, because Artie and I didnÕt know we were supposed to enjoy it. We missed a lot. Have the best time and know that people are going to try to take it from you.Ó He said, ÒDo what you know you want to do and try not to smoke too much more weed.Ó 
Judd: How did you get from there to doing The Color Purple? 
Whoopi: I met up with Steven Spielberg after the Broadway run, because he was getting ready to set up The Color Purple. He wanted me to bring my show to Amblin Studios, where he had a little theater, because he wasnÕt able to get to New York. By then, of course, I had a manager and an agent. And both of them say to me, ÒLook, we love your story. We love you. But when you do this thing for Steven, you cannot do Bleed T. You cannot.Ó 
Judd: Wait, whatÕs Bleed T? 
Whoopi: Bleed T is a piece that I wrote about what would happen if E.T. actually landed in the projects. And he canÕt find his way home because the phones donÕt work. He starts running the streets with Elliott, who works for a pimp. He then gets a Jheri curlÑremember those? And he starts dressing like a pimp. HeÕs got the shoes, because heÕs very short, and heÕs running chicks. Then, as his people come back to get him, he pulls out two machine guns and he machine-guns his people, because when you assimilate too much, you lose the spirit of who you are. That was the piece they didnÕt want me to do. So, I do the show for Spielberg, without that piece, and everyone is loving it. IÕm looking out the curtain before I come out, and itÕs Michael Jackson, itÕs Ashford and Simpson, and Quincy Jones. 
Judd: A low-pressure show. 
Whoopi: Now IÕm thinking, Fuck fuck fuck. So, I do the show. And they say, ÒMore!Ó I said, ÒI canÕt really do more.Ó And Steven says, ÒWhy not?Ó I said, ÒBecause my agent told me I shouldnÕt do this piece for you, because it would upset you.Ó He said, ÒWhat is it? You can tell me.Ó So, I tell him, and he says, ÒI want to see it.Ó So, I did it, the whole piece. And as IÕm doing it, I hear laughter. IÕm afraid to look at anybody in the moment, of course. And then it ends where I explain that, you know, E.T. assimilated and forgot who he was and where he came from. That stopped the laughter. Then I went over to do my bow, and you know how you see those movies where people just stand up and start clapping? ThatÕs what it turned into. And Spielberg said, ÒI never would have thought that was the direction that it would go in, that you bring it all the way back around and talk about assimilation.Ó I said, ÒWell, I always hear people talking about how you should learn how to speak English, you have to learn how to do this and that. I grew up in a neighborhood where the first generation never spoke English, and that helped us learn smatterings of different languages from our friends.Ó He said, ÒThat is a great way to learn. Would you like to do The Color Purple?Ó I was like, ÒYes, I would.Ó He said, ÒI want you to be Celie.Ó I said, ÒI think I need something smaller, because I donÕt know anything about making movies.Ó He said, ÒLeave that to me.Ó 
Judd: ItÕs remarkable to get those mentors and collaborators. You had Mike Nichols for theater, which is the best you can do. And then, immediately, you work with Quincy Jones and Steven Spielberg. ItÕs a real run of the exact right people to take you to the next level, who are at the top of their game. 
Whoopi: As it turns out, years later, someone said to me, ÒYou were such a disappointment. We all thought you would be the female Eddie Murphy.Ó I was like, ÒWait, I was the female Whoopi Goldberg.Ó I didnÕt know I was supposed to try to be Eddie Murphy. But I got to do the things that I thought were fun, like Fatal Beauty, which was not a deep movie, but I love those movies. And I got to do Sister Act and ClaraÕs Heart and all kinds of stuff. People kept saying, ÒYouÕre always playing a housekeeper.Ó I said, ÒYeah, man. God bless those women. Most of you guys would not have a life without the fucking housekeeper.Ó It doesnÕt seem degrading to me. I would meet up with directors and producers, and there was this one guy who was explaining their movie to me, and he put his hands in my hair and said, ÒWhat do we do with this shit?Ó I said, ÒYou mean the shit on my head? Well, we can put it in a wig. Or we can keep it like that. How would you like this shit to be?Ó I thought, I can either flip the fuck out or I can just recognize that you donÕt know any better. It wasnÕt a good match. And then, suddenly, it was good matches. I ended up with [producer] Jerry Zucker and [director] Emile Ardolino. 
Judd: It feels like show business was very different at that time, where you had megastars and big personalities. That seems to have gone away somewhat. 
Whoopi: Or weÕve moved to the next rung, and itÕs not your turn anymore; itÕs their turn. So you have to keep moving. I got in trouble, too, because I was talking shit about the president. I didnÕt work for five years. All my endorsements disappeared. 
Judd: This was in what year? 
Whoopi: This was the first four years of Kid Bush, during the run-up to the second four years. IÕd done a fundraiser for [John] Kerry and John Edwards, and I said, ÒYou know, I love bush, but someoneÕs been giving bush a bad name. And I think itÕs time that we keep bush where it should be and get your ass out there and vote.Ó That was the extent of it. And people said that I had been vulgar. Like, really, disgustingly vulgar. By the time I got home, all my endorsements were gone. Everything was gone. Five years of trying to understand what the problem was, because it was a fundraiser where other people were actually saying some really rough shit. The DNC had the video, and all they had to do was play the video and say, ÒShe didnÕt actually say anything like this.Ó But they didnÕt share the video, because they wanted to protect Meryl Streep and Paul Newman. They didnÕt want to put them in jeopardy. So, I took that. I ended up spending everything I had saved to keep my family afloat. And then, three years later, Barbara Walters asked me if I would join The View, and I say, ÒYou understand IÕm persona non grata?Ó 
And she said, ÒOh, yes.Ó But then she hired Rosie OÕDonnell, and then a year went by, and Rosie just imploded. And [Barbara] calls me back and is like, ÒOkay, really, will you come and do it?Ó And thatÕs how I got to The View. 
Judd: Does it feel good to do something like thatÑto become successful, in a gigantic way, for the same exact bold style that slowed you down? 
Whoopi: I just desperately needed a job. Desperately, desperately needed a fucking job. You know, if they said, ÒYouÕve got to go be Bozo with a red nose on your head and dance in front of the stereo store,Ó thatÕs where I would have been. It was a tough time. But then The View turned into something that was more than just a womenÕs talk show. 
Judd: Every time I go there, that set feels like it crackles with tension. It feels like thereÕs a lot happening in that building, and even if the people change, itÕs still that same tension. It must be stressful to know youÕre going into some verbal warfare? 
Whoopi: Well, the whole idea is to try to keep the verbal warfare on a scale where we can still have the conversation. We can be testy about it, but we canÕt be nasty. We have to be talking about the thing weÕre talking about and not personally zinging each other. For me, thatÕs the thing. Sometimes people come, and theyÕre ready to fight. ItÕs like, IÕm not fighting with you. I just want to know why you feel like that. Where did it come from? In my mind, I have to try to help people if theyÕre feeling like they donÕt know why somethingÕs messed up. For instance, I once had a discussion with a woman who was upset that her pastor said, ÒFuck America.Ó She was like, ÒI canÕt believe he would say such a thing.Ó I said, ÒMaybe itÕs because he fought in World War Two. He came home and had to get off the sidewalk to make way for white people to walk.Ó And she said, ÒWhat are you talking about?Ó I said, ÒWell, the Black fighters who came back home to America after fighting to liberate other countries were not free to vote in their country.Ó And she said, ÒI donÕt know anything about that.Ó I said, ÒBecause it doesnÕt have anything to do with you. ThatÕs why you donÕt know.Ó So, she came back two, three days later, and she said, ÒI started reading about all that stuff you were talking about. I had no idea.Ó I said, ÒI know. ThatÕs okay. But now you know.Ó She and I have been friends from then on. 

WILL FERRELL 
April 2020 
Judd: As you know, I went to the University of Southern California. I was there from 1985 to Õ87. Then I ran out of money and dropped out. 
Will: We were there roughly at the same time. 
Judd: But I went to cinema school, and you went to the University of Southern California and studied sports informationÑI still donÕt know what that means. 
Will: I was going to be a sportscaster. That was the dream. But they only offered the major for five years, I think, and then they stopped doing it because it was too hard. 
Judd: They got rid of the major. I remember seeing those kids at USC. You would see them walk around campus dressed up and looking like Bob Costas and Marv Albert. 
Will: ItÕd be those kids, and then the rest of the class would be athletes. I thought I was going to be on SportsCenter. But when I saw that you had to start out in places like Yuma, Arizona, and you work your way through the system, I decided, Well, I also want to try comedy, which seems equally as hard but more fun. It was probably harder to break through, but I still thought, IÕm not going to try the broadcasting thing. IÕm going to try this comedy thing. 
Judd: And you hadnÕt done any comedy up until then? 
Will: No comedy, other than entertaining my fraternity brothers on occasion. I had gone and seen a show the spring semester of senior year at the Groundlings Theatre on Melrose, in Hollywood, which is kind of the jumping-off place for everyone from Phil Hartman to Lisa Kudrow, to Jon Lovitz, to Laraine Newman from the original Saturday Night Live cast, to Conan OÕBrien. Tons of actors and writers have come through their school. I watched one of their shows and saw in the lobby afterward that they had a school that you could audition for, and I thought, Oh, that looks like that would be fun. 
Judd: And how long after Groundlings did you get the job at SNL? 
Will: From the time I took my first class until SNL, it was three years. 
Judd: ThatÕs crazy. 
Will: ItÕs pretty quick. 
Judd: Why werenÕt you terrified? Where did your confidence as a performer come from if you were still relatively new to this? 
Will: I had the best conversation with my dad, whoÕs a lifelong musician. He had an incredible career without Òmaking itÓ and yet still was a working musician, which is so hard to do. He played with the Righteous Brothers on and off for twenty years, and he played in Vegas, and heÕd had his own bands and this and that. HeÕd seen enough of the big time, so to speak, and we went to lunch one day, and I said to him, ÒI think IÕm going to try this comedy thing. I think IÕm going to give it a shot. Do you have any advice for me?Ó And his big gut talk to me was, ÒYou know what? I think you have the skill, but it takes a lot of luck. If you donÕt make it, donÕt worry about it. You can just try something else.Ó Like, donÕt worry if you fail, because itÕs a crapshoot anyway. And so, from that point, I was like, Oh, IÕm just playing the lottery here so I might as well just go have fun. And at the same time, I was trying to do everything from the Groundlings to those crazy Largo shows and just any stand-up comedy that became available. I tried to throw as many darts against the dartboard as I could. 
Judd: So, you werenÕt freaked out about not making it. 
Will: I was like, This is probably not going to happen. But I can always be a substitute school teacher. 
Judd: That took the pressure off of you, to not want it that much? 
Will: Exactly. In a weird way, it made it feel like a game. But thenÑcut to the most nervous moment I ever had, to the point of nearly shitting my pants: the night of the first episode of SNL for our grand cast, and I got to say the first lines. That was the moment where I was like, Oh, shoot. This is the thing youÕd always dreamed about doing and youÕre about to do it. Your mom is in the audience, sheÕs brought her friends, everyone back at your old theater is watchingÉAnd the moment hit me, and, sure enough, I flubbed. The first thing I ever say on the show, I flub. 
Judd: I want to talk a bit about your path to SNL. You had a sketch at the Groundlings, which was you yelling at a child, who we donÕt see, to get off the shed. You did that as your audition for Lorne, and then they put it on the first episode. 
Will: Right. 
Judd: So, at least you got to scream in your first sketch. That helped, I would assume. 
Will: That helped. Because I was part of the cold open, and thatÕs where I flubbed the line, and then the first sketch up was the BBQ dad yelling at his kids to get off the shed. So, I was able to really let loose. ItÕs basically just a benign backyard barbecue, with suburban people talking about everything from their jobs to playing golf. And I just keep looking off-camera and saying, ÒHey, get off the shed. Okay? You do that for me? Anyway, I donÕt know if you talked to Mr. Bilson at the office. I heard he threw his back out. 
Hey, buddy. I need you to get off the shed. Please! So, yeah, he said he was sidelined for about three weeks. GET OFF THE SHED!Ó 
Judd: ItÕs amazing that this very simple premise is what launched everything for you. 
Will: I became known during the audition as the ÒGet Off the Shed Guy.Ó There were two people singled out in our first show that year, Cheri Oteri and myself. And Cheri was singled out because she had a phenomenal first show. She was the breakout star. And then I was the second cast member mentioned by name because I was obnoxious and too loud. 
Judd: Little did they know that America would fall in love with that. 
Will: I clipped it out, and I put it in my office as a reminder that, Oh, people already hate you. 
Judd: How do you keep the takedowns from getting to you? You donÕt seem like the kind of person who would read a negative comment and ruminate over it. 
Will: IÕm definitely not impervious to it, by any means. But especially in that case, I was like, IÕm sorry, I know thatÕs funny. YouÕre just wrong. 
Judd: But people always have enjoyed you losing your composure. Is that something that you understood as being funny about you, even in high school? 
Will: I think it was the way I made my friends laugh. WeÕd go out into public looking like the normal guy next door. I would behave oddly and do strange things and get the best reaction. I knew it was cracking them up, and that registered with me as something I could go to. I donÕt know why it was fun to get that reaction from people that way, but it was. 
Judd: ThereÕs a genre of Will Ferrell comedy thatÕs the weird stuff. Right? Some of it is less weird. How do you divide, in your own mind, the type of comedy that really amuses you? 
Will: I think, especially as it pertains to Saturday Night Live, that there were sketches that were classified as a ten-to-one sketch, which meant that at ten minutes to one oÕclock, itÕs basically the last sketch of the night. The weirder stuff was usually saved until the end of the show, and I always relished trying to write those ten-to-one sketches. As much fun as I had doing the Spartan cheerleaders and the poppier things, IÕve always had weird thoughts pop into my head. Even just the notion of driving along and listening to Ò(DonÕt Fear) The Reaper,Ó by Blue Oyster Cult and hearing a faint sound of a cowbell. I donÕt know how I had that idea. I remember, the first time I heard that song, for some reason I focused on the cowbell, and I immediately thought, WhatÕs that guyÕs life like? Does he ever get to hang out? The sad weirdo whoÕs trying to be a part of the group really appeals to me. 
Judd: [Your] James Lipton [imitation] was one I find endlessly hilarious. 
Will: WhatÕs crazy about James Lipton is IÕd been watching Inside the Actors Studio, and I was a fan of the show. I was thinking this would be a fun character to play, but I hadnÕt written anything as of yet, and we were rehearsing, and one of the NBC pages at the show said, ÒBill MurrayÕs on the phone for you.Ó So, I ran to the page desk, which is right outside Studio 8H, and itÕs really Bill Murray. And he goes, ÒHey, how ya doing?Ó I was like, ÒUh, good. How are you, Bill?Ó I think weÕd only talked two other times prior to that, and he was like, ÒYou know who you should play? That host of Inside the Actors Studio. You should play James Lipton.Ó I go, ÒThatÕs so weird to say. I was literally thinking that myself,Ó and heÕs like, ÒYeah right. Take it easy.Ó Click. So, I was like, Oh, I have to write the sketch because Bill Murray told me IÕve got to write it. And that led to this funny relationship with James Lipton. 
Judd: ItÕs got to be a strange experience, to create this demented interpretation of someone and then actually meet that person. Who else did you meet that you had impersonated, that you then had to deal with face-to-face? 
Will: Well, that would be President Bush. I actually met him. But this is when he was still governor. It was during his presidential campaign, and he was at the show. TheyÕd gotten both Al Gore and him to pretape an opening piece for a political sketch package show, where we showed all the political sketches over the years. And they called me and said, ÒGovernor Bush is a huge fan. HeÕd love to meet you.Ó I walk in the studio, and itÕs massive press, like a hundred photographers and everything. No one from Saturday Night Live is making the introduction. Someone just pushes me and goes, ÒGo in there. Say hi.Ó And I walked in, and I startled him, and I think I literally had a weird beard on, from another sketch we were rehearsing, and I can tell he doesnÕt know. It was just awkward. 
Judd: Then you did the Broadway show that you wrote, and you were George Bush for months. 
Will: I really enjoyed getting a second chance to live in the character, doing eight shows a week, even though thatÕs one of the scariest things IÕve ever done. It was an amazing experience to get to do a satirical commentary on his presidency. And our opening night of previews was Inauguration Day, so it was still visceral for the New York crowd, in terms of their reaction to what I would say. There were moments when they would just yell things out at me, because they wanted to have a conversation with him. And I would end up improvising with the audience, which sometimes didnÕt really go anywhere, and other times it was kind of amazing. Overall, it was fun to hear his thoughts and have him wax poetic about the fictitious upbringing I wrote for him. We wove in the real facts with some made-up stuff. And every night, I would stand backstage going, Why did I agree to do this? It was terrifying. But by the end of the night, I was so happy. I would be like, Oh, itÕs so much fun. 
Judd: Did politicians or celebrities come to see the show and say hi afterward? 
Will: A few. It was a cross section there. We would always end the last chunk of the show with an improvised moment. Bush was famous for giving nicknames to everyone, so weÕd bring the house lights up, and I would say, ÒOkay, raise your hand and tell me what you do, your name, your professionÉIÕll give you a nickname.Ó So, IÕd go through the entire crowd and give names, and I would look out into the house, and it was just amazing. Once, I saw a guy sitting there and [I] was like, God, do I call on that guy? He looks exactly like Sting in a fishermanÕs sweater with a beard. I donÕt know if itÕs him, IÕll skip over it. And sure enough, that was Sting. It was always surreal, the whole thing. 
Judd: When you look back at your time at SNL, what are the first things that pop into your head that make you thrilled that they happened? 
Will: ItÕs hard to sum it all up, because there are so many of those moments. I just remember thinking to myself, This will be the hardest and yet most fun job youÕll ever have. Because you basically live there, and these dumb little ideas you may have on a Tuesday actually have a chance to be on national television by Saturday. I was always amazed at the fact that itÕs a live show, where you have a hundred people running around, changing sets and wardrobe. It never grew old to me. 
Judd: Anchorman was the first movie that you wrote, and for a lot of your movies, you seem to be playing, letÕs say, ignorant people who think theyÕre smart. What is it about a Ron Burgundy type that attracts you? 
Will: IÕve always been fascinated by the cocky American as a character. Growing up at the height of the 1980 Olympics and the Miracle on Ice ÑÒUSAÕs Number One!ÓÑI was the kid who asked, ÒWait, are we number one?Ó ItÕs always been fascinating to knock those people down a peg just because we can do it. ItÕs whatÕs great about this country at the same time.

BY JUDD APATOW 
Sicker in the Head: More Conversations About Life and Comedy ItÕs Garry ShandlingÕs Book Sick in the Head: Conversations About Life and Comedy I Found This Funny 
ABOUT THE AUTHOR 
JUDD APATOW is one of the most sought-after comedic minds in the industryÑhaving directed, produced, and written many of the biggest comedy films and hit TV shows of the last two decades. ApatowÕs most recent films include the Netflix comedy The Bubble, which he directed, produced, and co-wrote; the two-part George Carlin documentary for HBO, which he co-directed and produced; and UniversalÕs romantic comedy Bros, which he produced. Off-screen, Apatow gathered journal entries, photographs, and essays for an intimate portrait of Garry Shandling to create ItÕs Garry ShandlingÕs Book, a testament to the lasting impact Shandling had on Apatow personally and on the world. Published in 2019, the book expands on ApatowÕs Emmy AwardÐwinning HBO documentary The Zen Diaries of Garry Shandling. Apatow also authored the New York Times bestseller Sick in the Head and edited the collection I Found This Funny. Previous director credits include The 40-Year-Old Virgin, Knocked Up, Funny People, This Is 40, Trainwreck, May It Last: A Portrait of the Avett Brothers (co-directed with Mike Bonfiglio), and The King of Staten Island. He produced the Academy AwardÐ nominated The Big Sick and Bridesmaids, as well as Superbad, Pineapple Express, Anchorman, Step Brothers, Talladega Nights, Forgetting Sarah Marshall, and Popstar. For television, he executive produced Crashing (HBO), Girls, Freaks and Geeks, and Undeclared, and co-created The Ben Stiller Show and Love (Netflix). His Netflix comedy special, Judd Apatow: The Return, marked his comeback to the stage after a twenty-five-year hiatus.
